     u ∈ H¹₀, u′ = ∑bₖkπ√2 cos(kπx), so ‖u′‖₂² = ∑k²π²bₖ² ≥ π²∑bₖ² =
     π²‖u‖₂², with equality iff bₖ = 0 for k ≥ 2. The best constant is
     λ₁^(−1/2), λ₁ = π² the first Dirichlet eigenvalue of −d²/dx².
     RELLICH-KONDRACHOV: for bounded Ω, the inclusion H¹₀(Ω) ⊂ L²(Ω) is
     compact (on (0, 1): ‖u − P_Nu‖₂ ≤ ‖u′‖₂/((N + 1)π), a limit of finite-
     rank maps). It fails on R: translates u(x − n) are bounded in H¹ with
     no L²-convergent subsequence. Common mistakes: requiring only u ∈ Lᵖ
     and forgetting ∇u; treating a function with a jump as W^{1,p} (its
     derivative contains δ). Now you try: for which p is 1/|x| in
     W^{1,p}(B ⊂ R³)? (−1 > 1 − 3/p ⇔ p < 3/2)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Weak and norm convergence. For each sequence say whether it converges
    in norm (N), only weakly (W, give the limit), only weak* (W*, give the
    limit), or not at all (—).
    a) eₙ in ℓ²                         b) eₙ in ℓ¹
    c) eₙ in c₀                         d) eₙ/n in ℓ¹
    e) e₁ + eₙ in ℓ³                    f) sₙ = e₁ + ... + eₙ in c₀
    g) sₙ/n in ℓ²                       h) sₙ/√n in ℓ²
    i) sin(nx) in L²(0, 2π)             j) sin²(nx) in L²(0, 2π)
    k) |sin(nx)| in L²(0, π)            l) √n 𝟙_[0,1/n] in L²(0, 1)
    m) n𝟙_[0,1/n] in L¹(0, 1) (and viewed in C[0, 1]* = M[0, 1])
    n) 𝟙_[n,n+1] in L²(R), and in L¹(R)
    o) n^(1/3)𝟙_[0,1/n] in L²(0, 1)       p) e^(inx) in L²(−π, π)
    q) xⁿ in L²(0, 1)                   r) δ_{1/n} in C[0, 1]*
    s) eₙ in ℓ¹ viewed as c₀*           t) sₙ in ℓ^∞ viewed as (ℓ¹)*
    Answers:
      a) W, 0      b) — (Schur; test with (1, 1, ...) ∈ ℓ^∞)     c) W, 0
      d) N, 0      e) W, e₁      f) — (weakly Cauchy, no weak limit)
      g) N, 0 (norm 1/√n)        h) W, 0 (norm 1)       i) W, 0
      j) W, 1/2    k) W, 2/π     l) W, 0 (norm 1)
      m) — in L¹ (not uniformly integrable); W* to δ₀ in M[0, 1]
      n) W, 0 in L²; — in L¹ (test with 1 ∈ L^∞)
      o) N, 0 (norm n^(−1/6))    p) W, 0      q) N, 0 (norm (2n + 1)^(−1/2))
      r) W*, δ₀ (not weakly: test with μ ↦ μ({0}) ∈ M**; ‖δ_{1/n} − δ₀‖ = 2)
      s) W*, 0     t) W*, (1, 1, 1, ...)

M2. For each space give its dual, and say whether it is reflexive (R)
    and separable (S).
    a) ℓ³               b) c₀              c) ℓ¹              d) ℓ^∞
    e) C[0, 1]          f) L¹(0, 1)        g) L^∞(0, 1)       h) L⁴(R)
    i) H¹(0, 1)         j) W^{1,3}(0, 1)   k) M[0, 1] (measures, total
                                             variation norm)
    l) c (convergent sequences)             m) a closed subspace of ℓ²
    n) Is the weak topology of ℓ² metrisable? Is it metrisable on the
       closed unit ball?
    o) The weak closure of the unit sphere of ℓ².
    p) Is c₀ weakly sequentially complete? Is ℓ¹?
    q) The weak* closure of c₀ inside ℓ^∞ = (ℓ¹)*.
    r) Does J(B_X) equal B_{X**} for X = c₀?
    Answers:
      a) ℓ^(3/2); R, S          b) ℓ¹; not R, S       c) ℓ^∞; not R, S
      d) ba(N) (finitely additive measures) ⊋ ℓ¹; not R, not S
      e) M[0, 1] (regular signed Borel measures); not R, S
      f) L^∞(0, 1); not R, S   g) ⊋ L¹ (finitely additive); not R, not S
      h) L^(4/3)(R); R, S      i) itself (Hilbert); R, S
      j) reflexive (closed subspace of L³ × L³); R, S
      k) not R, not S (‖δₓ − δ_y‖ = 2 for x ≠ y, uncountably many)
      l) ℓ¹ (isomorphically); not R, S        m) itself; R, S
      n) no; yes (the dual ℓ² is separable)  o) the closed unit ball
      p) c₀ no (sₙ in M1 f); ℓ¹ yes         q) all of ℓ^∞
      r) no: J(B_{c₀}) is only weak* dense in B_{ℓ^∞} (Goldstine)

M3. Compact (C) or not (N)?
    a) diag(1/n) on ℓ²                 b) diag(1 + 1/n) on ℓ²
    c) diag(sin n) on ℓ²               d) the right shift S on ℓ²
    e) the weighted shift Teₙ = eₙ₊₁/n  f) x ↦ ⟨x, u⟩v on a Hilbert space
    g) Volterra V on L²[0, 1]           h) Volterra V on C[0, 1]
    i) M_x on L²[0, 1]                  j) kernel e^(−|x−y|) on L²[0, 1]
    k) kernel e^(−|x−y|) on L²(R)       l) Fourier transform on L²(R)
    m) inclusion C¹[0, 1] → C[0, 1]      n) inclusion H¹(0, 1) → L²(0, 1)
    o) inclusion H¹(R) → L²(R)          p) inclusion ℓ¹ → ℓ²
    q) projection onto span{e₂, e₄, e₆, ...}
    r) KB with K compact, B bounded     s) d/dx : H¹(0, 1) → L²(0, 1)
    t) any bounded operator ℓ² → ℓ¹
    u) the Hilbert matrix aₘₙ = 1/(m + n − 1) on ℓ²
    v) the matrix aₘₙ = 1/(m + n)² on ℓ²
    Answers:
      a) C   b) N   c) N (sin n ↛ 0)   d) N   e) C (limit of finite rank)
      f) C (rank 1)  g) C (Hilbert-Schmidt)   h) C (Arzelà-Ascoli)
      i) N   j) C (L² kernel on a bounded square)
      k) N (convolution: unitarily a multiplication by 2/(1 + ξ²))
      l) N (unitary up to √(2π))   m) C (Arzelà-Ascoli)   n) C (Rellich)
      o) N (translates)   p) N (eₙ has no ℓ²-convergent subsequence)
      q) N (infinite rank projection)   r) C (ideal)
      s) N (sin(nπx)/(nπ) bounded in H¹, images cos(nπx) orthogonal)
      t) C (Pitt's theorem)   u) N (bounded, norm π, spectrum [0, π])
      v) C (Hilbert-Schmidt: ∑1/(m + n)⁴ < ∞)

M4. Give the operator norm, the Hilbert-Schmidt norm and the non-zero
    eigenvalues (integral operators on L²[0, 1] unless stated).
    a) diag(1/n) on ℓ²                 b) diag(1/√n) on ℓ²
    c) kernel xy                       d) kernel 1
    e) kernel x + y                    f) kernel x − y
    g) kernel e^(x+y)                  h) kernel cos(x − y) on [0, 2π]
    i) kernel sin x sin y on [0, π]    j) kernel min(x, y)
    k) kernel min(x, y) − xy           l) Volterra kernel 𝟙_{y<x}
    m) matrix [[1, 2], [2, 1]]         n) matrix [[0, 1], [0, 0]]
    Answers:
      a) 1; π/√6 ≈ 1.28255; 1/n       b) 1; ∞ (compact, not HS); 1/√n
      c) 1/3; 1/3; 1/3 (eigenfunction x)          d) 1; 1; 1
      e) 1/2 + 1/√3 ≈ 1.07735; √(7/6) ≈ 1.08012; 1/2 ± 1/√3
      f) 1/(2√3) ≈ 0.28868; 1/√6 ≈ 0.40825; ±i/(2√3) (skew-adjoint)
      g) (e² − 1)/2 ≈ 3.19453 (all three)
      h) π; π√2; π (multiplicity 2: cos x, sin x)   i) π/2 (all three)
      j) 4/π² ≈ 0.40528; 1/√6 ≈ 0.40825; 1/((k − ½)²π²)
      k) 1/π² ≈ 0.10132; 1/√90 ≈ 0.10541; 1/(k²π²)
      l) 2/π ≈ 0.63662; 1/√2 ≈ 0.70711; none (σ = {0})
      m) 3; √10 ≈ 3.16228; 3, −1       n) 1; 1; none (nilpotent)

M5. Find σ(T) and classify its points (p = point, c = continuous,
    r = residual).
    a) diag(1/n) on ℓ²                b) right shift S on ℓ²(N)
    c) left shift L on ℓ²(N)          d) bilateral shift on ℓ²(Z)
    e) M_x on L²[0, 1]                f) M_φ, φ = 𝟙_[0,1/2], on L²[0, 1]
    g) M_{x²} on L²[−1, 1]             h) Volterra V on L²[0, 1]
    i) weighted shift Teₙ = eₙ₊₁/n     j) an orthogonal projection P ≠ 0, I
    k) −d²/dx² on L²(0, π), u(0) = u(π) = 0
    l) −i d/dx on L²(0, 2π), periodic   m) −d²/dx² on L²(R)
    n) the unitary Fourier transform (2π)^(−1/2)∫f e^(−ixξ) dx on L²(R)
    o) d/dx on L²(0, 1) with domain {u ∈ H¹ : u(0) = 0}
    p) the rotation [[0, −1], [1, 0]] on C²
    q) diag(dₙ), dₙ an enumeration of Q ∩ [0, 1]
    Answers:
      a) {0} ∪ {1/n}: 1/n p, 0 c     b) D̄: D r, T c     c) D̄: D p, T c
      d) T, all c (unitary)          e) [0, 1], all c
      f) {0, 1}, both p (infinite multiplicity)          g) [0, 1], all c
      h) {0}, c                      i) {0}, r (range ⊆ e₁^⊥)
      j) {0, 1}, both p              k) {1, 4, 9, ...}, all p
      l) Z, all p (eⁱⁿˣ)            m) [0, ∞), all c
      n) {1, −i, −1, i}, all p (Hermite functions)
      o) ∅ (the inverse is the Volterra operator: compact, quasinilpotent)
      p) {i, −i}, p                  q) [0, 1]: rationals p, irrationals c

M6. Fredholm and Volterra integral equations on [0, 1] (unless stated).
    Solve, or give the condition for solvability.
    a) f(x) − λ∫₀¹ f(y) dy = 1, λ ≠ 1
    b) f(x) − ∫₀¹ xy f(y) dy = x
    c) f(x) − 2∫₀¹ xy f(y) dy = x²
    d) f(x) − ½∫₀¹ f(y) dy = x
    e) f(x) − ∫₀¹ f(y) dy = g(x): when solvable? solve for g = x − 1/2
    f) the characteristic values λ (f = λKf) for the kernel x + y
    g) the characteristic value of the kernel x²y²
    h) f(x) − (1/2π)∫₀^{2π} cos(x − y)f(y) dy = cos x on [0, 2π]
    i) f(x) − (1/π)∫₀^π sin(x + y)f(y) dy = 1 on [0, π]
    j) the characteristic values of sin(x + y) on [0, π]
    k) f(x) − ∫₀ˣ f(y) dy = 1           l) f(x) − λ∫₀ˣ f(y) dy = 1
    m) f(x) = x + ∫₀ˣ (x − y)f(y) dy
    n) the Fredholm index of I − K (K compact), of S, of L, of Sᵏ
    Answers:
      a) f = 1/(1 − λ)                  b) f = 3x/2
      c) f = x² + 3x/2                  d) f = x + 1/2
      e) iff ∫₀¹ g = 0; f = x − 1/2 + C, C arbitrary
      f) 4√3 − 6 ≈ 0.92820 and −4√3 − 6 ≈ −12.92820 (reciprocals of
         1/2 ± 1/√3)
      g) 5 (eigenvalue ∫y⁴ = 1/5)       h) f = 2cos x
      i) f = 1 + (4/(3π))(sin x + 2cos x)
      j) ±2/π (eigenvalues ±π/2, eigenfunctions sin x ± cos x)
      k) f = eˣ (Volterra: unique for every λ)       l) f = e^(λx)
      m) f = sinh x (f″ = f, f(0) = 0, f′(0) = 1)
      n) 0; −1; +1; −k

M7. Spectral radius and functional calculus.
    a) r and ‖·‖₂ of [[0, 1], [0, 0]]     b) r of [[1, 10], [0, 1/2]]
    c) r of [[2, 3], [0, −4]]           d) r of [[0, 2], [8, 0]]
    e) r(V), V Volterra                 f) r(S), S the right shift
    g) r of the weighted shift with weights (5, 1, 1, 1, ...)
    h) σ(T² + T) if T = T* and σ(T) = [−1, 1]
    i) σ(T⁻¹) if σ(T) = {1, 2} ∪ [3, 4]
    j) σ(e^A) if A = A* and σ(A) = [0, ln 2]
    k) √A for A = [[5, 4], [4, 5]]       l) √A for A = [[2, 1], [1, 2]]
    m) e^A for A = [[0, −t], [t, 0]]      n) the spectral projection of
                                           [[2, 1], [1, 2]] for λ = 3
    o) ‖(M_x − λ)⁻¹‖ on L²[0, 1] for λ = 2, i, ½ + i/2, −3
    p) |A| and the polar decomposition of A = [[0, 2], [0, 0]]
    q) |S| and |L| for the shifts on ℓ²
    r) ‖f(T)‖ for T = diag(1/n) and f(t) = t(1 − t)
    Answers:
      a) 0 and 1           b) 1              c) 4             d) 4
      e) 0 (‖Vⁿ‖ ≤ 1/(n − 1)!)       f) 1          g) 1 (‖Tⁿ‖ = 5 for n ≥ 1)
      h) [−1/4, 2] (t² + t on [−1, 1])            i) {1, 1/2} ∪ [1/4, 1/3]
      j) [1, 2]            k) [[2, 1], [1, 2]]
      l) ½[[√3 + 1, √3 − 1], [√3 − 1, √3 + 1]] ≈ [[1.36603, 0.36603],
         [0.36603, 1.36603]]
      m) [[cos t, −sin t], [sin t, cos t]]       n) ½[[1, 1], [1, 1]]
      o) 1, 1, 2, 1/3
      p) |A| = [[0, 0], [0, 2]], A = U|A| with U = [[0, 1], [0, 0]] (a
         partial isometry)
      q) |S| = I (S*S = I); |L| = I − P, P the projection onto span{e₁}
      r) 1/4 (sup of t(1 − t) on {0} ∪ {1/n}, attained at t = 1/2)

M8. Banach algebras and Gelfand theory.
    a) σ(f) in C[0, 1] for f(x) = x² − x; for f(x) = eˣ
    b) the maximal ideals of C[0, 1]
    c) the maximal ideal space of ℓ¹(Z); of ℓ¹(N₀); of L¹(R); of c₀ ⊕ C1
       (convergent sequences c)
    d) σ(δ₁) in ℓ¹(Z) and in ℓ¹(N₀)
    e) σ(δ₁ + δ₋₁) in ℓ¹(Z), and its spectral radius
    f) ‖a‖ and r(a) for a = δ₀ + ½δ₁ in ℓ¹(Z)
    g) the inverse of δ₀ − ½δ₁ in ℓ¹(Z) and its norm
    h) the inverse of 5δ₀ + 2δ₁ + 2δ₋₁ in ℓ¹(Z)
    i) is δ₀ + δ₁ invertible in ℓ¹(Z)?
    j) is 2δ₀ + δ₁ invertible in ℓ¹(N₀)? inverse?
    k) is δ₀ + 2δ₁ invertible in ℓ¹(N₀)? in ℓ¹(Z)? inverse?
    l) σ(z) in the disc algebra A(D) and in C(T)
    m) the real Banach division algebras
    n) ‖a*a‖ versus ‖a‖² for a = δ₀ + δ₁ − δ₂ in ℓ¹(Z)
    o) ‖a‖ and r(a) for a = [[1, 1], [0, 1]] in M₂(C)
    p) the Gelfand transform of f ∈ L¹(R) and why it vanishes at ±∞
    q) is the group of invertible elements of C[0, 1] connected?
    Answers:
      a) [−1/4, 0]; [1, e]           b) Mₓ = {f : f(x) = 0}, x ∈ [0, 1]
      c) T; D̄; R (Fourier transform); N ∪ {∞} (one-point
         compactification)
      d) T; D̄                        e) [−2, 2]; 2 (= ‖a‖)
      f) 3/2 and 3/2                  g) ∑_{n≥0} 2⁻ⁿδₙ; norm 2
      h) ∑(1/3)(−1/2)^|n| δₙ (norm 1)
      i) no: â(−1) = 1 − 1 = 0
      j) yes (2 + z ≠ 0 on D̄); ∑_{n≥0} ½(−½)ⁿδₙ
      k) no in ℓ¹(N₀) (1 + 2z = 0 at z = −½ ∈ D̄); yes in ℓ¹(Z), inverse
         ∑_{k≥1} (−1)^(k−1)2⁻ᵏδ₋ₖ
      l) D̄; T                        m) R, C, H (Frobenius-Mazur)
      n) 5 ≠ 9: ℓ¹(Z) is not a C*-algebra
      o) (1 + √5)/2 ≈ 1.61803 and 1 (not normal)
      p) f̂(ξ) = ∫f e^(−ixξ) dx; Gelfand transforms of a non-unital algebra
         lie in C₀(Δ) (Riemann-Lebesgue lemma)
      q) yes: every non-vanishing f ∈ C([0, 1], C) is f = e^g (continuous
         logarithm), joined to 1 by e^(tg); for REAL C[0, 1] no (two
         components f > 0 and f < 0)

M9. Distributions on R: compute (δ_a = Dirac mass at a).
    a) H′                  b) (sign x)′         c) |x|′ and |x|″
    d) (x₊)″, x₊ = max(x, 0)                      e) (𝟙_[0,1])′
    f) ⌊x⌋′                g) (e^(−|x|))″       h) (H(x)sin x)″ + H(x)sin x
    i) (H(x)e^(ax))′ − aH(x)e^(ax)               j) xδ, xδ′, x²δ″
    k) (sin x)δ′, (cos x)δ′, eˣδ′                 l) δ(2x), δ(x² − 1)
    m) (log|x|)′            n) x · p.v.(1/x)
    o) the limits in D′ as n → ∞ of: n𝟙_[0,1/n], n/(π(1 + n²x²)),
       sin(nx), n sin(nx), sin(nx)/(πx), n(δ_{1/n} − δ₀), n²(δ_{1/n} − δ₀)
    p) the order of δ, δ′, p.v.(1/x), ∑_{n≥1} δₙ^(n)
    q) supp δ″, supp p.v.(1/x), supp(H′)
    r) which are tempered: eˣ, e^(x²), eˣcos(eˣ), log|x|, ∑_{n∈Z} δₙ
    Answers:
      a) δ         b) 2δ         c) sign x; 2δ        d) δ
      e) δ₀ − δ₁   f) ∑_{n∈Z} δₙ    g) e^(−|x|) − 2δ     h) δ
      i) δ         j) 0; −δ; 2δ
      k) −δ; δ′; δ′ − δ (rule: gδ′ = g(0)δ′ − g′(0)δ)
      l) ½δ; ½(δ₁ + δ₋₁)           m) p.v.(1/x)         n) 1
      o) δ; δ; 0; 0 (= ∫cos(nx)φ′ → 0); δ; −δ′; no limit (diverges
         like nφ′(0) whenever φ′(0) ≠ 0)
      p) 0; 1; 1; infinite (locally finite, but no global bound)
      q) {0}; R; {0}
      r) eˣ no; e^(x²) no; eˣcos(eˣ) yes (the derivative of sin(eˣ));
         log|x| yes; ∑δₙ yes

M10. Fourier transforms in S′(R) (f̂(ξ) = ∫f(x)e^(−ixξ) dx) and
     fundamental solutions.
     a) δ           b) δ_a          c) 1            d) δ′
     e) x           f) x²           g) e^(iax)      h) cos(ax)

     q) x e^(−x²/2)
     r) ∫_R (sin x/x)² dx and ∫_R dx/(1 + x²)² via Plancherel
     s) fundamental solutions of: d/dx, d/dx − a, d²/dx², d²/dx² + 1,
        −d²/dx² + k² (k > 0), Δ in R³, Δ in R², ∂_t − ∂²_x, ∂²_t − ∂²_x
     t) solve u″ − u = δ in S′

M11. Sobolev spaces. Decide membership, or compute.
     a) |x|^(1/2) on (−1, 1): for which p in W^{1,p}?
     b) |x| on (−1, 1): in W^{1,∞}? in W^{2,p}?
     c) H(x) on (−1, 1): in W^{1,1}?
     d) x^α on (0, 1): in H¹ for which α?
     e) |x|^α on B₁ ⊂ R³: in H¹ for which α?
     f) 1/|x| on B₁ ⊂ R³: in W^{1,p} for which p?
     g) log|x| on B₁ ⊂ R²: in W^{1,p} for which p?
     h) log log(e/|x|) on B₁ ⊂ R²: in H¹? bounded?
     i) 𝟙_[−1,1] ∈ H^s(R) for which s?   j) e^(−|x|) ∈ H^s(R)?
     k) (1 − |x|)₊ ∈ H^s(R)?             l) δ ∈ H^s(R)? in H^s(R³)?
     m) e^(−x²) ∈ H^s(R)?
     n) p* for W^{1,p}(Rⁿ) ⊂ L^{p*}: (n, p) = (3, 2), (4, 2), (2, 1),
        (5, 3)
     o) the Hölder exponent in Morrey's embedding for (n, p) = (1, 2),
        (2, 4), (3, 6), (3, 4)
     p) is H^s(Rⁿ) ⊂ C₀ for: H²(R³), H¹(R²), H^(3/4)(R), H²(R⁴)?
     q) q with W^{2,2}(R⁵) ⊂ L^q (1/q = 1/p − k/n)
     r) the best Poincaré constants on H¹₀(0, L) and H¹₀((0, π)²)
     s) compact or not: H¹(0, 1) ⊂ L²(0, 1); H¹(R) ⊂ L²(R);
        H¹(0, 1) ⊂ C[0, 1]; H¹₀(Ω) ⊂ L⁶(Ω) (Ω ⊂ R³ bounded)
     t) is the constant 1 in H¹₀(0, 1)?
     Answers:
       a) 1 ≤ p < 2    b) yes; no for every p (u″ = 2δ)
       c) no (H′ = δ is not a function)       d) α > 1/2 or α = 0
       e) α > −1/2     f) p < 3/2             g) p < 2
       h) yes (∫|∇u|² = 2π); no, unbounded at 0
       i) s < 1/2      j) s < 3/2             k) s < 3/2
       l) s < −1/2; s < −3/2                  m) all s
       n) 6; 4; 2; 15/2                       o) 1/2; 1/2; 1/2; 1/4
       p) yes; no; yes; no (need s > n/2)     q) 10
       r) L/π; 1/√2 (λ₁ = 1² + 1² = 2)
       s) compact; not compact; compact (C^{0,1/2} + Arzelà-Ascoli); not
          compact (6 = 2* is the critical exponent)
       t) no (its trace at 0 and 1 is 1 ≠ 0; H¹₀ ⊊ H¹)

M12. True (T) or false (F)? Give a counterexample for F.
     a) Every weakly convergent sequence is bounded.
     b) In ℓ² weak convergence implies norm convergence.
     c) In ℓ¹ weakly convergent sequences converge in norm.
     d) The closed unit ball of every Banach space is weakly compact.
     e) The closed unit ball of X* is weak* compact.
     f) A norm-closed convex set is weakly closed.
     g) A norm-closed subspace of a dual space X* is weak* closed.
     h) Every compact operator on an infinite-dimensional space has an
        eigenvalue.
     i) On a Hilbert space every compact operator is a norm limit of
        finite-rank operators.
     j) The same holds on every Banach space.
     k) For compact K and λ ≠ 0, K − λ injective ⇒ K − λ surjective.
     l) The spectrum of a self-adjoint operator consists of eigenvalues.
     m) r(T) = ‖T‖ for every bounded T.
     n) The spectrum of an element depends on the ambient Banach algebra.
     o) Every commutative unital C*-algebra is *-isomorphic to some C(K).
     p) Every distribution is a locally integrable function.
     q) The product of distributions is associative when defined.
     r) Every compactly supported distribution has finite order.
     s) W^{1,1}(0, 1) ⊂ C[0, 1].
     t) H¹(R²) ⊂ L^∞(R²).
     u) If u ∈ H¹(Ω), Ω connected, and ∇u = 0 a.e., u is constant.
     v) The inclusion H¹₀(Ω) ⊂ L²(Ω) is compact for every open Ω.
     Answers:
       a) T (uniform boundedness)          b) F: eₙ
       c) T (Schur)                        d) F: c₀ (sₙ), ℓ¹, C[0, 1]
       e) T (Banach-Alaoglu)               f) T (Mazur)
       g) F: c₀ ⊂ ℓ^∞ = (ℓ¹)* is weak* dense
       h) F: Volterra operator             i) T
       j) F: Enflo's space without the approximation property
       k) T (Fredholm alternative)         l) F: M_x on L²[0, 1]
       m) F: [[0, 1], [0, 0]] (T for normal T)
       n) T: z in A(D) versus C(T)         o) T (Gelfand-Naimark)
       p) F: δ
       q) F: (δ·x)·p.v.(1/x) = 0 but δ·(x·p.v.(1/x)) = δ
       r) T                                s) T (absolutely continuous
                                              representative)
       t) F: log log(e/|x|) cut off smoothly   u) T
       v) F: Ω = R (translates of a bump)

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: keep three "zoo" examples on the board and test every
  statement against them: the unit vectors eₙ (weak but not norm
  convergence), the right and left shifts (spectrum ≠ eigenvalues,
  residual versus point spectrum, index ±1), and the Volterra operator
  (compact, quasinilpotent, no eigenvalues, ‖V‖ = 2/π). For compact self-
  adjoint integral operators always convert Tu = λu into an ODE with
  boundary conditions by differentiating the kernel twice. In Banach
  algebras, compute spectra through the Gelfand transform (a Fourier
  series for ℓ¹(Z)). For distributions insist on the ritual "pair with φ,
  move the derivative, integrate by parts, read off the δ's from the
  jumps", and for Sobolev membership always use polar coordinates:
  ∫_{B₁} r^β dx < ∞ ⇔ β + n > 0. Harder: prove Banach-Alaoglu via
  Tychonoff and Eberlein-Šmulian for separable spaces; prove the spectral
  theorem for bounded self-adjoint operators via the Riesz representation
  of the functional f ↦ ⟨f(T)x, x⟩; prove Atkinson's theorem (Fredholm ⇔
  invertible modulo compacts) and compute the index of Toeplitz
  operators T_φ = −winding number of φ; prove the Gelfand-Naimark theorem
  using Stone-Weierstrass; show that every distribution supported at 0
  is a finite combination of derivatives of δ; prove the Gagliardo-
  Nirenberg-Sobolev inequality for p = 1 and deduce the general case;
  prove Rellich-Kondrachov via Fréchet-Kolmogorov; and use Lax-Milgram
  plus Rellich to prove that the Dirichlet Laplacian on a bounded domain
  has a discrete spectrum 0 < λ₁ ≤ λ₂ ≤ ... → ∞ with an orthonormal
  eigenbasis of L²(Ω).


================================================================================
TOPIC 12.3 — COMPLEX ANALYSIS (GRADUATE): RIEMANN MAPPING THEOREM, HARMONIC
FUNCTIONS, ENTIRE AND MEROMORPHIC FUNCTIONS, WEIERSTRASS AND MITTAG-LEFFLER,
ANALYTIC CONTINUATION, RIEMANN SURFACES (INTRODUCTION), ELLIPTIC FUNCTIONS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - HARMONIC FUNCTIONS: u ∈ C² with Δu = u_xx + u_yy = 0. Real parts of
    holomorphic functions are harmonic; on a SIMPLY CONNECTED domain every
    harmonic u has a HARMONIC CONJUGATE v (u + iv holomorphic), unique up
    to a constant; on C ∖ {0}, u = ln|z| has none. MEAN VALUE PROPERTY
    u(a) = (1/2π)∫u(a + re^{iθ}) dθ, MAXIMUM PRINCIPLE, and conversely a
    continuous function with the mean value property is harmonic.
  - POISSON INTEGRAL: for g continuous on ∂D, u(re^{iφ}) =
    (1/2π)∫P_r(φ − θ)g(θ) dθ, P_r(θ) = (1 − r²)/(1 − 2r cos θ + r²) =
    ∑r^|n| e^{inθ}, solves the DIRICHLET PROBLEM on D (u = g on ∂D).
    HARNACK: u ≥ 0 harmonic on D ⇒ ((1 − r)/(1 + r))u(0) ≤ u(z) ≤
    ((1 + r)/(1 − r))u(0), |z| = r. Liouville: a harmonic function on R²
    bounded below is constant. Uniform limits of harmonic functions are
    harmonic.
  - SCHWARZ LEMMA: f : D → D holomorphic, f(0) = 0 ⇒ |f(z)| ≤ |z|,
    |f′(0)| ≤ 1, equality (at one z ≠ 0 or for f′(0)) iff f(z) = e^{iθ}z.
    Aut(D) = {e^{iθ}φ_a}, φ_a(z) = (z − a)/(1 − āz), |a| < 1. SCHWARZ-PICK:
    |f′(z)| ≤ (1 − |f(z)|²)/(1 − |z|²); holomorphic self-maps of D do not
    increase the pseudo-hyperbolic distance |z − w|/|1 − w̄z|.
  - NORMAL FAMILIES: MONTEL — a locally uniformly bounded family of
    holomorphic functions is normal (every sequence has a locally uniformly
    convergent subsequence). HURWITZ — a locally uniform limit of
    zero-free (resp. injective) holomorphic functions on a domain is
    zero-free (injective) or constant.
  - RIEMANN MAPPING THEOREM: every simply connected domain Ω ⊊ C is
    conformally equivalent to D; for z₀ ∈ Ω there is a UNIQUE conformal
    f : Ω → D with f(z₀) = 0, f′(z₀) > 0. Fails for Ω = C (Liouville).
    Proof: √ trick gives an injective map into D; Montel + Hurwitz give an
    extremal map maximising |f′(z₀)|; the √ trick again shows it is onto.
    Non-simply connected domains have moduli: 1 < |z| < R₁ and
    1 < |z| < R₂ are equivalent iff R₁ = R₂.
  - STANDARD CONFORMAL MAPS: Möbius maps; Cayley C(z) = (z − i)/(z + i)
    (upper half-plane H → D); z ↦ z^α (sectors); e^z (strip 0 < Im z < π
    → H); (1 + z)/(1 − z) (D → right half-plane); Joukowski z + 1/z; sin z
    (half-strip → H). Aut(H) = PSL₂(R).
  - ENTIRE FUNCTIONS: M(r) = max_{|z|=r}|f|; ORDER ρ = lim sup
    ln ln M(r)/ln r; for f = ∑aₙzⁿ, ρ = lim sup n ln n/ln(1/|aₙ|). e^z,
    sin z have order 1, e^{z²} order 2, cos √z order 1/2, e^{e^z} order
    ∞. Liouville; |f(z)| ≤ A + B|z|ᴺ ⇒ f is a polynomial of degree ≤ N.
    LITTLE PICARD: a non-constant entire function omits at most one value.
  - JENSEN'S FORMULA: f holomorphic on |z| ≤ R, f(0) ≠ 0, zeros a₁, ...,
    aₙ in |z| < R: ln|f(0)| = ∑ln(|aₖ|/R) + (1/2π)∫ln|f(Re^{iθ})| dθ.
    Consequence: n(r) = O(r^{ρ+ε}); EXPONENT OF CONVERGENCE of the zeros
    ≤ ρ.
  - INFINITE PRODUCTS: ∏(1 + aₙ) converges absolutely iff ∑|aₙ| < ∞ (a
    convergent product is non-zero unless a factor is 0). WEIERSTRASS
    ELEMENTARY FACTORS E₀(z) = 1 − z, E_p(z) = (1 − z)exp(z + z²/2 + ... +
    z^p/p), |1 − E_p(z)| ≤ |z|^{p+1} for |z| ≤ 1.
  - WEIERSTRASS FACTORIZATION: for any sequence aₙ → ∞ (with multiplicity)
    there is an entire function with exactly these zeros, f(z) =
    z^m e^{g(z)}∏E_{pₙ}(z/aₙ), where ∑(r/|aₙ|)^{pₙ+1} < ∞ for all r.
    HADAMARD: if ρ < ∞, pₙ = p = genus of the zeros and g is a polynomial
    of degree ≤ ρ. Key products: sin πz = πz∏(1 − z²/n²); 1/Γ(z) =
    z e^{γz}∏(1 + z/n)e^{−z/n}.
  - MEROMORPHIC FUNCTIONS: holomorphic except for isolated poles. Every
    meromorphic function on C is a quotient of two entire functions;
    meromorphic functions on Ĉ are exactly the rational functions.
    MITTAG-LEFFLER: poles bₙ → ∞ with any prescribed principal parts Pₙ
    are realised by ∑(Pₙ(z) − Qₙ(z)), Qₙ a Taylor polynomial of Pₙ at 0.
    Key: π cot πz = 1/z + ∑_{n≥1}2z/(z² − n²); π²/sin²πz = ∑1/(z − n)².
  - GAMMA FUNCTION: Γ(z) = ∫₀^∞ t^{z−1}e^{−t} dt (Re z > 0) continues via
    Γ(z) = Γ(z + n)/(z(z + 1)...(z + n − 1)) to a meromorphic function with
    simple poles at −n, residue (−1)ⁿ/n!, and no zeros; Γ(z)Γ(1 − z) =
    π/sin πz; Γ(1/2) = √π.
  - ANALYTIC CONTINUATION: identity theorem ⇒ continuations are unique;
    function elements (f, D), continuation along curves (power-series
    chains); MONODROMY THEOREM: continuation along homotopic curves gives
    the same result, so on a simply connected domain unrestricted
    continuation gives a single-valued function. The radius of a Taylor
    series is the distance to the nearest singularity. NATURAL BOUNDARY:
    ∑z^{2ⁿ}, ∑z^{n!} (gap series). SCHWARZ REFLECTION: f real on a real
    segment extends by f(z̄) = conj f(z).
  - RIEMANN SURFACES: a connected Hausdorff space with holomorphically
    compatible charts. Examples: Ĉ, domains, tori C/Λ, the surfaces of √z
    and log z, compact curves y² = p(z). On a COMPACT Riemann surface
    every holomorphic function is constant; a non-constant holomorphic map
    between compact surfaces has a DEGREE. RIEMANN-HURWITZ: 2g_X − 2 =
    d(2g_Y − 2) + ∑(e_P − 1). y² = p(z), p of degree n with distinct
    roots, has genus ⌈n/2⌉ − 1.
  - ELLIPTIC FUNCTIONS: meromorphic f with f(z + ω) = f(z) for all ω in a
    lattice Λ = Zω₁ + Zω₂. LIOUVILLE'S THEOREMS: an entire elliptic
    function is constant; the residues in a period parallelogram sum to 0
    (so no elliptic function of order 1); #zeros = #poles = order;
    ∑zeros − ∑poles ∈ Λ (ABEL).
  - WEIERSTRASS ℘: ℘(z) = 1/z² + ∑'(1/(z − ω)² − 1/ω²), even, order 2,
    ℘ = z⁻² + 3G₄z² + 5G₆z⁴ + ..., G_{2k} = ∑'ω^{−2k};
    (℘′)² = 4℘³ − g₂℘ − g₃, g₂ = 60G₄, g₃ = 140G₆ = 4e₁e₂e₃, eᵢ = ℘(ωᵢ/2)
    distinct; Δ = g₂³ − 27g₃² ≠ 0, j = 1728g₂³/Δ. Every elliptic
    function lies in C(℘, ℘′); even ones in C(℘). z ↦ (℘(z), ℘′(z))
    identifies C/Λ with the cubic curve y² = 4x³ − g₂x − g₃.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. (a) Show u = x³ − 3xy² + y is harmonic on R², find a harmonic
    conjugate v and write u + iv as a function of z. (b) Prove the mean
    value property for harmonic functions and deduce the maximum
    principle. (c) Show ln|z| is harmonic on C ∖ {0} but has no harmonic
    conjugate there.
    Answer: (a) v = 3x²y − y³ − x + C, u + iv = z³ − iz + iC. (b) from
    Cauchy's formula. (c) a conjugate would be a continuous arg z.
    Teacher explains: (a) u_xx = 6x, u_yy = −6x, so Δu = 0. Solve the
    Cauchy-Riemann equations v_y = u_x = 3x² − 3y² and v_x = −u_y =
    6xy − 1. Integrating the first in y: v = 3x²y − y³ + h(x); then v_x =
    6xy + h′(x) = 6xy − 1 gives h(x) = −x + C. Check against z³ = x³ −
    3xy² + i(3x²y − y³) and −iz = y − ix: u + iv = z³ − iz (+ iC). A fast
    method: f′ = u_x − iu_y; set y = 0 to get f′(z) = 3z² − i, so f = z³ −
    iz + const. (b) On a disc D(a, R) inside the domain, u = Re f (the
    disc is simply connected). Cauchy: f(a) = (1/2πi)∮f(ζ)/(ζ − a) dζ =
    (1/2π)∫₀^{2π} f(a + re^{iθ}) dθ; take real parts. MAXIMUM PRINCIPLE: if
    u attains its max M at an interior point a, the average of u on each
    small circle is M while u ≤ M, so u ≡ M near a; the set {u = M} is
    open and closed, hence everything (connected domain). (c) Δ ln r =
    (1/r)(r · (1/r))′ = 0. If v were a conjugate, f = ln|z| + iv would
    satisfy f′ = 1/z (compute f′ = u_x − iu_y = (x − iy)/|z|² = 1/z), so
    ∮_{|z|=1} f′ dz = 0 (f′ has a primitive); but ∮dz/z = 2πi. Common
    mistakes: forgetting the integration "constant" h(x); claiming every
    harmonic function has a conjugate (only on simply connected domains).
    Now you try: the average of u = x³ − 3xy² + y over the circle
    |z − 1| = 2. (u(1, 0) = 1)

D2. (a) Solve the Dirichlet problem on the unit disc with boundary values
    g(θ) = cos²θ, and with g = 1 on the upper semicircle, 0 on the lower.
    (b) Prove Harnack's inequality from the Poisson kernel and deduce that
    a positive harmonic function on R² is constant.
    Answer: (a) u = 1/2 + (x² − y²)/2; u = 1/2 + (1/π)arctan(2y/(1 − x² −
    y²)), e.g. u(i/2) ≈ 0.79517. (b) (1 − r)/(1 + r) ≤ P_r ≤ (1 + r)/(1 − r).
    Teacher explains: for z = re^{iφ}, P_r(φ − θ) = Re((e^{iθ} + z)/
    (e^{iθ} − z)) = ∑r^|n|e^{in(φ−θ)}. Hence a boundary Fourier mode e^{inθ} is
    carried to r^|n|e^{inφ}, i.e. to zⁿ or z̄ⁿ. (a) cos²θ = 1/2 +
    (1/2)cos 2θ, and cos 2θ extends as Re z² = x² − y², so u = 1/2 +
    (x² − y²)/2; check: u(0) = 1/2 = average of cos². For the arc data use
    conformal invariance: w = i(1 + z)/(1 − z) maps D onto H, the upper arc
    onto (−∞, 0) and the lower arc onto (0, ∞). On H the harmonic function
    with these values is arg w/π. So u(z) = (1/π)arg(i(1 + z)/(1 − z)),
    which simplifies to 1/2 + (1/π)arctan(2y/(1 − |z|²)). At z = i/2:
    1/2 + arctan(4/3)/π ≈ 0.79517 (check by numerical Poisson integration).
    (b) For |z| = r: 1 − 2r cos t + r² lies between (1 − r)² and (1 + r)²,
    so (1 − r)/(1 + r) ≤ P_r ≤ (1 + r)/(1 − r). If u ≥ 0, multiply by u ≥ 0
    on the circle of radius ρ < 1, average, use u(0) = mean, and let ρ →
    1 (or rescale): Harnack. For u > 0 harmonic on R², apply it on
    D(0, R): u(z) ≤ ((R + |z|)/(R − |z|))u(0) → u(0) as R → ∞; likewise
    u(z) ≥ u(0). So u ≡ u(0). Common mistakes: applying Harnack to
    functions of both signs; forgetting the Poisson kernel is centred at
    φ. Now you try: u > 0 harmonic on D, u(0) = 5: bounds for u(1/3).
    (5/2 ≤ u ≤ 10)

D3. (a) Prove the Schwarz lemma. (b) Show every automorphism of D is
    e^{iθ}(z − a)/(1 − āz). (c) If f : D → D is holomorphic with f(0) =
    1/2, find the largest possible |f′(0)| and an extremal f.
    Answer: (c) 3/4, attained by f(z) = (z + 1/2)/(1 + z/2).
    Teacher explains: (a) g(z) = f(z)/z (g(0) = f′(0)) is holomorphic. On
    |z| = r < 1, |g| ≤ 1/r; by the maximum principle |g| ≤ 1/r on |z| ≤ r;
    let r → 1: |g| ≤ 1, i.e. |f(z)| ≤ |z| and |f′(0)| ≤ 1. If equality holds
    at an interior point, |g| attains its max, so g ≡ e^{iθ}. (b) φ_a(z) =
    (z − a)/(1 − āz) maps ∂D to ∂D (|e^{iθ} − a| = |1 − āe^{iθ}|), maps a
    to 0 and has inverse φ_{−a}, so it is an automorphism. Given any
    automorphism F with F(a) = 0, G = F ∘ φ_{−a} fixes 0; Schwarz on G and
    G⁻¹ gives |G′(0)| ≤ 1 and |1/G′(0)| ≤ 1, so G is a rotation and F =
    e^{iθ}φ_a. (c) Let h = φ_{1/2} ∘ f: then h(0) = 0, so |h′(0)| ≤ 1. Chain
    rule: h′(0) = φ′_{1/2}(1/2)f′(0) = f′(0)/(1 − 1/4). So |f′(0)| ≤ 3/4
    (Schwarz-Pick: 1 − |f(0)|²). Equality iff h is a rotation, e.g. f =
    φ_{−1/2}(z) = (z + 1/2)/(1 + z/2), f′(0) = 1 − 1/4 = 3/4. Common mistakes:
    applying the Schwarz lemma when f(0) ≠ 0; forgetting the conjugate ā.
    Now you try: f : D → D, f(1/2) = 0. Largest |f(0)|? (1/2, since
    Pick gives ρ(f(0), f(1/2)) ≤ ρ(0, 1/2) = 1/2)

D4. State the Riemann mapping theorem and prove it, using Montel's
    theorem and Hurwitz's theorem. Prove uniqueness of the normalised map.
    Answer: see the four steps below.
    Teacher explains: THEOREM: Ω ⊊ C simply connected, z₀ ∈ Ω ⇒ there is
    a unique biholomorphic f : Ω → D, f(z₀) = 0, f′(z₀) > 0. STEP 1 (a map
    exists): pick a ∉ Ω. z − a has no zeros on the simply connected Ω, so
    it has a holomorphic square root s(z), injective, and s(Ω) ∩ (−s(Ω)) =
    ∅. s(Ω) contains a disc D(w, δ), so s misses D(−w, δ) and
    δ/(s(z) + w) maps Ω injectively into D. Compose with an automorphism
    to send z₀ to 0. So the family ℱ = {f : Ω → D injective, f(z₀) = 0} is
    non-empty. STEP 2 (extremal map): ℱ is bounded by 1, so by MONTEL it
    is normal. Take fₙ ∈ ℱ with |fₙ′(z₀)| → sup = S > 0 and a subsequence
    fₙ → f locally uniformly. Then |f′(z₀)| = S > 0, so f is non-constant,
    hence INJECTIVE by HURWITZ; |f| ≤ 1 and the open mapping theorem give
    f(Ω) ⊆ D. So f ∈ ℱ. STEP 3 (f is onto): if w₀ ∈ D ∖ f(Ω), φ_{w₀} ∘ f
    has no zeros, so it has a square root g; put h = φ_{g(z₀)} ∘ g ∈ ℱ.
    Then f = Φ ∘ h with Φ = φ_{−w₀} ∘ (sq) ∘ φ_{−g(z₀)} a self-map of D
    fixing 0 that is NOT injective (it contains squaring), so |Φ′(0)| < 1
    by Schwarz, and |h′(z₀)| = |f′(z₀)|/|Φ′(0)| > S. Contradiction. Rotate
    to make f′(z₀) > 0. STEP 4 (uniqueness): if f, g are two such maps,
    f ∘ g⁻¹ is an automorphism of D fixing 0 with positive derivative, so
    it is the identity. Why the hypotheses: Ω = C fails since a bounded
    entire map is constant; an annulus fails since D is simply connected.
    Common mistakes: forgetting to show the limit f is injective and lands
    in the OPEN disc. Now you try: in Step 3 one computes |h′(z₀)| =
    |f′(z₀)|(1 + |w₀|)/(2√|w₀|). Evaluate the gain factor for w₀ = 1/4.
    (5/4)

D5. Find explicitly (a) a conformal map of the upper half-disc U = {|z| <
    1, Im z > 0} onto D, and the point sent to 0; (b) a conformal map of
    the strip S = {0 < Im z < π} onto D; (c) explain why the annulus
    A = {1 < |z| < 2} is not conformally equivalent to D, and why C is not.
    Answer: (a) f(z) = (w − i)/(w + i), w = ((1 + z)/(1 − z))²; f(i(√2 − 1))
    = 0. (b) (eᶻ − i)/(eᶻ + i). (c) topology; Liouville.
    Teacher explains: Build maps from a few "Lego bricks", tracking the
    boundary. (a) T(z) = (1 + z)/(1 − z) is Möbius, sends −1 ↦ 0, 1 ↦ ∞,
    0 ↦ 1. It maps the segment (−1, 1) onto (0, ∞), and the unit circle
    (through −1 and 1) onto a line through 0, namely the imaginary axis:
    T(e^{iθ}) = i cot(θ/2). The upper semicircle goes to the positive
    imaginary axis, so U ↦ first quadrant Q (test: T(i/2) = (3 + 4i)/5 ∈ Q).
    Squaring opens the quadrant (angle π/2) to the upper half-plane H, and
    the Cayley map C(w) = (w − i)/(w + i) takes H onto D. So f = C ∘ sq ∘ T.
    f(z) = 0 ⇔ T(z)² = i ⇔ T(z) = e^{iπ/4} ⇔ z = (e^{iπ/4} − 1)/(e^{iπ/4} +
    1) = i tan(π/8) = i(√2 − 1) ≈ 0.41421i. (b) eᶻ maps the horizontal line
    Im z = t onto the ray arg w = t, so S ↦ H bijectively; then Cayley.
    (c) A conformal map is a homeomorphism; D is simply connected and A is
    not (the circle |z| = 3/2 is not contractible in A). For C: a conformal
    f : C → D would be a bounded entire function, hence constant by
    Liouville. Common mistakes: using z² on a region containing 0 in its
    interior or symmetric points (not injective); forgetting that a Möbius
    map sends circles through a pole to lines. Now you try: map the sector
    0 < arg z < π/4 onto D. ((z⁴ − i)/(z⁴ + i))

D6. (a) Compute the orders of cos √z, e^{z²}, sin z · e^{z³} and ∑zⁿ/(n!)².
    (b) Prove: if f is entire and |f(z)| ≤ A + B|z|ᴺ, then f is a
    polynomial of degree ≤ N. (c) State Jensen's formula and use it to
    compute (1/2π)∫₀^{2π} ln|f(Re^{iθ})| dθ for f(z) = z² − 5z + 6 and R =
    5/2. (d) State Hadamard's theorem and show that an entire function of
    order ρ < 1 with no zeros is constant.
    Answer: (a) 1/2, 2, 3, 1/2. (b) Cauchy estimates. (c) ln(15/2) ≈
    2.01490. (d) f = e^{g}, deg g ≤ ρ < 1.
    Teacher explains: (a) cos √z = ∑(−1)ⁿzⁿ/(2n)! is entire (even in √z).
    M(r) = cosh √r (attained at z = −r), so ln M(r) ≈ √r and
    ln ln M(r)/ln r → 1/2: ρ = 1/2. |e^{z²}| = e^{Re z²} ≤ e^{r²}, with
    equality on the real axis: ρ = 2. For sin z · e^{z³} the factor e^{z³}
    dominates: ρ = 3. For ∑zⁿ/(n!)² use ρ = lim sup n ln n/ln(1/|aₙ|) =
    lim n ln n/(2 ln n!) = 1/2 (Stirling: ln n! ≈ n ln n). (b) Cauchy's
    estimate on |z| = r gives |aₖ| ≤ M(r)/rᵏ ≤ (A + Brᴺ)/rᵏ → 0 as r → ∞
    when k > N. (c) JENSEN:
       ln|f(0)| = ∑_{|aₖ|<R} ln(|aₖ|/R) + (1/2π)∫ln|f(Re^{iθ})| dθ.
    Here f(0) = 6, the zeros are 2 (inside |z| < 5/2) and 3 (outside). So
    the mean is ln 6 − ln(2/(5/2)) = ln 6 + ln(5/4) = ln(15/2) ≈ 2.01490.
    Check: the mean of ln|z − a| over |z| = R is ln max(R, |a|): ln(5/2) +
    ln 3 = ln(15/2) ✓. (d) HADAMARD: if f has finite order ρ and zeros aₙ
    (≠ 0), then f(z) = z^m e^{g(z)}∏E_p(z/aₙ) with p = genus ≤ ρ and g a
    polynomial of degree ≤ ρ. If there are no zeros, f = e^{g} with deg g
    ≤ ρ < 1, so g is constant. Consequently an entire function of finite
    NON-INTEGER order has infinitely many zeros (cos √z vanishes at z =
    ((k + 1/2)π)²). Common mistakes: using the minimum of |f| on the circle
    instead of the maximum; forgetting that Jensen counts only zeros
    INSIDE the circle. Now you try: order of ∑zⁿ/nⁿ. (1)

D7. (a) Prove |1 − E_p(z)| ≤ |z|^{p+1} for |z| ≤ 1. (b) Construct entire
    functions with simple zeros exactly at (i) the positive integers and
    (ii) the points √n, n ≥ 1. (c) Use Hadamard's theorem to prove sin πz
    = πz∏(1 − z²/n²), and deduce ∑1/n² = π²/6 and ∑1/n⁴ = π⁴/90.
    Answer: (b) ∏(1 − z/n)e^{z/n}; ∏E₂(z/√n). (c) compare the z² and z⁴
    coefficients.
    Teacher explains: (a) Write E_p = (1 − z)e^{Q}, Q = z + ... + z^p/p.
    Then Q′ = (1 − z^p)/(1 − z), so E_p′ = −e^{Q} + (1 − z^p)e^{Q} = −z^p e^Q.
    Hence −E_p′ has non-negative Taylor coefficients and a zero of order p
    at 0, so 1 − E_p(z) = z^{p+1}φ(z) with φ having non-negative
    coefficients and φ(1) = 1 − E_p(1) = 1. For |z| ≤ 1: |φ(z)| ≤ φ(|z|) ≤
    φ(1) = 1. (b) The product ∏E_{p}(z/aₙ) converges locally uniformly iff
    ∑(r/|aₙ|)^{p+1} < ∞ for all r, by (a) and the test ∑|1 − factor| < ∞.
    (i) aₙ = n: ∑1/n diverges, ∑1/n² converges: p = 1, f(z) =
    ∏(1 − z/n)e^{z/n}. (ii) aₙ = √n: need ∑n^{−(p+1)/2} < ∞, i.e. p = 2:
    f(z) = ∏(1 − z/√n)exp(z/√n + z²/(2n)). (c) sin πz has order 1 and
    simple zeros at Z; the genus of the zeros is 1. Hadamard: sin πz =
    z e^{a+bz}∏_{n≠0}(1 − z/n)e^{z/n} = z e^{a+bz}∏_{n≥1}(1 − z²/n²)
    (pair n with −n). Both sin πz/z and the product are even, so e^{bz} is
    even: b = 0; letting z → 0 gives e^a = π. Now expand:
       sin πz/(πz) = 1 − π²z²/6 + π⁴z⁴/120 − ...
       ∏(1 − z²/n²) = 1 − (∑1/n²)z² + (∑_{m<n}1/(m²n²))z⁴ − ...
    So ∑1/n² = π²/6 and ∑_{m<n}1/(m²n²) = ((∑1/n²)² − ∑1/n⁴)/2 = π⁴/120,
    giving ∑1/n⁴ = π⁴/36 − π⁴/60 = π⁴/90. At z = 1/2 we get WALLIS:
    2/π = ∏(1 − 1/(4n²)). Common mistakes: writing ∏(1 − z/n) without
    convergence factors (it diverges for z ≠ 0); forgetting the factor
    e^{g}. Now you try: put z = i in the sine product to find ∏(1 + 1/n²).
    (sinh π/π ≈ 3.67608)

D8. (a) State the Mittag-Leffler theorem. (b) Prove π cot πz = 1/z +
    ∑_{n≥1}2z/(z² − n²). (c) Deduce ∑_{n≥1}1/(n² + 1). (d) Prove that every
    meromorphic function on C is a quotient of two entire functions.
    Answer: (c) (π coth π − 1)/2 ≈ 1.07667. (d) multiply by a Weierstrass
    product that vanishes at the poles.
    Teacher explains: (a) Given bₙ → ∞ (distinct) and polynomials Pₙ
    without constant term, there is a meromorphic h whose poles are
    exactly the bₙ with principal parts Pₙ(1/(z − bₙ)); h = ∑(Pₙ(1/(z −
    bₙ)) − Qₙ(z)) with Qₙ a Taylor polynomial at 0 chosen so that
    |Pₙ − Qₙ| < 2⁻ⁿ on |z| ≤ |bₙ|/2. (b) The residue of π cot πz at each
    integer n is π cos(πn)/(π cos(πn)) = 1, and 1/(z − n) + 1/(z + n) =
    2z/(z² − n²) is O(1/n²) locally uniformly, so the series converges and
    g(z) = π cot πz − 1/z − ∑2z/(z² − n²) is ENTIRE. g has period 1 (the
    symmetric partial sums ∑_{|n|≤N}1/(z − n) change by 1/(z + 1 + N) −
    1/(z − N) → 0 under z ↦ z + 1). On the strip 0 ≤ Re z ≤ 1, cot πz is
    bounded for |Im z| ≥ 1 and the series is bounded there too (compare
    with ∫2|y|dt/(y² + t²) = 2π); on the compact part g is continuous. So
    g is a bounded entire function, a constant, and odd, hence 0. (c) Put
    z = i: π cot(πi) = −iπ coth π, 1/i = −i, 2i/(−1 − n²) = −2i/(n² + 1):
       −iπ coth π = −i − 2i∑1/(n² + 1) ⇒ ∑1/(n² + 1) = (π coth π − 1)/2,
    ≈ 1.07667 (numerical check: the first 1000 terms give 1.07567 plus a
    tail ≈ 1/1000). (d) Let h be meromorphic with poles bₙ of orders mₙ.
    By Weierstrass there is an entire g with zeros exactly at bₙ of order
    mₙ. Then gh has only removable singularities, so F = gh is entire and
    h = F/g. Common mistakes: summing ∑1/(z − n) without symmetric
    grouping or convergence terms (it diverges); forgetting that
    Mittag-Leffler controls only the poles — adding any entire function
    gives another solution. Now you try: use π/sin πz = 1/z +
    ∑(−1)ⁿ2z/(z² − n²) to find ∑_{n≥1}(−1)ⁿ/(n² + 1). ((π/sinh π − 1)/2 ≈
    −0.36399)

D9. (a) Let f(z) = ∑_{n≥1}zⁿ/n on D. Re-expand f about a = −1/2, find
    the radius of the new series and explain how this continues f beyond
    D. What happens when f is continued once around z = 1? (b) Continue
    √z along the unit circle starting from √1 = 1, and state the monodromy
    theorem. (c) Prove that |z| = 1 is a natural boundary for g(z) =
    ∑_{n≥0}z^{2ⁿ}.
    Answer: (a) f = −ln(3/2) + ∑_{k≥1}(2/3)ᵏ(z + 1/2)ᵏ/k, radius 3/2; one
    loop around 1 adds −2πi. (b) returns as −1. (c) g(rζ) → ∞ at every
    2ᵏ-th root of unity ζ.
    Teacher explains: (a) f = −log(1 − z) on D. Near a = −1/2, 1 − z =
    (3/2)(1 − (z + 1/2)/(3/2)), so
       f(z) = −ln(3/2) + ∑_{k≥1} (2/3)ᵏ (z + 1/2)ᵏ / k,
    a series with radius 3/2 = distance from −1/2 to the singularity 1.
    The disc |z + 1/2| < 3/2 sticks out of D (it contains −1.9), and on the
    overlap the two series agree (both equal −log(1 − z)); by the identity
    theorem this defines the unique continuation there. Chains of such
    discs continue f along every path in C ∖ {1}. Going once
    counterclockwise around 1, 1 − z winds once around 0, so log(1 − z)
    gains 2πi and f gains −2πi: f is not single-valued on C ∖ {1}.
    (b) Along z = e^{it}, the continuation is e^{it/2} (it is holomorphic
    in a neighbourhood of each point of the path and starts at 1). At t
    = 2π it equals e^{iπ} = −1. MONODROMY THEOREM: if a function element
    can be continued along every curve in a domain Ω, then continuations
    along two curves that are homotopic in Ω (fixed endpoints) end with
    the same element; hence on a SIMPLY CONNECTED Ω one gets a single-
    valued holomorphic function. For √z on C ∖ {0} the unit circle is not
    null-homotopic, so there is no contradiction. (c) Let ζ be a 2ᵏ-th root
    of unity. For n ≥ k, ζ^{2ⁿ} = 1, so for 0 < r < 1
       g(rζ) = ∑_{n<k}(rζ)^{2ⁿ} + ∑_{n≥k} r^{2ⁿ} → ∞ as r → 1⁻.
    Thus g cannot be continued to a neighbourhood of ζ. Such ζ are dense in
    the circle, so no arc of |z| = 1 admits a continuation: natural
    boundary. (HADAMARD GAP THEOREM: n_{k+1}/nₖ ≥ λ > 1 always gives a
    natural boundary.) Common mistakes: thinking the radius of a Taylor
    series is determined by the nearest point where f "looks bad" on the
    real line (1/(1 + x²) has radius 1 at 0 because of ±i); assuming that
    continuation along a closed path must return the same value. Now you
    try: Γ extends to C via Γ(z) = Γ(z + 3)/(z(z + 1)(z + 2)). Find the
    residue at z = −2. (Γ(1)/((−2)(−1)) = 1/2)

D10. (a) Define a Riemann surface and give an atlas for Ĉ. (b) Construct
     the compact Riemann surface X of y² = z(z − 1)(z − λ) (λ ≠ 0, 1),
     describe its charts at branch points, and compute its genus by the
     Riemann-Hurwitz formula. (c) Prove every holomorphic function on a
     compact Riemann surface is constant. (d) Genus of y² = z⁵ − 1?
     Answer: (b) a double cover of Ĉ branched at 0, 1, λ, ∞: g = 1, a
     torus. (c) maximum principle + compactness. (d) 2.
     Teacher explains: (a) A Riemann surface is a connected Hausdorff
     (second countable) space X with charts φ_α : U_α → C, homeomorphisms
     onto open sets, whose transition maps φ_β ∘ φ_α⁻¹ are holomorphic. Ĉ:
     U₀ = C with φ₀(z) = z, U₁ = Ĉ ∖ {0} with φ₁(z) = 1/z; the transition
     z ↦ 1/z is holomorphic on C ∖ {0}. (b) Over each z ∉ {0, 1, λ, ∞}
     there are two values ±y, so X is two sheets. Going once around 0
     alone, y = √(z(z − 1)(z − λ)) changes sign; the same around 1 and λ;
     around a huge circle y ~ z^{3/2} also changes sign, so ∞ is a branch
     point too. Cut-and-paste: cut two copies of Ĉ along [0, 1] and
     [λ, ∞] and glue crosswise: each slit becomes a tube, two spheres
     joined by two tubes = a torus. Charts: at a smooth point, z itself;
     at a branch point (a, 0) use t = y (≈ c√(z − a)); at ∞ use
     t = 1/√z-type coordinate, e.g. t = z/y. RIEMANN-HURWITZ for the degree
     2 map (z, y) ↦ z to Ĉ (genus 0): 2g − 2 = 2(0 − 2) + ∑(e_P − 1) =
     −4 + 4·1 = 0, so g = 1. (c) X compact, f holomorphic ⇒ |f| attains a
     maximum at some P; in a chart at P the ordinary maximum principle
     makes f constant near P; the set of points near which f ≡ f(P) is
     open and closed, so f is constant on the connected X. Hence the
     interesting objects on compact surfaces are MEROMORPHIC functions
     (like z and y above) and differentials. (d) z⁵ − 1 has 5 simple roots
     and odd degree, so ∞ is also a branch point: 6 branch points,
     2g − 2 = −4 + 6 = 2, g = 2. In general y² = p(z), deg p = n distinct
     roots, has genus ⌈n/2⌉ − 1. Common mistakes: forgetting ∞ as a
     branch point when n is odd; confusing the number of sheets with the
     genus. Now you try: genus of the Fermat cubic x³ + y³ = 1 (project
     to x: y³ = 1 − x³ branches with e = 3 at the three cube roots of 1,
     not at ∞). (2g − 2 = 3(−2) + 3·2 = 0: g = 1)

D11. Let f be a non-constant elliptic function for the lattice Λ, and P a
     period parallelogram whose boundary avoids zeros and poles of f.
     Prove LIOUVILLE'S THEOREMS: (i) f has a pole in P; (ii) the residues
     in P sum to 0; (iii) f has as many zeros as poles in P (with
     multiplicity); (iv) ∑zeros − ∑poles ∈ Λ. Deduce that f has order ≥ 2.
     For Λ = Z + iZ, decide whether there is an elliptic function with
     simple zeros at 1/4 and 3/4 and a double pole at 0 (only).
     Answer: contour integrals over ∂P; yes: ℘(z) − ℘(1/4).
     Teacher explains: (i) If f had no poles it would be continuous on the
     compact closure of P, hence bounded on C (every z is congruent to a
     point of P̄), so constant by Liouville. (ii) ∑Res = (1/2πi)∮_{∂P} f dz;
     opposite sides are translates by ω₁ or ω₂ traversed in opposite
     directions, and f takes the same values on them, so the integrals
     cancel: sum = 0. (iii) f′/f is elliptic with residues = (order of zero)
     at zeros and −(order of pole) at poles; apply (ii). (iv) (1/2πi)∮ z
     f′(z)/f(z) dz = ∑zeros − ∑poles. Pair the side [a, a + ω₁] with its
     translate by ω₂ (opposite direction): their total is
        (1/2πi)∫_a^{a+ω₁} (z − (z + ω₂)) f′/f dz = −ω₂ · (1/2πi)∫_a^{a+ω₁} f′/f,
     and the last integral is 2πik (f(a + ω₁) = f(a), so it is a change
     of log f: an integer multiple of 2πi). Similarly for the other pair.
     So the sum lies in Zω₁ + Zω₂ = Λ. ORDER: if f had only one simple pole,
     its residue would be the whole sum, 0: impossible. Application: zeros
     1/4 + 3/4 − (0 + 0) = 1 ∈ Λ, so Abel's condition holds. Indeed ℘(z) −
     ℘(1/4) has a double pole at 0 and vanishes at ±1/4, and −1/4 ≡ 3/4;
     1/4 ≢ −1/4 (not a half-period), so both zeros are simple. Common
     mistakes: counting zeros in a parallelogram whose boundary passes
     through a zero; forgetting that zeros/poles are counted modulo Λ.
     Now you try: same lattice, simple zeros at 1/4, 1/2 and a double pole
     at 0 only? (No: 3/4 ∉ Λ)

D12. (a) Show ∑_{ω∈Λ∖0}|ω|^{−s} converges iff s > 2, and deduce that ℘
     converges locally uniformly away from Λ. (b) Prove ℘ is even and
     elliptic. (c) Derive the Laurent series and the differential equation
     (℘′)² = 4℘³ − g₂℘ − g₃. (d) Show e₁, e₂, e₃ are distinct. (e) If
     e₁, e₂, e₃ = 2, −1/2, −3/2, find g₂, g₃, Δ, and ℘′(z)² where ℘(z) = 3.
     Answer: (e) g₂ = 13, g₃ = 6, Δ = 1225, ℘′² = 63.
     Teacher explains: (a) The lattice points with max(|m|, |n|) = k
     (ω = mω₁ + nω₂) number 8k and have |ω| between c₁k and c₂k. So the sum
     is comparable to ∑8k · k^{−s}, finite iff s − 1 > 1. For |z| ≤ R and
     |ω| ≥ 2R: |1/(z − ω)² − 1/ω²| = |z(2ω − z)|/|ω²(z − ω)²| ≤ 10R/|ω|³, a
     convergent (s = 3) majorant. (b) Replacing ω by −ω permutes Λ, so
     ℘(−z) = ℘(z). ℘′(z) = −2∑_{ω∈Λ}1/(z − ω)³ is obviously Λ-periodic.
     Hence ℘(z + ω₁) − ℘(z) = c is constant; at z = −ω₁/2 evenness gives
     c = ℘(ω₁/2) − ℘(−ω₁/2) = 0. (c) For |z| < min|ω|, 1/(z − ω)² − 1/ω² =
     ∑_{k≥1}(k + 1)zᵏ/ω^{k+2}; summing over ω the odd k cancel (Λ = −Λ):
        ℘ = z⁻² + 3G₄z² + 5G₆z⁴ + O(z⁶),  ℘′ = −2z⁻³ + 6G₄z + 20G₆z³ + ...
        ℘′² = 4z⁻⁶ − 24G₄z⁻² − 80G₆ + O(z²),
        4℘³ = 4z⁻⁶ + 36G₄z⁻² + 60G₆ + O(z²).
     So F = ℘′² − 4℘³ + 60G₄℘ + 140G₆ is elliptic, has no pole (all
     negative powers cancel) and F(0) = 0; by Liouville F ≡ 0. With g₂ =
     60G₄, g₃ = 140G₆: (℘′)² = 4℘³ − g₂℘ − g₃. (d) ℘′ is odd and periodic,
     so ℘′(ω/2) = −℘′(−ω/2) = −℘′(ω/2) = 0 at the three half-periods ω₁/2,
     ω₂/2, (ω₁ + ω₂)/2. ℘′ has order 3, so these are its only zeros, all
     simple. ℘ − e₁ has a DOUBLE zero at ω₁/2 and order 2, so no other
     zeros: e₂, e₃ ≠ e₁. Thus 4x³ − g₂x − g₃ = 4(x − e₁)(x − e₂)(x − e₃)
     with distinct roots, Δ = 16∏(eᵢ − eⱼ)² ≠ 0. (e) Vieta: e₁ + e₂ + e₃ = 0
     ✓; g₂ = −4(e₁e₂ + e₂e₃ + e₃e₁) = −4(−1 + 3/4 − 3) = 13; g₃ = 4e₁e₂e₃ =
     4 · (3/2) = 6. Check: 4x³ − 13x − 6 = (x − 2)(2x + 1)(2x + 3). Δ = 13³ −
     27 · 36 = 2197 − 972 = 1225 = 16(5/2)²(1)²(7/2)². At ℘ = 3: ℘′² = 108 −
     39 − 6 = 63. Common mistakes: g₃ = 4e₁e₂e₃, not −4e₁e₂e₃ (4x³ − g₂x −
     g₃ has constant term −g₃ = −4e₁e₂e₃); forgetting that ℘ alone is not
     obviously periodic (the constant c must be shown to vanish). Now you
     try: find ℘″ in terms of ℘. (℘″ = 6℘² − g₂/2)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Is u harmonic (on its natural domain)? If so, give a harmonic
    conjugate v (up to a constant) or say why none exists.
    a) x² − y²                        b) xy
    c) x² + y²                        d) eˣ cos y
    e) eˣ sin y                       f) x³ − 3xy² + 2x
    g) sin x cosh y                   h) x/(x² + y²)
    i) ln(x² + y²) on C ∖ {0}          j) e^{2x} cos 3y
    k) e^{x²−y²} cos 2xy               l) x³ + axy²: for which a?
    m) θ = arg z on C ∖ (−∞, 0]         n) x⁴ − 6x²y² + y⁴
    o) y/((x − 1)² + y²)
    p) ax³ + bx²y + 3xy² − y³: for which a, b?
    q) r² cos θ (polar)               r) rⁿ cos nθ (polar)
    Answers:
      a) 2xy          b) (y² − x²)/2     c) not harmonic (Δu = 4)
      d) eˣ sin y      e) −eˣ cos y       f) 3x²y − y³ + 2y
      g) cos x sinh y  h) −y/(x² + y²)
      i) harmonic, no conjugate on C ∖ {0} (would be 2 arg z); on the
         slit plane v = 2 Arg z
      j) not harmonic (Δu = −5e^{2x}cos 3y)
      k) e^{x²−y²} sin 2xy (u = Re e^{z²})          l) a = −3
      m) −ln|z| (θ − i ln r = −i Log z)             n) 4x³y − 4xy³
      o) (x − 1)/((x − 1)² + y²) (u = Re(i/(z − 1)))
      p) a = −1, b = 3                q) no (Δu = 3cos θ)
      r) yes, v = rⁿ sin nθ

M2. Dirichlet problems, mean values and Harnack. D is the unit disc.
    a) u on D with u = cos θ on ∂D     b) u = cos 2θ on ∂D
    c) u = cos²θ on ∂D                 d) u = sin³θ on ∂D
    e) u = cos θ + sin 2θ on ∂D
    f) u = 1 on the upper arc, 0 on the lower: u(0) and u(i/2)
    g) u = e^{cos θ}cos(sin θ) on ∂D: u(z) and u(0)
    h) u harmonic on |z| < 3, = x² − y² + 7 on |z| = 3: u(0)
    i) the Poisson kernel P_r(θ) for r = 1/2 at θ = 0 and θ = π
    j) (1/2π)∫₀^{2π} P_r(θ) dθ
    k) u > 0 harmonic on D, u(0) = 5: best bounds for u(1/3)
    l) u > 0 harmonic on |z| < 2, u(0) = 1: bounds on |z| = 1
    m) on H, u = 1 on (0, ∞), 0 on (−∞, 0): formula; u(i), u(1 + i),
       u(−1 + √3 i)
    n) on H, u = 1 on (−1, 1), 0 elsewhere on R: u(i), u(2i)
    o) on 1 < |z| < 2, u = 0 on |z| = 1, u = 1 on |z| = 2: formula, u(1.5)
    p) max and min of x² − y² on the closed unit disc
    Answers:
      a) x             b) x² − y²          c) 1/2 + (x² − y²)/2
      d) (3y − 3x²y + y³)/4                e) x + 2xy
      f) 1/2; 1/2 + arctan(4/3)/π ≈ 0.79517
      g) eˣ cos y (= Re eᶻ); 1               h) 7
      i) 3 and 1/3      j) 1                k) 5/2 ≤ u(1/3) ≤ 10
      l) 1/3 ≤ u ≤ 3 ((R − r)/(R + r) = 1/3)
      m) 1 − arg z/π; 1/2, 3/4, 1/3
      n) (arg(z − 1) − arg(z + 1))/π; 1/2, 1 − 2arctan(2)/π ≈ 0.29517
      o) ln|z|/ln 2; ln 1.5/ln 2 ≈ 0.58496
      p) 1 (at ±1) and −1 (at ±i), on the boundary

M3. Automorphisms, Möbius maps and the Schwarz lemma.
    a) the automorphism f of D with f(1/2) = 0, f′(1/2) > 0; f′(1/2)
    b) the automorphism f of D with f(0) = i/2, f′(0) > 0; f′(0)
    c) f : D → D holomorphic, f(0) = 1/2: max |f′(0)|
    d) f : D → D, f(0) = 0: max |f(1/2)|, max |f′(0)|
    e) f : D → D, f(0) = 0, f′(0) = 1: f = ?
    f) f : D → D, f(1/2) = 0: max |f(0)|
    g) pseudo-hyperbolic ρ(z, w) = |z − w|/|1 − w̄z|: ρ(1/2, −1/2),
       ρ(i/2, 1/2)
    h) hyperbolic distance ln((1 + ρ)/(1 − ρ)) from 0 to 1/2
    i) f : D → D, f(1/2) = 1/2: max |f′(1/2)|
    j) f : H → D, f(i) = 0: max |f′(i)|
    k) fixed points and type of T(z) = (z + 1/2)/(1 + z/2) on D
    l) check Schwarz-Pick for f(z) = z² at z = 1/2
    m) automorphisms of D fixing both 0 and 1/2
    n) is z ↦ (2z + 1)/(z + 1) an automorphism of H? z ↦ (z + 1)/(2z − 1)?
    o) the Blaschke product B(z) = z(z − 1/2)/(1 − z/2): zeros, |B| on
       ∂D, B′(0)
    p) the Möbius T with T(0) = 1, T(1) = i, T(∞) = −1; T(H) = ?
    Answers:
      a) (z − 1/2)/(1 − z/2) = (2z − 1)/(2 − z); 4/3
      b) (z + i/2)/(1 − iz/2); 3/4       c) 3/4        d) 1/2; 1
      e) the identity                   f) 1/2
      g) 4/5; 2√2/√17 ≈ 0.68599          h) ln 3 ≈ 1.09861
      i) 1 ((1 − |f|²)/(1 − |z|²) = 1)    j) 1/2 (Cayley has |C′(i)| = 1/2)
      k) ±1, both on ∂D: hyperbolic     l) |f′| = 1 ≤ (15/16)/(3/4) = 5/4
      m) only the identity              n) yes (det 1 > 0); no (det −3 < 0)
      o) 0 and 1/2; 1; −1/2             p) (i − z)/(i + z); D

M4. Conformal maps: find the image, or a map with the stated property.
    a) z² on the first quadrant        b) eᶻ on 0 < Im z < π
    c) eᶻ on 0 < Im z < π/2            d) (z − i)/(z + i) on H; image of
                                          the positive imaginary axis
    e) (1 + z)/(1 − z) on D, and on the upper half-disc
    f) a map of the sector 0 < arg z < π/3 onto H
    g) 1/z on {|z| > 1}                 h) a map of C ∖ (−∞, 0] onto D
    i) z + 1/z on the upper half-disc; on D ∖ {0}
    j) the map H → D with f(2i) = 0, f′(2i) > 0; f′(2i)
    k) a map of the strip |Im z| < π/2 onto D
    l) sin z on {−π/2 < Re z < π/2, Im z > 0}
    m) a map of H ∖ [0, i] onto H
    n) the Koebe function z/(1 − z)² on D
    o) are 1 < |z| < 2 and 2 < |z| < 4 conformally equivalent? 1 < |z| < 2
       and 1 < |z| < 3? D and C?
    p) a map of the upper half-disc onto D sending i(√2 − 1) to 0

      a) H             b) H              c) the first quadrant
      d) D; the segment (−1, 1) (it ↦ (t − 1)/(t + 1))
      e) Re w > 0; the first quadrant    f) z³
      g) D ∖ {0}        h) (√z − 1)/(√z + 1), principal √
      i) the lower half-plane; Ĉ ∖ [−2, 2]
      j) i(z − 2i)/(z + 2i); 1/4          k) tanh(z/2) = (eᶻ − 1)/(eᶻ + 1)
      l) H              m) √(z² + 1) (branch with values in H)
      n) C ∖ (−∞, −1/4]                 o) yes (z ↦ 2z); no; no
      p) (w − i)/(w + i), w = ((1 + z)/(1 − z))²

M5. Order ρ of entire functions, zeros and Jensen.
    a) 7z⁵ + 1                         b) e^{5z} (also the type)

M6. Infinite products and Weierstrass factors.
    a) ∏_{n≥2}(1 − 1/n²)               b) ∏_{n≥1}(1 + 1/n²)
    c) ∏_{n≥1}(1 − 1/(4n²))            d) ∏_{n≥1}(1 − 1/(9n²))
    e) ∏_{n≥2}(n³ − 1)/(n³ + 1)         f) ∏_{n≥1}(1 + 1/n)
    g) ∏_{n≥1}(1 + (−1)^{n+1}/n)        h) ∏_{n≥1}(1 + 1/n)e^{−1/n}
    i) ∏_{n≥0}(1 + z^{2ⁿ}) for |z| < 1; its value at z = 1/2
    j) Wallis: ∏_{n≥1}(2n)²/((2n − 1)(2n + 1))
    k) ∏_{n≥1}(1 + 1/n³) (use 1 + x³ = (1 + x)(1 + ωx)(1 + ω̄x))
    l) E₁(1/2), and check |1 − E₁(1/2)| ≤ (1/2)²
    m) smallest fixed p making ∏E_p(z/aₙ) converge for aₙ = n; n²; √n;
       n ln²n (n ≥ 2)
    n) an entire function with simple zeros exactly at −1, −2, −3, ...
    o) an entire function with zeros exactly at the Gaussian integers
       m + ni ≠ 0 (and at 0)
    p) does ∏(1 + (−1)ⁿ/√n) (n ≥ 2) converge to a non-zero limit?
    Answers:
      a) 1/2 (telescopes: (n − 1)(n + 1)/n²)
      b) sinh π/π ≈ 3.67608            c) 2/π ≈ 0.63662
      d) 3√3/(2π) ≈ 0.82699 (sin(π/3)/(π/3))
      e) 2/3            f) diverges (partial products n + 1 → ∞)
      g) 1 (partial products 2, 1, 4/3, 1, 6/5, 1, ...), although
         ∑1/n = ∞: only conditional convergence
      h) e^{−γ} ≈ 0.56146 (1/Γ(z) product at z = 1)
      i) 1/(1 − z) (binary expansion); 2
      j) π/2            k) cosh(π√3/2)/π ≈ 2.42819
      l) (1/2)e^{1/2} ≈ 0.82436; 0.17564 ≤ 0.25 ✓
      m) 1; 0; 2; 0
      n) ∏(1 + z/n)e^{−z/n}, e.g. 1/Γ(z + 1) = e^{γz}∏(1 + z/n)e^{−z/n}
      o) z∏'E₂(z/ω) (∑'|ω|⁻³ < ∞; this is the Weierstrass σ-function)
      p) no: ∑ln(1 + aₙ) ≈ ∑(aₙ − aₙ²/2) and ∑1/(2n) = ∞, so the product
         tends to 0

M7. Mittag-Leffler and partial-fraction series.
    a) the Mittag-Leffler expansion of π cot πz
    b) of π²/sin²πz                    c) of π/sin πz
    d) ∑_{n≥1}1/n² and ∑_{n≥1}1/n⁴ from the Laurent series of π cot πz
    e) ∑_{n≥1}1/n⁶                     f) ∑_{n≥1}1/(n² + 1)
    g) ∑_{n≥1}(−1)ⁿ/(n² + 1)            h) ∑_{n≥1}1/(n² + 4)
    i) ∑_{n∈Z}1/(n + 1/3)²             j) ∑_{n∈Z}1/(n + 1/2)²; deduce
                                          ∑_{k≥0}1/(2k + 1)²
    k) ∑_{n≥1}1/(n² − 1/4)             l) ∑_{n≥1}1/(n² − a²), a ∉ Z
    m) the expansion of π tan πz; check at z = 0.3
    n) the correction terms needed for poles at √n with residue 1
    o) a meromorphic function on C with simple poles at n² (n ≥ 1),
       residue 1
    p) the rational function on Ĉ with simple poles at 0, 1, residues 1,
       −1, vanishing at ∞
    q) the principal part of Γ at z = −2
    Answers:
      a) 1/z + ∑_{n≥1}2z/(z² − n²)
      b) ∑_{n∈Z}1/(z − n)²
      c) 1/z + ∑_{n≥1}(−1)ⁿ2z/(z² − n²)
      d) π cot πz = 1/z − 2∑ζ(2k)z^{2k−1}: π²/6 ≈ 1.64493, π⁴/90 ≈ 1.08232
      e) π⁶/945 ≈ 1.01734
      f) (π coth π − 1)/2 ≈ 1.07667
      g) (π/sinh π − 1)/2 ≈ −0.36399
      h) (2π coth 2π − 1)/8 ≈ 0.66040 (general (πa coth πa − 1)/(2a²))
      i) π²/sin²(π/3) = 4π²/3 ≈ 13.15947
      j) π²; π²/8 ≈ 1.23370
      k) 2 (telescoping check: 4/((2n − 1)(2n + 1)))
      l) (1 − πa cot πa)/(2a²)
      m) ∑_{n≥0}2z/((n + 1/2)² − z²); both sides ≈ 4.32403 at z = 0.3
      n) 1/(z − √n) + 1/√n + z/n (∑n^{−3/2} < ∞)
      o) ∑_{n≥1}1/(z − n²) (no correction: ∑1/n² < ∞)
      p) 1/z − 1/(z − 1) = −1/(z(z − 1))
      q) 1/(2(z + 2)) (residue (−1)²/2! = 1/2)

M8. The Gamma function and analytic continuation.
    a) Γ(−1/2)                          b) Γ(−3/2)
    c) Res_{z=−3}Γ                      d) Res_{z=−4}Γ
    e) Γ(1/3)Γ(2/3)                     f) |Γ(1/2 + i)|²

                                           1/(1 + z²) about 0, 1, 3
    i) radius of the Taylor series of Log z about −1 + i
    j) radius of the Taylor series of tan z and z/(eᶻ − 1) about 0

       √1 = 1

    m) z^{1/3} continued once counterclockwise around 0 from the value 2
       at z = 8
    n) √(z² − 1) (value √3 at z = 2) continued once around |z| = 2; once
       around |z − 1| = 1
    o) natural boundary on |z| = 1? ∑zⁿ; ∑z^{n!}; ∑zⁿ/n²; ∑2⁻ⁿz^{2ⁿ}
    p) the continuation of ∫₀^∞ e^{−zt} dt (Re z > 0)
    q) ζ(0) and ζ(−1) (continuation of ∑n^{−s})

       1 + 2i: the reflected value f(−i/2)

         branch than Log)
      j) π/2; 2π
      k) −1; 1          l) 6πi          m) 2e^{2πi/3} = −1 + i√3

      o) no (1/(1 − z)); yes; no (only z = 1 is singular); yes (Hadamard
         gaps, although the series converges uniformly on |z| ≤ 1)
      p) 1/z on C ∖ {0}                 q) −1/2; −1/12
      r) 1 − 2i (f(z̄) = conj f(z))

M9. Riemann surfaces: genus g (by Riemann-Hurwitz 2g_X − 2 = d(2g_Y − 2)
    + ∑(e_P − 1)), degrees and branch points.
    a) y² = z³ − z                     b) y² = z⁴ − 1
    c) y² = z⁵ − z                     d) y² = z⁶ − 1
    e) y² = z⁷ − 1                     f) y² = p(z), deg p = 2g + 1 or
                                          2g + 2, distinct roots
    g) y³ = 1 − z³ (Fermat cubic)      h) x⁴ + y⁴ = 1
    i) a smooth plane curve of degree 5 ((d − 1)(d − 2)/2)
    j) y³ = z(z − 1)                   k) yⁿ = z
    l) z ↦ z³ on Ĉ: ramification points and the R-H check
    m) number of branch points of ℘ : C/Λ → Ĉ, and its degree
    n) total ramification ∑(e_P − 1) of a rational map of degree 4
    o) Euler characteristic of a compact surface of genus 3
    p) how many sheets has the Riemann surface of √z? of log z? what is
       the surface of log z biholomorphic to?
    q) ramification of an unbranched map of degree d between tori
    r) a non-constant holomorphic map Ĉ → C/Λ? genus 2 → genus 3?
    s) the degree of (z³ + 1)/(z² − 4) as a map Ĉ → Ĉ
    t) the simply connected Riemann surfaces (uniformization)
    Answers:
      a) 1 (branch points 0, ±1, ∞)    b) 1 (±1, ±i; ∞ unbranched)
      c) 2 (0, ±1, ±i, ∞)              d) 2          e) 3
      f) g                             g) 1 (2g − 2 = 3(−2) + 3·2)
      h) 3 (2g − 2 = 4(−2) + 4·3)      i) 6
      j) 1 (e = 3 at 0, 1, ∞: 2g − 2 = 3(−2) + 6)
      k) 0              l) 0 and ∞ with e = 3: −2 = 3(−2) + 2 + 2 ✓
      m) 4 (over e₁, e₂, e₃, ∞); 2      n) 2d − 2 = 6
      o) 2 − 2g = −4    p) 2; infinitely many; C (via exp)
      q) R = 0 (0 = d · 0 + R)
      r) no (−2 = d · 0 + R ≥ 0 impossible); no (2 = 4d + R forces d < 1)
      s) 3              t) Ĉ, C, D

M10. Elliptic functions: basic facts (Λ = Zω₁ + Zω₂, P a period
     parallelogram; in g)–i) Λ = Z + iZ).
     a) the orders of ℘, ℘′, ℘², (℘′)², 1/℘, ℘℘′, ℘(2z)
     b) the number of zeros of ℘′ in P, and where they are
     c) the number of solutions of ℘(z) = c in P (c ≠ e₁, e₂, e₃); of
        ℘(z) = e₁
     d) an elliptic function of order 1?
     e) the sum of the residues of any elliptic function in P
     f) parity of ℘, ℘′, ℘℘′, ℘″
     g) simple zeros at 1/4, 3/4, double pole at 0: exists?
     h) simple zeros at 1/4, 1/2, double pole at 0: exists?
     i) simple poles at 0 and 1/2 with residues 1, −1? with residues 1, 1?
     j) the number of z ∈ C/Λ with 2z ≡ 0; with nz ≡ 0
     k) Weierstrass ζ: ζ′ = ?, Res_{0}ζ, is ζ elliptic? Legendre relation
     l) are sin z, tan z elliptic?
     m) write the even elliptic function ℘′² and the odd function ℘′³ in
        terms of ℘, ℘′
     n) the number of zeros of ℘′ − 1 in P
     o) ℘(z) = ℘(w) ⇔ ?
     Answers:
       a) 2, 3, 4, 6, 2, 5, 8 (double poles at the 4 points with 2z ∈ Λ)
       b) 3, simple, at ω₁/2, ω₂/2, (ω₁ + ω₂)/2
       c) 2 (z₀ and −z₀); 1 point, a double solution
       d) none (a lone simple pole would have residue 0)
       e) 0             f) even, odd, odd, even
       g) yes: ℘(z) − ℘(1/4) (1/4 + 3/4 − 0 = 1 ∈ Λ)
       h) no (3/4 ∉ Λ)
       i) yes: ζ(z) − ζ(z − 1/2) + c; no (residue sum 2 ≠ 0)
       j) 4 (0 and three half-periods); n²
       k) −℘; 1; no (ζ(z + ωᵢ) = ζ(z) + ηᵢ); η₁ω₂ − η₂ω₁ = 2πi
          (Im(ω₂/ω₁) > 0)
       l) no (one period only; a doubly periodic entire function is
          constant)
       m) 4℘³ − g₂℘ − g₃; ℘′(4℘³ − g₂℘ − g₃)
       n) 3 (order of ℘′)             o) z ≡ ±w (mod Λ)

M11. Computations with ℘ (℘ = z⁻² + c₁z² + c₂z⁴ + c₃z⁶ + ...).
     a) c₁, c₂, c₃ in terms of g₂, g₃
     b) G₈ in terms of G₄
     c) ℘″ and ℘‴ in terms of ℘, ℘′
     d) ℘⁽⁴⁾ as a polynomial in ℘
     e) g₂ = 4, g₃ = 0: e₁, e₂, e₃, Δ, j
     f) g₂ = 0, g₃ = 4: eᵢ, Δ, j
     g) g₂ = 13, g₃ = 6: eᵢ, Δ, j
     h) g₂ = 12, g₃ = 8: is there a lattice?
     i) how g₂, g₃, j change when Λ is replaced by 2Λ
     j) Λ = Z[i]: g₃, j, ℘((1 + i)/2)
     k) Λ = Z[i]: G₄, g₂, e₁ = ℘(1/2) numerically
     l) Λ = Z + e^{2πi/3}Z: g₂, j
     m) g₂ = 4, g₃ = 0, at a point with ℘(z) = 2: ℘′(z)², ℘″(z), and
        ℘(2z) from ℘(2z) = (1/4)(℘″/℘′)² − 2℘
     n) the addition formula for ℘(z + w)
     o) the points of order 2 on y² = 4x³ − 13x − 6
     Answers:
       a) g₂/20, g₃/28, g₂²/1200         b) 3G₄²/7
       c) 6℘² − g₂/2; 12℘℘′              d) 120℘³ − 18g₂℘ − 12g₃
       e) 0, ±1; 64; 1728                f) 1, ω, ω² (ω = e^{2πi/3}); −432; 0
       g) 2, −1/2, −3/2; 1225; 1728 · 2197/1225 ≈ 3099.12
       h) no: Δ = 1728 − 1728 = 0 (4x³ − 12x − 8 = 4(x − 2)(x + 1)²)
       i) g₂/16, g₃/64, j unchanged
       j) 0 (℘(iz) = −℘(z) forces G₆ = 0); 1728; 0
       k) ≈ 3.15121 (= Γ(1/4)⁸/(960π²)); ≈ 189.07; √g₂/2 ≈ 6.87519
       l) 0; 0
       m) 24; 22; 25/24
       n) ℘(z + w) = (1/4)((℘′(z) − ℘′(w))/(℘(z) − ℘(w)))² − ℘(z) − ℘(w)
       o) (2, 0), (−1/2, 0), (−3/2, 0) = (℘(ωᵢ/2), 0)

M12. True (T) or false (F)? Give a counterexample for F.
     a) Every simply connected open U ⊊ C is conformally equivalent to D.
     b) C is conformally equivalent to D.
     c) 1 < |z| < 2 and 1 < |z| < 3 are conformally equivalent.
     d) Every harmonic function on C ∖ {0} has a harmonic conjugate.
     e) A harmonic function on R² that is bounded below is constant.
     f) A uniform limit of harmonic functions is harmonic.
     g) f : D → D holomorphic with two distinct fixed points is the
        identity.
     h) An entire f with |f(z)| ≤ 1 + |z|^{3/2} is a polynomial of degree
        ≤ 1.
     i) An entire function without zeros has the form e^{g}, g entire.
     j) Any sequence with no finite accumulation point is the zero set of
        an entire function.
     k) ∏(1 + aₙ) converges to a non-zero limit whenever ∑aₙ converges.
     l) Every meromorphic function on C is a quotient of entire
        functions.
     m) Every meromorphic function on Ĉ is rational.
     n) Every power series with radius 1 extends past some point of
        |z| = 1.
     o) A locally uniform limit of injective holomorphic functions on a
        domain is injective.
     p) A compact Riemann surface carries a non-constant holomorphic
        function.
     q) There is an elliptic function with a single simple pole in P.
     r) Two tori C/Λ, C/Λ′ are biholomorphic iff Λ′ = λΛ for some λ ≠ 0.
     s) Continuation of a function element along two curves with the same
        endpoints always gives the same result.
     t) Every non-constant entire function takes every complex value.
     Answers:
       a) T (Riemann mapping theorem)    b) F: Liouville
       c) F: the modulus R is a conformal invariant
       d) F: ln|z|                       e) T (Harnack)
       f) T (mean value property passes to the limit)
       g) T (move one fixed point to 0, apply Schwarz)
       h) T (Cauchy estimates)          i) T (log on the simply connected C)
       j) T (Weierstrass)               k) F: aₙ = (−1)ⁿ/√n (product → 0)
       l) T                             m) T
       n) F: ∑z^{n!}                    o) F: zₙ(z) = z/n → 0 (constant);
                                           true if the limit is non-constant
       p) F: only constants              q) F: residue sum would be ≠ 0
       r) T (equivalently equal j-invariants)
       s) F: √z along the upper and lower halves of |z| = 1 (true for
          homotopic curves: monodromy)
       t) F: eᶻ omits 0 (Picard: at most one value is omitted)

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: keep a "dictionary" page — harmonic u ↔ Re f, Poisson ↔
  Fourier series with r^|n|, disc ↔ half-plane via Cayley — and always
  reduce a Dirichlet or Schwarz problem to the disc centred at 0 by an
  automorphism first. For conformal maps, track three boundary points and
  one interior point through every brick. For products and partial
  fractions, first ask "how fast do the zeros (poles) go to ∞?" to
  choose the convergence factors, then fix the leftover entire function
  by growth (Hadamard or Liouville) and parity. For elliptic functions,
  draw the period parallelogram and count zeros and poles before
  computing anything; for Riemann surfaces, list branch points including
  ∞ and apply Riemann-Hurwitz. Harder: prove Montel's theorem via
  Arzelà-Ascoli, Carathéodory's boundary extension theorem and the
  Schwarz-Christoffel formula; prove Picard's little theorem via the
  modular function λ or Bloch's theorem; prove Hadamard's factorisation
  theorem via Jensen and Borel-Carathéodory; show every compact Riemann
  surface of genus 1 is C/Λ and that j : H → C induces a bijection
  SL₂(Z)∖H ↔ C; construct the Riemann surface of an algebraic function
  as a sheaf of germs; and derive the Legendre relation and the
  σ-function product formula.


================================================================================
TOPIC 12.4 — SEVERAL COMPLEX VARIABLES (INTRODUCTION): HOLOMORPHIC FUNCTIONS
OF SEVERAL VARIABLES, CAUCHY INTEGRAL FORMULA ON POLYDISCS, HARTOGS
PHENOMENON, DOMAINS OF HOLOMORPHY, PSEUDOCONVEXITY, PLURISUBHARMONIC
FUNCTIONS, WEIERSTRASS PREPARATION THEOREM, THE ∂̄-PROBLEM
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - HOLOMORPHY: on an open Ω ⊆ Cⁿ, zⱼ = xⱼ + iyⱼ, WIRTINGER operators
    ∂/∂zⱼ = ½(∂/∂xⱼ − i∂/∂yⱼ), ∂/∂z̄ⱼ = ½(∂/∂xⱼ + i∂/∂yⱼ). A C¹ function f
    is HOLOMORPHIC iff ∂f/∂z̄ⱼ = 0 for all j (Cauchy-Riemann in each
    variable), iff f is locally a convergent power series ∑ c_α z^α
    (multi-indices α = (α₁, ..., αₙ), z^α = z₁^α₁···zₙ^αₙ). OSGOOD:
    continuous + holomorphic in each variable separately ⇒ holomorphic.
    HARTOGS' THEOREM ON SEPARATE ANALYTICITY: continuity is not needed.
  - POLYDISC Δ(a; r) = {|zⱼ − aⱼ| < rⱼ for all j}; its DISTINGUISHED
    BOUNDARY is the torus T = {|zⱼ − aⱼ| = rⱼ} (real dimension n, much
    smaller than the topological boundary). CAUCHY INTEGRAL FORMULA: if f
    is holomorphic near the closed polydisc then, for z ∈ Δ,
       f(z) = (2πi)⁻ⁿ ∫_T f(ζ) dζ₁···dζₙ / ((ζ₁ − z₁)···(ζₙ − zₙ)),
       ∂^α f(z) = α!(2πi)⁻ⁿ ∫_T f(ζ) dζ / (ζ − z)^(α+1).
    CAUCHY ESTIMATES: |∂^α f(a)| ≤ α! M / r^α, M = max_T |f|.
  - CONSEQUENCES as in one variable: holomorphic ⇒ C^∞, Taylor series
    converges on every polydisc inside Ω; IDENTITY THEOREM (vanishing on
    a non-empty open set, or all derivatives vanishing at one point, of a
    connected Ω ⇒ f ≡ 0); MAXIMUM PRINCIPLE; LIOUVILLE; Weierstrass and
    Montel theorems. log|f| and |f|ᵖ are plurisubharmonic.
  - NEW PHENOMENA for n ≥ 2: zeros are NEVER isolated (the zero set of f
    ≢ 0 is a complex hypersurface of complex dimension n − 1); isolated
    singularities do not exist; the ball and the bidisc are NOT
    biholomorphic (Poincaré), so there is no Riemann mapping theorem;
    power series converge on LOGARITHMICALLY CONVEX COMPLETE REINHARDT
    domains, e.g. {|z₁z₂| < 1} or {|z₁| + |z₂| < 1}, not only polydiscs.
  - REINHARDT DOMAINS: invariant under (z₁, ..., zₙ) ↦ (e^(iθ₁)z₁, ...);
    COMPLETE if also invariant under |zⱼ| ↦ tⱼ|zⱼ|, 0 ≤ tⱼ ≤ 1. Log image
    log Ω = {(log|z₁|, ..., log|zₙ|)}. ABEL'S LEMMA: if the terms c_α w^α
    are bounded, the series converges on the polydisc {|zⱼ| < |wⱼ|}.
  - HARTOGS EXTENSION THEOREM: n ≥ 2, Ω ⊆ Cⁿ a domain, K ⊂ Ω compact
    with Ω ∖ K connected ⇒ every f holomorphic on Ω ∖ K extends
    holomorphically to Ω. False for n = 1 (1/z on D ∖ {0}). HARTOGS
    FIGURE: H = {|z| < 1, |w| < 1, (|z| < a or |w| > b)} ⊂ D²; every f ∈
    𝒪(H) extends to D² (Cauchy integral in w on the circle |w| = r > b).
  - DOMAIN OF HOLOMORPHY: Ω such that there are no Ω₁, Ω₂ open, Ω₂
    connected, ∅ ≠ Ω₁ ⊆ Ω ∩ Ω₂, Ω₂ ⊄ Ω, with every f ∈ 𝒪(Ω) agreeing on
    Ω₁ with some F ∈ 𝒪(Ω₂) (i.e. no f "extends past the boundary" as a
    whole family). Every domain in C is one; convex domains, polydiscs,
    products, and Ω ∖ {g = 0} (Ω a domain of holomorphy) are; C² ∖ {0},
    spherical shells and Hartogs figures are NOT.
  - HOLOMORPHIC HULL K̂_Ω = {z ∈ Ω : |f(z)| ≤ max_K |f| for all f ∈
    𝒪(Ω)}; Ω is HOLOMORPHICALLY CONVEX if K ⊂⊂ Ω ⇒ K̂_Ω ⊂⊂ Ω.
    CARTAN-THULLEN: Ω is a domain of holomorphy ⇔ Ω is holomorphically
    convex ⇔ some f ∈ 𝒪(Ω) cannot be extended past any boundary point.
    Convex hull ⊇ K̂ (use e^(ℓ(z)), ℓ complex linear).
  - PLURISUBHARMONIC (psh) FUNCTIONS: φ : Ω → [−∞, ∞) upper semicontinuous
    and subharmonic on every complex line: λ ↦ φ(a + λb) subharmonic.
    For φ ∈ C²: psh ⇔ the COMPLEX HESSIAN (LEVI MATRIX) (∂²φ/∂zⱼ∂z̄ₖ) is
    positive semidefinite everywhere. Examples: log|f|, |f|² (f
    holomorphic), |z|², log(1 + |z|²), −log(1 − |z|²) on the ball, convex
    functions, max(φ₁, φ₂), sums, φ ∘ F for F holomorphic. Pluriharmonic
    (Hessian 0) = locally Re of a holomorphic function. Psh is invariant
    under biholomorphisms; convexity is not.
  - PSEUDOCONVEXITY: Ω is PSEUDOCONVEX if it has a continuous psh
    EXHAUSTION φ ({φ < c} ⊂⊂ Ω for all c), equivalently −log dist(z, ∂Ω)
    is psh. For C² boundary with defining function ρ (Ω = {ρ < 0}, dρ ≠
    0 on ∂Ω): LEVI PSEUDOCONVEX iff the LEVI FORM ∑ ρ_{jk̄}(p)tⱼt̄ₖ ≥ 0 for
    all complex tangent vectors t (∑ ∂ρ/∂zⱼ(p) tⱼ = 0), p ∈ ∂Ω; STRICTLY
    pseudoconvex if > 0 for t ≠ 0. Convex ⇒ pseudoconvex; strictly
    pseudoconvex domains are locally biholomorphic to strictly convex
    ones (Narasimhan's lemma).
  - LEVI PROBLEM (Oka 1942/53, Bremermann, Norguet 1954): pseudoconvex ⇔
    domain of holomorphy ⇔ holomorphically convex. A complete Reinhardt
    domain is a domain of holomorphy ⇔ log Ω is convex; a Hartogs domain
    {z ∈ D, |w| < e^(−φ(z))} is one ⇔ φ is subharmonic.
  - WEIERSTRASS PREPARATION THEOREM: f holomorphic near 0 ∈ C^(n−1) × C,
    f(0) = 0, f(0, ..., 0, w) ≢ 0 with a zero of order k at w = 0
    ("w-regular of order k"). Then near 0, f = u·P uniquely, u(0) ≠ 0 a
    unit, P(z′, w) = wᵏ + a_(k−1)(z′)w^(k−1) + ... + a₀(z′) a WEIERSTRASS
    POLYNOMIAL (aⱼ(0) = 0). For fixed small z′ the k roots of P are the
    zeros of f with |w| < δ. After a linear change of coordinates every f
    ≢ 0 is w-regular.
  - WEIERSTRASS DIVISION THEOREM: g = qP + r with r a polynomial in w of
    degree < k, q and r unique. Consequences: the ring 𝒪ₙ of germs at 0
    is a NOETHERIAN, local UNIQUE FACTORISATION DOMAIN (induction on n),
    not a PID for n ≥ 2 ((z, w) is not principal); zero sets are locally
    branched coverings of C^(n−1).
  - THE ∂̄-PROBLEM: given a (0,1)-form f = ∑ fⱼ dz̄ⱼ find u with ∂̄u =
    ∑ (∂u/∂z̄ⱼ) dz̄ⱼ = f. Necessary: ∂̄f = 0, i.e. ∂fⱼ/∂z̄ₖ = ∂fₖ/∂z̄ⱼ.
    In C: u(z) = (1/π)∫ f(ζ)/(z − ζ) dA(ζ) (CAUCHY-GREEN, f ∈ C_c¹); the
    general CAUCHY-POMPEIU formula u(z) = (2πi)⁻¹∮ u/(ζ − z) dζ − π⁻¹∬
    (∂u/∂ζ̄)/(ζ − z) dA. In Cⁿ, n ≥ 2, a compactly supported ∂̄-closed f
    has a COMPACTLY SUPPORTED solution (⇒ Hartogs). HÖRMANDER: on a
    pseudoconvex Ω, ∂̄u = f is solvable with L² estimates; this solves
    the Levi problem.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. (a) Using Wirtinger derivatives decide which are holomorphic on C²:
    f = e^(z₁z₂), g = z₁z̄₂ + z₂, h = Re(z₁)·z₂. (b) Prove that the real
    part of a holomorphic function is PLURIHARMONIC (∂²u/∂zⱼ∂z̄ₖ = 0 for
    all j, k). (c) Show that u = x₁y₂ is harmonic on R⁴ = C² but is not
    the real part of any holomorphic function.
    Answer: (a) only f. (b) ∂f/∂z̄ₖ = 0 and ∂f̄/∂zⱼ = 0. (c) Δu = 0 but
    ∂²u/∂z₁∂z̄₂ = i/4 ≠ 0.
    Teacher explains: Treat zⱼ and z̄ⱼ as independent variables:
    ∂zⱼ/∂z̄ₖ = 0, ∂z̄ⱼ/∂z̄ₖ = δⱼₖ, and f is holomorphic iff every
    ∂f/∂z̄ₖ = 0. (a) e^(z₁z₂) contains no z̄: holomorphic. ∂g/∂z̄₂ = z₁ ≠ 0:
    not holomorphic. Re z₁ = (z₁ + z̄₁)/2, so ∂h/∂z̄₁ = z₂/2 ≠ 0: not
    holomorphic, although h is holomorphic in z₂ for each fixed z₁
    (separate holomorphy needs EVERY variable). (b) Let f = u + iv, so
    u = (f + f̄)/2. Now ∂f/∂z̄ₖ = 0, and ∂f̄/∂zⱼ = conj(∂f/∂z̄ⱼ) = 0, hence
       ∂²u/∂zⱼ∂z̄ₖ = ½(∂/∂zⱼ)(∂f/∂z̄ₖ) + ½(∂/∂z̄ₖ)(∂f̄/∂zⱼ) = 0.
    (c) u = x₁y₂ is a product of coordinates in different variables, so
    Δu = ∑ (∂²/∂xⱼ² + ∂²/∂yⱼ²) u = 0. But with x₁ = (z₁ + z̄₁)/2, y₂ = (z₂ −
    z̄₂)/(2i): ∂u/∂z₁ = (z₂ − z̄₂)/(4i), then ∂/∂z̄₂ gives −1/(4i) = i/4 ≠ 0.
    So u is harmonic but not pluriharmonic: for n ≥ 2 pluriharmonic
    (n² conditions) is much stronger than harmonic (one condition).
    Common mistakes: thinking holomorphic in each variable with the others
    FIXED at one value is enough (it must hold for all values); confusing
    ∂/∂z̄ with the complex conjugate of ∂/∂z applied to f. Now you try: is
    |z₁|² − |z₂|² pluriharmonic? (no: ∂²/∂z₁∂z̄₁ = 1, though Δ = 4 − 4 = 0)

D2. Derive the Cauchy integral formula on the polydisc Δ = Δ(0; r) ⊂ C²,
    deduce that f is given by a power series on Δ, and prove the Cauchy
    estimates. Evaluate, with T = {|ζ| = 1, |η| = 1} positively oriented,
       I = (2πi)⁻² ∫_T e^(ζ+η) / ((ζ − ½)² η³) dζ dη.
    Answer: I = (∂/∂z)e^z|_(z=½) · (1/2!)(∂/∂w)²e^w|_(w=0) = √e/2 ≈ 0.82436.
    Teacher explains: Fix w with |w| < r₂. The map z ↦ f(z, w) is
    holomorphic near |z| ≤ r₁, so by the one-variable formula f(z, w) =
    (2πi)⁻¹∮_{|ζ|=r₁} f(ζ, w)/(ζ − z) dζ. For each such ζ, w ↦ f(ζ, w) is
    holomorphic, so f(ζ, w) = (2πi)⁻¹∮_{|η|=r₂} f(ζ, η)/(η − w) dη.
    Substituting (the integrand is continuous, so the iterated integral
    is an integral over T by Fubini):
       f(z, w) = (2πi)⁻² ∫_T f(ζ, η) dζ dη / ((ζ − z)(η − w)).
    EXPANSION: for (ζ, η) ∈ T and (z, w) ∈ Δ,
       1/((ζ − z)(η − w)) = ∑_{j,k≥0} zʲwᵏ / (ζ^(j+1) η^(k+1)),
    uniformly on T for (z, w) in a smaller closed polydisc; integrate
    term by term: f = ∑ c_jk zʲwᵏ with c_jk = (2πi)⁻² ∫_T f ζ^(−j−1)
    η^(−k−1) dζ dη. ESTIMATE: |dζ| = r₁dθ, |dη| = r₂dφ, so
       |c_jk| ≤ (2π)⁻² · M r₁^(−j−1) r₂^(−k−1) · (2πr₁)(2πr₂) = M/(r₁ʲr₂ᵏ),
    i.e. |∂ʲ_z∂ᵏ_w f(0)| ≤ j!k!M/(r₁ʲr₂ᵏ). Only the TORUS T is used — the
    distinguished boundary. For I: differentiating the formula gives
    (2πi)⁻² ∫_T f/((ζ − a)^(j+1)(η − b)^(k+1)) = ∂ʲ_z∂ᵏ_w f(a, b)/(j!k!).
    Here j = 1, k = 2, (a, b) = (½, 0), f = e^(z+w): I = e^(1/2)·1/2 ≈
    0.82436. Common mistakes: using the full boundary of Δ (dimension 3)
    instead of T (dimension 2); forgetting the factorials j!k!. Now you
    try: (2πi)⁻² ∫_T sin(ζη)/(ζ²η²) dζ dη. (1, the coefficient of zw)

D3. (a) Find the domain of convergence of ∑_{n≥0} (zw)ⁿ and of
    ∑_{j,k≥0} C(j + k, j) zʲwᵏ. (b) Prove that the domain of convergence
    of a power series in C² is a LOGARITHMICALLY CONVEX complete Reinhardt
    domain. (c) Why does the second series diverge at (0.9, −0.9) although
    its sum is holomorphic there?
    Answer: (a) {|zw| < 1}; {|z| + |w| < 1}. (b) Abel's lemma + the
    inequality |c_α z^α| = (|c_α a^α|)ᵗ(|c_α b^α|)^(1−t). (c) convergence
    depends only on (|z|, |w|).
    Teacher explains: (a) ∑(zw)ⁿ is geometric in zw: converges iff |zw| <
    1. This domain is unbounded and not a polydisc: its log image {x + y
    < 0} (x = log|z|, y = log|w|) is a half-plane. For the second series,
    absolute convergence at (z, w) means ∑ C(j + k, j)|z|ʲ|w|ᵏ =
    ∑ₙ (|z| + |w|)ⁿ < ∞ (binomial theorem), i.e. |z| + |w| < 1; the sum is
    1/(1 − z − w). (b) ABEL'S LEMMA: if |c_α a^α| ≤ M for all α then on
    the polydisc {|zⱼ| ≤ ρ|aⱼ|}, ρ < 1, we have |c_α z^α| ≤ Mρ^|α|, and
    ∑_α ρ^|α| < ∞: normal convergence. So the domain is a union of
    polydiscs centred at 0: complete Reinhardt. If the terms are bounded
    by M at a and at b, and |zⱼ| = |aⱼ|ᵗ|bⱼ|^(1−t), then
       |c_α z^α| = (|c_α||a^α|)ᵗ(|c_α||b^α|)^(1−t) ≤ Mᵗ M^(1−t) = M,
    so the terms are bounded on the "log-segment"; with Abel, log Ω is
    convex. (c) At (0.9, −0.9), |z| + |w| = 1.8 > 1: diverges, yet 1/(1 −
    z − w) = 1 there. The domain of convergence is Reinhardt (sees only
    moduli) while the function's natural domain {z + w ≠ 1} is not.
