      a) Z/5, 0            b) Z, Z (S¹ ∨ S²)       c) Z, 0 (gcd(2, 3) = 1)
      d) Z ⊕ Z/2, 0 (exponent sums 2, 0)            e) Z/2 ⊕ Z/2, 0
      f) Z/2 ⊕ Z/6, 0 (Smith form diag(2, 6), det = −12)
      g) Z/9, 0 (Smith form diag(1, 1, 9))
      h) Z/2, 0 (matrix [[1, 1], [1, −1]], det −2)
      i) 0, 0 (it is contractible)
      j) Z/2, 0 (killing b in the Klein bottle)
      k) Z, Z, Z/3, 0
      l) Z, Z/p, 0, Z
      m) Z, 0, Z², 0, Z
      n) Z, then Z/2 in odd degrees, 0 in even degrees > 0
      o) Z in every even degree, 0 in odd degrees
      p) Z/3 ⊕ Z/3 (rows (3, 0), (0, 3), (3, 3)) and Z (3 2-cells minus
         rank 2 of d₂)

M5. Euler characteristic and the Lefschetz number.
    a) χ(Sⁿ)                            b) χ(Σ_g)
    c) χ(N_h)                           d) χ(CPⁿ)
    e) χ(RPⁿ)                           f) χ(S² × Σ₂)
    g) the genus of a 3-sheeted connected cover of Σ₂
    h) χ(S² ∨ T²)                       i) χ(L(p, q))
    j) the Petersen graph (10 vertices, 15 edges): χ and rank H₁
    k) L(f) for f : Sⁿ → Sⁿ of degree d
    l) L(antipodal map of S²), and why this is consistent
    m) L(id_X)
    n) L(f) for f : T² → T², f induced by A = [[2, 1], [1, 1]]
    o) L(f) for (z, w) ↦ (z², w³) on S¹ × S¹
    p) L(f) for f : CP² → CP² with f*(x) = 3x
    q) does every map RP² → RP² have a fixed point?
    r) does every map T² → T² have a fixed point?
    s) χ of a closed orientable 3-manifold, and of S¹ ∨ S¹ ∨ S¹
    Answers:
      a) 1 + (−1)ⁿ            b) 2 − 2g            c) 2 − h
      d) n + 1                e) 1 (n even), 0 (n odd)
      f) 2 · (−2) = −4        g) χ = −6, so genus 4
      h) 2 + 0 − 1 = 1        i) 0                j) −5, 6
      k) 1 + (−1)ⁿd            l) 1 + (−1) = 0: the map has no fixed
                                  points
      m) χ(X)                 n) det(I − A) = −1 (1 − tr A + det A)
      o) det(I − diag(2, 3)) = 2
      p) 1 + 3 + 9 = 13       q) yes: H̃(RP²; Q) = 0, so L(f) = 1
      r) no: a translation (L = 0)
      s) 0 and −2

      m) Z/2, Z/2 (Z/2 ⊗ Z/2), Z/2 (Tor(Z/2, Z/2))

      o) 1, 4, 3, 1; 1 − 4 + 3 − 1 = −1 = 1 − 3 + 1 (ranks) ✓

M7. Künneth formula. Give the integral homology (H₀, H₁, ...) or the

M8. Cup products. Compute, or answer the question.
    a) In H*(T²) = Λ[a, b]: a ∪ b, b ∪ a, a ∪ a
    b) In H*(T³): (a₁ + a₂) ∪ (a₁ − a₂), and (a₁ + a₃)(a₂ − a₃)a₁
    c) Σ₂, symplectic basis a₁, b₁, a₂, b₂ (aᵢbᵢ = [Σ]*): (a₁ + 2a₂) ∪
       (3b₁ + b₂), and (a₁ + b₁)²
    d) In H*(CP³): (2x) ∪ (3x²), and (x + x²)²
    e) In H*(RP³; Z/2): a³, and in H*(RP²; Z/2): (a + a²)²
    f) H*(RP^∞; Z/2) and H*(RP^∞; Z) as rings
    g) In H*(S² × S²) = Λ-like ring with x² = y² = 0: (x + y)², (2x − y)²
    h) Intersection forms and signatures of CP², CP² # CP², CP² # CP̄²,
       S² × S², CP⁴ (on H⁴)
    i) Is S² × S² ≃ CP² # CP̄²?
    j) f : CP² → CP², f*(x) = −2x: deg f
    k) f : S² × S² → S² × S², f*(x) = 2x + y, f*(y) = x + 3y: deg f
    l) The degree on H⁴ of any map S⁴ → CP²
    m) Why is α ∪ α = 0 for every α ∈ H¹(X; Z)? For every α ∈ H³(X; Z)
       with H⁶(X; Z) torsion-free?
    n) Show there is no map g : RPⁿ → RPᵐ with g*(a) ≠ 0 in H¹(; Z/2)
       when n > m
    o) In C_η = S² ∪_η D⁴ (η the Hopf map): x² = ? (the Hopf invariant)
    p) In S¹ ∨ S¹ ∨ S² (classes α, β ∈ H¹, γ ∈ H²): α ∪ β, α ∪ γ
    Answers:
      a) [T²]*, −[T²]*, 0              b) −2a₁a₂; −a₁a₂a₃
      c) 3a₁b₁ + 2a₂b₂ = 5[Σ]*; a₁b₁ + b₁a₁ = 0
      d) 6x³; x² + 2x³ (x⁴ = 0)         e) a³ ≠ 0 (generator of H³); a²
                                          (cross terms 2a³ = 0)
      f) Z/2[a], |a| = 1; Z[y]/(2y), |y| = 2
      g) 2xy; −4xy
      h) (1), σ = 1; diag(1, 1), σ = 2; diag(1, −1), σ = 0;
         [[0, 1], [1, 0]], σ = 0; (1) generated by x², σ = 1
      i) no: even form vs odd form (though both have σ = 0)
      j) 4                             k) 2·3 + 1·1 = 7
      l) 0 (f*x ∈ H²(S⁴) = 0, so f*(x²) = 0)
      m) degree 1: α = f*θ for some f : X → S¹ = K(Z, 1), and θ² ∈
         H²(S¹) = 0; degree 3: α² = (−1)⁹α² gives 2α² = 0, so α² = 0 in
         the torsion-free group H⁶
      n) g*(a) = a′ would give a′ᵐ⁺¹ = g*(aᵐ⁺¹) = 0, but a′ᵐ⁺¹ ≠ 0 in
         H*(RPⁿ; Z/2) since m + 1 ≤ n
      o) C_η = CP², so x² generates H⁴: Hopf invariant ±1
      p) 0, 0

M9. Poincaré duality and its relatives. M denotes a closed connected
    manifold.
    a) M⁴ orientable, simply connected, b₂ = 5: all Hₖ(M) and χ
    b) M³ orientable, H₁ = Z/7: all Hₖ
    c) M⁵ orientable, H₁ = Z ⊕ Z/2, H₂ = Z³ ⊕ Z/3: H₃, H₄, H₅, χ
    d) M³ = RP² × S¹: H₃(M; Z) and dimensions of H*(M; Z/2)
    e) can a closed orientable 3-manifold have H₁ = Z/2, H₂ = Z/2?
    f) χ of any closed 7-manifold
    g) M⁶ orientable, b₁ = 0, b₂ = 2, b₃ = 4: b₄, b₅, χ
    h) the genus of a closed orientable surface with χ = −6
    i) the matrix of the cup pairing H¹(Σ_g) × H¹(Σ_g) → Z and its
       determinant
    j) is Hₙ₋₁(M; Z) torsion-free when M is orientable? and for RP²?
    k) Lefschetz duality: Hₖ(S¹ × D², S¹ × S¹) for k = 0, 1, 2, 3
    l) Alexander duality: Hₖ(S³ ∖ K), K a knot
    m) Alexander duality: H̃ₖ(S⁴ ∖ (S¹ ⊔ S¹))
    n) the Z/2-Betti numbers of RP⁴, checking symmetry
    o) M⁴ orientable with π₁ = Z/3 and b₂ = 0 (e.g. a spun lens space):
       all Hₖ(M)
    p) is there a closed orientable 4-manifold with intersection form
       (2)? with form diag(1, 1, −1)?
    Answers:
      a) Z, 0, Z⁵, 0, Z; χ = 7          b) Z, Z/7, 0, Z
      c) H₃ ≅ H² = Z³ ⊕ Z/2, H₄ ≅ H¹ = Z, H₅ = Z; χ = 1 − 1 + 3 − 3 + 1 − 1
         = 0
      d) 0 (non-orientable); 1, 2, 2, 1
      e) no: Tors H₂ ≅ Tors H₀ = 0
      f) 0                              g) 2, 0; χ = 1 + 2 − 4 + 2 + 1 = 2
      h) 4
      i) block diagonal with g blocks [[0, 1], [−1, 0]]; det = 1
      j) yes (≅ H¹ = Hom(H₁, Z)); no: H₁(RP²) = Z/2 (non-orientable)
      k) 0, 0, Z, Z (≅ H³, H², H¹, H⁰ of S¹ × D²)
      l) Z, Z, 0, 0 (H̃₁ ≅ H¹(K) = Z)    m) 0, 0, Z², Z
      n) 1, 1, 1, 1, 1
      o) Z, Z/3, Z/3, 0, Z (H₁ = π₁^ab = Z/3; Tors H₂ ≅ Tors H₁ and
         b₂ = 0 give H₂ = Z/3; H₃ ≅ H¹ = Hom(Z/3, Z) = 0)
      p) no: a unimodular form has determinant ±1, and (2) has det 2;
         yes: CP² # CP² # CP̄²

M10. Homotopy groups. Compute.
     a) π₁, π₂, π₃ of S³               b) πₙ(S¹), n ≥ 2
     c) π₂(T²), π₅(T⁷)                 d) π₂(RP²), π₃(RP²)
     e) π₃(RP³)                        f) π₂(S² × S²), π₃(S² × S²)
     g) π₂(S² ∨ S²)                    h) πₙ(Sⁿ ∨ Sⁿ), n ≥ 2
     i) πₙ(CP^∞) for all n             j) πₙ(RP^∞) for all n
     k) πₙ(Σ_g), g ≥ 1, n ≥ 2           l) π₂(S¹ ∨ S²)
     m) π₃(S² × S³)                    n) π₁, π₂, π₃ of SO(3)
     o) π₂(CPⁿ), π₃(CPⁿ) (n ≥ 2), π₂ₙ₊₁(CPⁿ)
     p) π₄(S³), π₅(S⁴), π₁₀₀(S⁹⁹)      q) π₄(S²), π₅(S³), π₇(S⁵)
     r) π₈(S⁵), π₁₀(S⁷) (stable n + 3 stem)
     s) π₇(S⁴)                         t) π₂(S² with two points
                                          identified)
     Answers:
       a) 0, 0, Z           b) 0                 c) 0, 0
       d) Z, Z (≅ π₃(S²))    e) Z                 f) Z², Z²
       g) Z² (Hurewicz)      h) Z²                i) Z for n = 2, else 0
       j) Z/2 for n = 1, else 0                   k) 0 (R² is the cover)
       l) Z[t, t⁻¹] (free abelian, infinitely generated)
       m) Z ⊕ Z             n) Z/2, 0, Z          o) Z, 0, Z
       p) Z/2, Z/2, Z/2 (stable n + 1 stem)
       q) Z/2, Z/2, Z/2 (n + 2 stem)              r) Z/24, Z/24
       s) Z ⊕ Z/12          t) Z[t, t⁻¹] (≃ S² ∨ S¹)

M11. Long exact sequences of fibrations and pairs.
     a) S¹ → S³ → S²: π₂(S²)          b) the same: π₃(S²), π₄(S²) from
                                          π₄(S³) = Z/2
     c) S³ → S⁷ → S⁴: show Z ⊆ π₇(S⁴)    d) path-loop: π₂(ΩS³), πₙ(ΩSⁿ⁺¹)
     e) SO(2) → SO(3) → S²: π₁(SO(3)), given that ∂ : π₂(S²) → π₁(SO(2))
        is multiplication by 2
     f) U(n − 1) → U(n) → S²ⁿ⁻¹: π₁(U(n))
     g) SU(n − 1) → SU(n) → S²ⁿ⁻¹: π₃(SU(3)), π₂(SU(n))
     h) Z → R → S¹: πₙ(S¹), n ≥ 2        i) S¹ → S^∞ → CP^∞: πₙ(CP^∞)
     j) Z/2 → Sⁿ → RPⁿ: πₖ(RPⁿ), 2 ≤ k
     k) S¹ → S²ⁿ⁺¹ → CPⁿ: πₖ(CPⁿ) for k ≥ 3
     l) F → E → B with E contractible: πₙ(B) in terms of F
     m) pair (Dⁿ, Sⁿ⁻¹): πₙ(Dⁿ, Sⁿ⁻¹)
     n) is η_* : π₃(S³) → π₃(S²) an isomorphism?
     o) S⁷ → S¹⁵ → S⁸: π₁₅(S⁸) contains which infinite cyclic group?
     p) fibration S¹ → T² → S¹ (projection): the boundary map π₂(S¹) →
        π₁(S¹)
     Answers:
       a) Z                  b) Z; Z/2 (πₙ(S²) ≅ πₙ(S³) for n ≥ 3)
       c) the fibre inclusion S³ → S⁷ is null-homotopic, so the LES
          splits: π₇(S⁴) ≅ π₇(S⁷) ⊕ π₆(S³) = Z ⊕ Z/12
       d) Z; πₙ₊₁(Sⁿ⁺¹) = Z
       e) Z/2: π₂(S²) = Z --2--> π₁(SO(2)) = Z → π₁(SO(3)) → π₁(S²) = 0
       f) Z (induction from U(1) = S¹; det : U(n) → S¹ induces it)
       g) Z (≅ π₃(SU(2)) = π₃(S³)); 0
       h) 0                  i) Z at n = 2 only (πₙ(CP^∞) ≅ πₙ₋₁(S¹))
       j) πₖ(Sⁿ)             k) πₖ(S²ⁿ⁺¹)
       l) πₙ(B) ≅ πₙ₋₁(F)    m) Z (≅ πₙ₋₁(Sⁿ⁻¹))
       n) yes (π₃(S¹) = π₂(S¹) = 0)
       o) Z (π₁₅(S⁸) ≅ Z ⊕ π₁₄(S⁷) = Z ⊕ Z/120)
       p) 0 (π₂(S¹) = 0; the sequence splits: π₁(T²) = Z²)

M12. True (T) or false (F)? Give a counterexample for F.
     a) Homotopy equivalent spaces have isomorphic homology groups.
     b) Spaces with isomorphic homology groups are homotopy equivalent.
     c) Spaces with isomorphic cohomology RINGS are homotopy equivalent.
     d) A simply connected CW complex with H̃_* = 0 is contractible.
     e) A CW complex with H̃_* = 0 is contractible.
     f) H¹(X; Z) is torsion-free for every X.
     g) H₁(X; Z) is torsion-free for every X.
     h) Hⁿ(X; Z) ≅ Hom(Hₙ(X), Z) for every X.
     i) Every closed n-manifold M (connected) has Hₙ(M; Z) ≅ Z.
     j) Every closed manifold is Z/2-orientable.
     k) πₙ(X) is abelian for n ≥ 2.
     l) πₖ(Sⁿ) = 0 for k > n.
     m) The cup product is commutative.
     n) If f : Sⁿ → Sⁿ has deg f ≠ (−1)ⁿ⁺¹ then f has a fixed point.
     o) Every closed odd-dimensional manifold has χ = 0.
     p) The Hurewicz map π₂(X) → H₂(X) is an isomorphism for every
        path-connected CW complex X.
     q) A map of CW complexes inducing isomorphisms on all πₙ is a
        homotopy equivalence.
     r) A map inducing isomorphisms on all Hₙ is a homotopy equivalence.
     s) Every covering map induces isomorphisms on πₙ for n ≥ 2.
     t) If M⁴ is a closed simply connected 4-manifold then H₂(M) is free.
     u) A finite CW complex has finitely generated homotopy groups.
     v) If L(f) = 0 then f has no fixed point.
     Answers:
       a) T
       b) F: T² and S¹ ∨ S¹ ∨ S²
       c) F: the Poincaré homology sphere P and S³ (π₁ differs)
       d) T (Hurewicz gives all πₙ = 0, then Whitehead)
       e) F: P minus an open ball is acyclic but π₁ ≠ 0
       f) T (Hom(H₁, Z) and Ext(H₀, Z) = 0)
       g) F: RP²
       h) F: H²(RP²) = Z/2 but H₂(RP²) = 0 (the Ext term)
       i) F: RP², Klein bottle (Hₙ = 0 when non-orientable)
       j) T
       k) T
       l) F: π₃(S²) = Z
       m) F: graded commutative; b ∪ a = −a ∪ b in T²
       n) T (fixed-point-free maps are homotopic to the antipodal map)
       o) T (Z/2 Poincaré duality)
       p) F: π₂(T²) = 0 but H₂(T²) = Z (needs simple connectivity)
       q) T (Whitehead's theorem)
       r) F: a degree-1 map P → S³ (true for simply connected complexes)
       s) T
       t) T (H₁ = 0, so Tors H₂ ≅ Tors H₁ = 0 by duality)
       u) F: π₂(S¹ ∨ S²) ≅ Z[t, t⁻¹] (true if simply connected, by Serre)
       v) F: the identity of a circle has L = 0 and fixes every point

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: make the student compute H_* of every space in M1 from a
  CW structure before touching any theorem, always writing the cellular
  chain complex as a row of Z's with the maps written on the arrows, and
  reading off kernels and cokernels (Smith normal form for matrices).
  Then insist on three reflexes: "homology is homotopy-invariant, so first
  simplify the space" (deformation retract, quotient by a contractible
  subcomplex); "cohomology = homology with torsion shifted up one
  degree" (UCT); and "when groups agree, compare rings" (T² vs S¹ ∨ S¹ ∨
  S², CP² vs S² ∨ S⁴). For homotopy groups, draw the Eckmann-Hilton
  picture and always ask whether a covering or a fibration is available.
  Harder: prove the Mayer-Vietoris sequence from the snake lemma and the
  small-simplices lemma; prove excision; derive the cellular boundary
  formula; prove Poincaré duality via the Mayer-Vietoris argument on
  convex open sets in Rⁿ; show that the Hopf invariant is 1 for the
  Hopf maps and use Steenrod squares (Adams) to show it is odd only for
  n = 2, 4, 8, so that Rⁿ is a division algebra only for n = 1, 2, 4, 8;
  compute H*(CPⁿ) via the Gysin sequence; prove Freudenthal's suspension
  theorem and compute π₄(S³) = Z/2; show that the Borsuk-Ulam theorem
  follows from the ring H*(RPⁿ; Z/2); and compute π₂ of a non-simply
  connected complex as a Z[π₁]-module.


================================================================================
TOPIC 12.10 — DIFFERENTIAL TOPOLOGY AND GEOMETRY: MANIFOLDS, VECTOR BUNDLES,
DIFFERENTIAL FORMS, DE RHAM COHOMOLOGY, RIEMANNIAN METRICS, CONNECTIONS,
CURVATURE, GEODESICS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - SMOOTH MANIFOLD of dimension n: a Hausdorff, second-countable space
    with an ATLAS of charts φ_α : U_α → Rⁿ whose TRANSITION MAPS φ_β ∘ φ_α⁻¹
    are C^∞; a maximal atlas is a SMOOTH STRUCTURE. Examples: Rⁿ, Sⁿ (two
    stereographic charts, transition u ↦ u/|u|²), RPⁿ and CPⁿ (charts
    {xᵢ ≠ 0}), tori Tⁿ = Rⁿ/Zⁿ, open subsets, products, matrix groups.
    Hausdorff matters: the line with two origins is locally Euclidean but
    not a manifold. PARTITIONS OF UNITY exist on every manifold.
  - TANGENT SPACE TₚM = derivations of germs at p, basis ∂/∂x¹, ..., ∂/∂xⁿ;
    the DIFFERENTIAL dFₚ : TₚM → T_{F(p)}N is the Jacobian in charts.
    IMMERSION (dF injective), SUBMERSION (dF surjective), EMBEDDING
    (immersion + homeomorphism onto image). INVERSE FUNCTION THEOREM,
    RANK THEOREM. REGULAR VALUE THEOREM: if c is a regular value of F :
    M → N then F⁻¹(c) is an embedded submanifold of dimension dim M −
    dim N. SARD: critical values have measure 0. WHITNEY: every n-manifold
    embeds in R²ⁿ.
  - MATRIX GROUPS: GL(n) (dim n²), SL(n) = det⁻¹(1) (n² − 1), O(n) (n(n −
    1)/2, tangent space at I = skew matrices), U(n) (n²), SU(n) (n² − 1),
    Sp(2n, R) (n(2n + 1)); Grassmannian G_k(Rⁿ) has dimension k(n − k).
  - VECTOR FIELDS AND FLOWS: a vector field X is a section of TM; its
    integral curves form a local FLOW φ_t (global if M is compact). LIE
    BRACKET [X, Y]f = X(Yf) − Y(Xf), in coordinates [X, Y]ⁱ = Xʲ∂ⱼYⁱ −
    Yʲ∂ⱼXⁱ; antisymmetric, Jacobi identity, [X, fY] = (Xf)Y + f[X, Y].
    [X, Y] = 0 iff the flows commute; [X, Y] measures the failure of the
    "square" φ₋ₛ ψ₋ₜ φₛ ψₜ to close (displacement ≈ st[X, Y]). FROBENIUS: a
    distribution is integrable iff it is closed under brackets.
  - VECTOR BUNDLE π : E → M of rank k: fibres Eₚ ≅ Rᵏ, LOCAL TRIVIALIZATIONS
    π⁻¹(U) ≅ U × Rᵏ, TRANSITION FUNCTIONS g_αβ : U_α ∩ U_β → GL(k) with the
    COCYCLE condition g_αβ g_βγ = g_αγ. Constructions: TM, T*M, ΛᵏT*M,
    ⊕ (Whitney sum), ⊗, Hom, dual, pullback. A rank-k bundle is trivial iff
    it has k everywhere-independent sections. Examples: the MÖBIUS line
    bundle (= tautological bundle over RP¹) is non-trivial; TS¹, TS³, TS⁷
    are trivial; TS² is not (HAIRY BALL); TSⁿ ⊕ ε¹ ≅ εⁿ⁺¹ (stably trivial).
    Real line bundles over M ↔ H¹(M; Z/2); complex ones ↔ H²(M; Z) (c₁).
  - DIFFERENTIAL FORMS Ωᵏ(M): sections of ΛᵏT*M, locally ∑ f_I dx^I.
    WEDGE product (α ∧ β = (−1)^{kl} β ∧ α), EXTERIOR DERIVATIVE d (the
    unique antiderivation with df = differential, d² = 0), PULLBACK F*
    (commutes with d and ∧), INTERIOR PRODUCT i_X, LIE DERIVATIVE with
    CARTAN'S MAGIC FORMULA L_X = d i_X + i_X d. In R³, d on 0-, 1-, 2-forms
    is grad, curl, div, and d² = 0 is curl grad = 0, div curl = 0.
  - ORIENTATION AND INTEGRATION: M is orientable iff it has a nowhere-zero
    n-form (iff an atlas with det(transition Jacobians) > 0); ∫_M ω defined
    with charts and a partition of unity. STOKES: ∫_M dω = ∫_{∂M} ω for
    compactly supported ω on an oriented manifold with boundary (Green,
    Gauss, classical Stokes, FTC are special cases). Consequence: on a
    closed manifold ∫_M dη = 0. DEGREE of F : M → N (closed, oriented,
    connected, same dimension): ∫_M F*ω = deg F ∫_N ω.
  - DE RHAM COHOMOLOGY Hᵏ_dR(M) = ker d / im d (closed / exact k-forms).
    H⁰ = locally constant functions = R^(#components). POINCARÉ LEMMA: on a
    star-shaped open set every closed k-form (k ≥ 1) is exact. HOMOTOPY
    INVARIANCE, MAYER-VIETORIS long exact sequence, KÜNNETH formula. The
    form dθ = (x dy − y dx)/(x² + y²) is closed, not exact on R² ∖ 0
    (∮ = 2π). For M closed, connected, oriented: Hⁿ(M) ≅ R via ∫_M, and
    POINCARÉ DUALITY Hᵏ ≅ (Hⁿ⁻ᵏ)*; non-orientable ⇒ Hⁿ = 0. DE RHAM'S
    THEOREM: Hᵏ_dR(M) ≅ Hᵏ(M; R) (singular). χ(M) = ∑(−1)ᵏ dim Hᵏ.
  - STANDARD COMPUTATIONS: Hᵏ(Sⁿ) = R for k = 0, n, else 0; H*(Tⁿ) has
    dimensions C(n, k); genus-g surface Σ_g: 1, 2g, 1; RPⁿ: R in degree 0
    (and n if n is odd); CPⁿ: R in even degrees 0, ..., 2n; Klein bottle:
    1, 1, 0; R² minus k points: 1, k.
  - POINCARÉ-HOPF: for a vector field with isolated zeros on a closed
    manifold, ∑ indices = χ(M); hence a closed M has a nowhere-zero vector
    field iff χ(M) = 0 (so on S^even every field vanishes somewhere).
  - RIEMANNIAN METRIC g: a smoothly varying inner product on each TₚM,
    locally g = gᵢⱼ dxⁱ dxʲ. Exists on every manifold (partition of unity).
    Length L(γ) = ∫|γ′| dt, distance d(p, q) = inf L, volume form √det g
    dx¹ ∧ ... ∧ dxⁿ. Models: Euclidean Rⁿ, round Sⁿ, HYPERBOLIC upper half
    plane (dx² + dy²)/y² with cosh d(z, w) = 1 + |z − w|²/(2 Im z Im w),
    Poincaré disk 4|dz|²/(1 − |z|²)². Induced metrics on submanifolds.
  - CONNECTIONS on a bundle E: ∇ : Γ(TM) × Γ(E) → Γ(E), C^∞-linear in X,
    LEIBNIZ in s: ∇_X(fs) = (Xf)s + f∇_X s. Locally ∇ = d + A (A a matrix
    of 1-forms); the difference of two connections is a tensor, but the
    Christoffel symbols are NOT a tensor. PARALLEL TRANSPORT along curves;
    HOLONOMY. LEVI-CIVITA THEOREM: a unique torsion-free (∇_X Y − ∇_Y X =
    [X, Y]) metric (Xg(Y, Z) = g(∇_X Y, Z) + g(Y, ∇_X Z)) connection, given
    by the KOSZUL formula; Γᵏᵢⱼ = ½gᵏˡ(∂ᵢgⱼₗ + ∂ⱼgᵢₗ − ∂ₗgᵢⱼ).
  - CURVATURE R(X, Y)Z = ∇_X∇_Y Z − ∇_Y∇_X Z − ∇_{[X,Y]}Z (a tensor; for a
    bundle F = dA + A ∧ A). Symmetries R_{abcd} = −R_{bacd} = −R_{abdc} =
    R_{cdab}, first Bianchi R(X,Y)Z + cyclic = 0; n²(n² − 1)/12 independent
    components. SECTIONAL K(σ) = ⟨R(X,Y)Y, X⟩/|X ∧ Y|², RICCI Ric(Y, Z) =
    tr(X ↦ R(X,Y)Z), SCALAR s = tr Ric. Constant curvature κ: Sⁿ (κ = 1,
    Ric = (n − 1)g, s = n(n − 1)), Rⁿ (0), Hⁿ (−1). For surfaces K = Gauss
    curvature, s = 2K; THEOREMA EGREGIUM: K is intrinsic (mean curvature is
    not). Formulas: dr² + G(r)²dθ² ⇒ K = −G″/G; e^{2f}(dx² + dy²) ⇒ K =
    −e^{−2f}Δf; graph z = f(x, y) ⇒ K = (f_xx f_yy − f_xy²)/(1 + |∇f|²)².
  - GAUSS-BONNET: ∫_M K dA + ∫_{∂M} k_g ds + ∑(exterior angles) = 2πχ(M).
    Closed surfaces: ∫K dA = 2πχ = 2π(2 − 2g). Geodesic triangles: angle
    sum − π = ∫_T K dA (sphere: excess = area; H²: defect = area).
  - GEODESICS: ∇_γ′γ′ = 0, i.e. ẍᵏ + Γᵏᵢⱼ ẋⁱ ẋʲ = 0; critical points of
    energy/length; constant speed. Examples: lines in Rⁿ, great circles on
    Sⁿ, vertical lines and semicircles ⟂ R in H², helices on a cylinder.
    EXPONENTIAL MAP exp_p(v) = γ_v(1), a diffeomorphism near 0 (normal
    coordinates); geodesics are LOCALLY minimizing. CLAIRAUT: r cos ψ =
    const on surfaces of revolution. HOPF-RINOW: for connected M, metric
    completeness ⇔ geodesic completeness ⇔ closed bounded sets compact, and
    then any two points are joined by a minimizing geodesic.
  - JACOBI FIELDS AND COMPARISON: J″ + R(J, γ′)γ′ = 0; CONJUGATE points
    (antipodes on Sⁿ at distance π) — past one a geodesic stops minimizing.
    BONNET-MYERS: complete, Ric ≥ (n − 1)k g with k > 0 ⇒ diam ≤ π/√k, M
    compact, π₁ finite. CARTAN-HADAMARD: complete, K ≤ 0 ⇒ exp_p is a
    covering, universal cover ≅ Rⁿ (no metric of K ≤ 0 on Sⁿ).

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. (a) Show that the two stereographic projections σ_N, σ_S : Sⁿ ∖ {pole}
    → Rⁿ form a smooth atlas, computing the transition map. (b) Give an
    atlas of RPⁿ and the transition map for n = 1. (c) Why is the "line
    with two origins" not a manifold?
    Answer: (a) σ_S ∘ σ_N⁻¹(u) = u/|u|² on Rⁿ ∖ 0, a C^∞ involution.
    (b) Uᵢ = {[x] : xᵢ ≠ 0}, φᵢ[x] = (x₀/xᵢ, ..., x̂ᵢ, ..., xₙ/xᵢ); for n =
    1, u ↦ 1/u. (c) It is not Hausdorff.
    Teacher explains: Write points of Sⁿ ⊂ Rⁿ⁺¹ as (x, t), x ∈ Rⁿ, |x|² +
    t² = 1, N = (0, 1), S = (0, −1). The line from N through (x, t) hits
    the plane t = 0 at σ_N(x, t) = x/(1 − t); similarly σ_S(x, t) = x/(1 +
    t). Inverting: if u = σ_N(x, t) then |u|² = (1 + t)/(1 − t), so
       σ_N⁻¹(u) = (2u, |u|² − 1)/(|u|² + 1).
    Then σ_S(σ_N⁻¹(u)) = [2u/(|u|² + 1)] / [1 + (|u|² − 1)/(|u|² + 1)] =
    2u/(2|u|²) = u/|u|². This is smooth on σ_N(Sⁿ ∖ {N, S}) = Rⁿ ∖ 0 and
    is its own inverse, so both transition maps are C^∞: the atlas is
    smooth. (b) On Uᵢ the ratio coordinates are well defined because
    rescaling x does not change them; on U₀ ∩ U₁ ⊂ RP¹, u = x₁/x₀ and v =
    x₀/x₁ = 1/u, smooth for u ≠ 0. (c) Take two copies of R glued along
    R ∖ 0. The two origins 0₁, 0₂ have no disjoint neighbourhoods, yet
    every point has a chart to R. Without Hausdorff, limits of sequences
    are not unique and partitions of unity can fail. Common mistakes:
    thinking one chart suffices for Sⁿ (Sⁿ is compact, Rⁿ is not, so no
    single chart can cover it); forgetting to check smoothness in BOTH
    directions. Now you try: give the transition map of CP¹ with charts
    [z₀ : z₁] ↦ z₁/z₀ and z₀/z₁. (w = 1/z, holomorphic on C ∖ 0)

D2. Prove that O(n) = {A : AᵀA = I} is a submanifold of M_n(R) ≅ R^{n²}
    of dimension n(n − 1)/2 and identify T_I O(n). Do the same for SL(n).
    Answer: I is a regular value of F(A) = AᵀA : M_n → Sym_n; T_I O(n) =
    skew-symmetric matrices; SL(n) has dimension n² − 1 and T_I SL(n) =
    trace-zero matrices.
    Teacher explains: F takes values in the SYMMETRIC matrices Sym_n, a
    space of dimension n(n + 1)/2 (this choice of target is the key).
    Differentiate F along A + sH: dF_A(H) = d/ds|₀ (A + sH)ᵀ(A + sH) =
    AᵀH + HᵀA. Surjectivity at A ∈ O(n): given S ∈ Sym_n put H = AS/2;
    then AᵀH + HᵀA = S/2 + Sᵀ/2 = S. So I is a regular value and O(n) =
    F⁻¹(I) is a submanifold of dimension
       n² − n(n + 1)/2 = n(n − 1)/2,
    with tangent space ker dF_I = {H : H + Hᵀ = 0}. (n = 2: dimension 1,
    two circles; n = 3: dimension 3.) For SL(n): det is a polynomial, and
    d(det)_A(H) = det(A) tr(A⁻¹H) (Jacobi's formula; at A = I it is
    tr H). For det A = 1, H = A gives the value n ≠ 0, so the differential
    is onto R: 1 is a regular value and dim SL(n) = n² − 1, T_I SL(n) =
    {tr H = 0}. Common mistakes: taking the target to be M_n (then dF is
    never onto and the theorem gives nothing); claiming det⁻¹(0) is a
    manifold (0 is not a regular value: d det = 0 at A = 0). Now you try:
    dim U(n), using F(A) = A*A into Hermitian matrices. (2n² − n² = n²)

D3. Let X = ∂_x and Y = x ∂_y on R². (a) Compute [X, Y]. (b) Compute the
    flows φ_t of X and ψ_s of Y and the point ψ₋ₛ φ₋ₜ ψₛ φₜ(x, y). (c) Show
    that X = −y∂_x + x∂_y and Y = x∂_x + y∂_y commute and interpret.
    Answer: (a) ∂_y; (b) φ_t(x, y) = (x + t, y), ψ_s(x, y) = (x, y + sx),
    the loop ends at (x, y + st); (c) [X, Y] = 0: rotations commute with
    dilations.
    Teacher explains: (a) Apply to a test function f: X(Yf) = ∂_x(x f_y) =
    f_y + x f_xy and Y(Xf) = x f_xy, so [X, Y]f = f_y, i.e. [X, Y] = ∂_y.
    (b) Integral curves of X: ẋ = 1, ẏ = 0, so φ_t(x, y) = (x + t, y). Of
    Y: ẋ = 0, ẏ = x, so ψ_s(x, y) = (x, y + sx). Now follow the "square":
       (x, y) →φ_t (x + t, y) →ψ_s (x + t, y + s(x + t))
              →φ₋ₜ (x, y + sx + st) →ψ₋ₛ (x, y + st).
    The square fails to close by st in the ∂_y direction: exactly st[X, Y],
    the general second-order picture. (c) Using [X, Y]ⁱ = Xʲ∂ⱼYⁱ − Yʲ∂ⱼXⁱ:
    X(Y-components) = (X(x), X(y)) = (−y, x); Y(X-components) = (Y(−y),
    Y(x)) = (−y, x); difference 0. The flows (rotation by t, scaling by
    eˢ) commute, as they must when the bracket vanishes. Common mistakes:
    forgetting the second-order terms cancel (xf_xy appears twice); sign
    conventions — [X, Y] = XY − YX, so [Y, X] = −∂_y. Now you try:
    [∂_x, eˣ ∂_y]. (eˣ ∂_y)

D4. (a) Define the Möbius line bundle over S¹ via transition functions and
    prove it is not trivial. (b) Show that TS¹ and TS³ are trivial and that
    TS² is not. (c) Is Möbius ⊕ Möbius trivial?
    Answer: (a) cover S¹ by two arcs; g = +1 on one component of the
    overlap and −1 on the other; every section vanishes somewhere. (b)
    TS³: x ↦ xi, xj, xk (quaternions); TS²: χ(S²) = 2 ≠ 0. (c) Yes.
    Teacher explains: (a) Let U, V be two open arcs covering S¹; U ∩ V has
    two components W₊, W₋. Glue U × R and V × R by (p, t) ~ (p, g(p)t)
    with g = 1 on W₊, g = −1 on W₋ (the cocycle condition is automatic with
    two sets). Equivalently E = [0, 1] × R/((0, t) ~ (1, −t)). A section is
    a continuous s : [0, 1] → R with s(1) = −s(0); by the intermediate
    value theorem s has a zero. The trivial bundle S¹ × R has the nowhere-
    zero section 1, so E is not trivial. (b) S¹: (x, y) ↦ (−y, x) is a
    nowhere-zero tangent field, so TS¹ ≅ S¹ × R. S³ = unit quaternions; for
    q ∈ S³ the vectors qi, qj, qk are orthogonal to q (Re(q̄ · qi) = Re(i) =
    0) and orthonormal, giving three independent fields: TS³ is trivial.
    For S², a trivialization would give a nowhere-zero vector field, but by
    Poincaré-Hopf the indices of any field sum to χ(S²) = 2 ≠ 0 (hairy
    ball). (c) Möbius ⊕ Möbius has transition function g·I₂ = −I₂ on W₋,
    and −I₂ lies in the path component of I₂ in GL(2) (rotation by π);
    deforming g to I₂ shows the bundle is trivial. Common mistakes: "the
    total space is non-orientable, so the bundle has no section" — it has
    sections, just no NOWHERE-ZERO one; thinking "orientable ⇒ trivial"
    in rank ≥ 2 (TS² is orientable, non-trivial). Now you try: why is
    every real line bundle over S² trivial? (S² is simply connected:
    H¹(S²; Z/2) = 0)

D5. On R² let ω = x dy − y dx. (a) Compute dω. (b) Compute the pullback of
    dx ∧ dy and of ω under polar coordinates and check F*(dω) = d(F*ω).
    (c) Use Cartan's formula to compute L_X(dx ∧ dy) for X = x∂_x + y∂_y.
    (d) Prove d² = 0 on functions in coordinates.
    Answer: (a) 2 dx ∧ dy; (b) r dr ∧ dθ and r² dθ; (c) 2 dx ∧ dy;
    (d) symmetry of mixed partials.
    Teacher explains: (a) dω = dx ∧ dy − dy ∧ dx = 2 dx ∧ dy. (b) With x =
    r cos θ, y = r sin θ:
       dx = cos θ dr − r sin θ dθ,   dy = sin θ dr + r cos θ dθ,
       dx ∧ dy = r cos²θ dr ∧ dθ − r sin²θ dθ ∧ dr = r dr ∧ dθ,
       x dy − y dx = r cos θ (r cos θ dθ) + r sin θ (r sin θ dθ) = r² dθ
    (the dr terms cancel). Check: d(r² dθ) = 2r dr ∧ dθ = F*(2 dx ∧ dy).
    This naturality F* d = d F* is why forms are the right objects to
    integrate: no Jacobians have to be remembered, they are built in.
    (c) L_X = d i_X + i_X d. Here d(dx ∧ dy) = 0 and i_X(dx ∧ dy) = dx(X)
    dy − dy(X) dx = x dy − y dx, whose d is 2 dx ∧ dy. So L_X(dx ∧ dy) =
    2 dx ∧ dy: the flow (x, y) ↦ eᵗ(x, y) scales areas by e^{2t}, whose
    derivative at t = 0 is 2. (d) d(df) = d(∑ ∂ᵢf dxⁱ) = ∑ᵢⱼ ∂ⱼ∂ᵢf dxʲ ∧
    dxⁱ; the coefficient is symmetric in (i, j) and dxʲ ∧ dxⁱ is
    antisymmetric, so the sum is 0. Common mistakes: writing dx ∧ dx = 2 dx
    ∧ dx instead of 0; forgetting the sign in i_X (it is an antiderivation).
    Now you try: pull back dx ∧ dy under (u, v) ↦ (u², v). (2u du ∧ dv)

D6. Let ω = (x dy ∧ dz + y dz ∧ dx + z dx ∧ dy)/r³ on R³ ∖ 0, r = |x|.
    (a) Show dω = 0. (b) Compute ∫_{S²} ω. (c) Deduce, via Stokes, that ω is
    not exact, so H²_dR(R³ ∖ 0) ≠ 0. (d) Verify Stokes for x dy on the
    unit disc.
    Answer: (a) div(x/r³) = 0; (b) 4π; (c) exact would force ∫ = 0;
    (d) both sides equal π.
    Teacher explains: STOKES: ∫_M dη = ∫_{∂M} η. (a) dω = (∂_x(x/r³) +
    ∂_y(y/r³) + ∂_z(z/r³)) dx ∧ dy ∧ dz, and ∂_x(x/r³) = 1/r³ − 3x²/r⁵;
    summing, 3/r³ − 3r²/r⁵ = 0. (b) On the unit sphere r = 1 and ω
    restricts to x dy ∧ dz + y dz ∧ dx + z dx ∧ dy = i_N(dx ∧ dy ∧ dz)
    with N the outward normal, i.e. the AREA FORM. So ∫_{S²} ω = 4π. (Or:
    for the closed ball, ∫_{S²}(x dy∧dz + ...) = ∫_B 3 dV = 4π.) (c) If ω
    = dη on R³ ∖ 0, then since S² is a closed manifold, ∫_{S²} ω = ∫_{S²}
    dη = ∫_{∂S²} η = 0, a contradiction. So [ω] ≠ 0 (in fact it spans
    H² ≅ R, since R³ ∖ 0 ≃ S²). The same form measures flux: any closed
    surface enclosing 0 gives 4π, one not enclosing 0 gives 0 (Gauss's law).
    (d) D = unit disc: ∫_D d(x dy) = ∫_D dx ∧ dy = π; ∂D: x = cos t, y =
    sin t, ∫₀^{2π} cos²t dt = π. ✓ Common mistakes: applying Stokes to ω
    on the ball (ω is singular at 0, so B is not in its domain);
    forgetting the orientation (outward normal first). Now you try: ∮ x dy
    around the ellipse x²/4 + y²/9 = 1. (area = 6π)

D7. (a) Prove the Poincaré lemma for 1-forms on a star-shaped open set
    U ⊆ Rⁿ. (b) Show that dθ = (x dy − y dx)/(x² + y²) is closed but not
    exact on R² ∖ 0, and describe H¹_dR(R² ∖ 0).
    Answer: (a) f(x) = ∫₀¹ ∑aᵢ(tx) xᵢ dt is a primitive; (b) ∮_{S¹} dθ =
    2π; H¹ ≅ R, every closed ω = c dθ + df with c = (1/2π)∮ω.
    Teacher explains: (a) Let ω = ∑aᵢ dxᵢ be closed: ∂ⱼaᵢ = ∂ᵢaⱼ. U is
    star-shaped about 0, so the segment t ↦ tx lies in U; define f(x) =
    ∫₀¹ ∑ᵢ aᵢ(tx) xᵢ dt (the line integral of ω along it). Differentiating
    under the integral (smooth integrand on a compact interval):
       ∂ⱼf(x) = ∫₀¹ [aⱼ(tx) + ∑ᵢ t xᵢ ∂ⱼaᵢ(tx)] dt
              = ∫₀¹ [aⱼ(tx) + ∑ᵢ t xᵢ ∂ᵢaⱼ(tx)] dt = ∫₀¹ d/dt[t aⱼ(tx)] dt
              = aⱼ(x).
    So df = ω. (b) With P = −y/ρ, Q = x/ρ, ρ = x² + y²: Q_x = (y² − x²)/ρ²
    = P_y, so d(dθ) = 0. On the unit circle (cos t, sin t), dθ pulls back
    to dt and ∮ = 2π. If dθ = df, then ∮ df = f(end) − f(start) = 0: so it
    is not exact. The name: locally dθ = d(arg), but arg has no single-
    valued branch on R² ∖ 0. Given any closed ω, put c = (1/2π)∮_{S¹}ω;
    then ω − c dθ has zero period on every loop (all loops in R² ∖ 0 are
    homotopic to multiples of S¹), so its line integral from a base point
    defines f with df = ω − c dθ. Hence H¹(R² ∖ 0) = R[dθ]. Common
    mistakes: concluding "closed ⇒ exact" without a star-shaped (or simply
    connected) domain; thinking dθ is exact because it is "d of θ" (θ is
    not a function on R² ∖ 0). Now you try: find a primitive of 2xy dx +
    x² dy on R². (x²y)

D8. (a) Use Mayer-Vietoris to compute H*_dR(Sⁿ). (b) State Künneth and
    compute H*_dR(T²) with explicit generators. (c) Give H* of the genus-g
    surface and check χ.
    Answer: (a) R in degrees 0 and n, 0 otherwise. (b) H⁰ = R, H¹ = R²
    (dx, dy), H² = R (dx ∧ dy). (c) 1, 2g, 1; χ = 2 − 2g.
    Teacher explains: MAYER-VIETORIS for M = U ∪ V: the short exact
    sequence 0 → Ω(M) → Ω(U) ⊕ Ω(V) → Ω(U ∩ V) → 0 (restrictions,
    difference; surjective by a partition of unity) gives
       ... → Hᵏ(M) → Hᵏ(U) ⊕ Hᵏ(V) → Hᵏ(U ∩ V) →δ Hᵏ⁺¹(M) → ...
    (a) Take U = Sⁿ ∖ N, V = Sⁿ ∖ S, both ≅ Rⁿ (cohomology R in degree 0
    only, Poincaré lemma), with U ∩ V ≅ Sⁿ⁻¹ × R ≃ Sⁿ⁻¹. For k ≥ 1 the
    middle terms Hᵏ(U) ⊕ Hᵏ(V) vanish, so δ : Hᵏ(Sⁿ⁻¹) ≅ Hᵏ⁺¹(Sⁿ). In low
    degree: 0 → H⁰(Sⁿ) → R² → H⁰(Sⁿ⁻¹) → H¹(Sⁿ) → 0. For n ≥ 2, Sⁿ⁻¹ is
    connected, the map R² → R, (a, b) ↦ a − b is onto, so H¹(Sⁿ) = 0 and
    H⁰ = R. Start with S¹ (n = 1: H⁰(S⁰) = R², so H¹(S¹) = R) and induct:
    Hⁿ(Sⁿ) ≅ Hⁿ⁻¹(Sⁿ⁻¹) ≅ ... ≅ H¹(S¹) = R, all other positive degrees 0.
    (b) KÜNNETH: Hᵏ(M × N) ≅ ⊕_{i+j=k} Hⁱ(M) ⊗ Hʲ(N) (finite-dimensional
    cohomology). T² = S¹ × S¹: dims 1, 1 + 1, 1. Generators on R²/Z²: dx
    and dy (closed, periods 1 around the two circles, so not exact) and
    dx ∧ dy (∫_{T²} = 1 ≠ 0). (c) Σ_g: H⁰ = R (connected), H² = R
    (closed, oriented), and H¹ = R^{2g} (from the 4g-gon or Mayer-
    Vietoris); χ = 1 − 2g + 1 = 2 − 2g. Common mistakes: forgetting to
    check the connecting map in degree 0; applying Künneth to non-finite-
    type spaces carelessly. Now you try: dimensions of H*(S² × S²).
    (1, 0, 2, 0, 1)

D9. The hyperbolic upper half-plane H² = {y > 0}, g = (dx² + dy²)/y².
    (a) Compute the length of the vertical segment from ai to bi (0 < a <
    b) and prove it is the shortest curve. (b) Compute the length of the
    horizontal segment from i to 1 + i and compare with d(i, 1 + i) =
    arccosh(3/2). (c) Why does every manifold admit a Riemannian metric?
    Answer: (a) ln(b/a); (b) 1 > arccosh(3/2) ≈ 0.96242; horizontal lines
    are not geodesics. (c) glue local Euclidean metrics with a partition
    of unity; positive-definiteness is preserved by convex combinations.
    Teacher explains: (a) For γ(t) = (x(t), y(t)), L(γ) = ∫ √(ẋ² + ẏ²)/y
    dt. On the vertical segment x = 0, y = t ∈ [a, b]: L = ∫_a^b dt/t =
    ln(b/a). For ANY curve from ai to bi,
       L(γ) ≥ ∫ |ẏ|/y dt ≥ |∫ ẏ/y dt| = ln(b/a),
    with equality iff ẋ ≡ 0 and ẏ ≥ 0: the vertical segment is the unique
    minimizer, hence a geodesic, and d(ai, bi) = ln(b/a). Check with the
    formula cosh d = 1 + |z − w|²/(2 Im z Im w): for i, 2i, 1 + 1/4 = 5/4
    = cosh(ln 2) ✓. (b) Along y = 1 the metric is dx², length 1. But
    cosh d(i, 1 + i) = 1 + 1/2 = 3/2, d ≈ 0.96242 < 1: the geodesic (the
    arc of the circle centred at 1/2 of radius √5/2) bulges upward, where
    the metric is smaller. As y → 0 distances blow up: the real axis is at
    infinite distance ("boundary at infinity"). (c) Cover M by charts
    U_α with Euclidean metrics g_α = ∑(dxᵢ)², take a partition of unity
    ρ_α subordinate to it and put g = ∑ρ_α g_α: locally finite, smooth,
    and at each point a convex combination of inner products is an inner
    product. (The same trick FAILS for Lorentzian metrics: S² has none.)
    Common mistakes: measuring hyperbolic lengths with the Euclidean
    metric; assuming straight lines are geodesics. Now you try: d(i, e·i).
    (1)

D10. (a) Derive the Koszul formula and deduce the Christoffel symbols of
     the Levi-Civita connection. (b) Compute them for the round sphere g =
     dθ² + sin²θ dφ².
     Answer: (a) 2g(∇_X Y, Z) = Xg(Y, Z) + Yg(X, Z) − Zg(X, Y) + g([X, Y],
     Z) − g([X, Z], Y) − g([Y, Z], X); Γᵏᵢⱼ = ½gᵏˡ(∂ᵢgⱼₗ + ∂ⱼgᵢₗ − ∂ₗgᵢⱼ).
     (b) Γ^θ_φφ = −sin θ cos θ, Γ^φ_θφ = Γ^φ_φθ = cot θ, all others 0.
     Teacher explains: (a) Assume ∇ is metric and torsion-free. Write metric
     compatibility for the three cyclic permutations:
        Xg(Y, Z) = g(∇_X Y, Z) + g(Y, ∇_X Z),
        Yg(Z, X) = g(∇_Y Z, X) + g(Z, ∇_Y X),
        Zg(X, Y) = g(∇_Z X, Y) + g(X, ∇_Z Y).
     Add the first two, subtract the third and use ∇_A B − ∇_B A = [A, B]
     to replace each pair: the result is the Koszul formula. Its right side
     does not involve ∇, so ∇ is UNIQUE; conversely, defining ∇_X Y by it
     (the right side is C^∞-linear in Z) gives a connection with both
     properties, so it EXISTS. With coordinate fields ([∂ᵢ, ∂ⱼ] = 0):
     2g(∇_{∂ᵢ}∂ⱼ, ∂ₗ) = ∂ᵢgⱼₗ + ∂ⱼgᵢₗ − ∂ₗgᵢⱼ; writing ∇_{∂ᵢ}∂ⱼ = Γᵏᵢⱼ∂ₖ
     and raising the index gives the formula; torsion-free ⇔ Γᵏᵢⱼ = Γᵏⱼᵢ.
     (b) g_θθ = 1, g_φφ = sin²θ, g_θφ = 0; the only non-constant entry is
     g_φφ, depending on θ, with ∂_θ g_φφ = 2 sin θ cos θ. Then
        Γ^θ_φφ = ½ g^θθ(−∂_θ g_φφ) = −sin θ cos θ,
        Γ^φ_θφ = ½ g^φφ ∂_θ g_φφ = (2 sin θ cos θ)/(2 sin²θ) = cot θ,
     and all others vanish (e.g. Γ^θ_θθ = ½∂_θ g_θθ = 0). Geometric check:
     ∇_{∂φ}∂φ = −sin θ cos θ ∂θ points toward the equator: the latitude
     circles curve away from being geodesics except at θ = π/2. Common
     mistakes: forgetting the factor ½ or g^{kl}; treating Γ as a tensor.
     Now you try: Christoffel symbols of dr² + r²dθ². (Γ^r_θθ = −r,
     Γ^θ_rθ = 1/r)

D11. (a) For g = dr² + G(r)² dθ², show K = −G″/G using the curvature
     tensor. Evaluate for G = r, sin r, sinh r. (b) Verify Gauss-Bonnet for
     the geodesic disc of radius ρ < π on the unit sphere. (c) Use Gauss-
     Bonnet to find the area of a hyperbolic triangle with angles π/3,
     π/4, π/6, and show the torus carries no metric with K < 0.
     Answer: (a) K = −G″/G: 0, 1, −1. (b) 2π(1 − cos ρ) + 2π cos ρ = 2π.
     (c) π/4; ∫_{T²} K dA = 2πχ(T²) = 0.
     Teacher explains: (a) As in D10 (with sin θ replaced by G): Γ^r_θθ =
     −GG′, Γ^θ_rθ = G′/G, others 0. So ∇_r ∂_r = 0, ∇_r ∂_θ = (G′/G)∂_θ,
     ∇_θ ∂_θ = −GG′∂_r. Then
        ∇_r∇_θ∂_θ = −(GG′)′ ∂_r = −(G′² + GG″)∂_r,
        ∇_θ∇_r∂_θ = (G′/G)∇_θ∂_θ = −G′² ∂_r,
     and [∂_r, ∂_θ] = 0, so R(∂_r, ∂_θ)∂_θ = −GG″ ∂_r. K = ⟨R(∂_r, ∂_θ)∂_θ,
     ∂_r⟩/(|∂_r|²|∂_θ|²) = −GG″/G² = −G″/G. G = r (flat polar): 0; G =
     sin r (unit sphere, r = distance from the pole): 1; G = sinh r
     (hyperbolic plane in polar form): −1. (b) The disc D_ρ has area ∫₀^{2π}
     ∫₀^ρ sin r dr dθ = 2π(1 − cos ρ). Its boundary circle r = ρ has length
     2π sin ρ and geodesic curvature k_g = G′/G = cot ρ (the divergence of
     the unit normal ∂_r), so ∫k_g ds = 2π cos ρ. Sum: 2π = 2πχ(disc) ✓.
     (c) For a geodesic triangle, ∫_T K dA = (α + β + γ) − π. With K = −1,
     Area = π − (π/3 + π/4 + π/6) = π − 3π/4 = π/4. For T², χ = 0, so ∫K dA
     = 0: K must be positive somewhere if it is negative somewhere; K < 0
     everywhere is impossible (as is K > 0). Common mistakes: using
     interior instead of exterior angles; forgetting ∫k_g ds on the
     boundary. Now you try: area of the geodesic triangle on the unit
     sphere with three right angles. (π/2: an octant, 4π/8)

D12. On the unit sphere g = dθ² + sin²θ dφ²: (a) write the geodesic
     equations and decide which of the equator, the meridians and the
     latitude θ = θ₀ are geodesics; (b) parallel transport a vector once
     around the latitude θ = θ₀ and find the holonomy angle; (c) find the
     first conjugate point along a geodesic and explain why arcs longer
     than π do not minimize; (d) state Hopf-Rinow.
     Answer: (a) θ̈ − sin θ cos θ φ̇² = 0, φ̈ + 2 cot θ θ̇ φ̇ = 0; equator and
     meridians yes, other latitudes no. (b) rotation by 2π cos θ₀ relative
     to the frame, i.e. holonomy 2π(1 − cos θ₀) = cap area (mod 2π).
     (c) at distance π (the antipode).
     Teacher explains: (a) ẍᵏ + Γᵏᵢⱼẋⁱẋʲ = 0 with the symbols of D10 (the
     cross term appears twice). Meridian φ = const, θ = t: both equations
     hold. Equator θ = π/2, φ = t: cos θ = 0, φ̈ = 0 ✓. Latitude θ = θ₀ ∉
     {0, π/2, π}, φ = t: first equation gives −sin θ₀ cos θ₀ ≠ 0 ✗. (b) V =
     a∂_θ + b∂_φ along φ ↦ (θ₀, φ): ∇_φ V = 0 reads a′ − sin θ₀ cos θ₀ b = 0,
     b′ + cot θ₀ a = 0. In the orthonormal frame e₁ = ∂_θ, e₂ = ∂_φ/sin θ₀,
     with c = b sin θ₀: a′ = cos θ₀ c, c′ = −cos θ₀ a — a rotation at rate
     cos θ₀. After φ = 2π the vector has turned by 2π cos θ₀ relative to
     the frame; since 2π cos θ₀ ≡ −2π(1 − cos θ₀) (mod 2π), the holonomy
     angle is (up to orientation sign) 2π(1 − cos θ₀), exactly the area
     of the polar cap: holonomy = ∫K dA, a local form of Gauss-Bonnet.
     (θ₀ = π/2: no net rotation; θ₀ → 0: rotation → 0 as well.)
     (c) JACOBI: along a unit-speed geodesic in constant curvature 1, J″ +
     J = 0, so J(t) = sin t vanishes at t = π: all geodesics from p
     reconverge at −p (conjugate point). An arc of length π + ε can be
     shortened: the complementary arc has length π − ε. In general,
     geodesics minimize up to (at most) the first conjugate point. (d)
     HOPF-RINOW: for connected (M, g) the following are equivalent: (M, d)
     complete; every geodesic extends for all time; closed bounded sets
     are compact; exp_p is defined on all of TₚM for some p. Then any two
     points are joined by a minimizing geodesic. Counterexample without
     completeness: R² ∖ 0, points (±1, 0). Common mistakes: "geodesics
     always minimize" (only locally); forgetting to reduce mod 2π
     in (b). Now you try: holonomy around θ₀ = π/3. (π)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Give the dimension (as a real manifold).
    a) S⁵                              b) RP⁴
    c) CP³                             d) T⁷
    e) GL(3, R)                        f) SL(3, R)
    g) O(4)                            h) U(3)
    i) SU(3)                           j) Sp(4, R) (4 × 4 symplectic)
    k) the Stiefel manifold V₂(R⁵) of orthonormal 2-frames in R⁵
    l) the Grassmannian G₂(R⁵)         m) the total space of TS³
    n) {x ∈ R⁴ : x₁² + x₂² − x₃² − x₄² = 1}
    o) the rank-1 matrices in M₂(R)    p) the complex Grassmannian G₂(C⁴)
    q) the unit tangent bundle of S²   r) S² × RP³ × T²
    s) O(n) for general n; how many components?
    Answers:
      a) 5       b) 4       c) 6       d) 7       e) 9       f) 8
      g) 6       h) 9       i) 8       j) 10 (n(2n + 1), n = 2)
      k) 7 (nk − k(k + 1)/2)            l) 6 (k(n − k))     m) 6
      n) 3 (1 is a regular value)       o) 3 (r(m + n − r))
      p) 8 (2k(n − k))                  q) 3 (≅ SO(3) ≅ RP³)
      r) 7                              s) n(n − 1)/2; 2 components
         (det = ±1)

M2. Is c a regular value of f? Is f⁻¹(c) a smooth embedded submanifold,
    and of what dimension?
    a) f = x² + y², c = 1              b) f = x² + y², c = 0
    c) f = x² − y², c = 0              d) f = x² − y², c = 1
    e) f = x² + y² − z², c = 0         f) f = x² + y² − z², c = −1
    g) f = y² − x³, c = 0
    h) f = xyz on R³, c = 1 (also: number of components)
    i) f = x⁴ + y⁴, c = 1
    j) f = det on M₂(R), c = 0 and c = 1
    k) f = (x² + y² + z², z) : R³ → R², c = (1, 0)
    l) critical points and values of the height z on S²
    m) critical points of f = x³ − 3x + y² and their type
    n) critical values of the height x on the torus ((2 + cos u) cos v,
       (2 + cos u) sin v, sin u)
    Answers:
      a) regular; S¹, dim 1
      b) not regular (the gradient vanishes at 0); the level set is a
         point — still a 0-manifold: regularity is sufficient, not
         necessary
      c) not regular; the cross {y = ±x} is not a manifold at 0
      d) regular; hyperbola, dim 1, 2 components
      e) not regular; double cone, not a manifold at the vertex
      f) regular; two-sheeted hyperboloid, dim 2
      g) not regular; the cusp is homeomorphic to R but NOT a smooth
         embedded submanifold at 0
      h) regular (∇f = 0 needs two zero coordinates, so f = 0); dim 2,
         4 components (sign patterns +++, +−−, −+−, −−+)
      i) regular; dim 1 (a "squircle")
      j) c = 0 not regular (d det = 0 at A = 0; det⁻¹(0) is a cone);
         c = 1 regular: SL(2, R), dim 3
      k) regular (Jacobian rank 2 where (x, y) ≠ 0); the circle
         x² + y² = 1, z = 0
      l) (0, 0, ±1); values ±1
      m) (1, 0) minimum (Hessian diag(6, 2)); (−1, 0) saddle
         (diag(−6, 2)); values −2, 2
      n) ±3 and ±1 (four critical points: u, v ∈ {0, π})

M3. Lie brackets, flows and differentials on Rⁿ.
    a) [∂_x, ∂_y]                       b) [∂_x, x ∂_y]
    c) [x ∂_x, ∂_x]                     d) [x ∂_x, x² ∂_x]
    e) [x ∂_y, y ∂_x]                   f) [x ∂_x + y ∂_y, x ∂_y − y ∂_x]
    g) [∂_x, eˣ ∂_y]                    h) [y ∂_x, y ∂_y]
    i) [∂_x, x²y ∂_y]                   j) [∂_x, x² ∂_x] on R
    k) on R³: [L_x, L_y] with L_x = y∂_z − z∂_y, L_y = z∂_x − x∂_z
    l) [∂_r, ∂_θ] in polar coordinates
    m) the flow of x ∂_x on R
    n) the flow of x² ∂_x from x₀ > 0; is the field complete?
    o) the flow of −y ∂_x + x ∂_y
    p) F(x, y) = (x + y, x − y) = (u, v): F_*(∂_x) and F_*(∂_y)
    q) dF at (1, 2) for F(x, y) = (x², xy); is F a local diffeomorphism
       there?
    r) the displacement of the "square" of flows of ∂_x and x ∂_y after
       times t = 0.1, s = 0.2
    Answers:
      a) 0        b) ∂_y       c) −∂_x      d) x² ∂_x
      e) x ∂_x − y ∂_y         f) 0         g) eˣ ∂_y     h) −y ∂_x
      i) 2xy ∂_y               j) 2x ∂_x
      k) y ∂_x − x ∂_y = −L_z (L_z = x∂_y − y∂_x)        l) 0
      m) φ_t(x) = eᵗx          n) φ_t(x₀) = x₀/(1 − t x₀), defined only
         for t < 1/x₀: not complete (blows up in finite time)
      o) rotation by angle t   p) ∂_u + ∂_v and ∂_u − ∂_v
      q) [[2, 0], [2, 1]], det 2 ≠ 0: yes
      r) 0.02 in the y-direction (st[X, Y] = st ∂_y, exact here)

M4. Exterior algebra and calculus. Simplify or compute.
    a) (dx + dy) ∧ (dx − dy)
    b) (x dx + y dy) ∧ (−y dx + x dy)
    c) d(x²y dz)                       d) d(xy dx + yz dy + zx dz)
    e) d(xy dy ∧ dz)                   f) d(f dx ∧ dy ∧ dz) on R³
    g) d(eˣ sin y dx + eˣ cos y dy)
    h) α ∧ α for α = dx ∧ dy + dz ∧ dw on R⁴
    i) α ∧ α for any 1-form α
    j) i_X(dx ∧ dy) with X = ∂_x + 2∂_y
    k) i_X(dx ∧ dy ∧ dz) with X = x∂_x + y∂_y + z∂_z
    l) L_X(dx) with X = x² ∂_x          m) L_X(x dy) with X = ∂_x
    n) dim Λᵏ(R⁵)* for k = 0, ..., 5

    p) d(d(x²y³))
    q) for which a is ω = y dx + a x dy closed?
    r) the 3-form dx ∧ dy ∧ dz evaluated on (e₁ + e₂, e₂, e₃ + e₁)

      e) y dx ∧ dy ∧ dz           f) 0 (no 4-forms on R³)
      g) 0 (closed; = d(eˣ sin y))
      h) 2 dx ∧ dy ∧ dz ∧ dw (≠ 0, so α is not decomposable)
      i) 0                        j) dy − 2 dx
      k) x dy ∧ dz + y dz ∧ dx + z dx ∧ dy
      l) 2x dx                    m) dy
      n) 1, 5, 10, 10, 5, 1       o) 3 dx ∧ dy ∧ dz
      p) 0                        q) a = 1 (dω = (a − 1) dx ∧ dy)
      r) det[[1,1,0],[0,1,0],[1,0,1]] = 1

M5. Pullbacks, integrals and Stokes.
    a) F*(dx ∧ dy), F(u, v) = (u², v)
    b) F*(dx ∧ dy) in polar coordinates
    c) F*(dx ∧ dy ∧ dz) in spherical coordinates (ρ, φ, θ), x = ρ sin φ
       cos θ, y = ρ sin φ sin θ, z = ρ cos φ
    d) γ*(x dy − y dx), γ(t) = (cos t, sin t)
    e) ∮_{unit circle} (x dy − y dx)

    g) ∫_γ (y dx + x dy), γ(t) = (t, t²), 0 ≤ t ≤ 1
    h) ∫_{S²} (x dy ∧ dz + y dz ∧ dx + z dx ∧ dy), unit sphere, outward
    i) ∫_{|x| = 5} (x dy ∧ dz + y dz ∧ dx + z dx ∧ dy)/r³ in R³
    j) ∮_{unit circle} (−y³ dx + x³ dy)
    k) ∫_{[0,1]³} d(xyz dx ∧ dy)
    l) ∫_{T²} dx ∧ dy on T² = R²/Z², and ∫_{T²} d(sin(2πx) dy)
    m) ∫_{S²} d(z dx)
    n) the area of the unit S² as ∫ sin φ dφ dθ

    p) the degree of the antipodal map of Sⁿ
    q) the degree of z ↦ z̄ on S¹, and of z ↦ z³z̄⁵ on S¹
    Answers:

      c) ρ² sin φ dρ ∧ dφ ∧ dθ      d) dt

      j) 3π/2 (Green: ∬ 3(x² + y²) dA)
      k) 1/4 (= ∫ xy dV; only the face z = 1 contributes on ∂)
      l) 1 and 0                    m) 0 (Stokes, ∂S² = ∅)
      n) 4π                         o) n dθ; deg n
