M6. De Rham cohomology: give the Betti numbers (b₀, b₁, ..., b_top) and χ.
    a) R⁵                            b) S¹
    c) S³                            d) T³
    e) Σ₂ (closed orientable genus 2)
    f) RP²                           g) RP³
    h) CP²                           i) the Klein bottle
    j) the open Möbius band          k) R² minus 3 points
    l) S² minus 3 points             m) S² × S¹
    n) S² × S⁴                       o) SO(3)
    p) RP⁴                           q) CPⁿ (general n)
    r) T² minus a point              s) R³ minus a circle
    t) the solid torus S¹ × D²
    Answers:
      a) (1); χ = 1                   b) (1, 1); 0
      c) (1, 0, 0, 1); 0              d) (1, 3, 3, 1); 0
      e) (1, 4, 1); −2                f) (1, 0, 0); 1
      g) (1, 0, 0, 1); 0              h) (1, 0, 1, 0, 1); 3
      i) (1, 1, 0); 0                 j) (1, 1, 0) (≃ S¹); 0
      k) (1, 3); −2                   l) (1, 2); −1
      m) (1, 1, 1, 1); 0              n) (1, 0, 1, 0, 1, 0, 1); 4
      o) (1, 0, 0, 1) (≅ RP³); 0      p) (1, 0, 0, 0, 0); 1
      q) 1 in each even degree 0, ..., 2n; χ = n + 1
      r) (1, 2, 0) (≃ wedge of two circles); −1
      s) (1, 1, 1, 0) (≃ S¹ ∨ S²); 1  t) (1, 1, 0, 0) (≃ S¹); 0

M7. Closed? Exact? Give a primitive, or the period ∮ over the unit
    circle (counterclockwise) when the form is closed but not exact.
    Domain R² ∖ 0 unless stated.
    a) 2x dx + 2y dy                   b) y dx + x dy
    c) y dx − x dy                     d) (x dx + y dy)/(x² + y²)
    e) (−y dx + x dy)/(x² + y²)
    f) (x dy − y dx + 3x dx + 3y dy)/(x² + y²)
    g) (x dy − y dx)/(x² + 4y²)        h) x dy on R²
    i) sin y dx + x cos y dy           j) (x dx + y dy)/(x² + y²)²
    k) (x dy − y dx)/(x² + y²) on the half-plane x > 0
    l) (x dy − y dx)/(x² + y²) on R³ minus the z-axis
    m) any closed 1-form on R³ ∖ 0: exact?
    n) cos²θ dθ on S¹: its class as a multiple of [dθ]
    o) on T² = R²/Z²: dx, sin(2πx) dx, cos²(2πy) dy
    p) (x dy − y dx)/(x² + y²) + (x dy − (y − 3) dx)/(x² + (y − 3)²) on
       R² ∖ {(0, 0), (0, 3)}: periods around each puncture
    Answers:
      a) exact: x² + y²               b) exact: xy
      c) not closed (d = −2 dx ∧ dy)  d) exact: ½ ln(x² + y²)
      e) closed, not exact; period 2π
      f) closed, not exact; period 2π (= dθ + 3 d(½ ln(x² + y²)))
      g) closed, not exact; period π (∫₀^{2π} dt/(1 + 3 sin²t) = π):
         its class is ½[dθ]
      h) not closed                   i) exact: x sin y
      j) exact: −1/(2(x² + y²))       k) exact: arctan(y/x)
      l) closed, not exact; period 2π around the axis
      m) yes (H¹(R³ ∖ 0) = H¹(S²) = 0)
      n) ½[dθ] (cos²θ dθ = ½ dθ + d(sin 2θ/4))
      o) dx: closed, not exact (period 1); sin(2πx) dx = d(−cos(2πx)/(2π))
         exact; cos²(2πy) dy: class ½[dy]
      p) 2π around each puncture (4π around both)

M8. Vector bundles. Answer briefly.
    a) Is TS¹ trivial?                  b) Is TS² trivial?
    c) For which n is TSⁿ trivial?      d) Is TS² ⊕ ε¹ trivial?
    e) Is the Möbius bundle trivial? Is Möbius ⊕ Möbius?
    f) Iso classes of real line bundles over S¹, S², T², RP³
    g) c₁ of the tautological line bundle over CP¹, and of TCP¹
    h) rank of TM ⊕ T*M, and of Λ²T*M, for dim M = 5
    i) Does T² carry a nowhere-zero vector field? Does Σ₂?
    j) Is every orientable real line bundle trivial?
    k) Is RP² orientable? Is RP³ parallelizable?
    l) the normal bundle of RP¹ ⊂ RP²
    m) the normal bundle of Sⁿ ⊂ Rⁿ⁺¹
    n) the transition Jacobian of TS² in stereographic charts at u = (1, 0)
    o) Is every vector bundle over R^k trivial?
    p) Sections of the trivial bundle M × Rᵏ are ... ?
    q) the Euler number e(TS²)[S²] and e(TΣ_g)[Σ_g]
    Answers:
      a) yes          b) no (hairy ball, χ = 2)
      c) n = 0, 1, 3, 7 (Adams)          d) yes (stably trivial)
      e) no; yes      f) 2, 1, 4, 2 (H¹(M; Z/2))
      g) −1 and 2 (= χ(S²))              h) 10 and 10
      i) yes (χ = 0); no (χ = −2)        j) yes
      k) no; yes (RP³ ≅ SO(3), a Lie group)
      l) the Möbius bundle (a neighbourhood of a line in RP² is a Möbius
         band)                           m) trivial (the unit normal)
      n) (|u|²I − 2uuᵀ)/|u|⁴ = diag(−1, 1)
      o) yes (contractible base)         p) smooth maps M → Rᵏ
      q) 2 and 2 − 2g

M9. Metrics, Christoffel symbols, lengths and areas.
    a) Christoffel symbols of dr² + r² dθ²
    b) of a²(dθ² + sin²θ dφ²) (sphere of radius a)
    c) of the half-plane (dx² + dy²)/y²
    d) of the surface of revolution dt² + f(t)² dφ²
    e) of e^{2x}(dx² + dy²)
    f) in H²: the length of the segment from i to 3i, and of the
       horizontal segment from 2i to 4 + 2i
    g) d_{H²}(i, 1 + i)                h) d_{H²}(2i, 3 + 6i)
    i) circumference and area of the circle of radius π/3 on the unit S²
    j) circumference and area of a hyperbolic circle of radius 1
    k) the hyperbolic area of {0 < x < 1, y > 1} in H²
    l) the area of {|z| < 1/2} in the Poincaré disk 4|dz|²/(1 − |z|²)²,
       and its hyperbolic radius
    m) the induced metric on the cylinder x² + y² = 1 in (θ, z), and on
       the cone z = √(x² + y²) in (r, θ)
    n) the pullback of dx² + dy² + dz² under t ↦ (cos t, sin t, t), and
       the length of one turn
    o) |∂_φ| at θ = π/6 on the unit sphere
    p) the volume density √det g of R³ in spherical coordinates
    Answers:
      a) Γ^r_θθ = −r, Γ^θ_rθ = 1/r      b) Γ^θ_φφ = −sin θ cos θ,
         Γ^φ_θφ = cot θ (scaling does not change Γ)
      c) Γ^x_xy = −1/y, Γ^y_xx = 1/y, Γ^y_yy = −1/y
      d) Γ^t_φφ = −f f′, Γ^φ_tφ = f′/f
      e) Γ^x_xx = 1, Γ^x_yy = −1, Γ^y_xy = 1 (others 0)
      f) ln 3 ≈ 1.09861 and 2          g) arccosh(3/2) ≈ 0.96242
      h) arccosh(49/24) ≈ 1.34069 (cosh d = 1 + 25/24)
      i) 2π sin(π/3) = π√3 ≈ 5.44140; 2π(1 − cos(π/3)) = π
      j) 2π sinh 1 ≈ 7.38401; 2π(cosh 1 − 1) ≈ 3.41228
      k) 1 (∫₀¹∫₁^∞ dy dx/y²)
      l) 4π/3; radius 2 artanh(1/2) = ln 3 (check 2π(cosh ln 3 − 1)
         = 4π/3)
      m) dθ² + dz² (flat!); 2 dr² + r² dθ²
      n) 2 dt²; 2π√2 ≈ 8.88577     o) 1/2      p) ρ² sin φ

M10. Curvature. Compute.
     a) K of the sphere of radius 3
     b) K of dr² + sinh²r dθ²          c) K of dr² + cosh²r dθ²
     d) K of (dx² + dy²)/y²
     e) K of 4(dx² + dy²)/(1 + x² + y²)²
     f) K of 4(dx² + dy²)/(1 − x² − y²)²
     g) K of e^{2(x² + y²)}(dx² + dy²) at the origin
     h) the torus r² du² + (R + r cos u)² dv² with R = 2, r = 1: K at
        u = 0, π/2, π; and ∫K dA
     i) K of the graph z = x² + y² at (0, 0) and at (1, 0)
     j) K of z = xy and of z = x² − y² at the origin
     k) sectional, Ricci and scalar curvature of the unit S³
     l) scalar curvature of the unit S² and of H³
     m) area of a geodesic triangle on the unit sphere with angles
        π/2, π/2, π/3
     n) area of a hyperbolic triangle with angles π/3, π/4, π/6, and of
        an ideal triangle (all angles 0)
     o) ∫_{Σ₃} K dA for any metric; the area of Σ₂ with K ≡ −1
     p) the cone dr² + (r sin α)²dθ²: K for r > 0 and the angle defect
        at the vertex
     q) number of independent components of the Riemann tensor for
        n = 2, 3, 4
     r) R(X, Y)Z in constant curvature κ
     Answers:
       a) 1/9      b) −1      c) −1 (G″/G = 1)      d) −1
       e) 1 (stereographic round sphere)            f) −1 (Poincaré disk)
       g) −4 (K = −e^{−2f}Δf, Δ(x² + y²) = 4, f(0) = 0)
       h) 1/3, 0, −1 (K = cos u/(r(R + r cos u))); ∫K dA = 0 (χ = 0)
       i) 4 and 4/25 (K = (f_xx f_yy − f_xy²)/(1 + |∇f|²)²)
       j) −1 and −4
       k) K ≡ 1, Ric = 2g, s = 6        l) 2 and −6
       m) π/3 (angle excess)            n) π/4; π
       o) −8π (2πχ, χ = −4); 4π         p) 0; 2π(1 − sin α)
       q) 1, 6, 20 (n²(n² − 1)/12)
       r) κ(g(Y, Z)X − g(X, Z)Y)

M11. Geodesics, distance, completeness.
     a) the geodesics of Euclidean R² and of the flat cylinder
     b) which are geodesics of S²: equator, meridian, the 45° latitude?
     c) the geodesic equations of H² = {(dx² + dy²)/y²}
     d) the unit-speed geodesic up the imaginary axis from i
     e) the geodesic of H² through i and 1 + i; through i and 2 + i
     f) the distance on the unit S² from (1, 0, 0) to (0, 1, 0), and to
        (1, 1, 1)/√3
     g) the great-circle distance from the North Pole to the equator on
        a sphere of radius 6371 km
     h) the first conjugate point along a geodesic of the sphere of
        radius a; of H²
     i) Clairaut: a geodesic leaves the equator of the unit sphere at 30°
        to it. Its highest latitude?
     j) injectivity radius of the unit S², the round RP², and the flat
        torus R²/Z²
     k) exp_N(v) on the unit S² for |v| = π; for |v| = 2π
     l) Bonnet-Myers: an upper bound for diam M if M is complete with
        Ric ≥ 4(n − 1)g
     m) complete or not: Euclidean R²; R² ∖ 0; H²; the open unit disc with
        the Euclidean metric; any compact Riemannian manifold
     n) does S² (or any Sⁿ, n ≥ 2) admit a metric with K ≤ 0?
     o) number of minimizing geodesics joining antipodal points of S²
     Answers:
       a) straight lines; helices t ↦ (cos(at), sin(at), bt) (incl. lines
          and circles)
       b) yes, yes, no
       c) ẍ − (2/y)ẋẏ = 0, ÿ + (ẋ² − ẏ²)/y = 0
       d) γ(t) = i eᵗ
       e) circle centre 1/2, radius √5/2; circle centre 1, radius √2
       f) π/2 ≈ 1.57080; arccos(1/√3) ≈ 0.95532
       g) 6371π/2 ≈ 10007.5 km
       h) at distance πa; none (K ≤ 0 ⇒ no conjugate points)
       i) 30° (r cos ψ = cos 30° ⇒ r_min = √3/2 = cos(latitude))
       j) π, π/2, 1/2
       k) the South Pole; the North Pole again
       l) π/2 (π/√k with k = 4)
       m) yes; no; yes; no; yes
       n) no (Cartan-Hadamard: the universal cover would be Rⁿ, but Sⁿ
          is compact and simply connected; for S² also Gauss-Bonnet)
       o) infinitely many (every half great circle)

M12. True (T) or false (F)? Give a counterexample for F.
     a) Every closed form is exact.
     b) On a star-shaped open subset of Rⁿ every closed k-form, k ≥ 1, is
        exact.
     c) Every smooth manifold admits a Riemannian metric.
     d) Every manifold admits a Lorentzian metric.
     e) Every vector bundle over a contractible manifold is trivial.
     f) A vector bundle with a nowhere-zero section is trivial.
     g) A closed manifold with χ = 0 has a nowhere-zero vector field.
     h) H¹_dR(M) is finite-dimensional for every manifold M.
     i) ∇_X Y is C^∞-linear in Y.
     j) The difference of two connections is a tensor.
     k) The Christoffel symbols form a tensor.
     l) Gaussian curvature depends only on the metric.
     m) Mean curvature depends only on the metric.
     n) Geodesics minimize length between their endpoints.
     o) In a connected complete Riemannian manifold any two points are
        joined by a minimizing geodesic.
     p) The same holds without completeness.
     q) A closed orientable surface with K > 0 everywhere is diffeomorphic
        to S².
     r) The torus admits a metric with K < 0 everywhere.
     s) Every compact surface embedded in R³ has a point with K > 0.
     t) de Rham cohomology is a homotopy invariant.
     u) The regular value theorem's converse holds: if F⁻¹(c) is a
        submanifold then c is a regular value.
     v) Every orientable rank-2 bundle over S² is trivial.
     Answers:
       a) F: dθ on R² ∖ 0            b) T (Poincaré lemma)
       c) T (partition of unity)     d) F: S² (would need a line field;
                                        χ(S²) ≠ 0)
       e) T                          f) F: Möbius ⊕ ε¹ over S¹ (non-
                                        orientable, so not trivial)
       g) T (Hopf)                   h) F: R² minus Z × {0} (H¹ has
                                        infinite dimension)
       i) F: ∇_X(fY) = (Xf)Y + f∇_X Y (it is C^∞-linear in X)
       j) T                          k) F: 0 in Cartesian, Γ^r_θθ = −r in
                                        polar coordinates of R²
       l) T (Theorema Egregium)      m) F: plane (H = 0) and cylinder
                                        (H = 1/2) are locally isometric
       n) F: a great-circle arc longer than π
       o) T (Hopf-Rinow)             p) F: (−1, 0), (1, 0) in R² ∖ 0
       q) T (Gauss-Bonnet: χ > 0)    r) F: ∫K dA = 2πχ = 0
       s) T (at a point farthest from the origin K ≥ 1/R² > 0)
       t) T                          u) F: f = x², c = 0 (f⁻¹(0) = {0})
       v) F: TS² (Euler number 2)

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: keep ONE worked model for every concept and return to it —
  S² with (θ, φ) for metrics, Christoffel symbols, geodesics, curvature
  and Gauss-Bonnet; H² for negative curvature and "straight lines that
  look curved"; R² ∖ 0 with dθ for closed-versus-exact and for H¹; the
  Möbius band for non-trivial bundles. Always compute a form by first
  writing dx, dy in the new variables, then expanding the wedge (never
  memorize Jacobians), and always check an answer with Stokes or
  Gauss-Bonnet (total curvature 2πχ is a free sanity check). For
  cohomology, first ask "what is it homotopy equivalent to?" and then use
  Mayer-Vietoris only on the small pieces. Harder: prove Sard's theorem
  and the Whitney embedding theorem; prove de Rham's theorem via good
  covers and the Čech-de Rham double complex; prove Poincaré duality for
  compactly supported cohomology; derive the second variation formula
  and use it to prove Bonnet-Myers and Synge's theorem; prove Cartan-
  Hadamard; compute the curvature of a U(1) connection on the Hopf bundle
  and show (i/2π)∫F = −1 (Chern-Weil); and show that Sⁿ has a nowhere-zero
  vector field iff n is odd.


================================================================================
TOPIC 12.11 — LIE GROUPS AND LIE ALGEBRAS: STRUCTURE, ROOT SYSTEMS,
CLASSIFICATION OF SEMISIMPLE LIE ALGEBRAS, REPRESENTATIONS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - LIE GROUP: a group G that is a smooth manifold with smooth
    multiplication and inversion. Its LIE ALGEBRA g = T_eG with the
    bracket of left-invariant vector fields. For a closed subgroup G ⊆
    GL(n) (a matrix group, closed by CARTAN'S CLOSED SUBGROUP THEOREM
    automatically a Lie group) g = {X : e^(tX) ∈ G for all t} and
    [X, Y] = XY − YX. Standard list: gl(n) (n²), sl(n): tr X = 0 (n² − 1),
    so(n): Xᵀ = −X (n(n − 1)/2), u(n): X* = −X (n²), su(n) (n² − 1),
    sp(2n, R): XᵀJ + JX = 0 (n(2n + 1)), Heisenberg (3).
  - EXPONENTIAL MAP exp : g → G, t ↦ exp(tX) is the ONE-PARAMETER SUBGROUP
    with velocity X; exp is a local diffeomorphism at 0; det e^X = e^(tr X).
    BAKER-CAMPBELL-HAUSDORFF: log(e^X e^Y) = X + Y + ½[X, Y] + (1/12)([X,
    [X, Y]] + [Y, [Y, X]]) + ..., so the bracket determines the group law
    near e. exp is onto for compact connected G but NOT for SL(2, R):
    tr(exp X) ≥ −2 for X ∈ sl(2, R), so diag(−2, −½) is not exponential.
    A homomorphism Φ : G → H has dΦ : g → h with Φ(exp X) = exp(dΦ X);
    Ad : G → GL(g), Ad(g)Y = gYg⁻¹, and d(Ad) = ad, ad X(Y) = [X, Y].
  - LIE CORRESPONDENCE: Lie subalgebras of g ↔ connected Lie subgroups of G;
    if G is SIMPLY CONNECTED every Lie algebra homomorphism g → h lifts to
    a group homomorphism G → H. Every finite-dimensional real Lie algebra
    is the Lie algebra of a unique simply connected group G̃ (Lie III, via
    ADO'S THEOREM: every Lie algebra embeds in some gl(n)); the connected
    groups with Lie algebra g are G̃/Γ, Γ a discrete central subgroup.
    Model: SU(2) ≅ S³ → SO(3) is a double cover (kernel ±I), su(2) ≅ so(3),
    π₁(SO(3)) = Z/2; the universal cover of SL(2, R) is not a matrix group.
  - ABSTRACT LIE ALGEBRAS (over a field, here R or C): bilinear,
    alternating bracket with the JACOBI identity. Ideals, quotients, ad.
    DERIVED series g ⊇ [g, g] ⊇ ...; SOLVABLE if it reaches 0. LOWER
    CENTRAL series Cᵏ⁺¹ = [g, Cᵏ]; NILPOTENT if it reaches 0. Examples:
    strictly upper triangular nₙ (nilpotent), upper triangular bₙ (solvable,
    not nilpotent), aff(1): [x, y] = y (solvable), e(2) (solvable).
  - ENGEL: g is nilpotent iff ad X is nilpotent for every X. LIE'S THEOREM
    (C, char 0): a solvable g ⊆ gl(V) has a common eigenvector, hence is
    upper triangular in some basis; so [g, g] is nilpotent. Fails over R
    (so(2) on R²). The RADICAL rad g = largest solvable ideal; g is
    SEMISIMPLE if rad g = 0 (equivalently no nonzero abelian ideals).
    LEVI: g = rad g ⋊ s with s semisimple (e.g. sl(2) ⋉ C²). REDUCTIVE =
    semisimple ⊕ abelian (gl(n), u(n)).
  - KILLING FORM κ(X, Y) = tr(ad X ad Y): symmetric, ad-invariant
    κ([X, Y], Z) = κ(X, [Y, Z]). For sl(n): κ = 2n tr(XY); so(n): (n − 2)
    tr(XY); sp(2n): (2n + 2) tr(XY); gl(n): 2n tr(XY) − 2 tr X tr Y.
    CARTAN'S CRITERIA: g is solvable iff κ(g, [g, g]) = 0; g is semisimple
    iff κ is NONDEGENERATE. Semisimple ⇒ g = g₁ ⊕ ... ⊕ gₖ (simple
    ideals), g = [g, g], all derivations inner. Compact G semisimple ⇒ κ
    negative definite (su(2): signature (0, 3); sl(2, R): (2, 1)).
  - WEYL'S THEOREM: every finite-dimensional representation of a semisimple
    Lie algebra over C is COMPLETELY REDUCIBLE (proof: the CASIMIR element
    Ω = ∑XᵢXⁱ, dual bases for a nondegenerate invariant form). Fails for
    solvable algebras: b₂ on C² has an invariant line with no invariant
    complement.
  - CARTAN SUBALGEBRA h (maximal toral: abelian, ad-semisimple) of a complex
    semisimple g; ROOT SPACE DECOMPOSITION g = h ⊕ ⊕_{α∈Φ} g_α, g_α = {X :
    [H, X] = α(H)X}. Facts: dim g_α = 1; Φ = −Φ; [g_α, g_β] = g_{α+β} when
    α + β ∈ Φ; each α gives an sl(2)-triple (E_α, H_α, F_α) with
    α(H_α) = 2; κ restricted to h is nondegenerate, and via it Φ spans a
    real Euclidean space E. sl(n): h = traceless diagonal, roots εᵢ − εⱼ.
  - ROOT SYSTEM Φ ⊆ E: finite, spans E, 0 ∉ Φ; Rα ∩ Φ = {±α}; the
    REFLECTION s_α(β) = β − ⟨β, α^∨⟩α maps Φ to Φ, where ⟨β, α^∨⟩ =
    2(β, α)/(α, α) ∈ Z. Then ⟨β, α^∨⟩⟨α, β^∨⟩ = 4cos²θ ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3}:
    angles 90°, 60°/120°, 45°/135°, 30°/150°, length ratios² 1, 2, 3. The
    WEYL GROUP W = ⟨s_α⟩ is finite and acts simply transitively on the
    Weyl chambers. Rank 2: A₁ × A₁, A₂, B₂, G₂ (4, 6, 8, 12 roots).
  - SIMPLE ROOTS Δ = {α₁, ..., αₗ}: a basis such that every root is an
    integer combination with all coefficients ≥ 0 (POSITIVE Φ⁺) or all ≤ 0.
    CARTAN MATRIX aᵢⱼ = ⟨αᵢ, αⱼ^∨⟩ (aᵢᵢ = 2, aᵢⱼ ∈ {0, −1, −2, −3},
    aᵢⱼ = 0 ⇔ aⱼᵢ = 0, positive definite after symmetrising). DYNKIN
    DIAGRAM: nodes αᵢ, aᵢⱼaⱼᵢ edges, arrow toward the short root. W is
    generated by the simple reflections sᵢ; ρ = ½∑_{α>0} α.
  - CLASSIFICATION (KILLING-CARTAN): complex simple Lie algebras ↔
    connected Dynkin diagrams: Aₗ = sl(l + 1), Bₗ = so(2l + 1), Cₗ = sp(2l),
    Dₗ = so(2l) (l ≥ 4), and E₆, E₇, E₈, F₄, G₂. Dimensions l(l + 2),
    l(2l + 1), l(2l + 1), l(2l − 1), 78, 133, 248, 52, 14; dim g = rank +
    |Φ|. SERRE'S THEOREM rebuilds g from its Cartan matrix. Low-rank
    coincidences: A₁ = B₁ = C₁, B₂ = C₂ (so(5) ≅ sp(4)), A₃ = D₃ (so(6) ≅
    sl(4)), D₂ = A₁ × A₁ (so(4) ≅ sl(2) ⊕ sl(2)).
  - REPRESENTATIONS OF sl(2, C): for each n ≥ 0 exactly one irreducible
    V(n), dim n + 1, h-eigenvalues (WEIGHTS) n, n − 2, ..., −n, basis
    vₖ with h vₖ = (n − 2k)vₖ, f vₖ = (k + 1)vₖ₊₁, e vₖ = (n − k + 1)vₖ₋₁.
    CLEBSCH-GORDAN: V(m) ⊗ V(n) = V(m + n) ⊕ V(m + n − 2) ⊕ ... ⊕ V(|m − n|).
    Casimir Ω = ef + fe + ½h² acts on V(n) by n(n + 2)/2.
  - HIGHEST WEIGHT THEORY: weights λ ∈ h*; the WEIGHT LATTICE P = {λ :
    ⟨λ, α^∨⟩ ∈ Z}, FUNDAMENTAL WEIGHTS ⟨ωᵢ, αⱼ^∨⟩ = δᵢⱼ, dominant P⁺ =
    ∑Nωᵢ, root lattice Q ⊆ P with |P/Q| = det(Cartan matrix) = order of
    the centre of the simply connected group. THEOREM: finite-dimensional
    irreducibles ↔ dominant integral λ (quotients of VERMA modules
    U(g) ⊗_{U(b)} C_λ; PBW: U(g) has basis of ordered monomials).
  - WEYL CHARACTER FORMULA χ_λ = ∑_w ε(w)e^{w(λ+ρ)} / ∑_w ε(w)e^{wρ} and
    WEYL DIMENSION FORMULA dim V(λ) = ∏_{α>0} ⟨λ + ρ, α^∨⟩/⟨ρ, α^∨⟩. For
    sl(3): dim V(a, b) = (a + 1)(b + 1)(a + b + 2)/2 (3, 8, 6, 10, 27, ...).
    Tensor products: 3 ⊗ 3̄ = 8 ⊕ 1, 3 ⊗ 3 = 6 ⊕ 3̄, 8 ⊗ 8 = 27 ⊕ 10 ⊕ 10̄
    ⊕ 8 ⊕ 8 ⊕ 1.
  - COMPACT LIE GROUPS: bi-invariant HAAR measure; averaging gives an
    invariant inner product, so every representation is unitary and
    completely reducible (the UNITARY TRICK transfers this to sl(n, C)).
    Characters: ∫χ_Vχ̄_W dg = dim Hom_G(V, W); PETER-WEYL: matrix
    coefficients of irreducibles are dense in L²(G). MAXIMAL TORUS T:
    every element is conjugate into T; W = N(T)/T. SU(2): χₙ(θ) =
    sin((n + 1)θ)/sin θ, Haar density (2/π)sin²θ dθ on [0, π]. SO(3) has
    irreducibles only of ODD dimension 2l + 1 (spherical harmonics).

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. (a) Determine the Lie algebras of SL(n, R), SO(n), U(n), SU(n) and
    Sp(2n, R) and their dimensions. (b) Show that for a closed subgroup G ⊆
    GL(n), g = {X : e^(tX) ∈ G ∀t} is closed under the bracket. (c) Show
    that su(2) ≅ so(3) as real Lie algebras.
    Answer: (a) tr X = 0 (n² − 1); Xᵀ = −X (n(n − 1)/2); X* = −X (n²);
    X* = −X, tr X = 0 (n² − 1); XᵀJ + JX = 0 (n(2n + 1)). (b) [X, Y] is
    the derivative of Ad(e^(tX))Y. (c) uₖ = −(i/2)σₖ and Lₖ both satisfy
    [1, 2] = 3 cyclically.
    Teacher explains: (a) Differentiate the defining equation at t = 0.
    det e^(tX) = e^(t tr X) = 1 for all t ⇔ tr X = 0. If A(t)ᵀA(t) = I with
    A(0) = I, A′(0) = X, then Xᵀ + X = 0; conversely (e^(tX))ᵀ = e^(tXᵀ) =
    e^(−tX) = (e^(tX))⁻¹. The same for X* = −X in U(n). For Sp(2n, R),
    J = [[0, I], [−I, 0]], write X = [[A, B], [C, D]]; XᵀJ + JX = 0 gives
    D = −Aᵀ, B = Bᵀ, C = Cᵀ: n² + 2·n(n + 1)/2 = n(2n + 1) parameters.
    (b) g is a vector space: cX ∈ g trivially, and X + Y ∈ g by the Lie
    product formula e^(X+Y) = lim (e^(X/k)e^(Y/k))ᵏ and closedness of G.
    For X, Y ∈ g, e^(tX)e^(sY)e^(−tX) = exp(s e^(tX)Ye^(−tX)) ∈ G for all
    s, so Z(t) = e^(tX)Ye^(−tX) ∈ g; g is a closed subspace, so Z′(0) =
    XY − YX ∈ g. (c) With the Pauli matrices σₖ, σ₁σ₂ = iσ₃, so [u₁, u₂] =
    (−i/2)²·2iσ₃ = −(i/2)σ₃ = u₃, cyclically. In so(3), L₁ = E₃₂ − E₂₃,
    L₂ = E₁₃ − E₃₁, L₃ = E₂₁ − E₁₂ (infinitesimal rotations about the axes)
    satisfy [L₁, L₂] = L₃ cyclically. So uₖ ↦ Lₖ is an isomorphism (both
    are R³ with the cross product). Common mistakes: forgetting that u(n)
    contains iI (so dim u(n) = n², not n² − 1); confusing the real
    dimension of SL(2, C) (6) with its complex dimension (3). Now you try:
    the dimension of Sp(4, R) and of SO(5). (10 and 10: B₂ = C₂)

D2. (a) For X ∈ sl(2, R) show X² = −det(X)·I and obtain a closed formula
    for e^X; compute exp [[0, 4], [1, 0]]. (b) Prove tr(e^X) ≥ −2, with
    equality only for e^X = −I. Deduce that A = diag(−2, −½) and B =
    [[−1, 1], [0, −1]] are not exponentials although SL(2, R) is connected.
    (c) Write A as a product of two exponentials.
    Answer: (a) δ = −det X = λ² > 0: cosh λ I + (sinh λ/λ)X; δ = −θ² < 0:
    cos θ I + (sin θ/θ)X; δ = 0: I + X. exp [[0, 4], [1, 0]] = [[cosh 2,
    2 sinh 2], [½ sinh 2, cosh 2]] ≈ [[3.76220, 7.25372], [1.81343,
    3.76220]]. (b) tr A = −5/2; tr B = −2 but B ≠ −I. (c) A = e^(πJ)
    e^(diag(ln 2, −ln 2)), J = [[0, −1], [1, 0]].
    Teacher explains: Cayley-Hamilton: X² − (tr X)X + (det X)I = 0 and
    tr X = 0 give X² = δI with δ = −det X. Split the power series of e^X
    into even and odd powers: X^(2k) = δᵏI, X^(2k+1) = δᵏX. If δ = λ² > 0
    this gives cosh λ I + (sinh λ/λ)X, trace 2cosh λ ≥ 2. If δ = −θ² < 0
    it gives cos θ I + (sin θ/θ)X, trace 2cos θ ∈ [−2, 2]; the trace is −2
    only when cos θ = −1, and then sin θ = 0 so e^X = −I. If δ = 0 (X
    nilpotent), e^X = I + X, trace 2. Hence tr e^X ≥ −2 always, and −2
    only at −I. For [[0, 4], [1, 0]], δ = 4, λ = 2 and (sinh 2)/2·X gives
    the stated matrix (check det = cosh²2 − sinh²2 = 1). A and B violate
    the trace condition, so exp : sl(2, R) → SL(2, R) is NOT onto. Still
    A = (−I)·diag(2, ½) with −I = e^(πJ) (δ = −π², θ = π) and diag(2, ½) =
    e^(diag(ln 2, −ln 2)): in a connected group every element is a product
    of exponentials. Common mistakes: "connected ⇒ exp onto" (true for
    compact connected groups and for simply connected nilpotent groups,
    false in general); forgetting the case δ = 0. Now you try: exp
    [[0, −1], [4, 0]]. (δ = −4, θ = 2: [[cos 2, −½ sin 2], [2 sin 2,
    cos 2]])

D3. Construct the double cover SU(2) → SO(3) via the adjoint
    representation. Show that exp(θu₃), u₃ = −(i/2)σ₃, maps to the rotation
    by θ about the third axis, and deduce π₁(SO(3)) = Z/2.
    Answer: Ad : SU(2) → SO(su(2), −2tr) ≅ SO(3) is onto with kernel {±I};
    exp(2πu₃) = −I ↦ identity; SU(2) ≅ S³ is simply connected.
    Teacher explains: su(2) ≅ R³ with basis u₁, u₂, u₃ and inner product
    ⟨X, Y⟩ = −2 tr(XY), for which tr(uⱼuₖ) = −½δⱼₖ makes the uₖ
    orthonormal. Ad(g)X = gXg⁻¹ preserves su(2) and the trace form, so
    Ad(g) ∈ O(3); SU(2) = {[[a, −b̄], [b, ā]] : |a|² + |b|² = 1} ≅ S³ is
    connected, so Ad(g) ∈ SO(3). KERNEL: gXg⁻¹ = X for all X ∈ su(2)
    means g commutes with su(2), hence with its complex span sl(2, C);
    as C² is irreducible, Schur gives g scalar, so g = ±I. ONTO: d(Ad) =
    ad is injective (su(2) has zero centre), so the image is a
    3-dimensional compact connected subgroup of SO(3), open and closed,
    hence all of SO(3). For the rotation: Ad(exp θu₃) = exp(θ ad u₃), and [u₃,
    u₁] = u₂, [u₃, u₂] = −u₁, so ad u₃ is the infinitesimal rotation of the (u₁,
    u₂) plane and exp(θ ad u₃) is rotation by θ about u₃. Now exp(θu₃) =
    diag(e^(−iθ/2), e^(iθ/2)); at θ = 2π this is −I, which maps to the identity
    rotation. The loop of rotations θ ↦ R_θ (0 ≤ θ ≤ 2π) lifts to a PATH from I
    to −I, so it is not contractible; SO(3) ≅ S³/{±1} = RP³ and π₁ = Z/2.
    Common mistakes: thinking that isomorphic Lie algebras give isomorphic
    groups; forgetting ±I both map to the identity. Now you try: the image
    of [[0, −1], [1, 0]] = exp(πu₂). (rotation by π about the u₂-axis)

D4. (a) Compute the derived and lower central series of b₂ (upper
    triangular 2 × 2), of the Heisenberg algebra n₃ ([x, y] = z) and of b₃.
    (b) State Engel's and Lie's theorems. (c) Show Lie's theorem fails over
    R and complete reducibility fails for solvable algebras.
    Answer: (a) b₂ ⊃ CE₁₂ ⊃ 0 (solvable), Cᵏ(b₂) = CE₁₂ for all k ≥ 1
    (not nilpotent); n₃ ⊃ Cz ⊃ 0 both ways (nilpotent); b₃ ⊃ n₃ ⊃ CE₁₃ ⊃ 0
    (derived length 3). (c) so(2) on R²; b₂ on C².
    Teacher explains: (a) In b₂ = span(E₁₁, E₂₂, E₁₂): [E₁₁, E₁₂] = E₁₂,
    [E₂₂, E₁₂] = −E₁₂, [E₁₁, E₂₂] = 0. So [b₂, b₂] = CE₁₂, which is
    abelian: derived series reaches 0. But C² = [b₂, CE₁₂] = CE₁₂ again
    (E₁₁ keeps acting by 1), so the lower central series is stuck: b₂ is
    solvable, not nilpotent (ad E₁₁ has eigenvalue 1, not nilpotent,
    consistent with Engel). In n₃, [n₃, n₃] = Cz is central, so [n₃, Cz] =
    0: nilpotent. For b₃: [b₃, b₃] = n₃ (strictly upper), [n₃, n₃] = CE₁₃,
    then 0. (b) ENGEL: g nilpotent ⇔ ad X nilpotent ∀X ∈ g. LIE: if g ⊆
    gl(V) is solvable, V ≠ 0 finite-dimensional over C, there is a common
    eigenvector; by induction V has a g-stable flag, i.e. g is upper
    triangular in a suitable basis. (c) so(2) = R·J, J = [[0, −1], [1, 0]]
    is abelian, but J has no real eigenvector. b₂ acts on C²: the line
    Ce₁ is invariant; any invariant line is an eigenline of E₁₂, i.e. Ce₁
    again, so Ce₁ has no invariant complement. Common mistakes: confusing
    "each element nilpotent as a matrix" with "ad-nilpotent" (I ∈ gl(n)
    is ad-nilpotent but not nilpotent); forgetting Lie's theorem needs an
    algebraically closed field of characteristic 0. Now you try: is e(2)
    ([J, P₁] = P₂, [J, P₂] = −P₁, [P₁, P₂] = 0) nilpotent? (no: solvable,
    Cᵏ = span(P₁, P₂) for all k ≥ 1)

D5. (a) Compute the Killing form of sl(2, C) in the basis (e, h, f) and
    check κ(X, Y) = 4 tr(XY). (b) Compute the Killing form of aff(1)
    ([x, y] = y) and of n₃. (c) Prove: if κ is nondegenerate then g is
    semisimple.
    Answer: (a) Gram matrix [[0, 0, 4], [0, 8, 0], [4, 0, 0]], det −128.
    (b) aff(1): κ(x, x) = 1, others 0 (degenerate); n₃: κ ≡ 0. (c) an
    abelian ideal lies in the radical of κ.
    Teacher explains: (a) [h, e] = 2e, [h, f] = −2f, [e, f] = h. In the
    basis (e, h, f): ad h = diag(2, 0, −2); ad e: e ↦ 0, h ↦ −2e, f ↦ h;
    ad f: e ↦ −h, h ↦ 2f, f ↦ 0. κ(h, h) = 4 + 0 + 4 = 8. ad e ad f sends
    e ↦ ad e(−h) = 2e, h ↦ ad e(2f) = 2h, f ↦ 0: trace 4 = κ(e, f). ad e
    and ad f are nilpotent and shift weights, so κ(e, e) = κ(f, f) =
    κ(e, h) = κ(f, h) = 0. Compare tr(hh) = 2, tr(ef) = 1: κ = 4 tr(XY)
    (the general formula 2n tr(XY) with n = 2). det = −4·8·4 = −128 ≠ 0.
    (b) aff(1): ad x = diag(0, 1) in (x, y), ad y: x ↦ −y, y ↦ 0. So
    κ(x, x) = 1, κ(y, y) = 0, κ(x, y) = tr(x ↦ −y, y ↦ 0) = 0. It kills
    [g, g] = Cy, as Cartan's solvability criterion predicts. In n₃ every
    ad X ad Y maps into the centre and kills it: nilpotent, trace 0.
    (c) If g is not semisimple, the last nonzero term a of the derived
    series of rad g is a nonzero ABELIAN IDEAL. For a ∈ a, y ∈ g, ad a ad y
    maps g into a and a into [a, a] = 0, so (ad a ad y)² = 0 and κ(a, y) =
    0 for all y: a is in the radical of κ, contradiction. (The converse,
    semisimple ⇒ nondegenerate, uses Cartan's solvability criterion on
    the ideal g^⊥.) Common mistakes: using tr(XY) instead of tr(ad X ad Y);
    believing κ ≡ 0 forces nilpotency (only solvability: Cx ⋉ C² with
    ad x = diag(1, i) has κ ≡ 0). Now you try: the Killing form of so(3)
    in the basis L₁, L₂, L₃. (−2δⱼₖ: negative definite, as for any compact
    semisimple group)

D6. Classify the finite-dimensional irreducible representations of
    sl(2, C). Then decompose V(3) ⊗ V(2) and compute the Casimir
    eigenvalue on V(n).
    Answer: one V(n) for each n ≥ 0, dim n + 1, weights n, n − 2, ..., −n;
    V(3) ⊗ V(2) = V(5) ⊕ V(3) ⊕ V(1); Ω = ef + fe + ½h² acts by n(n + 2)/2.
    Teacher explains: Let V be irreducible. From [h, e] = 2e: if hv = μv
    then h(ev) = (μ + 2)ev, so e raises weights by 2 and f lowers by 2.
    Only finitely many eigenvalues of h exist, so there is a HIGHEST WEIGHT
    VECTOR v₀ ≠ 0 with hv₀ = λv₀, ev₀ = 0. Put vₖ = fᵏv₀/k!. Then hvₖ =
    (λ − 2k)vₖ, fvₖ = (k + 1)vₖ₊₁, and by induction evₖ = (λ − k + 1)vₖ₋₁:
       evₖ = (1/k)e f vₖ₋₁ = (1/k)(hvₖ₋₁ + f evₖ₋₁)
           = (1/k)((λ − 2k + 2) + (k − 1)(λ − k + 2))vₖ₋₁ = (λ − k + 1)vₖ₋₁.
    The nonzero vₖ are independent (distinct weights); let vₙ be the last
    nonzero one. Then 0 = evₙ₊₁ = (λ − n)vₙ, so λ = n ∈ N. span(v₀, ...,
    vₙ) is invariant, hence equals V: V ≅ V(n), unique up to isomorphism.
    Tensor product: weights of V(3) ⊗ V(2) are sums of {3, 1, −1, −3} and
    {2, 0, −2}: 5 (×1), 3 (×2), 1 (×3), −1 (×3), −3 (×2), −5 (×1). Peel
    off V(5) (weights 5, ..., −5), then V(3), then V(1): dimensions 6 +
    4 + 2 = 12 ✓. Casimir: Ω commutes with sl(2), so it is scalar on V(n)
    (Schur); on v₀: efv₀ = e v₁ = nv₀, fev₀ = 0, ½h²v₀ = ½n²v₀; total
    n + n²/2 = n(n + 2)/2. Common mistakes: listing weights n, n − 1, ...
    (steps are 2); forgetting |m − n| as the lowest term. Now you try:
    V(2) ⊗ V(2). (V(4) ⊕ V(2) ⊕ V(0))

D7. Find the root space decomposition of sl(3, C): Cartan subalgebra,
    roots, simple roots, Cartan matrix, and the Weyl group. Check
    [g_α, g_β] = g_{α+β} on an example.
    Answer: h = traceless diagonal; roots αᵢⱼ = εᵢ − εⱼ (i ≠ j), 6 roots,
    root vectors Eᵢⱼ; Δ = {ε₁ − ε₂, ε₂ − ε₃}; Cartan matrix [[2, −1], [−1,
    2]] (type A₂); W = S₃, order 6.
    Teacher explains: For H = diag(a₁, a₂, a₃), ∑aᵢ = 0: [H, Eᵢⱼ] = (aᵢ −
    aⱼ)Eᵢⱼ. So g = h ⊕ ⊕_{i≠j} CEᵢⱼ, each root space 1-dimensional, with
    root αᵢⱼ(H) = aᵢ − aⱼ. Positive roots (i < j): α₁ = ε₁ − ε₂, α₂ =
    ε₂ − ε₃, α₁ + α₂ = ε₁ − ε₃; every positive root is a nonnegative
    integer combination of α₁, α₂, so they are simple. The sl(2)-triple
    for α₁ is (E₁₂, H₁ = E₁₁ − E₂₂, E₂₁); the coroot is H₁, and
    ⟨α₂, α₁^∨⟩ = α₂(H₁) = a₂ − a₃ at H₁ = diag(1, −1, 0), i.e. −1.
    Symmetrically ⟨α₁, α₂^∨⟩ = −1. With κ = 6 tr on h, all roots have the
    same length and the angle between α₁, α₂ is 120° (4cos²θ = 1). The
    Weyl group acts on h by permuting the aᵢ: W = S₃, 6 elements, equal
    to N(T)/T for T ⊆ SU(3) diagonal. Example: [E₁₂, E₂₃] = E₁₃ ∈ g_{α₁+α₂},
    while [E₁₂, E₁₃] = 0 since 2α₁ + α₂ is not a root. dim sl(3) = 2 + 6
    = 8 ✓. Common mistakes: taking all diagonal matrices (that is gl(3));
    forgetting negative roots when counting. Now you try: the number of
    roots and positive roots of sl(4). (12 and 6)

D8. State the axioms of a (reduced) root system. Prove that for roots
    β ≠ ±α the product ⟨β, α^∨⟩⟨α, β^∨⟩ lies in {0, 1, 2, 3}, and list the
    possible angles and length ratios. Prove that if (α, β) > 0, β ≠ α,
    then α − β is a root.
    Answer: 4cos²θ ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3}: θ = 90°; 60°, 120° (ratio 1); 45°,
    135° (ratio 2); 30°, 150° (ratio 3). If (α, β) > 0 one of the Cartan
    integers is 1, and a reflection produces α − β.
    Teacher explains: AXIOMS (E Euclidean): (R1) Φ finite, spans E, 0 ∉ Φ;
    (R2) the only multiples of α in Φ are ±α; (R3) s_α(Φ) = Φ, where
    s_α(v) = v − ⟨v, α^∨⟩α; (R4) ⟨β, α^∨⟩ = 2(β, α)/(α, α) ∈ Z. Now
       ⟨β, α^∨⟩⟨α, β^∨⟩ = 4(α, β)²/(|α|²|β|²) = 4cos²θ ∈ [0, 4],
    and it is an integer by (R4). The value 4 means cos θ = ±1, so β is a
    multiple of α, excluded by (R2). So 4cos²θ ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3}, and both
    integers have the same sign. Also |β|²/|α|² = ⟨β, α^∨⟩/⟨α, β^∨⟩. Table
    (with |β| ≥ |α|):
       ⟨α, β^∨⟩  ⟨β, α^∨⟩   θ       |β|²/|α|²
          0         0      90°     any
          ±1        ±1     60°/120°    1
          ±1        ±2     45°/135°    2
          ±1        ±3     30°/150°    3
    If (α, β) > 0 and β ≠ α, the table shows ⟨α, β^∨⟩ = 1 or ⟨β, α^∨⟩ =
    1. In the first case s_β(α) = α − β ∈ Φ; in the second s_α(β) = β − α
    ∈ Φ, so α − β = −(β − α) ∈ Φ. Dually (α, β) < 0 ⇒ α + β ∈ Φ. Common
    mistakes: forgetting that 90° allows any length ratio (A₁ × A₁ with
    different lengths is still a root system); computing ⟨β, α^∨⟩ with
    |β|² in the denominator. Now you try: the angle and length ratio when
    ⟨β, α^∨⟩ = −3. (150°, |β|² = 3|α|²; then ⟨α, β^∨⟩ = −1)

D9. Describe the root systems B₂, C₂ and G₂: roots, simple roots,
    positive roots, Cartan matrices, highest roots and Weyl groups. Find
    the α-string through β in G₂.
    Answer: B₂ = {±e₁, ±e₂, ±e₁ ± e₂}, α₁ = e₁ − e₂, α₂ = e₂, Φ⁺ = {α₁, α₂,
    α₁ + α₂, α₁ + 2α₂}, Cartan [[2, −2], [−1, 2]], W dihedral of order 8.
    C₂ = {±2eᵢ, ±e₁ ± e₂} ≅ B₂. G₂: Φ⁺ = {α, β, α + β, 2α + β, 3α + β,
    3α + 2β}, Cartan [[2, −1], [−3, 2]], W dihedral of order 12; the
    string is β, β + α, β + 2α, β + 3α.
    Teacher explains: B₂ (so(5)): long roots ±e₁ ± e₂ (length² 2), short
    ±eᵢ. With α₁ = e₁ − e₂, α₂ = e₂: α₁ + α₂ = e₁, α₁ + 2α₂ = e₁ + e₂ =
    highest root, height 3. a₁₂ = 2(α₁, α₂)/(α₂, α₂) = 2(−1)/1 = −2, a₂₁ =
    2(−1)/2 = −1; 4cos²θ = 2: angle 135°. W is generated by reflections in
    lines at 45°; s₁s₂ is rotation by 90°, of order 4, so W is dihedral
    of order 8 = the signed permutations of (e₁, e₂). C₂ (sp(4)) has long
    roots ±2eᵢ and short ±e₁ ± e₂: rotating by 45° and rescaling by
    1/√2 carries it to B₂, the root-level shadow of so(5) ≅ sp(4). G₂:
    take α = (1, 0) and β = (−3/2, √3/2): |β|² = 3, (α, β) = −3/2, cos θ =
    −√3/2, θ = 150°. Then α + β = (−½, √3/2) and 2α + β = (½, √3/2) are short,
    3α + β = (3/2, √3/2) and 3α + 2β = (0, √3) are long; together with negatives
    that is 12 roots, and dim g₂ = 2 + 12 = 14. ⟨α, β^∨⟩ = 2(−3/2)/3 = −1, ⟨β,
    α^∨⟩ = 2(−3/2)/1 = −3. The α-string through β is β − pα, ..., β +
    qα with p − q = ⟨β, α^∨⟩ = −3 and p = 0 (β − α is not a root, both
    simple), so q = 3. The highest root is 3α + 2β (height 5 = h − 1,
    Coxeter number h = 6), and s_αs_β is rotation by 60°: W is dihedral of
    order 12. Common mistakes: mixing up which simple root is long in the
    Cartan matrix (convention aᵢⱼ = ⟨αᵢ, αⱼ^∨⟩); listing 2α + 2β, which is
    not a root. Now you try: ρ = half the sum of the positive roots of
    G₂. (5α + 3β)

D10. State the classification of complex simple Lie algebras and give,
     for each type, the rank, number of roots and dimension. Prove that a
     Dynkin diagram cannot contain a cycle (e.g. the triangle with single
     edges).
     Answer: Aₗ (l ≥ 1), Bₗ (l ≥ 2), Cₗ (l ≥ 3), Dₗ (l ≥ 4), E₆, E₇, E₈,
     F₄, G₂; |Φ| = l(l + 1), 2l², 2l², 2l(l − 1), 72, 126, 240, 48, 12;
     dim = l + |Φ|. Cycles give a nonzero vector of length 0.
     Teacher explains: THEOREM: two complex semisimple Lie algebras are
     isomorphic iff their root systems are, and root systems ↔ Cartan
     matrices ↔ Dynkin diagrams; simple ↔ connected. Every connected
     diagram is one of the list, and each occurs (classical matrix
     algebras, Serre relations for the exceptional ones). Table:
        type   algebra      dim        |Φ|        |W|
        Aₗ     sl(l + 1)    l(l + 2)    l(l + 1)   (l + 1)!
        Bₗ     so(2l + 1)   l(2l + 1)   2l²        2ˡl!
        Cₗ     sp(2l)       l(2l + 1)   2l²        2ˡl!
        Dₗ     so(2l)       l(2l − 1)   2l(l − 1)  2ˡ⁻¹l!
        G₂, F₄, E₆, E₇, E₈: 14, 52, 78, 133, 248.
     E.g. dim E₈ = 8 + 240 = 248. WHY NO CYCLES: replace the simple roots
     by unit vectors vᵢ; for joined nodes 2(vᵢ, vⱼ) = −√(aᵢⱼaⱼᵢ) ≤ −1,
     otherwise 0. For v = ∑vᵢ over n nodes,
        0 < |v|² = n + ∑_{i<j} 2(vᵢ, vⱼ) ≤ n − (number of edges),
     since the vᵢ are independent (v ≠ 0). So a connected subdiagram on n
     nodes has fewer than n edges: it is a TREE, and in particular a cycle
     is impossible. For the triangle directly: (vᵢ, vⱼ) = −½, |v|² = 3 −
     3 = 0, contradiction. Similar arguments bound the degree of a node
     (< 4 counted with multiplicity) and exclude all other trees, leaving
     exactly the list above. Common mistakes: listing B₁, C₂, D₃ as new
     types (B₁ = A₁, C₂ = B₂, D₃ = A₃); forgetting the Cartan subalgebra
     when computing dim g from |Φ|. Now you try: dim E₇ from |Φ| = 126.
     (133)

D11. For sl(3, C) define the fundamental weights, find the weights of
     C³, derive dim V(a, b) = (a + 1)(b + 1)(a + b + 2)/2 from the Weyl
     dimension formula, and decompose 3 ⊗ 3 and 3 ⊗ 3̄.
     Answer: ⟨ωᵢ, αⱼ^∨⟩ = δᵢⱼ; weights of C³: ε₁ = ω₁, ε₂ = ω₂ − ω₁, ε₃ =
     −ω₂; 3 ⊗ 3 = V(2, 0) ⊕ V(0, 1) = 6 ⊕ 3̄; 3 ⊗ 3̄ = V(1, 1) ⊕ V(0, 0) =
     8 ⊕ 1.
     Teacher explains: THEOREM OF THE HIGHEST WEIGHT: every irreducible
     finite-dimensional V has a unique highest weight λ (killed by all
     positive root vectors), λ is dominant integral, λ = aω₁ + bω₂ with
     a, b ∈ N, and each such λ occurs exactly once: V(a, b). On C³ the
     weight of eᵢ is εᵢ; pairing with the coroots H₁ = diag(1, −1, 0), H₂
     = diag(0, 1, −1) gives coordinates (1, 0), (−1, 1), (0, −1). So C³ =
     V(1, 0) with highest weight ω₁, and its dual has weights −εᵢ, highest
     −ε₃ = ω₂: 3̄ = V(0, 1). WEYL DIMENSION: ρ = ω₁ + ω₂ and the positive
     coroots are α₁^∨, α₂^∨, α₁^∨ + α₂^∨, so with λ + ρ = (a + 1)ω₁ + (b +
     1)ω₂,
        dim V(a, b) = (a + 1)/1 · (b + 1)/1 · (a + b + 2)/2.
     E.g. V(1, 1) = 2·2·4/2 = 8 (the adjoint), V(2, 0) = 6, V(3, 0) = 10,
     V(2, 2) = 27. TENSOR PRODUCTS by peeling highest weights: 3 ⊗ 3 has
     highest weight 2ω₁, and V(2, 0) = Sym²C³ (6); the rest Λ²C³ has
     highest weight ε₁ + ε₂ = ω₂: 9 = 6 + 3. 3 ⊗ 3̄ = End C³ = sl(3) ⊕ CI:
     highest weight ω₁ + ω₂, 9 = 8 + 1. Common mistakes: using ε-coordinates
     in the dimension formula without converting; forgetting that V(a, b)*
     ≅ V(b, a). Now you try: dim V(2, 1) and dim V(4, 0). (15 and 15)

D12. Let G = SU(2) and T = {t_θ = diag(e^(iθ), e^(−iθ))}. Using the Weyl
     integration formula ∫_G f dg = (2/π)∫₀^π f(t_θ) sin²θ dθ for class
     functions f, show the characters χₙ(θ) = sin((n + 1)θ)/sin θ are
     orthonormal, compute ∫_G χ₁⁴ dg, and determine the irreducible
     representations of SO(3).
     Answer: ∫χₘχₙ = δₘₙ; ∫χ₁⁴ = 2 = multiplicity of V(0) in V(1)^⊗4;
     SO(3) has one irreducible of each odd dimension 2l + 1.
     Teacher explains: V(n) = homogeneous polynomials of degree n in two
     variables; t_θ acts on the weight vectors by e^(i(n−2k)θ), so
        χₙ(θ) = e^(inθ) + e^(i(n−2)θ) + ... + e^(−inθ)
              = sin((n + 1)θ)/sin θ   (geometric series).
     Then χₘχₙ sin²θ = sin((m + 1)θ) sin((n + 1)θ), and
        (2/π)∫₀^π sin((m + 1)θ) sin((n + 1)θ) dθ = δₘₙ.
     Since ∫χ_Vχ̄_W = dim Hom_G(V, W) (Schur plus Haar averaging), this
     reproves that the V(n) are irreducible and pairwise non-isomorphic,
     and Peter-Weyl says there are no others. ∫χ₁⁴: χ₁ = 2cos θ, so
        (2/π)∫₀^π 16cos⁴θ sin²θ dθ = (2/π)·16·(π/16) = 2,
     matching V(1)^⊗4 = V(4) ⊕ 3V(2) ⊕ 2V(0). SO(3) = SU(2)/{±I}: an
     irreducible of SO(3) is a V(n) on which −I = t_π acts trivially;
     −I acts by (−1)ⁿ, so n = 2l is even: dimension 2l + 1. These are the
     spherical harmonics of degree l, and a rotation by φ has character
     1 + 2cos φ + ... + 2cos lφ = sin((l + ½)φ)/sin(φ/2) (φ = 2θ). Common
     mistakes: forgetting the density sin²θ (T is not all of G); using
     Haar measure on T alone. Now you try: ∫_{SU(2)} χ₁⁶ dg. (5, the
     Catalan number C₃)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Give the (real) dimension of the Lie group.
    a) GL(3, R)                        b) SL(4, R)
    c) O(5)                            d) SO(3)
    e) U(3)                            f) SU(4)
    g) Sp(4, R)                        h) Sp(2) (compact, 2 × 2 quaternionic)
    i) SO(3, 1) (Lorentz group)        j) SL(2, C) as a real group
    k) the Heisenberg group H₃         l) E(3) = R³ ⋊ SO(3)
    m) the Poincaré group R⁴ ⋊ SO(3, 1) n) invertible upper triangular 4 × 4
    o) unipotent upper triangular 4 × 4 p) Aff(2) = R² ⋊ GL(2, R)
    q) SO(8)                           r) GL(3, C) as a real group
    s) compact G₂ and compact E₈       t) the flag manifold SU(3)/T
    u) the sphere SO(5)/SO(4)          v) Sp(2n, R) for n = 3
    Answers:
      a) 9       b) 15      c) 10      d) 3       e) 9       f) 15
      g) 10      h) 10      i) 6       j) 6       k) 3       l) 6
      m) 10      n) 10      o) 6       p) 6       q) 28      r) 18
      s) 14 and 248         t) 6 (= number of roots)
      u) 4 (10 − 6)         v) 21

M2. Brackets and exponentials (e = E₁₂, f = E₂₁, h = E₁₁ − E₂₂,
    J = [[0, −1], [1, 0]]).
    a) [e, f]                          b) [h, e] and [h, f]
    c) exp(te)                         d) exp(θJ)
    e) exp(πJ) and exp(2πJ)            f) exp [[1, 1], [0, 1]]
    g) exp [[0, 1], [1, 0]]            h) det exp [[1, 2], [3, 4]]
    i) exp [[0, 1, 0], [0, 0, 1], [0, 0, 0]]
    j) [e, [e, f]]                     k) the matrix of ad e in the basis
                                          (e, h, f)
    l) Ad(g)e for g = diag(2, ½)       m) exp [[0, 4], [1, 0]]
    n) BCH to order t²: log(e^(te)e^(tf))
    o) is −I in exp(sl(2, R))?          p) is [[−1, 1], [0, −1]] in
                                          exp(sl(2, R))? in exp(sl(2, C))?
    q) is diag(−2, −½) in exp(sl(2, C))?
    r) the one-parameter subgroup of SO(3) generated by L₃
    Answers:
      a) h       b) 2e, −2f             c) [[1, t], [0, 1]]
      d) [[cos θ, −sin θ], [sin θ, cos θ]]    e) −I and I
      f) e·[[1, 1], [0, 1]]             g) [[cosh 1, sinh 1], [sinh 1,
         cosh 1]] ≈ [[1.54308, 1.17520], [1.17520, 1.54308]]
      h) e⁵ ≈ 148.41316                  i) [[1, 1, ½], [0, 1, 1], [0, 0, 1]]
      j) −2e (= [e, h])                 k) columns (0, 0, 0), (−2, 0, 0),
         (0, 1, 0): e ↦ 0, h ↦ −2e, f ↦ h
      l) 4e      m) [[cosh 2, 2 sinh 2], [½ sinh 2, cosh 2]]
      n) t(e + f) + (t²/2)h
      o) yes (exp πJ)                    p) no; no (X² = δI forces e^X
         diagonalisable or of trace 2)
      q) yes (diag(ln 2 + iπ, −ln 2 − iπ))
      r) rotations about the z-axis, t ↦ R_t

M3. Classify: A = abelian, N = nilpotent non-abelian, S = solvable not
    nilpotent, SS = semisimple (say whether simple), R = reductive not
    semisimple, M = none of these (give a Levi decomposition).
    a) R³ with zero bracket            b) Heisenberg n₃
    c) aff(1): [x, y] = y              d) sl(2, C)
    e) gl(2, C)                        f) b₃ (upper triangular 3 × 3)
    g) n₄ (strictly upper 4 × 4)       h) so(3)
    i) so(4)                           j) e(2)
    k) sl(2, C) ⋉ C² (natural action)  l) u(2)
    m) sl(2, R) ⊕ sl(2, R)             n) [x, y] = y, [x, z] = z, [y, z] = 0
    o) sl(2, R)                        p) so(3, 1) (real)
    q) a 1-dimensional Lie algebra     r) the derived length of b₃
    s) the nilpotency class of n₄      t) the Lie algebra of the Poincaré
                                          group (real)
    Answers:
      a) A        b) N        c) S        d) SS, simple
      e) R (= sl(2) ⊕ C)                  f) S        g) N
      h) SS, simple (compact form of A₁)  i) SS, not simple (so(3) ⊕ so(3))
      j) S        k) M: radical C², Levi factor sl(2)
      l) R (= su(2) ⊕ u(1))               m) SS, not simple
      n) S        o) SS, simple
      p) SS, simple as a real algebra (≅ sl(2, C)_R; its complexification
         sl(2, C) ⊕ sl(2, C) is not simple)
      q) A (not simple, by convention)    r) 3         s) 3
      t) M: radical R⁴ (translations), Levi factor so(3, 1)

M4. Killing forms. Compute.
    a) sl(2, C): κ(h, h)                 b) sl(2, C): κ(e, f)
    c) sl(2): det of the Gram matrix in (e, h, f)
    d) sl(3): κ(H, H), H = diag(1, −1, 0)  e) sl(3): κ(E₁₂, E₂₁)
    f) sl(n): κ(X, Y) in terms of tr(XY)
    g) gl(2): κ(I, X)                   h) gl(2): κ(E₁₁, E₁₁)
    i) so(3): κ(L₁, L₁)                 j) so(4): κ(L, L), L = E₁₂ − E₂₁
    k) so(5): κ(L, L), same L           l) sp(4): κ in terms of tr(XY)
    m) Heisenberg n₃                    n) aff(1): the Gram matrix in (x, y)
    o) e(2) in the basis (J, P₁, P₂)
    p) Cx ⋉ C² with ad x = diag(1, i): κ(x, x)
    q) the signature of κ on sl(2, R)   r) the signature of κ on su(2)
    Answers:
      a) 8        b) 4        c) −128     d) 12       e) 6
      f) 2n tr(XY)                        g) 0 (gl(2) not semisimple)
      h) 2 (2n tr(XY) − 2 tr X tr Y = 4 − 2)
      i) −2       j) −4 (2 tr(L²))        k) −6 (3 tr(L²))
      l) 6 tr(XY)                         m) 0
      n) [[1, 0], [0, 0]]                 o) diag(−2, 0, 0)
      p) 0 (1 + i² = 0; κ ≡ 0 yet ad x is not nilpotent: solvable, not
         nilpotent)
      q) (2, 1) (κ(h, h) = 8, κ(e ± f, e ± f) = ±8)
      r) (0, 3), negative definite

M5. Representations of sl(2, C).
    a) weights of V(4)                   b) dim V(7)
    c) V(1) ⊗ V(1)                       d) V(2) ⊗ V(2)
    e) V(3) ⊗ V(2)                       f) V(1)^⊗3
    g) V(1)^⊗4                           h) Sym²V(2) and Λ²V(2)
    i) Sym³V(1)                          j) Sym²V(3) and Λ²V(3)
    k) Casimir ef + fe + ½h² on V(3)    l) the representation with weights
                                           3, 1, 1, −1, −1, −3
    m) weights 2, 2, 0, 0, 0, −2, −2     n) e f v₀ on V(n), v₀ highest
    o) the adjoint representation        p) sl(3) restricted to sl(2)
                                           embedded in the top-left corner
    q) sl(3) restricted to the principal sl(2) (C³ = V(2))
    r) SO(3) on quadratic polynomials in x, y, z (dim 6)
    s) the multiplicity of V(0) in V(1)^⊗6
    Answers:
      a) 4, 2, 0, −2, −4                  b) 8
      c) V(2) ⊕ V(0)                      d) V(4) ⊕ V(2) ⊕ V(0)
