M12. True (T) or false (F)? Give a counterexample for F.
     a) exp is onto for every connected Lie group.
     b) exp is onto for every compact connected Lie group.
     c) Every Lie group is isomorphic to a closed subgroup of some GL(n).
     d) Every finite-dimensional real Lie algebra is the Lie algebra of
        some Lie group.
     e) Groups with isomorphic Lie algebras are isomorphic.
     f) Every finite-dimensional representation of a solvable Lie
        algebra over C is completely reducible.
     g) Every finite-dimensional representation of sl(n, C) is
        completely reducible.
     h) A Lie algebra with Killing form identically 0 is nilpotent.
     i) A Lie algebra with nondegenerate Killing form is semisimple.
     j) Lie's theorem holds over R.
     k) Every subalgebra of a semisimple Lie algebra is semisimple.
     l) Every ideal of a semisimple Lie algebra is semisimple.
     m) The direct sum of two simple Lie algebras is simple.
     n) Root systems with isomorphic Weyl groups are isomorphic.
     o) The sum of two roots is a root or zero.
     p) Every positive root is a sum of simple roots.
     q) Every irreducible finite-dimensional representation of an
        abelian Lie algebra over C is 1-dimensional.
     r) so(4) is simple.
     s) SO(3) has an irreducible representation of dimension 2.
     t) The Killing form of a compact semisimple Lie algebra is negative
        definite.
     Answers:
       a) F: SL(2, R) (diag(−2, −½))       b) T
       c) F: the universal cover of SL(2, R)
       d) T (Ado's theorem + Lie III)      e) F: SU(2) and SO(3)
       f) F: b₂ on C²                      g) T (Weyl)
       h) F: Cx ⋉ C², ad x = diag(1, i) (solvable, by Cartan's criterion)
       i) T (Cartan's criterion)           j) F: so(2) on R²
       k) F: a Cartan subalgebra (abelian), or a Borel b ⊆ sl(n)
       l) T (it is a sum of some of the simple factors)
       m) F: sl(2) ⊕ sl(2) has the ideal sl(2) ⊕ 0
       n) F: Bₙ and Cₙ (n ≥ 3) share W = (Z/2)ⁿ ⋊ Sₙ
       o) F: in A₁ × A₁, α + β is not a root; also α + α = 2α
       p) T (as a sum with repetitions: ∑kᵢαᵢ, kᵢ ∈ N)
       q) T (commuting operators have a common eigenvector)
       r) F: so(4) ≅ so(3) ⊕ so(3)          s) F: only odd dimensions
       t) T

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: make everything concrete in sl(2) and sl(3) first — write
  the 3 × 3 matrices Eᵢⱼ, compute [H, Eᵢⱼ] = (aᵢ − aⱼ)Eᵢⱼ by hand, and
  draw the six roots of A₂ as a hexagon before ever saying "root system";
  then draw B₂ (a square with its diagonals) and G₂ (a star of David with
  a hexagon) and read off angles and length ratios from the pictures.
  For every representation question, insist on the routine "list the
  weights with multiplicities, peel off the highest weight, repeat", and
  check the total dimension at the end. Keep the dictionary group ↔
  algebra visible: SU(2) → SO(3) is the example to return to whenever
  someone claims the Lie algebra determines the group. Harder: prove
  Engel's and Lie's theorems and Cartan's criterion; prove Weyl's
  theorem with the Casimir element; show dim g_α = 1 and that the roots
  form a root system; complete the classification of connected Dynkin
  diagrams by excluding every tree outside the list; prove the Weyl
  character formula via the Weyl integration formula for a compact group
  (or via Verma modules and the Casimir), derive Kostant's multiplicity
  formula, and show that |P/Q| equals the order of the centre of the
  simply connected group (Z/n for SU(n), Z/4 or Z/2 × Z/2 for Spin(2n)).


================================================================================
TOPIC 12.12 — PROBABILITY (MEASURE-THEORETIC): CONDITIONAL EXPECTATION,
MARTINGALES, BROWNIAN MOTION, WEAK CONVERGENCE, CHARACTERISTIC FUNCTIONS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - PROBABILITY SPACE (Ω, ℱ, P): a measure space with P(Ω) = 1. A RANDOM
    VARIABLE is a measurable X : Ω → R; its LAW is μ_X = P ∘ X⁻¹ on 𝔅(R),
    with distribution function F(x) = P(X ≤ x). E X = ∫X dP, and E g(X) =
    ∫g dμ_X. σ(X) = {X⁻¹(B)} is the information carried by X; Y is
    σ(X)-measurable iff Y = h(X) for a Borel h (Doob-Dynkin lemma).
  - CONDITIONAL EXPECTATION: for X ∈ L¹ and a sub-σ-algebra 𝒢 ⊆ ℱ,
    E[X | 𝒢] is the (a.s. unique) 𝒢-measurable Y ∈ L¹ with E[Y𝟙_G] =
    E[X𝟙_G] for all G ∈ 𝒢. EXISTENCE by Radon-Nikodym (ν(G) = E[X𝟙_G] ≪ P
    on 𝒢), or, for X ∈ L², as the ORTHOGONAL PROJECTION of X onto the
    closed subspace L²(𝒢): E[X | 𝒢] is the best 𝒢-measurable predictor.
  - ELEMENTARY CASES: if 𝒢 is generated by a partition {G₁, G₂, ...} with
    P(Gᵢ) > 0, then E[X | 𝒢] = E[X𝟙_{Gᵢ}]/P(Gᵢ) on Gᵢ. With a joint
    density f(x, y), E[Y | X] = h(X), h(x) = ∫y f(x, y) dy / ∫f(x, y) dy.
    Gaussian vectors: E[Y | X] = EY + (Cov(X, Y)/Var X)(X − EX) (linear!).
  - PROPERTIES (all a.s.): linearity; monotonicity; E[E[X | 𝒢]] = EX;
    TOWER E[E[X | 𝒢] | ℋ] = E[X | ℋ] for ℋ ⊆ 𝒢; TAKING OUT WHAT IS KNOWN
    E[ZX | 𝒢] = Z E[X | 𝒢] for bounded 𝒢-measurable Z; INDEPENDENCE:
    E[X | 𝒢] = EX if X ⫫ 𝒢; conditional JENSEN φ(E[X | 𝒢]) ≤ E[φ(X) | 𝒢]
    for convex φ (so ‖E[X | 𝒢]‖_p ≤ ‖X‖_p); conditional MCT, Fatou, DCT.
    LAW OF TOTAL VARIANCE Var X = E[Var(X | 𝒢)] + Var(E[X | 𝒢]).
  - MARTINGALES: a FILTRATION (ℱₙ) is an increasing sequence of σ-algebras;
    (Mₙ) is ADAPTED if Mₙ is ℱₙ-measurable, and a MARTINGALE if also
    E|Mₙ| < ∞ and E[Mₙ₊₁ | ℱₙ] = Mₙ (SUB: ≥, SUPER: ≤). Examples: sums of
    independent mean-0 steps, Sₙ² − nσ², products of independent mean-1
    factors, exp(θSₙ)/φ(θ)ⁿ, (q/p)^{Sₙ}, Doob martingales E[X | ℱₙ],
    Pólya urn proportions, Zₙ/μⁿ for a branching process. A convex function
    of a martingale is a submartingale (if integrable). DOOB DECOMPOSITION:
    a submartingale is Mₙ + Aₙ with M a martingale, A predictable
    increasing, Aₙ = ∑_{k<n} E[X_{k+1} − X_k | ℱ_k].
  - STOPPING TIMES: τ with {τ ≤ n} ∈ ℱₙ (hitting times yes, "last visit"
    times no). The stopped process M_{τ∧n} is a martingale. OPTIONAL
    STOPPING THEOREM: E M_τ = E M₀ if (i) τ is bounded, or (ii) (M_{τ∧n})
    is uniformly integrable (e.g. |M_{τ∧n}| ≤ K), or (iii) Eτ < ∞ and the
    increments are bounded. It FAILS for simple random walk stopped at the
    hitting time of 1 (E S_τ = 1 ≠ 0). Uses: gambler's ruin, Wald's
    identities E S_τ = μEτ and E(S_τ − τμ)² = σ²Eτ, waiting-time puzzles.
  - DOOB'S INEQUALITIES: for a non-negative submartingale, P(max_{k≤n} X_k
    ≥ λ) ≤ E Xₙ/λ (MAXIMAL); ‖max_{k≤n} X_k‖_p ≤ (p/(p − 1))‖Xₙ‖_p for
    p > 1 (Lᵖ). Kolmogorov's inequality is the case X = Sₙ².
  - CONVERGENCE: the UPCROSSING LEMMA (b − a)E Uₙ[a, b] ≤ E(Xₙ − a)⁺ gives
    DOOB'S THEOREM: sup E|Xₙ| < ∞ ⇒ Xₙ → X∞ a.s., X∞ ∈ L¹. L¹ convergence
    needs UNIFORM INTEGRABILITY (sup_n E[|Xₙ|; |Xₙ| > K] → 0 as K → ∞);
    for martingales, UI ⇔ L¹ convergent ⇔ Mₙ = E[M∞ | ℱₙ]; Lᵖ-bounded
    (p > 1) ⇒ Lᵖ convergence. LÉVY UPWARD: E[X | ℱₙ] → E[X | ℱ∞].
    Counterexample: Mₙ = ∏ξᵢ, ξᵢ = 0 or 2 (prob ½ each): Mₙ → 0 a.s. but
    E Mₙ = 1, so no L¹ convergence. Reverse martingales give the SLLN.
  - BROWNIAN MOTION (B_t)_{t≥0}: B₀ = 0, independent increments, B_t − B_s
    ~ N(0, t − s), continuous paths. Equivalently a centred Gaussian
    process with Cov(B_s, B_t) = min(s, t) and continuous paths. Exists
    (Lévy-Ciesielski series, or Kolmogorov extension + continuity
    criterion E|X_t − X_s|^α ≤ C|t − s|^{1+β}). INVARIANCES: −B, scaling
    c^{−1/2}B_{ct}, time inversion tB_{1/t}, B_{s+t} − B_s (Markov
    property; STRONG MARKOV at stopping times).
  - PATH PROPERTIES: a.s. nowhere differentiable, Hölder of every order
    < ½ but not ½; QUADRATIC VARIATION ∑(ΔB)² → t in L² (and a.s. along
    dyadic partitions), hence INFINITE total variation; LAW OF THE
    ITERATED LOGARITHM lim sup B_t/√(2t log log t) = 1; zero set is a
    perfect null set; BROWNIAN BRIDGE B_t − tB₁, Cov = s(1 − t), s ≤ t.
  - BROWNIAN MARTINGALES AND HITTING TIMES: B_t, B_t² − t,
    exp(θB_t − θ²t/2), B_t³ − 3tB_t are martingales. For τ = exit time of
    (−a, b): P(B_τ = b) = a/(a + b), Eτ = ab. T_a = inf{t : B_t = a} is
    finite a.s. with E T_a = ∞ and E e^{−λT_a} = e^{−a√(2λ)}. REFLECTION
    PRINCIPLE: P(max_{s≤t} B_s ≥ a) = 2P(B_t ≥ a), so max_{s≤t} B_s has
    the law of |B_t|, and T_a has density a(2πt³)^{−1/2}e^{−a²/2t}. With
    drift μ > 0 and unit variance: P(ever reach −a) = e^{−2μa}.
  - WEAK CONVERGENCE (convergence in distribution) μₙ ⇒ μ: ∫f dμₙ → ∫f dμ
    for every bounded continuous f. PORTMANTEAU: ⇔ lim sup μₙ(F) ≤ μ(F)
    for closed F ⇔ lim inf μₙ(G) ≥ μ(G) for open G ⇔ μₙ(A) → μ(A) when
    μ(∂A) = 0 ⇔ (on R) Fₙ(x) → F(x) at continuity points of F. In prob.
    ⇒ in distribution; the converse holds only for a constant limit.
    SLUTSKY, CONTINUOUS MAPPING, SKOROKHOD REPRESENTATION, HELLY
    SELECTION; PROKHOROV: tight ⇔ relatively compact. Weak convergence
    does NOT give E Xₙ → E X without uniform integrability.
  - CHARACTERISTIC FUNCTION φ_X(t) = E e^{itX}: φ(0) = 1, |φ| ≤ 1,
    uniformly continuous, φ(−t) = conj φ(t), positive definite (BOCHNER:
    these characterise ch.f.s); φ_{aX+b}(t) = e^{ibt}φ(at); φ_{X+Y} =
    φ_Xφ_Y for independent X, Y. MOMENTS: E|X|ᵏ < ∞ ⇒ φ ∈ Cᵏ and φ⁽ᵏ⁾(0)
    = iᵏ E Xᵏ. Table: N(μ, σ²) ↦ e^{iμt − σ²t²/2}, Poisson(λ) ↦
    e^{λ(e^{it} − 1)}, Exp(λ) ↦ λ/(λ − it), U[−a, a] ↦ sin(at)/(at),
    Cauchy ↦ e^{−|t|}, Laplace ↦ 1/(1 + t²).
  - INVERSION AND UNIQUENESS: μ(a, b) + ½μ{a, b} = lim_{T→∞} (1/2π)
    ∫_{−T}^{T} (e^{−ita} − e^{−itb})/(it) φ(t) dt; if ∫|φ| < ∞, X has the
    bounded continuous density f(x) = (1/2π)∫e^{−itx}φ(t) dt; for integer
    X, P(X = k) = (1/2π)∫_{−π}^{π} e^{−itk}φ(t) dt. φ determines μ.
  - LÉVY CONTINUITY THEOREM: μₙ ⇒ μ ⇒ φₙ → φ pointwise; conversely if
    φₙ → g pointwise and g is continuous AT 0, then g is a ch.f. and
    μₙ ⇒ μ_g. CLT (Lindeberg-Lévy): i.i.d., mean μ, variance σ² ∈ (0, ∞)
    ⇒ (Sₙ − nμ)/(σ√n) ⇒ N(0, 1). LINDEBERG-FELLER: triangular arrays with
    s_n^{−2}∑E[X_k²; |X_k| > εs_n] → 0. POISSON LIMIT Bin(n, λ/n) ⇒
    Poisson(λ); WLLN via φ(t/n)ⁿ → e^{iμt}; DELTA METHOD; DONSKER: the
    rescaled random walk converges to Brownian motion in C[0, 1].

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Roll a fair die; Ω = {1, ..., 6}, X(ω) = ω. (a) Compute E[X | 𝒢] for
    𝒢 = σ({X even}) and for ℋ = σ({1, 2}, {3, 4}, {5, 6}). (b) Verify the
    defining property E[Y𝟙_G] = E[X𝟙_G] for G = {2, 4, 6}. (c) Verify the
    law of total variance for ℋ.
    Answer: (a) E[X | 𝒢] = 4 on evens, 3 on odds; E[X | ℋ] = 1.5, 3.5,
    5.5 on the three blocks. (b) both sides 2. (c) 35/12 = 1/4 + 8/3.
    Teacher explains: When 𝒢 comes from a partition, a 𝒢-measurable Y must
    be CONSTANT on each block, and the defining property with G = the
    block forces the constant to be the average of X over the block:
       E[X | 𝒢] = ∑ᵢ (E[X𝟙_{Gᵢ}]/P(Gᵢ)) 𝟙_{Gᵢ}.
    (a) On {2, 4, 6} the average is (2 + 4 + 6)/3 = 4; on {1, 3, 5} it is
    3. For ℋ the block averages are 1.5, 3.5, 5.5. (b) Y = E[X | 𝒢]:
    E[Y𝟙_G] = 4 · P(G) = 4 · ½ = 2, and E[X𝟙_G] = (2 + 4 + 6)/6 = 2 ✓.
    (c) Var X = 91/6 − 49/4 = 35/12. Within each block of ℋ, X takes two
    values 1 apart, so Var(X | ℋ) = 1/4 everywhere. E[X | ℋ] takes 1.5,
    3.5, 5.5 with probability 1/3 each: variance (4 + 0 + 4)/3 = 8/3. And
    1/4 + 8/3 = 3/12 + 32/12 = 35/12 ✓. Note E[X | ℋ] is a RANDOM
    VARIABLE, not a number, and E[E[X | ℋ]] = 3.5 = EX. Common mistakes:
    giving a single number for E[X | 𝒢]; averaging over the wrong block;
    forgetting to weight by probabilities when blocks are unequal. Now you
    try: two fair dice, S = X₁ + X₂. Find E[X₁ | S]. (S/2, by symmetry:
    E[X₁ | S] = E[X₂ | S] and they add to S)

D2. (a) Prove existence and a.s. uniqueness of E[X | 𝒢] for X ∈ L¹.
    (b) Show that for X ∈ L², E[X | 𝒢] minimises E(X − Z)² over
    𝒢-measurable Z ∈ L². (c) Prove the tower property and "taking out
    what is known".
    Answer: (a) Radon-Nikodym applied to ν(G) = E[X𝟙_G] on (Ω, 𝒢);
    uniqueness from E[(Y − Y′)𝟙_{Y>Y′}] = 0. (b) X − E[X | 𝒢] ⊥ L²(𝒢).
    (c) check the defining identity.
    Teacher explains: (a) First X ≥ 0. ν(G) = E[X𝟙_G] is a finite measure
    on 𝒢 with ν ≪ P|_𝒢 (P(G) = 0 ⇒ ν(G) = 0). Radon-Nikodym gives a
    𝒢-measurable Y = dν/dP ≥ 0 with E[Y𝟙_G] = ν(G) = E[X𝟙_G]. In general
    take X = X⁺ − X⁻. Uniqueness: if Y, Y′ both work, G = {Y > Y′} ∈ 𝒢,
    so E[(Y − Y′)𝟙_G] = 0 with a non-negative integrand; hence P(G) = 0,
    and symmetrically, Y = Y′ a.s. (b) L²(𝒢) is a closed subspace of
    L²(ℱ) (a limit in L² of 𝒢-measurable functions has a 𝒢-measurable
    a.s. limit along a subsequence). Let Y be the orthogonal projection
    of X. Then E[(X − Y)Z] = 0 for all Z ∈ L²(𝒢); taking Z = 𝟙_G gives
    the defining identity, so Y = E[X | 𝒢], and Pythagoras gives E(X −
    Z)² = E(X − Y)² + E(Y − Z)² ≥ E(X − Y)². (c) TOWER (ℋ ⊆ 𝒢): for H ∈
    ℋ ⊆ 𝒢, E[E[X | 𝒢]𝟙_H] = E[X𝟙_H], so E[X | 𝒢] and X have the same
    conditional expectation given ℋ. KNOWN FACTOR: for Z = 𝟙_A, A ∈ 𝒢,
    and G ∈ 𝒢: E[𝟙_A E[X | 𝒢]𝟙_G] = E[E[X | 𝒢]𝟙_{A∩G}] = E[X𝟙_{A∩G}] =
    E[(𝟙_A X)𝟙_G]; extend to simple Z by linearity, bounded Z by DCT.
    Common mistakes: forgetting that uniqueness is only a.s.; using the
    tower property in the wrong direction (E[E[X | ℋ] | 𝒢] = E[X | ℋ]
    trivially, since E[X | ℋ] is already 𝒢-measurable). Now you try: show
    E[X | 𝒢] = EX when X is independent of 𝒢. (E[X𝟙_G] = EX · P(G))

D3. (a) (X, Y) has density f(x, y) = x + y on [0, 1]². Find E[Y | X] and
    check E[E[Y | X]] = EY. (b) (X, Y) is Gaussian with EX = EY = 0,
    Var X = 4, Var Y = 9, Cov(X, Y) = 3. Find E[Y | X] and Var(Y | X).
    (c) Let X, Y be i.i.d. U[0, 1] and M = max(X, Y). Find E[X | M].
    Answer: (a) (3X + 2)/(6X + 3); EY = 7/12. (b) (3/4)X, 27/4.
    (c) 3M/4.
    Teacher explains: (a) The marginal of X is f_X(x) = ∫₀¹(x + y) dy =
    x + ½, and ∫₀¹ y(x + y) dy = x/2 + 1/3. So
       E[Y | X = x] = (x/2 + 1/3)/(x + 1/2) = (3x + 2)/(6x + 3).
    Check: E h(X) = ∫₀¹ ((x/2 + 1/3)/(x + ½))(x + ½) dx = ∫₀¹(x/2 + 1/3) dx
    = 1/4 + 1/3 = 7/12, and EY = ∫₀¹ y(y + ½) dy = 1/3 + 1/4 = 7/12 ✓.
    (b) Put Y = aX + W with W = Y − aX; choose a so Cov(X, W) = 0: a =
    3/4. For jointly Gaussian vectors, uncorrelated means INDEPENDENT, so
    E[Y | X] = aX + EW = (3/4)X and Var(Y | X) = Var W = 9 − (3/4)²·4 =
    9 − 9/4 = 27/4, a constant. (c) Given M = m, either X is the max
    (probability ½, X = m) or Y is (X uniform on [0, m]). So E[X | M] =
    ½M + ½(M/2) = 3M/4. Check: EM = 2/3 and E(3M/4) = 1/2 = EX ✓.
    Common mistakes: forgetting to divide by the marginal density; in
    (b), using Cov/Var Y instead of Cov/Var X; in (c), treating X as
    uniform on [0, M] without the atom at M. Now you try: X, Y i.i.d.
    Exp(1); find E[X | X + Y]. ((X + Y)/2)

D4. Let ξ₁, ξ₂, ... be i.i.d. with P(ξ = 1) = P(ξ = −1) = ½, Sₙ = ξ₁ +
    ... + ξₙ, ℱₙ = σ(ξ₁, ..., ξₙ). Show that (a) Sₙ, (b) Sₙ² − n,
    (c) e^{θSₙ}/(cosh θ)ⁿ are martingales, and (d) Sₙ² is a
    submartingale; give its Doob decomposition. (e) A Pólya urn starts
    with one red and one blue ball; each draw returns the ball with one
    more of the same colour. Show the red proportion Rₙ is a martingale.
    Answer: in each case compute E[· | ℱₙ] using that ξₙ₊₁ ⫫ ℱₙ;
    Sₙ² = (Sₙ² − n) + n.
    Teacher explains: The recipe: write Mₙ₊₁ in terms of ℱₙ-measurable
    quantities and ξₙ₊₁, take out what is known, use independence.
    (a) E[Sₙ + ξₙ₊₁ | ℱₙ] = Sₙ + Eξ = Sₙ. (b) (Sₙ + ξ)² = Sₙ² + 2Sₙξ + 1,
    so E[Sₙ₊₁² − (n + 1) | ℱₙ] = Sₙ² + 2Sₙ · 0 + 1 − n − 1 = Sₙ² − n.
    (c) E[e^{θ(Sₙ+ξ)} | ℱₙ] = e^{θSₙ}E e^{θξ} = e^{θSₙ}cosh θ; divide by
    (cosh θ)ⁿ⁺¹. (d) By (b), Sₙ² = Mₙ + Aₙ with Mₙ = Sₙ² − n a martingale
    and Aₙ = n predictable increasing: that IS the Doob decomposition.
    Integrability: |Sₙ| ≤ n, so all moments are finite. (e) After n draws
    there are n + 2 balls, of which r = Rₙ(n + 2) are red. The next draw
    is red with probability Rₙ, giving (r + 1)/(n + 3); otherwise r/(n +
    3). So E[Rₙ₊₁ | ℱₙ] = (Rₙ(r + 1) + (1 − Rₙ)r)/(n + 3) = (r + Rₙ)/(n +
    3) = Rₙ(n + 3)/(n + 3) = Rₙ ✓. Common mistakes: forgetting to check
    E|Mₙ| < ∞; conditioning on the wrong σ-algebra (Rₙ is adapted to the
    draws, not to the number of balls alone). Now you try: show Sₙ³ −
    3nSₙ is a martingale. (expand (Sₙ + ξ)³; Eξ² = 1, Eξ³ = 0)

D5. (a) State the optional stopping theorem. (b) A symmetric walk starts
    at 0 and stops at τ, the first hit of b > 0 or −a < 0. Find P(S_τ = b)
    and Eτ. (c) A gambler with 2 units bets 1 unit each time, winning with
    probability p = 0.4, until he has 5 or 0. Find the probability he
    reaches 5 and the expected duration. (d) Show that optional stopping
    fails for τ = inf{n : Sₙ = 1}.
    Answer: (b) a/(a + b), Eτ = ab. (c) 40/211 ≈ 0.18957, 1110/211 ≈
    5.26066. (d) E S_τ = 1 ≠ 0 = S₀.
    Teacher explains: (a) If M is a martingale and τ a stopping time,
    E M_τ = E M₀ provided τ is bounded, or M_{τ∧n} is dominated by an
    integrable variable (e.g. bounded), or Eτ < ∞ with bounded increments.
    Always prove P(τ < ∞) = 1 first. (b) |S_{τ∧n}| ≤ max(a, b), so OST
    applies to Sₙ: 0 = bP + (−a)(1 − P) ⇒ P = a/(a + b). Now Sₙ² − n:
    E S²_{τ∧n} = E(τ ∧ n); let n → ∞ (bounded convergence on the left,
    MCT on the right): Eτ = b²P + a²(1 − P) = (b²a + a²b)/(a + b) = ab.
    (c) Here q = 0.6 and the martingale is r^{Sₙ} with r = q/p = 3/2
    (E r^ξ = p·r + q/r = 0.6 + 0.4 = 1). Start 2, absorb at 0, 5:
       r² = P·r⁵ + (1 − P)·1 ⇒ P = (r² − 1)/(r⁵ − 1) = (5/4)/(211/32)
         = 40/211 ≈ 0.18957.
    For the duration use Sₙ − n(p − q) = Sₙ + 0.2n: E S_τ + 0.2Eτ = 2, and
    E S_τ = 5 · 40/211 = 200/211, so Eτ = (2 − 200/211)/0.2 = 1110/211
    ≈ 5.26066. (d) The symmetric walk is recurrent, so τ < ∞ a.s. and
    S_τ = 1. But Eτ = ∞ and S_{τ∧n} is not bounded below, so none of the
    hypotheses holds — the "doubling strategy" paradox. Common mistakes:
    using the symmetric formula a/(a + b) for a biased walk; forgetting
    to justify n → ∞. Now you try: in (b) with a = 3, b = 7, find Eτ and
    P(S_τ = −3). (21, 7/10)

D6. (a) State and prove Doob's maximal inequality for a non-negative
    submartingale. (b) Deduce Kolmogorov's inequality. (c) For simple
    random walk, bound P(max_{k≤100}|S_k| ≥ 30) and E max_{k≤100} S_k².
    Answer: (a) λP(max_{k≤n} X_k ≥ λ) ≤ E[Xₙ; max ≥ λ] ≤ E Xₙ.
    (c) ≤ 1/9; ≤ 400.
    Teacher explains: (a) Let τ = min{k ≤ n : X_k ≥ λ} (τ = n if none) and
    A = {max_{k≤n} X_k ≥ λ} = {X_τ ≥ λ}. On A, X_τ ≥ λ; so
       λP(A) ≤ E[X_τ; A] = ∑_{k≤n} E[X_k; τ = k] ≤ ∑_{k≤n} E[Xₙ; τ = k]
             = E[Xₙ; A] ≤ E Xₙ,
    where E[X_k; τ = k] ≤ E[Xₙ; τ = k] because {τ = k} ∈ ℱ_k and
    E[Xₙ | ℱ_k] ≥ X_k. (b) If Sₙ is a sum of independent mean-0 L²
    variables, Sₙ² is a non-negative submartingale (Jensen), so P(max
    |S_k| ≥ λ) = P(max S_k² ≥ λ²) ≤ E Sₙ²/λ² = Var Sₙ/λ². (c) E S₁₀₀² =
    100, so P(max|S_k| ≥ 30) ≤ 100/900 = 1/9. Doob's L² inequality: E max
    S_k² ≤ (2/(2 − 1))² E S₁₀₀² = 4 · 100 = 400. Compare Chebyshev for
    the single time 100: the MAXIMUM costs nothing extra in (b). The
    proof pattern (stop at the first time the event happens) is worth
    memorising. Common mistakes: applying the maximal inequality to a
    submartingale that takes negative values (use |M| or M² or M⁺);
    thinking Doob's L¹ version (p = 1) holds — it fails without an
    L log L condition. Now you try: a non-negative martingale with M₀ = 1;
    bound P(sup_n Mₙ ≥ 10). (1/10, letting n → ∞)

D7. (a) State Doob's martingale convergence theorem and sketch the
    upcrossing proof. (b) Let Mₙ = ξ₁⋯ξₙ with ξᵢ i.i.d., P(ξ = 0) =
    P(ξ = 2) = ½. Show Mₙ → 0 a.s. but not in L¹. (c) Same with ξ = ½ or
    3/2. (d) Find the limit law of the Pólya urn proportion of D4(e).
    Answer: (a) L¹-bounded (sub)martingales converge a.s.; (b) Mₙ = 0 as
    soon as one ξᵢ = 0, but E Mₙ = 1; (c) → 0 a.s. since E ln ξ =
    ½ln(3/4) ≈ −0.14384 < 0; (d) Uniform[0, 1].
    Teacher explains: (a) THEOREM: if (Xₙ) is a submartingale with sup E Xₙ⁺
    < ∞, then Xₙ → X∞ a.s. and E|X∞| < ∞. Proof idea: fix rationals a < b
    and let Uₙ be the number of upcrossings of [a, b] by X₀, ..., Xₙ. Bet
    one unit whenever the path has gone below a, until it exceeds b: the
    gains form a martingale transform, and each completed upcrossing
    earns ≥ b − a, giving (b − a)E Uₙ ≤ E(Xₙ − a)⁺ ≤ sup E Xₙ⁺ + |a|. By
    MCT, U∞ < ∞ a.s. If Xₙ did not converge, lim inf < a < b < lim sup
    for some rationals, forcing U∞ = ∞; countably many pairs (a, b), so
    Xₙ converges a.s.; Fatou gives E|X∞| ≤ lim inf E|Xₙ| < ∞.
    (b) E[Mₙ₊₁ | ℱₙ] = Mₙ Eξ = Mₙ; Mₙ ≥ 0 so E|Mₙ| = 1 (L¹-bounded).
    P(Mₙ ≠ 0) = 2⁻ⁿ → 0, so Mₙ → 0 a.s. But E|Mₙ − 0| = 1 ↛ 0: the
    martingale is NOT uniformly integrable (all the mass sits on an event
    of probability 2⁻ⁿ with value 2ⁿ). (c) ln Mₙ = ∑ ln ξᵢ, and by the
    SLLN (ln Mₙ)/n → E ln ξ = ½(ln ½ + ln 3/2) = ½ln(3/4) < 0, so ln Mₙ →
    −∞ and Mₙ → 0 a.s. although E Mₙ = 1. (d) Rₙ ∈ [0, 1] is a bounded
    martingale, hence converges a.s. AND in L¹ to R∞. One checks by
    induction that after n draws the number of added red balls is
    uniform on {0, 1, ..., n}; so Rₙ = (k + 1)/(n + 2) with probability
    1/(n + 1) each, which converges weakly to U[0, 1]. So R∞ ~ U[0, 1] and
    E R∞ = ½ = R₀ (UI!). Common mistakes: "a.s. convergence + martingale
    ⇒ E M∞ = E M₀" (false: (b)); forgetting that non-negative
    martingales are automatically L¹-bounded. Now you try: start the urn
    with 2 red and 1 blue; find P(R∞ > ½). (R∞ ~ Beta(2, 1): 3/4)

D8. Define standard Brownian motion. (a) Show it is the centred Gaussian
    process with Cov(B_s, B_t) = min(s, t). (b) Compute Var(B₁ + B₂ + B₃)
    and E[B₁²B₂²]. (c) Show P(B₁ > 0, B₂ > 0) = 3/8. (d) Prove that
    W_t = tB_{1/t} (W₀ = 0) and c^{−1/2}B_{ct} are Brownian motions.
    Answer: (b) 14 and 4. (c) 1/4 + arcsin(1/√2)/(2π) = 3/8. (d) same
    covariance min(s, t); continuity of W at 0 from B_t/t → 0.
    Teacher explains: DEFINITION: B₀ = 0; for 0 ≤ t₁ < ... < tₖ the
    increments B_{t₂} − B_{t₁}, ..., B_{tₖ} − B_{tₖ₋₁} are independent;
    B_t − B_s ~ N(0, t − s); t ↦ B_t is continuous a.s. (a) Any finite
    vector (B_{t₁}, ..., B_{tₖ}) is a linear image of independent Gaussian
    increments, hence Gaussian. For s ≤ t, Cov(B_s, B_t) = Cov(B_s, B_s
    + (B_t − B_s)) = Var B_s = s. Conversely a Gaussian process with this
    covariance has uncorrelated, hence independent, increments.
    (b) Var(∑B_{tᵢ}) = ∑ᵢ∑ⱼ min(tᵢ, tⱼ) = (1 + 2 + 3) + 2(1 + 1 + 2) = 14.
    Write B₂ = B₁ + Y, Y ⫫ B₁, Y ~ N(0, 1): E[B₁²(B₁ + Y)²] = E B₁⁴ +
    2E[B₁³]E Y + E B₁² E Y² = 3 + 0 + 1 = 4. (c) (B₁, B₂/√2) is a standard
    bivariate normal with correlation ρ = 1/√2. For such a pair, P(X > 0,
    Y > 0) = 1/4 + arcsin(ρ)/(2π) (rotate to independent coordinates:
    the quadrant becomes a wedge of angle π/2 + arcsin ρ). With arcsin
    (1/√2) = π/4: 1/4 + 1/8 = 3/8. (d) Both are centred Gaussian. For W:
    Cov(W_s, W_t) = st·min(1/s, 1/t) = st/t = s for s ≤ t. Continuity for
    t > 0 is clear; at 0, W_t = B_u/u with u = 1/t → ∞, and B_u/u → 0
    a.s. by the SLLN for B_n (plus a bound on oscillation between
    integers). For scaling: Cov = c⁻¹ min(cs, ct) = min(s, t). Common
    mistakes: writing Cov(B_s, B_t) = st or = |t − s|; forgetting to check
    path continuity in (d). Now you try: Var(B₁ − 2B₃ + B₄)? (1 + 12 + 4
    + 2(−2 + 1 − 6) = 3)

D9. (a) Show that B_t² − t and exp(θB_t − θ²t/2) are martingales.
    (b) For τ = exit time of (−a, b), prove P(B_τ = b) = a/(a + b) and
    Eτ = ab. (c) For T_a = inf{t : B_t = a}, a > 0, show E e^{−λT_a} =
    e^{−a√(2λ)}; deduce P(T_a < ∞) = 1 and E T_a = ∞. (d) With drift,
    X_t = B_t + μt, μ > 0, find P(X ever reaches −a). Evaluate for μ = 1,
    a = 2.
    Answer: (c) E e^{−T₁/2} = e⁻¹ ≈ 0.36788. (d) e^{−2μa}; e⁻⁴ ≈
    0.01832.
    Teacher explains: (a) For s < t write B_t = B_s + Y, Y ⫫ ℱ_s, Y ~
    N(0, t − s). E[B_t² | ℱ_s] = B_s² + 2B_s·0 + (t − s), so E[B_t² − t |
    ℱ_s] = B_s² − s. E[e^{θB_t} | ℱ_s] = e^{θB_s}E e^{θY} = e^{θB_s}
    e^{θ²(t−s)/2}; rearrange. (b) As in D5, now in continuous time:
    |B_{τ∧t}| ≤ max(a, b) and τ < ∞ a.s., so 0 = bP − a(1 − P), and from
    E B²_{τ∧t} = E(τ ∧ t) with t → ∞, Eτ = b²·a/(a + b) + a²·b/(a + b) =
    ab. (c) Take θ = √(2λ) and M_t = exp(θB_t − λt). Up to T_a, B_t ≤ a,
    so 0 ≤ M_{t∧T_a} ≤ e^{θa}: bounded. On {T_a < ∞}, M_{T_a} = e^{θa −
    λT_a}; on {T_a = ∞}, M_t ≤ e^{θa − λt} → 0. Bounded convergence:
       1 = E M₀ = e^{θa} E[e^{−λT_a}𝟙_{T_a<∞}] ⇒ E e^{−λT_a} = e^{−a√(2λ)}.
    Let λ ↓ 0: P(T_a < ∞) = e⁰ = 1. The derivative d/dλ e^{−a√(2λ)} =
    −(a/√(2λ))e^{−a√(2λ)} → −∞ as λ ↓ 0, so E T_a = ∞. With a = 1, λ =
    1/2: e⁻¹. (d) exp(θX_t) is a martingale iff θμ + θ²/2 = 0: θ = −2μ.
    Stop at hitting −a or b: e^{2μa}P + e^{−2μb}(1 − P) = 1, so P = (1 −
    e^{−2μb})/(e^{2μa} − e^{−2μb}) → e^{−2μa} as b → ∞. With μ = 1, a = 2:
    e⁻⁴ ≈ 0.01832. Common mistakes: applying OST to e^{θB_t} without the
    compensator; using θ = −√(2λ) (then M is unbounded before T_a).
    Now you try: E τ² for the exit time of (−a, a), using the martingale
    B_t⁴ − 6tB_t² + 3t². (5a⁴/3)

D10. (a) State and prove the reflection principle and deduce the law of
     M_t = max_{s≤t} B_s. (b) Compute P(M₁ ≥ 1), the density of T_a and
     the median of T₁. (c) Show ∑_{k=1}^{n} (B_{kt/n} − B_{(k−1)t/n})² → t
     in L², and deduce that Brownian paths have infinite variation.
     Answer: (a) P(M_t ≥ a) = 2P(B_t ≥ a), M_t ~ |B_t|.
     (b) 2(1 − Φ(1)) ≈ 0.31731; a(2πt³)^{−1/2}e^{−a²/(2t)}; ≈ 2.19811.
     (c) the sum has mean t and variance 2t²/n.
     Teacher explains: (a) By the strong Markov property at T_a, (B_{T_a+s}
     − a)_{s≥0} is a Brownian motion independent of ℱ_{T_a}, so it is
     equally likely to be above or below 0 at the remaining time t − T_a:
        P(M_t ≥ a, B_t < a) = P(M_t ≥ a, B_t > a) = P(B_t > a)
     (the last because B_t > a forces M_t ≥ a). Adding, P(M_t ≥ a) =
     2P(B_t ≥ a) = P(|B_t| ≥ a). (b) P(M₁ ≥ 1) = 2(1 − Φ(1)) = 0.31731.
     Since {T_a ≤ t} = {M_t ≥ a}, P(T_a ≤ t) = 2(1 − Φ(a/√t)); differentiate
     in t: f(t) = 2φ(a/√t)·a/(2t^{3/2}) = a(2πt³)^{−1/2}e^{−a²/(2t)}. The
     density decays like t^{−3/2}, so E T_a = ∞ (consistent with D9).
     Median: 2(1 − Φ(1/√t)) = ½ ⇒ 1/√t = Φ⁻¹(3/4) = 0.67449 ⇒ t ≈ 2.19811.
     (c) Let Δₖ be the increments: independent N(0, t/n). So E∑Δₖ² = t
     and Var ∑Δₖ² = n·2(t/n)² = 2t²/n → 0 (Var(Z²) = 2 for Z ~ N(0, 1)).
     Hence Qₙ → t in L². If the path had finite variation V, then Qₙ ≤
     max|Δₖ| · V → 0 by uniform continuity — contradicting Qₙ → t > 0
     (along an a.s. convergent subsequence). Common mistakes: forgetting
     the factor 2 in the reflection principle; "reflecting" at a
     deterministic time instead of a stopping time. Now you try:
     P(M₁ ≥ 1, B₁ ≤ 0)? (reflect: P(B₁ ≥ 2) = 1 − Φ(2) ≈ 0.02275)

D11. (a) Define convergence in distribution and prove that, on R, it is
     equivalent to Fₙ(x) → F(x) at continuity points of F. (b) Find the
     limits in distribution of n·min(U₁, ..., Uₙ) (Uᵢ i.i.d. U[0, 1]) and
     of max(E₁, ..., Eₙ) − ln n (Eᵢ i.i.d. Exp(1)). (c) Let Xₙ = n with
     probability 1/n and 0 otherwise: find the weak limit and compare
     E Xₙ. (d) State Slutsky's theorem and apply it to the t-statistic.
     Answer: (b) Exp(1); Gumbel F(x) = exp(−e^{−x}). (c) δ₀, but E Xₙ = 1.
     (d) √n(X̄ − μ)/Sₙ ⇒ N(0, 1).
     Teacher explains: (a) Xₙ ⇒ X means E f(Xₙ) → E f(X) for all bounded
     continuous f. (⇒) Given x with P(X = x) = 0 and ε > 0, take f ≤ 𝟙_{(−∞,
     x]} ≤ g continuous, with f = 1 on (−∞, x − ε], g = 0 on [x + ε, ∞).
     Then F(x − ε) ≤ E f(X) = lim E f(Xₙ) ≤ lim inf Fₙ(x) ≤ lim sup Fₙ(x)
     ≤ lim E g(Xₙ) = E g(X) ≤ F(x + ε); let ε → 0 using continuity at x.
     (⇐) Build Yₙ = Fₙ⁻¹(U), Y = F⁻¹(U) on one space (Skorokhod): Yₙ → Y
     a.s., then use bounded convergence. (b) P(n min > x) = (1 − x/n)ⁿ →
     e^{−x}: Exp(1). P(max − ln n ≤ x) = (1 − e^{−x}/n)ⁿ → exp(−e^{−x}).
     (c) Fₙ(x) = 1 − 1/n → 1 for 0 ≤ x < n, so Xₙ ⇒ δ₀; yet E Xₙ = 1 ↛ 0:
     the sequence is not UI (the mass n · 1/n "escapes to infinity").
     (d) SLUTSKY: Xₙ ⇒ X and Yₙ → c in probability (c constant) ⇒ Xₙ + Yₙ
     ⇒ X + c, XₙYₙ ⇒ cX, Xₙ/Yₙ ⇒ X/c (c ≠ 0). By the CLT √n(X̄ − μ)/σ ⇒
     N(0, 1), and σ/Sₙ → 1 in probability (SLLN), so the product ⇒
     N(0, 1). Common mistakes: requiring convergence at EVERY x (fails for
     δ_{1/n} ⇒ δ₀ at x = 0); applying Slutsky when Yₙ converges only in
     distribution to a NON-constant. Now you try: n(1 − max Uᵢ)?
     (Exp(1))

D12. (a) Compute the characteristic function of N(0, 1) and of Poisson(λ).
     (b) Prove the CLT for i.i.d. variables with finite variance using
     Lévy's continuity theorem. (c) Show Bin(n, λ/n) ⇒ Poisson(λ).
     (d) Estimate P(S ≥ 60) for S = number of heads in 100 fair tosses.
     (e) Show e^{−n}∑_{k=0}^{n} nᵏ/k! → ½.
     Answer: (a) e^{−t²/2}, e^{λ(e^{it}−1)}. (b) φ(t/√n)ⁿ → e^{−t²/2}.
     (c) (1 + λ(e^{it} − 1)/n)ⁿ → e^{λ(e^{it}−1)}. (d) ≈ 0.02872 (exact
     0.02844). (e) CLT for Poisson(n).
     Teacher explains: (a) φ(t) = ∫e^{itx}e^{−x²/2}dx/√(2π). Differentiate
     under the integral (|ix e^{−x²/2}| is integrable) and integrate by
     parts: φ′(t) = −tφ(t), φ(0) = 1, so φ(t) = e^{−t²/2}. Poisson:
     ∑e^{−λ}λᵏe^{itk}/k! = e^{−λ}e^{λe^{it}}. (b) WLOG μ = 0, σ = 1.
     Since EX² < ∞, φ(u) = 1 − u²/2 + o(u²) as u → 0. Then
        φ_{Sₙ/√n}(t) = φ(t/√n)ⁿ = (1 − t²/(2n) + o(1/n))ⁿ → e^{−t²/2},
     (use |aⁿ − bⁿ| ≤ n|a − b| for |a|, |b| ≤ 1). The limit is the ch.f. of
     N(0, 1), continuous at 0, so by Lévy Sₙ/√n ⇒ N(0, 1). (c) The ch.f.
     of Bin(n, p) is (1 − p + pe^{it})ⁿ = (1 + λ(e^{it} − 1)/n)ⁿ →
     exp(λ(e^{it} − 1)). (d) ES = 50, sd = 5. With continuity correction,
     P(S ≥ 59.5) ≈ 1 − Φ(9.5/5) = 1 − Φ(1.9) = 0.02872; the exact binomial
     tail is 0.02844. (e) If Xₙ ~ Poisson(n) = sum of n i.i.d. Poisson(1),
     the sum equals P(Xₙ ≤ n) = P((Xₙ − n)/√n ≤ 0) → Φ(0) = ½. Common
     mistakes: expanding φ to second order without E X² < ∞; forgetting
     that Lévy needs continuity of the limit AT 0 (N(0, n) has φₙ → 𝟙_{t=0},
     no weak limit). Now you try: the ch.f. of Exp(1) and from it E X³.
     (1/(1 − it); φ‴(0) = 6i³ ⇒ E X³ = 6)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Conditional expectation on discrete spaces. X is a fair die unless
    stated; X₁, X₂ are two independent fair dice, S = X₁ + X₂. Give the
    conditional expectation as a function (value on each block).
    a) E[X | σ({X ≤ 3})]                b) E[X | σ({X even})]
    c) E[X² | σ({X even})]              d) E[X | σ({1, 2}, {3, 4}, {5, 6})]
    e) E[X | {∅, Ω}]                     f) E[X | 2^Ω]
    g) E[X₁ | S]                         h) E[S | X₁]
    i) E[X₁X₂ | X₁]                      j) E[max(X₁, X₂) | X₁ = 3]
    k) E[X₁ | max(X₁, X₂) = 3]           l) Var(X | X even) on {X even}
    m) Var(E[X | 𝒢]) for 𝒢 as in d)
    n) Ω = [0, 1] with Lebesgue measure, 𝒢 = σ([0, ½)), X(ω) = ω²
    o) same 𝒢, X(ω) = sin(πω)
    p) N = number of heads in 10 fair tosses, I = 𝟙{first toss is H}:
       E[I | N]
    q) X ~ Poisson(2), Y ~ Poisson(3) independent: E[X | X + Y = 10]
    r) X, Y i.i.d. Geometric on {1, 2, ...}: E[X | X + Y = 9]
    Answers:
      a) 2 on {1, 2, 3}, 5 on {4, 5, 6}    b) 4 on evens, 3 on odds
      c) 56/3 on evens, 35/3 on odds       d) 1.5, 3.5, 5.5
      e) 3.5 (a constant)                  f) X itself
      g) S/2          h) X₁ + 3.5          i) 3.5X₁
      j) 4 ((3 + 3 + 3 + 4 + 5 + 6)/6)
      k) 12/5 (pairs (3,1), (3,2), (3,3), (1,3), (2,3))
      l) 8/3 (56/3 − 16)                   m) 8/3
      n) 1/12 on [0, ½), 7/12 on [½, 1]
      o) 2/π on both halves (≈ 0.63662)    p) N/10
      q) 4 (X | X + Y = n is Bin(n, 2/5))
      r) 9/2 (given the sum, X is uniform on {1, ..., 8})

M2. Conditional expectations with densities, Gaussians and symmetry.
    a) f(x, y) = x + y on [0, 1]²: E[Y | X = x]; its value at x = 0, 1
    b) f(x, y) = 2 on 0 < y < x < 1: E[Y | X] and E[X | Y]
    c) f(x, y) = e^{−x} on 0 < y < x: E[Y | X] and E[X | Y]
    d) (X, Y) Gaussian, means 0, Var X = 4, Var Y = 9, Cov = 3: E[X | Y]
       and Var(X | Y)
    e) X ~ N(1, 1), Z ~ N(0, 1) independent, Y = 2X + Z: E[X | Y],
       Var(X | Y)
    f) U ~ U[0, 1]: E[U | U²] and E[U | |U − ½|]
    g) X ~ N(0, 1): E[X | X²], E[X⁺ | |X|], E[X² | X⁺]
    h) N ~ Poisson(λ), given N, X ~ Bin(N, p): E[X | N], EX, Var X
    i) random sum S = ξ₁ + ... + ξ_N, N ⫫ (ξᵢ), N geometric on {1, 2,
       ...} with mean 4, Eξ = 2, Var ξ = 3: ES and Var S
    j) Brownian motion: E[B₂ | B₁], E[B₁ | B₂]
    k) E[B_t² | ℱ_s] and E[B_t³ | ℱ_s] for s < t
    l) X, Y i.i.d. U[0, 1], M = max(X, Y): E[X | M]
    m) X, Y i.i.d. Exp(1), m = min(X, Y): E[X | m]
    n) X, Y i.i.d. N(0, 1): E[X | X + Y] and E[X² | X + Y]
    Answers:
      a) (3x + 2)/(6x + 3); 2/3 and 5/9
      b) X/2 and (1 + Y)/2                c) X/2 and Y + 1
      d) Y/3 and 4 − 1 = 3                e) 1 + (2/5)(Y − 2) and 1/5
      f) U (σ(U²) = σ(U)) and ½ (U = ½ ± |U − ½| equally likely)
      g) 0, |X|/2, X² on {X > 0} and 1 on {X ≤ 0} (E[X² | X < 0] = 1)
      h) pN, pλ, pλ (X ~ Poisson(pλ))
      i) 8 and 4·3 + 12·4 = 60 (Var N = (1 − p)/p² = 12, p = 1/4)
      j) B₁ and B₂/2                     k) B_s² + (t − s); B_s³ + 3(t − s)B_s
      l) 3M/4                             m) m + ½
      n) (X + Y)/2 and (X + Y)²/4 + ½

M3. Martingale (M), submartingale (sub), supermartingale (super) or
    none? ξᵢ i.i.d. ±1 fair unless stated, Sₙ = ∑_{i≤n} ξᵢ, ℱₙ natural.
    a) Sₙ                                b) Sₙ²
    c) Sₙ² − n                           d) Sₙ when P(ξ = 1) = 0.6
    e) (2/3)^{Sₙ} when P(ξ = 1) = 0.6    f) Sₙ − 0.2n when P(ξ = 1) = 0.6
    g) |Sₙ|                              h) e^{θSₙ}/(cosh θ)ⁿ
    i) Sₙ³ − 3nSₙ                        j) max_{k≤n} S_k
    k) ∏_{i≤n}(1 + ξᵢ/2)                 l) 2ⁿ𝟙{ξ₁ = ... = ξₙ = 1}
    m) e^{−Sₙ}                           n) nSₙ
    o) −Sₙ²                              p) Zₙ/μⁿ (branching, mean μ)
    q) B_t³ − 3tB_t                      r) B_t⁴ − 6tB_t² + 3t²
    s) e^{B_t}                           t) e^{−t}B_t
    u) E[X | ℱₙ] for a fixed X ∈ L¹     v) Sₙ/n (natural filtration)
    Answers:
      a) M      b) sub    c) M      d) sub    e) M      f) M
      g) sub (convex of M)      h) M      i) M      j) sub
      k) M (factors of mean 1)  l) M (double-or-nothing)
      m) sub (E e^{−ξ} = cosh 1 > 1)
      n) none (E[(n + 1)Sₙ₊₁ | ℱₙ] = (n + 1)Sₙ, which is above or below
         nSₙ according to the sign of Sₙ)
      o) super  p) M      q) M      r) M
      s) sub (E[e^{B_t} | ℱ_s] = e^{B_s + (t−s)/2})
      t) none (E[e^{−t}B_t | ℱ_s] = e^{−t}B_s)   u) M (Doob)
      v) none (E[Sₙ₊₁/(n + 1) | ℱₙ] = Sₙ/(n + 1); it is a REVERSE
         martingale for the backward filtration σ(Sₙ, Sₙ₊₁, ...))

M7. Brownian martingales, hitting times and the reflection principle.
    M_t = max_{s≤t} B_s, T_a = inf{t : B_t = a}.

      g) e^{−3√(1/9)} = e⁻¹ ≈ 0.36788    h) 1/Φ⁻¹(3/4)² ≈ 2.19811

         2tanh(½) ≈ 0.92423
      n) λ = 9/2         o) 1 − (2/π)arcsin(1/√2) = 1/2
      p) (2/π)arcsin(1/2) = 1/3            q) ∞; 1

M8. Find the weak limit (or say "no weak limit").
    a) δ_{1/n}                           b) N(0, 1/n)
    c) N(n, 1)                           d) N(0, n)
    e) uniform on {1/n, 2/n, ..., 1}     f) Bin(n, 3/n)
    g) (1/n)·Geometric(λ/n) (on {1, 2, ...})
    h) n·min(U₁, ..., Uₙ), Uᵢ i.i.d. U[0, 1]
    i) max(E₁, ..., Eₙ) − ln n, Eᵢ i.i.d. Exp(1)
    j) n(1 − max(U₁, ..., Uₙ))           k) Student's t with n d.f.
    l) (χ²ₙ − n)/√(2n)                   m) density 1 + sin(2πnx) on [0, 1]
    n) max(C₁, ..., Cₙ)/n, Cᵢ i.i.d. standard Cauchy
    o) XₙYₙ + Yₙ where Xₙ ⇒ N(0, 1) and Yₙ → 2 in probability
    p) Xₙ = n with probability 1/n, else 0: the limit, and lim E Xₙ
    q) (Poisson(n) − n)/√n
    r) √n(Ū − ½)·√12, Ū the mean of n i.i.d. U[0, 1]
    s) Sₙ/n for i.i.d. Cauchy Cᵢ
    Answers:
      a) δ₀       b) δ₀       c) none (not tight: mass escapes to ∞)
      d) none (Fₙ(x) → ½ for every x; not a distribution function)
      e) U[0, 1]  f) Poisson(3)           g) Exp(λ)
      h) Exp(1)   i) Gumbel, F(x) = exp(−e^{−x})
      j) Exp(1)   k) N(0, 1)              l) N(0, 1)
      m) U[0, 1] (∫f·(1 + sin 2πnx) → ∫f by Riemann-Lebesgue, although the
         densities do not converge)
      n) Fréchet: P(limit ≤ x) = exp(−1/(πx)), x > 0
      o) N(2, 4)  p) δ₀; E Xₙ = 1 for all n (not UI)
      q) N(0, 1)  r) N(0, 1)
      s) standard Cauchy (it is Cauchy for every n: no LLN)

M9. Characteristic functions: compute φ(t) = E e^{itX}, or identify the
    law from φ.
    a) Bernoulli(p)                      b) Bin(n, p)
    c) Poisson(λ)                        d) Geometric(p) on {1, 2, ...}
    e) U[−a, a]                          f) U[0, 1]
    g) Exp(λ)                            h) Gamma(k, λ)
    i) N(μ, σ²)                          j) standard Cauchy
    k) Laplace, density ½e^{−|x|}        l) X = ±1 with probability ½ each
    m) φ(t) = cos²t                      n) φ(t) = e^{−t²}
    o) φ(t) = 1/(1 − 2it)                p) φ(t) = (1/3 + (2/3)e^{it})⁵
    q) φ(t) = exp(2(e^{it} − 1) − t²/2)  r) φ(t) = (1 − |t|)⁺
    s) φ(t) = e^{3it − 2|t|}             t) φ(t) = ½ + ½e^{−t²/2}
    Answers:
      a) 1 − p + pe^{it}                 b) (1 − p + pe^{it})ⁿ
      c) exp(λ(e^{it} − 1))              d) pe^{it}/(1 − (1 − p)e^{it})
      e) sin(at)/(at)                    f) (e^{it} − 1)/(it)
      g) λ/(λ − it)                      h) (λ/(λ − it))ᵏ
      i) exp(iμt − σ²t²/2)               j) e^{−|t|}
      k) 1/(1 + t²)                      l) cos t
      m) values −2, 0, 2 with probabilities ¼, ½, ¼ (sum of two
         independent ±1; cos²t = (1 + cos 2t)/2)
      n) N(0, 2)                         o) Exp with mean 2 (λ = 1/2)
      p) Bin(5, 2/3)
      q) Poisson(2) + an independent N(0, 1)
      r) density (1 − cos x)/(πx²) (Pólya)
      s) 3 + 2C, C standard Cauchy (location 3, scale 2)
      t) the mixture ½δ₀ + ½N(0, 1)

M10. Moments, inversion, and "is it a characteristic function?"
     a) φ = e^{−t²/2}: E X⁴ from φ⁽⁴⁾(0)
     b) Poisson(λ): EX and EX² from φ′(0), φ″(0)
     c) φ = (1 − it)⁻²: EX and EX²     d) φ = cos t: EX² and EX⁴
     e) φ = 1/(1 + t²): Var X          f) is cos(t²) a ch.f.?
     g) is e^{−t⁴} a ch.f.?            h) is e^{−|t|^{1/2}} a ch.f.?
     i) is (1 + t)/2 a ch.f.?          j) is 1/(1 + t²) = |1/(1 − it)|² a
                                          ch.f.? of what?
     k) is sin t/t a ch.f.?            l) is 1/(2 − e^{it}) a ch.f.?
     m) the density with φ = e^{−|t|}, by the inversion formula
     n) integer-valued X with φ = cos²t: P(X = 0) = (1/2π)∫_{−π}^{π} φ
     o) the density with φ = (sin t/t)²
     p) ∫_R (sin t/t)² dt, via Plancherel and o)
     q) the cumulants (coefficients of (it)ᵏ/k! in ln φ) of Poisson(λ)
     r) E|X| for φ = e^{−|t|}? is φ differentiable at 0?
     Answers:
       a) 3        b) λ, λ + λ²        c) 2, 6        d) 1, 1
       e) 2
       f) no (cos t² = 1 − t⁴/2 + ... gives φ″(0) = 0, so EX² = 0, X = 0,
          φ ≡ 1: contradiction)
       g) no (same argument)            h) yes (symmetric ½-stable law)
       i) no (φ(0) = ½ ≠ 1)
       j) yes: X − X′ with X, X′ i.i.d. Exp(1), the Laplace law
       k) yes (U[−1, 1])
       l) yes: Geometric on {0, 1, ...} with P(X = k) = 2^{−k−1}
       m) 1/(π(1 + x²))                 n) 1/2
       o) (2 − |x|)/4 on [−2, 2] (sum of two independent U[−1, 1])
       p) π (= 2π∫f², f = ½ on [−1, 1])
       q) all equal to λ
       r) E|X| = ∞; no (φ has a corner at 0)

M11. CLT and related approximations (use Φ; continuity correction for
     integer variables).
     a) S = heads in 100 fair tosses: P(S ≥ 60)
     b) 400 fair tosses: P(190 ≤ S ≤ 210)
     c) sum of 48 i.i.d. U[0, 1]: P(S > 26)
     d) sum of 100 fair dice: P(S ≥ 380)
     e) X ~ Poisson(100): P(X ≤ 90)
     f) smallest n with P(|Sₙ/n − ½| ≤ 0.01) ≳ 0.95 for fair tosses
     g) X ~ χ²₂₀₀: P(X > 220)
     h) lim e^{−n}∑_{k≤n} nᵏ/k!
     i) delta method: limit law of √n(X̄ₙ² − 1) for i.i.d. Exp(1)
     j) limit law of √n(ln X̄ₙ − ln λ) for i.i.d. Poisson(λ)
     k) limit law of √n(2√X̄ₙ − 2√λ) for i.i.d. Poisson(λ)
     l) sample median of n i.i.d. U[0, 1]: limit law of √n(med − ½)
     m) independent X_k = ±k (prob ½ each): does Lindeberg hold? s_n²?
     n) independent X_k = ±2ᵏ: does Lindeberg hold? lim max_k σ_k²/s_n²
     o) independent Bernoulli(1/k), Nₙ = ∑_{k≤n}: centring and scaling
        for a normal limit
     Answers:
       a) 1 − Φ(1.9) ≈ 0.02872 (exact 0.02844)
       b) 2Φ(1.05) − 1 ≈ 0.70628 (exact 0.70629)
       c) 1 − Φ(1) ≈ 0.15866 (mean 24, variance 4)
       d) 1 − Φ(29.5/17.078) ≈ 0.04205 (exact 0.04203)
       e) Φ(−0.95) ≈ 0.17106 (exact 0.17139)
       f) 1.96 · 0.5/√n ≤ 0.01 ⇒ n ≥ 9604
       g) 1 − Φ(1) ≈ 0.15866 (exact 0.15828)
       h) 1/2      i) N(0, 4) (g(x) = x², g′(1)² · 1 = 4)
       j) N(0, 1/λ)                     k) N(0, 1) (variance stabilising)
       l) N(0, 1/4) (variance 1/(4f(½)²))
       m) yes (|X_k| ≤ n = o(s_n)); s_n² = n(n + 1)(2n + 1)/6
       n) no; 3/4 (the last term dominates; no CLT)
       o) (Nₙ − Hₙ)/√(Hₙ − Hₙ⁽²⁾) ⇒ N(0, 1), Hₙ = ∑1/k ~ ln n (the
          number of records)

M12. True (T) or false (F)? Give a counterexample for F.
     a) E[E[X | 𝒢]] = EX for X ∈ L¹.
     b) If X ⫫ 𝒢 then E[X | 𝒢] = EX.
     c) If E[X | Y] = EX then X and Y are independent.
     d) E[X | 𝒢] = E[X | ℋ] a.s. whenever σ-algebras 𝒢 ⊆ ℋ.
     e) Every L¹-bounded martingale converges in L¹.
     f) Every UI martingale converges a.s. and in L¹.
     g) E M_τ = E M₀ for every martingale and a.s. finite stopping time τ.
     h) The last time before 10 that a random walk visits 0 is a
        stopping time.
     i) Brownian motion is a.s. differentiable at t = 0.
     j) Brownian paths have finite total variation on [0, 1].
     k) B_t² is a martingale.
     l) Convergence in distribution implies convergence in probability.
     m) Xₙ ⇒ X and Yₙ ⇒ Y imply Xₙ + Yₙ ⇒ X + Y.
     n) A pointwise limit of characteristic functions is a ch.f.
     o) If φₙ → φ pointwise and φ is a ch.f., then μₙ ⇒ μ.
     p) If φ_X is real-valued then X is symmetric.
     q) If φ_X is differentiable at 0 then E|X| < ∞.
     r) Xₙ ⇒ X implies E Xₙ → EX.
     s) Every tight sequence of laws on R has a weakly convergent
        subsequence.
     t) Xₙ ⇒ c (a constant) implies Xₙ → c in probability.
     Answers:
       a) T        b) T
       c) F: Z ~ N(0, 1), X = Z, Y = Z²: E[X | Y] = 0 = EX, yet X and Y
          are dependent
       d) F: 𝒢 trivial, ℋ = ℱ gives EX versus X (the tower property
          only says E[E[X | ℋ] | 𝒢] = E[X | 𝒢])
       e) F: ∏ξᵢ with ξ ∈ {0, 2}      f) T
       g) F: simple random walk stopped at the first hit of 1
       h) F: deciding it requires the future path
       i) F: B_t/t = W_{1/t} with W = time inversion, a BM, and
          lim sup_{u→∞} W_u = +∞
       j) F (quadratic variation 1 forces infinite variation)
       k) F: E[B_t² | ℱ_s] = B_s² + t − s; B_t² − t is
       l) F: X ~ N(0, 1), Xₙ = −X for all n: Xₙ ⇒ X but |Xₙ − X| = 2|X|
       m) F: Xₙ = Z, Yₙ = −Z, Y = Z (sum 0, not 2Z)
       n) F: e^{−nt²/2} → 𝟙_{t=0} (N(0, n))
       o) T (Lévy)     p) T (φ_{−X} = conj φ_X = φ_X)
       q) F: symmetric X with P(|X| > x) ~ 1/(x ln x) (only the converse
          direction holds)
       r) F: Xₙ = n w.p. 1/n (M8 p))     s) T (Helly-Prokhorov)
       t) T

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: teach conditional expectation first on FINITE partitions
  ("average over the block you are in"), and insist that E[X | 𝒢] is a
  random variable; then treat the general definition as the same idea
  with blocks replaced by events of 𝒢. For martingales, drill the one-line
  check E[Mₙ₊₁ | ℱₙ] = Mₙ by writing Mₙ₊₁ via Mₙ and the new independent
  step. Before every optional stopping argument, have the student WRITE
  which hypothesis (bounded τ, bounded stopped process, Eτ < ∞ with
  bounded increments) holds, and keep the doubling-strategy walk as the
  warning example. For Brownian motion, reduce everything to Gaussian
  vectors with covariance min(s, t), and draw the reflected path. For
  weak convergence, always compute Fₙ(x) or φₙ(t) explicitly; keep δ_n
  (not tight), N(0, n) (limit not continuous at 0) and "n w.p. 1/n"
  (means do not converge) as the three standard pathologies. Harder:
  construct Brownian motion by the Lévy-Ciesielski (Haar/Schauder)
  series and prove Kolmogorov's continuity criterion; prove the strong
  Markov property and the full reflection principle for the joint law
  of (M_t, B_t); prove nowhere differentiability (Paley-Wiener-Zygmund)
  and the law of the iterated logarithm; prove Lindeberg-Feller and the
  Berry-Esseen bound; prove Prokhorov's theorem and Donsker's invariance
  principle; establish the martingale central limit theorem and
  Azuma-Hoeffding; and study backward martingales to prove the SLLN and
  de Finetti's theorem.


================================================================================
TOPIC 12.13 — PARTIAL DIFFERENTIAL EQUATIONS (GRADUATE): SOBOLEV SPACES,
ELLIPTIC REGULARITY, WEAK SOLUTIONS, PARABOLIC AND HYPERBOLIC EQUATIONS,
VARIATIONAL METHODS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - WEAK DERIVATIVES: for u, v ∈ L¹_loc(Ω), v = ∂ᵅu weakly if ∫u ∂ᵅφ =
    (−1)^|α| ∫vφ for every φ ∈ C_c^∞(Ω). Weak derivatives are unique a.e.
    and agree with classical ones for C^|α| functions. Examples: |x|′ =
    sgn x on (−1, 1); the Heaviside function H has NO weak derivative (its
    distributional derivative is δ ∉ L¹_loc). In one variable u ∈ W^{1,1}
    iff u agrees a.e. with an absolutely continuous function (so the
    Cantor function is not weakly differentiable).
  - SOBOLEV SPACES: W^{k,p}(Ω) = {u ∈ Lᵖ : ∂ᵅu ∈ Lᵖ for |α| ≤ k}, norm
    ‖u‖ = (∑_{|α|≤k} ‖∂ᵅu‖ₚᵖ)^{1/p}; a Banach space, reflexive for 1 < p
    < ∞, and Hᵏ = W^{k,2} is a Hilbert space. MEYERS-SERRIN: C^∞ ∩ W^{k,p}
    is dense in W^{k,p}(Ω) (1 ≤ p < ∞). W₀^{k,p}(Ω) = closure of C_c^∞(Ω);
    W₀^{k,p}(Rⁿ) = W^{k,p}(Rⁿ) but H₀¹(Ω) ≠ H¹(Ω) for bounded Ω.
  - POWER-FUNCTION TEST on the unit ball B ⊂ Rⁿ: |x|^(−a) ∈ Lᵖ(B) iff ap <
    n, and ∈ W^{1,p}(B) iff (a + 1)p < n. ln ln(4/|x|) ∈ W^{1,n}(B) is
    UNBOUNDED for n ≥ 2 (W^{1,n} ⊄ L^∞).
  - SOBOLEV EMBEDDINGS: (i) p < n: GAGLIARDO-NIRENBERG-SOBOLEV ‖u‖_{p*} ≤
    C‖∇u‖ₚ on W^{1,p}(Rⁿ), p* = np/(n − p) (forced by scaling u(λx));
    (ii) p = n: W^{1,n} ⊂ L^q for all q < ∞; (iii) p > n: MORREY W^{1,p} ⊂
    C^{0,γ}, γ = 1 − n/p. Iterating: W^{k,p} ⊂ L^q with 1/q = 1/p − k/n
    if kp < n, and Hᵏ(Rⁿ) ⊂ C⁰ if k > n/2. On bounded Lipschitz Ω the same
    holds, and RELLICH-KONDRACHOV makes W^{1,p} ⊂ L^q COMPACT for q < p*
    (not at q = p*, and never on all of Rⁿ: translations).
  - TRACES AND POINCARÉ: for bounded C¹ (or Lipschitz) Ω the TRACE map T :
    W^{1,p}(Ω) → Lᵖ(∂Ω) is bounded, Tu = u|∂Ω for continuous u, and ker T
    = W₀^{1,p}(Ω). POINCARÉ: ‖u‖ₚ ≤ C‖∇u‖ₚ on W₀^{1,p}(Ω), Ω bounded; on
    (0, L) with p = 2 the best constant is L/π. Poincaré-Wirtinger: ‖u −
    ū‖ₚ ≤ C‖∇u‖ₚ on W^{1,p}(Ω), Ω connected Lipschitz.
  - FRACTIONAL SPACES VIA FOURIER: Hˢ(Rⁿ) = {u : (1 + |ξ|²)^{s/2}û ∈ L²},
    ‖u‖²_{Hˢ} = (2π)⁻ⁿ∫(1 + |ξ|²)ˢ|û|² dξ. Hˢ ⊂ C_b for s > n/2; δ ∈ Hˢ iff
    s < −n/2; 𝟙_[−1,1] ∈ Hˢ(R) iff s < 1/2. H⁻¹(Ω) = (H₀¹(Ω))′.
  - WEAK SOLUTIONS OF ELLIPTIC PROBLEMS: for Lu = −∂ᵢ(aᵢⱼ∂ⱼu) + bᵢ∂ᵢu +
    cu, UNIFORMLY ELLIPTIC (aᵢⱼξᵢξⱼ ≥ θ|ξ|², θ > 0) with bounded
    coefficients, u ∈ H₀¹(Ω) is a weak solution of Lu = f, u = 0 on ∂Ω,
    if B[u, v] = ∫(aᵢⱼ∂ⱼu∂ᵢv + bᵢ∂ᵢu v + cuv) = ⟨f, v⟩ for all v ∈
    H₀¹. Neumann conditions are NATURAL (built into the form on H¹);
    Dirichlet conditions are ESSENTIAL (built into the space).
  - LAX-MILGRAM: B bounded (|B[u, v]| ≤ M‖u‖‖v‖) and COERCIVE (B[u, u] ≥
    α‖u‖²) on a Hilbert space H ⇒ for every f ∈ H′ a unique u with B[u, v]
    = f(v) ∀v, and ‖u‖ ≤ ‖f‖/α. Symmetry is NOT needed (symmetric case =
    Riesz). GÅRDING: B[u, u] ≥ (θ/2)‖∇u‖² − γ‖u‖², so B + γ is coercive.
  - FREDHOLM ALTERNATIVE AND SPECTRUM: since H₀¹ ⊂ L² is compact, either
    Lu = f has a unique weak solution for every f, or Lu = 0 has non-zero
    solutions, and then Lu = f is solvable iff f ⊥ ker L* (finite-
    dimensional). The Dirichlet Laplacian has eigenvalues 0 < λ₁ < λ₂ ≤
    ... → ∞ with an L²-orthonormal basis of eigenfunctions; λ₁ = min ∫|∇u|²
    /∫u² (RAYLEIGH), simple, eigenfunction of one sign. On (0, L): λₖ =
    (kπ/L)²; on the square (0, π)²: m² + n².
  - ELLIPTIC REGULARITY: interior: aᵢⱼ ∈ C¹, f ∈ L² ⇒ u ∈ H²_loc with
    ‖u‖_{H²(V)} ≤ C(‖f‖_{L²} + ‖u‖_{L²}) (difference quotients); f ∈ Hᵏ,
    aᵢⱼ ∈ C^{k+1} ⇒ u ∈ H^{k+2}_loc; smooth data ⇒ u ∈ C^∞. Up to the
    boundary if ∂Ω ∈ C²: u ∈ H² ∩ H₀¹, ‖u‖_{H²} ≤ C‖f‖_{L²}. Other scales:
    SCHAUDER f ∈ C^{0,α} ⇒ u ∈ C^{2,α}; CALDERÓN-ZYGMUND f ∈ Lᵖ ⇒ u ∈
    W^{2,p} (1 < p < ∞). FAILURES: f ∈ C⁰ does not give u ∈ C²; at a
    corner of angle ω the solution behaves like r^{π/ω} sin(πθ/ω), not in
    H² when ω > π (L-shaped domain: r^{2/3}). WEYL: weakly harmonic ⇒
    smooth.
  - MAXIMUM PRINCIPLES: weak: Lu ≤ 0 (no zero-order term) in Ω ⇒ max_Ω̄ u
    = max_∂Ω u; strong: an interior maximum forces u constant (Ω
    connected); HOPF boundary lemma. Harmonic functions satisfy the MEAN
    VALUE property. Comparison: −Δu = f with 0 ≤ f ≤ M sandwiches u.
    Classification of au_xx + 2bu_xy + cu_yy: b² − ac < 0 ELLIPTIC, = 0
    PARABOLIC, > 0 HYPERBOLIC.
  - VARIATIONAL METHODS: a minimiser of E(u) = ∫F(x, u, ∇u) satisfies the
    EULER-LAGRANGE equation −div(F_p) + F_u = 0 weakly (Dirichlet
    principle: ∫½|∇u|² − fu ↦ −Δu = f; p-Laplacian; minimal surface).
    DIRECT METHOD: coercive (E(u) ≥ α‖u‖^q − β) + weakly lower semi-
    continuous (F convex in ∇u) on a reflexive space ⇒ minimiser exists.
    Failures: non-convex integrands (Bolza ∫((u′)² − 1)² + u²: inf 0 not
    attained) and non-coercive ones (Weierstrass ∫x²(u′)²). Critical
    points that are not minima: MOUNTAIN PASS (Palais-Smale) for −Δu =
    u³ in bounded Ω ⊂ R³ (subcritical: 3 < 2* − 1 = 5).
  - PARABOLIC (HEAT) EQUATION: u_t − Δu = f. On Rⁿ, u = Φ(·, t) * g with
    HEAT KERNEL Φ(x, t) = (4πt)^(−n/2) e^(−|x|²/(4t)), ∫Φ = 1: infinite
    speed of propagation, instant C^∞ SMOOTHING, maximum principle on the
    parabolic boundary. On a bounded domain: u = ∑ gₖ e^(−λₖt) wₖ and
    ENERGY ESTIMATE d/dt ½‖u‖² = −‖∇u‖² ≤ −λ₁‖u‖², so ‖u(t)‖ ≤ e^(−λ₁t)
    ‖g‖. WEAK SOLUTION: u ∈ L²(0, T; H₀¹), u′ ∈ L²(0, T; H⁻¹) (hence u ∈
    C([0, T]; L²)), ⟨u′, v⟩ + B[u, v] = (f, v); built by GALERKIN
    approximation + energy estimates + weak compactness. The BACKWARD heat
    equation is ILL-POSED (Hadamard).
  - HYPERBOLIC (WAVE) EQUATION: u_tt − c²Δu = 0. D'ALEMBERT (n = 1): u =
    ½(g(x + ct) + g(x − ct)) + (1/(2c))∫_{x−ct}^{x+ct} h; KIRCHHOFF (n = 3),
    POISSON (n = 2, by descent). ENERGY E(t) = ½∫(u_t² + c²|∇u|²) is
    CONSERVED; FINITE SPEED: u(x₀, t₀) depends only on data in B(x₀, ct₀);
    HUYGENS' principle (sharp fronts) in odd n ≥ 3, not in n = 2. No
    smoothing: singularities travel along characteristics x ± ct = const.
    Weak solution: u ∈ L²(0, T; H₀¹), u′ ∈ L²(0, T; L²), u″ ∈ L²(0, T;
    H⁻¹), again by Galerkin.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. (a) Show that u(x) = |x| has weak derivative sgn x on (−1, 1).
    (b) Show that the Heaviside function H = 𝟙_(0,1) on (−1, 1) has no
    weak derivative. (c) For which p is u(x) = |x|^{1/2} in W^{1,p}(−1, 1)?
    Answer: (a) integrate by parts on each half; (b) the derivative would
    have to be δ; (c) 1 ≤ p < 2.
    Teacher explains: (a) For φ ∈ C_c^∞(−1, 1), split the integral:
       ∫₋₁¹ |x|φ′ dx = ∫₋₁⁰ (−x)φ′ + ∫₀¹ xφ′
                     = [−xφ]₋₁⁰ + ∫₋₁⁰ φ + [xφ]₀¹ − ∫₀¹ φ
                     = −∫₋₁¹ sgn(x) φ dx,
    because the boundary terms vanish (φ(±1) = 0 and x = 0 at the joint).
    That is exactly the definition ∫uφ′ = −∫vφ with v = sgn x ∈ L^∞, so
    |x| ∈ W^{1,p}(−1, 1) for every p ≤ ∞. (b) Suppose v ∈ L¹_loc with
    ∫Hφ′ = −∫vφ. The left side is ∫₀¹ φ′ = −φ(0). So ∫vφ = φ(0) for all
    φ. Taking φ supported in (0, 1) or in (−1, 0) gives v = 0 a.e. there,
    hence ∫vφ = 0 for every φ, contradicting φ(0) ≠ 0. The distributional
    derivative δ is not a function. (c) The same splitting shows the weak
    derivative is the classical one, v = ½ sgn(x)|x|^{−1/2} (the boundary
    term |x|^{1/2}φ vanishes at 0). ∫|v|ᵖ = 2·2⁻ᵖ∫₀¹ x^{−p/2} dx < ∞ iff
    p/2 < 1. So u ∈ W^{1,p} iff p < 2, although u is continuous. Common
    mistakes: thinking "differentiable a.e." suffices (H′ = 0 a.e., but H
    is not weakly differentiable; the Cantor function is the same trap);
    forgetting to check the boundary term at the singular point. Now you
    try: the weak derivative of max(x, 0) on (−1, 1). (H = 𝟙_(0,1))

D2. Let B be the unit ball in Rⁿ and u(x) = |x|^(−a), a > 0. Prove that u
    ∈ W^{1,p}(B) iff (a + 1)p < n. Decide whether |x|^(−1/2) ∈ H¹(B) for
    n = 3, and show that w = ln ln(4/|x|) ∈ H¹(B) for n = 2 although w is
    unbounded.
    Answer: need ∫_B |x|^(−(a+1)p) < ∞; n = 3, a = ½: (3/2)·2 = 3 ⊀ 3, so
    NOT in H¹; ∫|∇w|² = 2π∫₀¹ dr/(r ln²(4/r)) < ∞.
    Teacher explains: Away from 0, u is smooth with ∇u = −a|x|^(−a−2)x, so
    |∇u| = a|x|^(−a−1). In polar coordinates ∫_B |x|^(−β) dx = ωₙ₋₁∫₀¹
    r^(n−1−β) dr is finite iff β < n. Hence |∇u| ∈ Lᵖ iff (a + 1)p < n,
    and u ∈ Lᵖ iff ap < n (weaker). It remains to show the classical
    gradient is the WEAK one. Cut out B_ε: for φ ∈ C_c^∞(B),
       ∫_{B∖B_ε} u ∂ᵢφ = −∫_{B∖B_ε} ∂ᵢu φ − ∫_{∂B_ε} uφνᵢ dS,
    and |∫_{∂B_ε} uφνᵢ| ≤ ‖φ‖_∞ ε^(−a)·ωₙ₋₁εⁿ⁻¹ → 0 since a < n − 1
    (implied by (a + 1)p < n). Let ε → 0 (DCT, both integrands L¹). For n
    = 3, a = ½, p = 2: (a + 1)p = 3 = n, borderline, so |∇u|² ~ r⁻³ and
    ∫r⁻³r² dr = ∞: NOT in H¹. (|x|^(−1/4) is: 2.5 < 3.) For n = 2: ∇w =
    −x/(|x|² ln(4/|x|)), so |∇w| = 1/(r ln(4/r)) and
       ∫_B |∇w|² = 2π∫₀¹ dr/(r ln²(4/r)) = 2π[1/ln(4/r)]₀¹ = 2π/ln 4,
    finite, and ∫w² < ∞ too; yet w(x) → ∞ as x → 0. So H¹ ⊄ L^∞ in R²:
    the Morrey case needs p > n strictly. Common mistakes: forgetting the
    boundary term (for a ≥ n − 1 it does not vanish); confusing the
    conditions for u and ∇u. Now you try: for which a is |x|^(−a) ∈ H¹ of
    the unit ball in R⁵? (a < 3/2)

D3. (a) Use scaling to show that an inequality ‖u‖_{L^q(Rⁿ)} ≤ C‖∇u‖_{Lᵖ}
    for all u ∈ C_c^∞(Rⁿ) can only hold if q = p* = np/(n − p). (b) State
    the Sobolev, Morrey and Rellich-Kondrachov theorems and apply them to
    H¹ and H² in dimension n = 3.
    Answer: (a) scaling gives λ^{−n/q} = λ^{1−n/p}; (b) H¹(Ω) ⊂ L⁶,
    compactly into L^q for q < 6; H² ⊂ C^{0,1/2}.
    Teacher explains: (a) Put u_λ(x) = u(λx). Then ‖u_λ‖_q = λ^(−n/q)‖u‖_q
    and ∇u_λ = λ(∇u)(λx), so ‖∇u_λ‖ₚ = λ^(1−n/p)‖∇u‖ₚ. The inequality for
    u_λ reads λ^(−n/q)‖u‖_q ≤ Cλ^(1−n/p)‖∇u‖ₚ for all λ > 0; letting λ → 0
    or ∞ gives a contradiction unless −n/q = 1 − n/p, i.e. 1/q = 1/p −
    1/n, q = np/(n − p). (b) GNS: for 1 ≤ p < n, ‖u‖_{p*} ≤ C‖∇u‖ₚ on
    W^{1,p}(Rⁿ); on bounded Lipschitz Ω, W^{1,p}(Ω) ⊂ L^q for q ≤ p*.
    MORREY: p > n ⇒ |u(x) − u(y)| ≤ C|x − y|^{1−n/p}‖∇u‖ₚ, so W^{1,p} ⊂
    C^{0,1−n/p}. RELLICH-KONDRACHOV: bounded sequences in W^{1,p}(Ω) have
    subsequences converging in L^q for q < p* (Ω bounded Lipschitz). In n
    = 3, p = 2: 2* = 6/1 = 6, so H¹(Ω) ⊂ L⁶(Ω), compact into L², L⁴,
    L^{5.9} but NOT into L⁶ (concentrating bubbles u(λx) rescaled keep the
    L⁶ norm). For H²: ∇u ∈ H¹ ⊂ L⁶, so u ∈ W^{1,6}, and 6 > 3 gives
    Morrey exponent 1 − 3/6 = ½: H²(Ω) ⊂ C^{0,1/2}(Ω̄). Common mistakes:
    applying GNS when p ≥ n (p* is then negative or infinite); claiming
    compactness at the critical exponent or on unbounded domains. Now you
    try: W^{2,2}(R⁵) ⊂ L^q for which q? (1/q = 1/2 − 2/5, q = 10)

D4. Prove the Poincaré inequality ∫₀ᴸ u² ≤ (L/π)²∫₀ᴸ (u′)² for u ∈
    H₀¹(0, L), show the constant is sharp, and check it on u = x(1 − x)
    with L = 1. Why is there no such inequality on H¹(0, L)?
    Answer: expand in sine series; equality for sin(πx/L); 1/30 ≤ (1/π²)
    (1/3); constants violate it on H¹.
    Teacher explains: The functions sₖ = √(2/L) sin(kπx/L), k ≥ 1, form an
    orthonormal basis of L²(0, L), and so do the cosines √(2/L)cos(kπx/L)
    (with 1/√L) — they are used for u′. For u ∈ H₀¹ write u = ∑aₖsₖ.
    Integration by parts (valid because u(0) = u(L) = 0) gives the cosine
    coefficients of u′ as (kπ/L)aₖ, so by Parseval
       ∫(u′)² = ∑(kπ/L)²aₖ² ≥ (π/L)²∑aₖ² = (π/L)²∫u².
    Equality iff aₖ = 0 for k ≥ 2, i.e. u = c sin(πx/L): sharp. This is
    the statement λ₁ = (π/L)² for −u″ = λu, u(0) = u(L) = 0. Check: u =
    x(1 − x): ∫u² = ∫(x² − 2x³ + x⁴) = 1/3 − 1/2 + 1/5 = 1/30, ∫(u′)² =
    ∫(1 − 2x)² = 1/3, and 1/30 ≈ 0.0333 ≤ (1/π²)(1/3) ≈ 0.0338 ✓ (very
    close, since x(1 − x) is nearly a multiple of sin πx). On H¹(0, L)
    the constant u = 1 has u′ = 0 but ∫u² = L > 0, so no inequality; the
    right replacement is Poincaré-Wirtinger ∫(u − ū)² ≤ (L/π)²∫(u′)²
    (Neumann λ₁ = (π/L)²). Common mistakes: using the inequality without
    zero boundary values; getting the constant as L/(2π) or L². Now you
    try: the best constant C in ‖u‖₂ ≤ C‖u′‖₂ on H₀¹(0, 3). (3/π)

D5. State the Lax-Milgram theorem and use it to prove that −u″ + u = f,
    u(0) = u(1) = 0 has a unique weak solution u ∈ H₀¹(0, 1) for every f ∈
    L²(0, 1) (indeed every f ∈ H⁻¹). Solve it explicitly for f = 1 and
    compute u(½).
    Answer: B[u, v] = ∫(u′v′ + uv) is the H¹ inner product;
    u = 1 − cosh(x − ½)/cosh(½), u(½) = 1 − sech(½) ≈ 0.11318.
    Teacher explains: WEAK FORM: multiply by v ∈ C_c^∞ (or H₀¹) and
    integrate the u″ term by parts; the boundary term u′v vanishes because
    v(0) = v(1) = 0:
       B[u, v] := ∫₀¹ (u′v′ + uv) dx = ∫₀¹ fv dx =: F(v)   ∀v ∈ H₀¹.
    Conversely a smooth weak solution is a classical one (integrate back).
    LAX-MILGRAM: H Hilbert, B bilinear with |B[u, v]| ≤ M‖u‖‖v‖ and
    B[u, u] ≥ α‖u‖², F ∈ H′ ⇒ there is exactly one u with B[u, v] = F(v)
    for all v, and ‖u‖ ≤ ‖F‖/α. Here H = H₀¹ with the H¹ norm, B is the
    inner product itself, so M = α = 1, and |F(v)| ≤ ‖f‖₂‖v‖₂ ≤ ‖f‖₂‖v‖_{H¹}
    by Cauchy-Schwarz. So there is a unique weak solution with ‖u‖_{H¹} ≤
    ‖f‖₂. (Since B is symmetric, this is just the Riesz representation
    theorem; Lax-Milgram is needed when a first-order term makes B
    non-symmetric.) For f = 1: the general solution of u″ = u − 1 is u = 1
    + A cosh(x − ½) + C sinh(x − ½). Symmetry about ½ forces C = 0, and u(0)
    = 0 gives A = −1/cosh(½). So
       u(x) = 1 − cosh(x − ½)/cosh(½),  u(½) = 1 − 1/cosh(½) ≈ 0.11318.
    Common mistakes: putting the boundary condition into F instead of into
    the space; forgetting that coercivity must be in the FULL H¹ norm (here
    automatic; for −u″ alone it needs Poincaré). Now you try: the weak
    solution of −u″ = 1, u(0) = u(1) = 0, and its value at ½.
    (x(1 − x)/2; 1/8)

D6. Let Lu = −u″ + bu′ + cu on (0, 1) with constants b, c and Dirichlet
    conditions. (a) For which c is the bilinear form coercive on H₀¹?
    (b) Prove the Gårding inequality for Lu = −∂ᵢ(aᵢⱼ∂ⱼu) + bᵢ∂ᵢu + cu.
    (c) Use the Fredholm alternative to decide when −u″ − π²u = f,
    u(0) = u(1) = 0, is solvable; solve it for f = sin 2πx.
    Answer: (a) c > −π² (any b); (b) B[u, u] ≥ (θ/2)‖∇u‖² − γ‖u‖²;
    (c) iff ∫₀¹ f sin πx = 0; u = sin(2πx)/(3π²) + C sin πx.
    Teacher explains: (a) B[u, v] = ∫(u′v′ + bu′v + cuv). For v = u the
    middle term is b∫u′u = (b/2)[u²]₀¹ = 0. So B[u, u] = ∫(u′)² + c∫u².
    If c ≥ 0 this is ≥ ∫(u′)². If −π² < c < 0, Poincaré ∫u² ≤ π⁻²∫(u′)²
    gives B[u, u] ≥ (1 + c/π²)∫(u′)², and ∫(u′)² controls the H¹ norm
    (again by Poincaré). If c ≤ −π², take u = sin πx: B[u, u] = (π² + c)/2
    ≤ 0, not coercive. (b) Ellipticity gives ∫aᵢⱼ∂ⱼu∂ᵢu ≥ θ‖∇u‖². The
    lower-order terms: |∫bᵢ∂ᵢu u| ≤ ‖b‖_∞‖∇u‖‖u‖ ≤ (θ/2)‖∇u‖² +
    (‖b‖²_∞/(2θ))‖u‖² (Young), and |∫cu²| ≤ ‖c‖_∞‖u‖². So
       B[u, u] ≥ (θ/2)‖∇u‖² − γ‖u‖²,   γ = ‖b‖²_∞/(2θ) + ‖c‖_∞,
    and B + γ(·, ·) is coercive: L + γ is uniquely solvable (Lax-Milgram).
    (c) Write L = L₀ − γ with L₀ invertible; L₀⁻¹ : L² → H₀¹ ⊂ L² is
    COMPACT (Rellich), so the Fredholm alternative for I − γL₀⁻¹ applies.
    Here ker L = span{sin πx} (π² is the first eigenvalue) and L is
    symmetric, so −u″ − π²u = f is solvable iff ∫₀¹ f(x) sin πx dx = 0.
    For f = sin 2πx the condition holds (orthogonality). Try u = A sin 2πx:
    −u″ − π²u = (4π² − π²)A sin 2πx, so A = 1/(3π²), plus any C sin πx.
    For f = 1: ∫ sin πx = 2/π ≠ 0, NO solution (resonance). Common
    mistakes: thinking uniqueness fails ⇒ existence fails for every f;
    forgetting that the kernel adds a free constant C. Now you try: is
    −u″ − 4π²u = 1, u(0) = u(1) = 0 solvable? (yes: ∫₀¹ sin 2πx = 0)

D7. (a) For u ∈ C_c^∞(Rⁿ) prove ∫|D²u|² = ∫(Δu)², and explain how the
    difference-quotient method turns this into interior H² regularity for
    weak solutions of −Δu = f ∈ L². (b) On the L-shaped (re-entrant
    corner) domain show that u = r^{2/3} sin(2θ/3) is harmonic, vanishes
    on the edges θ = 0 and θ = 3π/2, lies in H¹ near 0 but not in H².
    Answer: (a) integrate by parts twice; (b) |D²u| ~ r^(−4/3) and
    ∫r^(−8/3) r dr = ∞.
    Teacher explains: (a) ∫(Δu)² = ∑ᵢ,ⱼ∫∂ᵢᵢu ∂ⱼⱼu. Move one ∂ᵢ from ∂ᵢᵢu
    onto ∂ⱼⱼu and then one ∂ⱼ back (no boundary terms, compact support):
       ∫∂ᵢᵢu ∂ⱼⱼu = −∫∂ᵢu ∂ᵢⱼⱼu = ∫∂ᵢⱼu ∂ᵢⱼu.
    Summing: ∫(Δu)² = ∑∫(∂ᵢⱼu)² = ∫|D²u|². So ALL second derivatives are
    controlled by the Laplacian alone. For a weak solution we cannot
    differentiate twice, so we use DIFFERENCE QUOTIENTS Dₖʰu(x) = (u(x +
    heₖ) − u(x))/h. Test the weak equation with v = −Dₖ⁻ʰ(ζ²Dₖʰu), ζ a
    cut-off equal to 1 on V ⊂⊂ Ω; after "discrete integration by parts"
    one gets ∫ζ²|∇Dₖʰu|² ≤ C(‖f‖² + ‖u‖²_{H¹}) uniformly in h. A uniform
    bound on ‖Dₖʰ∇u‖_{L²(V)} implies ∂ₖ∇u ∈ L²(V) (weak compactness), so u
    ∈ H²_loc with ‖u‖_{H²(V)} ≤ C(‖f‖₂ + ‖u‖₂). Iterating gives f ∈ Hᵏ ⇒
    u ∈ H^{k+2}_loc, and f ∈ C^∞ ⇒ u ∈ C^∞ (Sobolev embedding). (b) In
    polar coordinates Δ = ∂ᵣ² + r⁻¹∂ᵣ + r⁻²∂_θ², and for rᵅ sin(αθ):
    (α(α − 1) + α − α²)r^{α−2} sin αθ = 0 ✓. With α = 2/3, sin(2θ/3) = 0 at
    θ = 0 and θ = 3π/2 ✓. |∇u| ~ r^(−1/3): ∫r^(−2/3) r dr < ∞, so u ∈ H¹.
    |D²u| ~ r^(−4/3): ∫r^(−8/3) r dr = ∫r^(−5/3) dr = ∞, so u ∉ H². Thus
    −Δ(ζu) = f ∈ L² (ζ a cut-off; f vanishes near 0) with u ∉ H²: global H²
    regularity FAILS without a smooth (or convex) boundary. In general rᵅ,
    α = π/ω, is in H² iff α > 1 iff ω < π (convex corners are fine).
    Common mistakes: claiming the interior argument works up to any
    boundary; thinking f ∈ C⁰ gives u ∈ C² (false even in the interior: u
    = (x² − y²)√(−ln r) has continuous Δu but u_xx → ∞). Now you try: for
    the slit domain ω = 2π, which Hˢ contains r^{1/2} sin(θ/2) near 0?
    (s < 3/2)

D8. (a) Show that a minimiser of the Rayleigh quotient R(u) = ∫|∇u|²/∫u²
    over H₀¹(Ω) ∖ {0} is a weak eigenfunction −Δu = λ₁u, and that it can be
    taken non-negative. (b) Find all Dirichlet eigenvalues of (0, π)² and
    the multiplicity of 50. (c) Bound λ₁ of the unit square with the trial
    function u = xy(1 − x)(1 − y).
    Answer: (a) differentiate R(u + εv) at ε = 0; |u| is also a minimiser;
    (b) m² + n², m, n ≥ 1; 50 has multiplicity 3; (c) λ₁ ≤ 20 (exact
    2π² ≈ 19.739).
    Teacher explains: (a) The infimum λ₁ is attained: normalise ∫u² = 1,
    a minimising sequence is bounded in H₀¹, so a subsequence converges
    weakly in H₀¹ and STRONGLY in L² (Rellich), ∫u² = 1 survives, and ∫|∇u|²
    is weakly lower semicontinuous. At the minimiser, for each v the
    function ε ↦ R(u + εv) has a minimum at ε = 0, so
       0 = d/dε R = (2∫∇u·∇v − 2λ₁∫uv)/∫u²,
    i.e. ∫∇u·∇v = λ₁∫uv ∀v: the weak form of −Δu = λ₁u. Since |∇|u|| =
    |∇u| a.e., R(|u|) = R(u), so |u| is a minimiser too, hence an
    eigenfunction, and by the strong maximum principle (Harnack) |u| > 0
    in Ω: the first eigenfunction does not change sign and λ₁ is simple.
    (b) Separate: sin mx sin ny gives λ = m² + n². 50 = 1 + 49 = 49 + 1 =
    25 + 25: (m, n) = (1, 7), (7, 1), (5, 5): multiplicity 3. The first
    ones: 2, 5 (×2), 8, 10 (×2), 13 (×2). (c) u′ factors: ∫₀¹(1 − 2x)² dx =
    1/3 and ∫₀¹ x²(1 − x)² dx = 1/30. So ∫|∇u|² = 2·(1/3)(1/30) = 1/45,
    ∫u² = (1/30)² = 1/900, and R(u) = 900/45 = 20 ≥ λ₁ = 2π² ≈ 19.739 ✓:
    any trial function gives an UPPER bound. Common mistakes: using trial
    functions that do not vanish on the boundary (they are not in H₀¹ and
    the bound is wrong); forgetting the multiplicity from swapping m, n.
    Now you try: the Rayleigh quotient of 1 − r² on the unit disc, and
    compare with λ₁ = j₀,₁² ≈ 5.7832. (6)

D9. (a) Prove by the DIRECT METHOD that E(u) = ∫_Ω (½|∇u|² − fu) has a
    minimiser on H₀¹(Ω) (Ω bounded, f ∈ L²), and that it is the weak
    solution of −Δu = f. Compute min E for Ω = (0, 1), f = 1. (b) Show
    that the Bolza functional J(u) = ∫₀¹ (((u′)² − 1)² + u²) dx on
    W₀^{1,4}(0, 1) has infimum 0 but no minimiser. Which hypothesis of the
    direct method fails?
    Answer: (a) coercive + weakly l.s.c. + reflexive; min E = −1/24.
    (b) sawtooth functions with slopes ±1; the integrand is not convex in
    u′.
    Teacher explains: (a) THREE STEPS. (1) Coercive: by Cauchy-Schwarz and
    Poincaré (‖u‖₂ ≤ C_P‖∇u‖₂), ∫fu ≤ ‖f‖C_P‖∇u‖ ≤ ¼‖∇u‖² + C_P²‖f‖², so
    E(u) ≥ ¼‖∇u‖² − C_P²‖f‖²: E is bounded below and minimising sequences
    are bounded in H₀¹. (2) Compactness: H₀¹ is a Hilbert space, so a
    bounded sequence has a weakly convergent subsequence uₖ ⇀ u. (3) Lower
    semicontinuity: ‖∇u‖² ≤ lim inf ‖∇uₖ‖² (norms are weakly l.s.c.; more
    generally ∫F(∇u) is weakly l.s.c. when F is CONVEX), and u ↦ ∫fu is a
    continuous linear functional, so E(u) ≤ lim inf E(uₖ) = inf E: u is a
    minimiser. EULER-LAGRANGE: 0 = d/dε E(u + εv)|₀ = ∫∇u·∇v − ∫fv for
    all v, the weak form of −Δu = f (the Dirichlet principle). Strict
    convexity gives uniqueness. For (0, 1), f = 1: u = x(1 − x)/2, ∫(u′)²
    = ∫(½ − x)² = 1/12, ∫u = 1/12, so min E = 1/24 − 1/12 = −1/24. (Using
    the equation, min E = −½∫fu in general.) (b) J ≥ 0. Let uₖ be the
    sawtooth with slopes ±1 and 2k teeth of height 1/(2k): then (u′ₖ)² = 1
    a.e. and |uₖ| ≤ 1/(2k), so J(uₖ) ≤ 1/(4k²) → 0: inf J = 0. If J(u) =
    0 then u = 0 AND |u′| = 1 a.e., impossible. The minimising sequence is
    bounded in W^{1,4} and uₖ ⇀ 0, but J(0) = 1 > 0 = lim J(uₖ): J is
    NOT weakly l.s.c., because p ↦ (p² − 1)² is not convex (its convex
    envelope is ((p² − 1)₊)², whose relaxed problem has minimiser 0).
    Common mistakes: forgetting to check coercivity (Weierstrass: ∫₋₁¹
    x²(u′)² with u(±1) = ±1 has inf 0, not attained); assuming weak limits
    preserve nonlinear quantities. Now you try: minimise ∫₀¹ ((u′)² + u²)
    with u(0) = 0, u(1) = 1. (u = sinh x/sinh 1, min = coth 1 ≈ 1.31304)

D10. Solve u_t = u_xx on (0, π), u(0, t) = u(π, t) = 0, u(x, 0) = x(π − x)
     by separation of variables and compute u(π/2, 1). Prove the energy
     estimate ‖u(t)‖₂ ≤ e^(−t)‖u(0)‖₂ and deduce uniqueness. What is the
     weak formulation, and why is the backward problem ill-posed?
     Answer: u = ∑_{k odd} (8/(πk³)) e^(−k²t) sin kx; u(π/2, 1) ≈ 0.93679.
     Teacher explains: Separation u = X(x)T(t) gives −X″ = λX, X(0) =
     X(π) = 0, so X = sin kx, λ = k², and T = e^(−k²t). Expand the data:
        bₖ = (2/π)∫₀^π x(π − x) sin kx dx = 4(1 − (−1)ᵏ)/(πk³),
     i.e. 8/(πk³) for k odd and 0 for k even. So u(x, t) = ∑_{k odd}
     8/(πk³) e^(−k²t) sin kx. At (π/2, 1): (8/π)(e⁻¹ − e⁻⁹/27 + e⁻²⁵/125 −
     ...) ≈ 0.93680 − 0.00001 ≈ 0.93679. The factors e^(−k²t) kill high
     modes: u(·, t) is C^∞ (even analytic) for every t > 0 — SMOOTHING.
     ENERGY: multiply by u and integrate: d/dt ½∫u² = ∫u u_xx = −∫u_x²
     (boundary terms vanish) ≤ −∫u² by Poincaré on (0, π) (λ₁ = 1). So
     d/dt ‖u‖² ≤ −2‖u‖², ‖u(t)‖ ≤ e^(−t)‖u(0)‖. If u, w solve the same
     problem, u − w has zero data, so ‖u − w‖ ≡ 0: uniqueness. WEAK FORM:
     u ∈ L²(0, T; H₀¹), u′ ∈ L²(0, T; H⁻¹), ⟨u′, v⟩ + ∫u_x v_x = ⟨f, v⟩ for
     all v ∈ H₀¹ and a.e. t, u(0) = g; Galerkin approximations uₘ =
     ∑_{k≤m} dₖ(t) sin kx satisfy the same energy estimates uniformly in m,
     and a weak limit is the solution. BACKWARD: for u_t = −u_xx take
     uₙ = n⁻¹ e^(n²t) sin nx: data ‖uₙ(0)‖_∞ = 1/n → 0 but at t = 1 the
     amplitude eⁿ²/n → ∞: no continuous dependence (Hadamard). Common
     mistakes: using the Neumann eigenfunctions for Dirichlet data;
     dropping the sign in ∫u u_xx = −∫u_x². Now you try: u_t = u_xx on
     (0, π) with Neumann conditions and u(x, 0) = cos²x. (u = ½ +
     ½e^(−4t)cos 2x → ½)

D11. (a) Derive d'Alembert's formula for u_tt = c²u_xx on R and compute
     u(1, 2) for c = 1, u(x, 0) = 1/(1 + x²), u_t(x, 0) = cos x.
     (b) Prove conservation of energy and FINITE SPEED OF PROPAGATION:
     if the data vanish on [x₀ − t₀, x₀ + t₀] then u(x₀, t₀) = 0 (c = 1).
     (c) Contrast with the heat equation.
     Answer: (a) u(1, 2) = 0.3 + cos 1 sin 2 ≈ 0.79129; (b) the energy in
     the backward cone is non-increasing.
     Teacher explains: (a) In characteristic variables ξ = x + ct, η =
     x − ct the equation becomes u_ξη = 0, so u = F(x + ct) + G(x − ct).
     Fitting u(x, 0) = g, u_t(x, 0) = h gives
        u(x, t) = ½(g(x + ct) + g(x − ct)) + (1/(2c))∫_{x−ct}^{x+ct} h(s) ds.
     Here g-part: ½(g(3) + g(−1)) = ½(1/10 + 1/2) = 0.3; h-part: ½(sin 3 −
     sin(−1)) = ½(sin 3 + sin 1) = cos 1 sin 2 ≈ 0.49129. Total ≈ 0.79129.
     (b) E(t) = ½∫(u_t² + u_x²) dx: E′ = ∫(u_t u_tt + u_x u_xt) = ∫(u_t u_xx
     + u_x u_xt) = ∫(u_x u_t)_x dx = 0 for decaying (or Dirichlet/Neumann)
     solutions. For the CONE, let e(t) = ½∫_{x₀−(t₀−t)}^{x₀+(t₀−t)} (u_t² +
     u_x²) dx. Differentiating (Leibniz rule; the interval shrinks at speed
     1 at both ends)
        e′(t) = [u_x u_t]_{ends} − ½(u_t² + u_x²)|_{right} − ½(u_t² +
                u_x²)|_{left} ≤ 0,
     since |u_x u_t| ≤ ½(u_t² + u_x²). As e(0) = 0 and e ≥ 0, e ≡ 0, so u
     is constant in the cone, and it is 0 at t = 0: u(x₀, t₀) = 0. The same
     argument in Rⁿ shows u(x₀, t₀) depends only on data in B(x₀, ct₀).
     (c) The heat kernel is positive everywhere, so heat data supported in
     [−1, 1] give u > 0 at every x for every t > 0: INFINITE speed, but
     smoothing. The wave equation has FINITE speed but NO smoothing: a kink
     in g travels along x ± ct forever. In R³ (Kirchhoff) u(x, t) depends
     only on data on the SPHERE |y − x| = ct (Huygens), in R² on the whole
     disc (waves leave a "tail"). Common mistakes: writing 1/c instead of
     1/(2c) in front of the integral; forgetting the boundary terms in e′.
     Now you try: c = 1, g = 0, h = 𝟙_[−1,1]: u(0, 5) and u(3, 1). (1 and 0)

D12. For u ∈ L²(Rⁿ) with û(ξ) = ∫e^(−ix·ξ)u(x) dx define ‖u‖²_{Hˢ} =
     (2π)⁻ⁿ∫(1 + |ξ|²)ˢ|û|² dξ. (a) Check ‖u‖²_{H¹} = ∫(u² + |∇u|²) and
     compute it for u = e^(−|x|) on R both ways. (b) Prove Hˢ(Rⁿ) ⊂
     C_b(Rⁿ) for s > n/2. (c) For which s are δ, 𝟙_[−1,1] and e^(−|x|) in
     Hˢ(R)?
     Answer: (a) 2; (b) û ∈ L¹ by Cauchy-Schwarz; (c) s < −1/2, s < 1/2,
     s < 3/2.
     Teacher explains: (a) ∂ⱼu has transform iξⱼû, so by Plancherel
     (2π)⁻ⁿ∫(1 + |ξ|²)|û|² = ‖u‖² + ‖∇u‖². For u = e^(−|x|): u′² = u², and
     ∫e^(−2|x|) = 1, so ‖u‖²_{H¹} = 1 + 1 = 2. In Fourier: û = 2/(1 + ξ²),
     so (1/2π)∫(1 + ξ²)·4/(1 + ξ²)² dξ = (2/π)∫dξ/(1 + ξ²) = (2/π)π = 2 ✓.
     (b) By Cauchy-Schwarz,
        ∫|û| = ∫(1 + |ξ|²)^{−s/2}·(1 + |ξ|²)^{s/2}|û|
             ≤ (∫(1 + |ξ|²)^{−s} dξ)^{1/2}·(2π)^{n/2}‖u‖_{Hˢ},
     and ∫(1 + |ξ|²)^{−s} dξ < ∞ iff 2s > n. So û ∈ L¹, and u(x) =
     (2π)⁻ⁿ∫e^{ix·ξ}û(ξ) dξ is continuous (DCT), bounded by C‖u‖_{Hˢ}, and
     → 0 at ∞ (Riemann-Lebesgue). (c) The rule: if |û| ~ |ξ|^(−β) at ∞
     then u ∈ Hˢ(R) iff 2s − 2β < −1, i.e. s < β − ½. δ̂ = 1 (β = 0): s <
     −½. (𝟙_[−1,1])^ = 2 sin ξ/ξ (β = 1): s < ½ — a jump costs half a
     derivative. (e^(−|x|))^ = 2/(1 + ξ²) (β = 2): s < 3/2; the kink costs
     another half. In Rⁿ, δ ∈ Hˢ iff s < −n/2, so δ ∈ H⁻¹(R) (point loads
     on a string are fine) but δ ∉ H⁻¹(R²) (a point load on a membrane
     has infinite energy: the solution is ln r ∉ H¹). Common mistakes:
     forgetting the (2π)⁻ⁿ normalisation; treating s = n/2 as enough (ln
     ln(4/|x|) ∈ H¹(R²) is unbounded). Now you try: for which s is the
     hat function (1 − |x|)₊ in Hˢ(R)? (û = 4 sin²(ξ/2)/ξ², s < 3/2)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Find the weak derivative (or gradient) on the given domain, or say
    "none" if it does not exist in L¹_loc.
    a) |x| on (−1, 1)                  b) x|x| on (−1, 1)
    c) max(x, 0) on (−1, 1)            d) H = 𝟙_(0,1) on (−1, 1)
    e) sgn x on (−1, 1)                f) |x|^{1/2} on (−1, 1)
    g) min(1, |x|) on (−2, 2)          h) |x₁| on the unit disc in R²
    i) |x| (Euclidean norm) on Rⁿ      j) ln|x| on (−1, 1)
    k) ln|x| on the unit disc in R²    l) x₁/|x| on the unit disc in R²
    m) the Cantor function on (0, 1)   n) x² sin(1/x) on (−1, 1)
    o) x sin(1/x) on (−1, 1)           p) distributional derivative of H
    q) second distributional derivative of |x| on (−1, 1)
    r) (1 − |x|)₊ on R: first and second distributional derivatives
    Answers:
      a) sgn x          b) 2|x|           c) H = 𝟙_(0,1)       d) none (δ)
      e) none (2δ)      f) ½ sgn(x)|x|^(−1/2) (∈ Lᵖ for p < 2)
      g) sgn(x)𝟙_{|x|<1}                  h) (sgn x₁, 0)
      i) x/|x|          j) none (1/x ∉ L¹_loc; distributionally p.v. 1/x)
      k) x/|x|² (∈ Lᵖ for p < 2)          l) (x₂², −x₁x₂)/|x|³
      m) none (not absolutely continuous; classical derivative 0 a.e.)
      n) 2x sin(1/x) − cos(1/x) (value 0 at 0): bounded, u ∈ W^{1,∞}
      o) none (infinite variation near 0)  p) δ
      q) 2δ             r) −sgn(x)𝟙_{|x|<1}; δ₋₁ − 2δ₀ + δ₁

M2. Membership: is u in the given space? (B = unit ball of Rⁿ.)
    a) |x|^(−1/2) ∈ H¹(B), n = 3        b) |x|^(−1/4) ∈ H¹(B), n = 3
    c) |x|⁻¹ ∈ W^{1,1}(B), n = 3         d) |x|⁻¹ ∈ H¹(B), n = 3
    e) |x|⁻¹ ∈ H¹(B), n = 5              f) |x|^(−a) ∈ H¹(B), n = 4: which a?
    g) |x|^(−a) ∈ W^{1,p}(B), n = 10, p = 4: which a?
    h) ln|x| ∈ H¹(B), n = 2              i) ln|x| ∈ W^{1,p}(B), n = 2: which p?
    j) ln ln(4/|x|) ∈ H¹(B), n = 2       k) ln ln(4/|x|) ∈ L^∞(B)
    l) x₁/|x| ∈ H¹(B), n = 2             m) x₁/|x| ∈ H¹(B), n = 3
    n) |x|^{1/2} ∈ W^{1,p}(−1, 1): which p?
    o) 𝟙_{x₁>0} ∈ H¹(B), any n           p) the constant 1 ∈ H₀¹(B)
    q) x(1 − x) ∈ H₀¹(0, 1)               r) sin(πx) ∈ H²(0, 1) ∩ H₀¹(0, 1)
    s) e^(−|x|) ∈ H²(R)                  t) |x|^b ∈ W^{1,p}(B), b > 0: which p?
    Answers:
      a) no ((a + 1)p = 3 = n)        b) yes (2.5 < 3)     c) yes (2 < 3)
      d) no (4 > 3)                    e) yes (4 < 5)       f) a < 1
      g) a < 3/2 ((a + 1)·4 < 10)      h) no (∫r⁻²r dr = ∞)  i) p < 2
      j) yes (∫|∇u|² = 2π/ln 4)        k) no (unbounded)
      l) no (|∇u| = 1/r, p < 2 only)   m) yes (∫r⁻² r² dr < ∞)
      n) 1 ≤ p < 2                     o) no (jump across x₁ = 0)
      p) no (trace 1 ≠ 0)              q) yes     r) yes
      s) no (u″ = u − 2δ)              t) (1 − b)p < n: all p if b ≥ 1

M3. Sobolev exponents and embeddings.
    a) p* for n = 3, p = 2             b) p* for n = 2, p = 1
    c) p* for n = 3, p = 1             d) p* for n = 4, p = 2
    e) p* for n = 5, p = 3             f) p* for n = 10, p = 2
    g) Morrey exponent for W^{1,4}(R³)  h) Morrey exponent for W^{1,4}(R²)
    i) H¹(R) ⊂ C^{0,γ}: γ = ?          j) W^{1,∞}: which Hölder class?
    k) H²(R³) ⊂ C^{0,γ}: γ = ?         l) W^{2,2}(R⁵) ⊂ L^q: q = ?

                                          Hᵏ(R⁴) ⊂ C⁰

    r) is H¹(Ω) ⊂ L⁵(Ω) compact (same Ω)?

    Answers:

      n) 3       o) 3 (k − 1 > 3/2)   p) 3

      s) no (translates of a bump)     t) yes (absolutely continuous)

M5. Dirichlet eigenvalues and Rayleigh quotients R(u) = ∫|∇u|²/∫u².

    n) the unit disc: λ₁ (j₀,₁ ≈ 2.40483)
    o) R(1 − r²) on the unit disc      p) λ₁ of the disc of radius 2
    q) number of eigenvalues ≤ 50 of (0, π)², counted with multiplicity

      i) 10          j) 12              k) π² ≈ 9.86960
      l) 14 (∫u′² = 2/15, ∫u² = 1/105)  m) 3/π ≈ 0.95493
      n) 5.78319     o) 6 (2π/(π/3))     p) 5.78319/4 ≈ 1.44580
