M7. Curves in projective space: degree, genus, cohomology.
    a) genus of a smooth plane curve of degree 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6
    b) smooth plane quartic C: h⁰(O_C(1)), h⁰(O_C(2)), h⁰(O_C(3))
    c) smooth plane quintic: K_C and h⁰(K_C)
    d) complete intersection of type (2, 2) in P³: degree, genus
    e) type (2, 3)       f) type (3, 3)       g) type (2, 4)
    h) type (2, 2, 2) in P⁴: degree, K_C, genus
    i) the rational normal curve in Pⁿ: degree, genus, h⁰(O_C(1))
    j) geometric genus of a plane quartic with 1 node; with 3 nodes; of a
       plane sextic with 4 nodes and 1 cusp
    k) arithmetic and geometric genus of the nodal cubic y²z = x³ + x²z
    l) canonical model of a non-hyperelliptic curve of genus 3; genus 4;
       general genus 5 (ambient space and degree)
    m) deg K for g = 4, and the degree of the canonical map of a
       hyperelliptic curve of genus g onto its image
    n) a smooth hyperplane section of the Veronese surface v₂(P²) ⊆ P⁵:
       degree and genus
    Answers:
      a) 0, 0, 1, 3, 6, 10               b) 3, 6, 10 (RR: 4k + 1 − 3 for
                                            k ≥ 2)
      c) O_C(2), h⁰ = 6 = g              d) 4, 1
      e) 6, 4        f) 9, 10       g) 8, 9 (2g − 2 = ab(a + b − 4))
      h) 8, O_C(1), 5 (2g − 2 = 8)
      i) n, 0, n + 1                     j) 2; 0; 10 − 5 = 5
      k) pₐ = 1, geometric genus 0 (normalization P¹)
      l) quartic in P²; (2, 3) complete intersection in P³ (degree 6);
         (2, 2, 2) complete intersection in P⁴ (degree 8)
      m) 6; 2 (it factors through the double cover of a rational normal
         curve of degree g − 1)
      n) degree 4 (a conic embedded by O(2)), genus 0

M8. Riemann-Roch on a smooth projective curve C of genus g: find ℓ(D).
    a) g = 0, deg D = 5                  b) g = 0, deg D = −1
    c) g = 1, deg D = 4                  d) g = 1, D = 0; D of degree 0,
                                            not principal
    e) g = 2, deg D = 3                  f) g = 2, D = K
    g) g = 3, deg D = 5                  h) g = 3, deg D = 4, D ≁ K
    i) g = 3, D = K                      j) g = 5, deg D = 20
    k) g = 10, D = 2K                    l) g ≥ 1, D = P a point
    m) elliptic (E, O): ℓ(nO) for n = 0, 1, 2, 3, 6
    n) Weierstrass gaps at any point of an elliptic curve
    o) g = 2: gaps at a Weierstrass point and at a general point
    p) g = 3 plane quartic: gaps at a general point; at an ordinary flex
       P (tangent line cuts 3P + Q); at a hyperflex (tangent cuts 4P)
    q) Clifford: the largest ℓ(D) for deg D = 4 on a curve of genus 5
    r) the least n such that every divisor of degree n is very ample,
       g = 2
    s) the least degree of a non-constant map C → P¹ when g = 2
    Answers:
      a) 6       b) 0       c) 4       d) 1; 0
      e) 2       f) 2       g) 3       h) 2 (= 4 + 1 − 3, ℓ(K − D) = 0)
      i) 3       j) 16      k) 27 (= 3g − 3)          l) 1
      m) 1, 1, 2, 3, 6      n) {1}
      o) {1, 3} (ℓ(2P) = 2: x has a double pole); {1, 2}
      p) {1, 2, 3}; {1, 2, 4} (ℓ(3P) = 1 + ℓ(Q) = 2, ℓ(4P) = 2 + ℓ(Q − P)
         = 2); {1, 2, 5} (ℓ(4P) = ℓ(K) = 3)
      q) 3 (equality iff C is hyperelliptic)
      r) 5 (= 2g + 1)       s) 2 (every genus-2 curve is hyperelliptic)

M9. Riemann-Hurwitz and genus computations (char 0).
    a) y² = x³ − x       b) y² = x⁵ − 1       c) y² = x⁶ − 1
    d) y² = x⁷ + 1       e) y² = h(x), h squarefree of degree 10
    f) y² = x(x − 1)(x − 2)···(x − 7)
    g) y³ = h(x), h squarefree of degree 6
    h) y³ = x⁴ − 1       i) the Fermat curves of degree 4 and 5
    j) an unramified double cover of a genus-3 curve
    k) an unramified degree-n cover of an elliptic curve
    l) a degree-2 map from genus 2 to genus 1: number of branch points
    m) a degree-3 map from genus g to P¹ with only simple ramification:
       number of branch points
    n) z ↦ z³ on P¹: ramification points and ∑(e_P − 1)
    o) Hurwitz bound for |Aut C| when g = 2, 3, 7
    p) geometric genus of y² = x³ + x²; of a plane quartic with one cusp
    q) genus of smooth curves of bidegree (3, 3), (2, 5), (1, n) on
       P¹ × P¹
    Answers:
      a) 1       b) 2       c) 2       d) 3       e) 4       f) 3
      g) 4 (6 total ramification points, ∞ unramified: 2g − 2 = −6 + 12)
      h) 3       i) 3, 6    j) 5 (2g − 2 = 2·4)       k) 1
      l) 2 (2 = 2·0 + r)    m) 2g + 4
      n) 0 and ∞, each e = 3; 4 = 2·3 − 2
      o) 84, 168 (attained by the Klein quartic), 504 (attained by the
         Fricke-Macbeath curve)
      p) 0; 2               q) 4, 4, 0

M10. Surfaces: intersection numbers, adjunction, RR, Noether.
     a) on P²: conic · cubic; (line)²; K²
     b) on Q = P¹ × P¹: (1, 0)², (1, 1)², (2, 3)·(1, 4), K_Q, K_Q²
     c) Bl_p P²: E², K², (H − E)², (H − E)·E
     d) P² blown up at r points: K², e, rank of Pic
     e) smooth surface of degree d in P³: K², and its value for d = 3, 4, 5
     f) quintic surface: χ(O), e, p_g, q
     g) quartic (K3) surface: χ(O), e, p_g
     h) genus of a smooth plane section of the quintic surface
     i) a smooth curve C on a K3 with C² = 4; with C² = −2
     j) χ(P², O(4)); χ(Q, O(2, 3)) by surface Riemann-Roch
     k) arithmetic genus of curves in the classes 2H − E and 3H − 2E on
        Bl_p P²
     l) Hodge index on Q: signature of the form; D = (1, −1): D·(1, 1)
        and D²
     m) signature of the intersection form on Bl_r P²
     n) degree of the del Pezzo surface Bl_r P² for r = 5, 6, 8
     o) can a smooth rational curve with C² = −2 be contracted to a
        smooth point?
     Answers:
       a) 6; 1; 9                b) 0, 2, 11, (−2, −2), 8
       c) −1, 8, 0, 1            d) 9 − r, 3 + r, r + 1 (12 = K² + e ✓)
       e) d(d − 4)²: 3, 0, 5
       f) 5, 55, 4 (= h⁰(O_X(1))), 0 (χ = 1 − 0 + 4 ✓; 60 = 5 + 55 ✓)
       g) 2, 24, 1               h) 6 (2g − 2 = H·2H = 10)
       i) g = 3; g = 0 (a (−2)-curve)
       j) 15; 1 + ½(2·5 + 3·4) = 12 = 3·4 ✓
       k) 0 (conics through p); 0 (C² = 5, K·C = −7: nodal cubics at p
          become smooth rational curves)
       l) (1, 1); 0 and −2 < 0 ✓      m) (1, r)
       n) 4 (two quadrics in P⁴), 3 (cubic surface), 1
       o) no (Castelnuovo needs C² = −1); it contracts to an A₁ (cone)
          singularity

M11. Divisors and Picard groups.
     a) div of (x − 1)²/(x(x − 2)) on P¹  b) div of x³ on P¹
     c) div of (x − 1)/(x − 2)³ on P¹
     d) Pic Pⁿ; Pic Aⁿ; Pic(A¹ ∖ {0})
     e) Pic(P¹ × P¹); Pic(Bl_r P²)
     f) Pic(P² ∖ L), L a line; Pic(P² ∖ conic); Pic(P² ∖ smooth cubic)
     g) Pic(Spec Z); Pic(Spec Z[√−5])
     h) Cl and Pic of the cone Spec k[x, y, z]/(xy − z²)
     i) Pic⁰ of an elliptic curve E; of the nodal cubic; of the cuspidal
        cubic
     j) on E : y² = x³ − x (O at infinity): div(x), div(y)
     k) is (1, 0) − O principal on that E? is 2((1, 0) − O)?
     l) degree of O_C(1) on a plane curve of degree d
     m) Pic of a smooth plane cubic
     n) deg K_{P¹}; K_{Pⁿ}; K of a smooth hypersurface of degree d in Pⁿ
     o) is every Weil divisor Cartier on a smooth variety?
     Answers:
       a) 2[1] − [0] − [2] (no zero or pole at ∞)   b) 3[0] − 3[∞]
       c) [1] − 3[2] + 2[∞]        d) Z; 0; 0
       e) Z²; Z^(r+1)              f) 0; Z/2; Z/3 (Cl(X ∖ Z) = Cl X/⟨Z⟩)
       g) 0; Z/2 (the class of (2, 1 + √−5))
       h) Cl = Z/2 (generated by the line x = z = 0, with 2·line =
          div(x)); Pic = 0
       i) E itself (P ↦ [P − O]); k* (multiplicative group); k (additive
          group)
       j) div(x) = 2[(0, 0)] − 2[O]; div(y) = [(0, 0)] + [(1, 0)] +
          [(−1, 0)] − 3[O]
       k) no (it corresponds to the non-zero point (1, 0) of E); yes:
          div(x − 1)
       l) d       m) Z ⊕ E(k) (degree, and Pic⁰ ≅ E)
       n) −2; O(−n − 1); O(d − n − 1) (adjunction)
       o) yes (local rings are UFDs)

M12. True (T) or false (F)? Give a counterexample for F.
     a) Every open subset of an affine variety is affine.
     b) A bijective morphism of varieties is an isomorphism.
     c) Every irreducible closed subset of a scheme has a unique generic
        point.
     d) A surjective map of sheaves is surjective on global sections.
     e) Hⁱ(P¹, F) = 0 for every sheaf F of abelian groups and i ≥ 2.
     f) Every regular function on a connected projective variety is
        constant.
     g) For every g ≥ 0 there is a smooth plane curve of genus g.
     h) Every smooth projective curve of genus 2 is hyperelliptic.
     i) Every smooth projective curve of genus 0 over C is ≅ P¹.
     j) On a normal variety Pic = Cl.
     k) If deg D > 0 then ℓ(D) > 0.
     l) If deg D ≥ g then ℓ(D) > 0.
     m) The intersection form on a smooth projective surface is positive
        definite.
     n) Spec k[x]/(x²) ≅ Spec k as schemes.
     o) The underlying space of A² = A¹ × A¹ is the set product.
     p) Every smooth cubic surface over C contains exactly 27 lines.
     q) Every smooth projective surface has a unique minimal model.
     r) The Nullstellensatz holds over every field.
     s) A projective variety is affine only if it is a finite set of
        points.
     t) A non-constant map of curves X → Y implies g_X ≥ g_Y.
     Answers:
       a) F: A² ∖ {0} (D5)          b) F: A¹ → cusp (D3)
       c) T (schemes are sober)     d) F: exp on C*; O → O_{0, ∞} on P¹
       e) T (Grothendieck vanishing on a noetherian space of dim 1)
       f) T                         g) F: genera are (d − 1)(d − 2)/2 =
                                       0, 1, 3, 6, ...; 2 is missed
       h) T (K gives a degree-2 map to P¹)       i) T (D9)
       j) F: the cone xy = z² (Cl = Z/2, Pic = 0)
       k) F: a general degree-1 divisor on a genus-2 curve (effective
          classes of degree 1 form a curve in the surface Pic¹)
       l) T (ℓ(D) ≥ deg D + 1 − g ≥ 1)
       m) F: E² = −1 on a blow-up; (1, −1)² = −2 on P¹ × P¹
       n) F: same space, but k[x]/(x²) ≇ k (a nilpotent)
       o) F: A² has generic points of curves, e.g. (y − x²), not pairs
       p) T        q) F: P² and P¹ × P¹ are minimal and birational
       r) F: R, with x² + 1
       s) T (if X is affine and projective, k[X] = Γ(X, O) is a finite
          product of copies of k, so X is a finite set)
       t) T (Riemann-Hurwitz, D10)

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: keep ONE dictionary on the board all term — ideals ↔ sets,
  rings ↔ spaces, modules ↔ sheaves, divisors ↔ line bundles — and make
  every abstract statement land on four test cases: A¹ (Spec k[x]), P¹,
  the node/cusp, and Spec Z. Compute Čech cohomology of O(d) on P¹ by hand
  until the "negative-exponent monomials" picture of Hⁿ(Pⁿ, O(d)) is
  automatic; then every genus in the course comes from a short exact
  sequence 0 → O(−d) → O → O_C → 0 plus Serre duality. For curves drill
  the three inequalities ℓ(D) ≥ deg D + 1 − g, deg D > 2g − 2 ⇒ equality,
  deg < 0 ⇒ ℓ = 0, and always ask "which points are ramified, and with
  what e?" before quoting Riemann-Hurwitz. For surfaces, write K, C² and
  K·C in a table and check adjunction every time. Harder: prove Serre's
  vanishing and finiteness theorems via Čech cohomology on Proj; prove
  Serre duality on curves via répartitions (adèles); prove that a curve
  of genus g ≥ 2 has at most (g − 1)g(g + 1) Weierstrass points counted
  with weight and that |Aut C| ≤ 84(g − 1); derive surface Riemann-Roch
  from curve Riemann-Roch and adjunction; prove the Hodge index theorem
  from Riemann-Roch; show every smooth cubic surface is P² blown up at 6
  points and compute the incidence graph of its 27 lines (the
  Schläfli graph, automorphism group W(E₆)); and study the scheme-
  theoretic fibres of Spec Z[x]/(f) → Spec Z to see ramification as
  geometry.


================================================================================
TOPIC 12.15 — ALGEBRAIC NUMBER THEORY: DEDEKIND DOMAINS, RAMIFICATION, CLASS
GROUPS, DIRICHLET UNIT THEOREM, LOCAL FIELDS, p-ADIC NUMBERS, ADELES AND
IDELES
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - NUMBER FIELD K: a finite extension of Q, [K : Q] = n = r₁ + 2r₂ (r₁
    real embeddings, r₂ pairs of complex ones). RING OF INTEGERS O_K = the
    elements whose monic minimal polynomial lies in Z[x] (the integral
    closure of Z in K); O_K ≅ Zⁿ as a group. Quadratic K = Q(√d), d
    squarefree: O_K = Z[√d] if d ≡ 2, 3 (mod 4), Z[(1 + √d)/2] if d ≡ 1
    (mod 4); cyclotomic: O = Z[ζₘ]; Q(∛2): O = Z[∛2].
  - TRACE, NORM, DISCRIMINANT: Tr(α) = ∑σᵢ(α), N(α) = ∏σᵢ(α) over the n
    embeddings; disc(ω₁, ..., ωₙ) = det(Tr(ωᵢωⱼ)) = det(σᵢ(ωⱼ))². For an
    integral basis this is d_K; disc(Z[α]) = disc(f) = [O_K : Z[α]]²·d_K.
    d_K = d or 4d for Q(√d); disc(x³ + ax + b) = −4a³ − 27b²; d(Q(ζ_p)) =
    (−1)^((p−1)/2) p^(p−2). STICKELBERGER: d_K ≡ 0, 1 (mod 4); sign of d_K
    = (−1)^r₂. If disc(f) is squarefree, or f is Eisenstein at every
    p with p² | disc(f), then O_K = Z[α].
  - DEDEKIND DOMAIN: a Noetherian, integrally closed domain in which every
    non-zero prime is maximal. O_K is Dedekind; so is every PID and the
    coordinate ring of a smooth affine curve. MAIN THEOREM: every non-zero
    ideal factors UNIQUELY as a product of prime ideals; the fractional
    ideals form a free abelian group I_K on the primes; "to contain is to
    divide" (𝔞 ⊇ 𝔟 ⇔ 𝔞 | 𝔟). Localisations O_𝔭 are DVRs. Dedekind + UFD ⇔
    PID. Non-examples: Z[√−3] (not integrally closed: (2, 1 + √−3)² =
    2(2, 1 + √−3)), k[x, y] (dimension 2).
  - IDEAL NORM N(𝔞) = |O_K/𝔞|: multiplicative, N((α)) = |N(α)|, N(𝔭) = p^f
    for 𝔭 | p. In Z[√−5]: 6 = 2·3 = (1 + √−5)(1 − √−5), but as ideals
    (6) = 𝔭₂²𝔭₃𝔭₃′ with 𝔭₂ = (2, 1 + √−5), 𝔭₃ = (3, 1 + √−5), 𝔭₃′ =
    (3, 1 − √−5): unique factorisation is restored at the level of ideals.
  - SPLITTING OF PRIMES: pO_K = 𝔓₁^e₁ ··· 𝔓_g^e_g with ∑eᵢfᵢ = n (eᵢ =
    RAMIFICATION index, fᵢ = [O_K/𝔓ᵢ : F_p] RESIDUE degree). p RAMIFIES
    (some eᵢ > 1) ⇔ p | d_K (Dedekind), so only finitely many ramify.
    DEDEKIND-KUMMER: if O_K = Z[α] (or just p ∤ [O_K : Z[α]]) and f ≡
    ∏gᵢ^eᵢ (mod p) with gᵢ irreducible, then pO_K = ∏(p, gᵢ(α))^eᵢ with fᵢ =
    deg gᵢ. Warning: Dedekind's field x³ + x² − 2x + 8 has 2 | [O_K : Z[α]]
    for EVERY α.
  - QUADRATIC AND CYCLOTOMIC LAWS: in Q(√d), p odd splits / is inert /
    ramifies as (d/p) = 1 / −1 / 0; p = 2 splits if d ≡ 1 (mod 8), is inert
    if d ≡ 5 (mod 8), ramifies if d ≡ 2, 3 (mod 4). In Q(ζₘ), p ∤ m: e = 1,
    f = order of p mod m, g = φ(m)/f; (p) = (1 − ζ_p)^(p−1) is totally
    ramified in Q(ζ_p).
  - GALOIS CASE (HILBERT THEORY): Gal(L/K) permutes the 𝔓 | 𝔭
    transitively, so all eᵢ = e, fᵢ = f and efg = n. DECOMPOSITION group
    D_𝔓 = {σ : σ𝔓 = 𝔓} (order ef), INERTIA group I_𝔓 (order e), D/I ≅
    Gal(F_𝔓/F_𝔭) cyclic, generated by FROBENIUS: Frob(x) ≡ x^N𝔭 mod 𝔓. For
    Q(ζₘ), Frob_p = (ζ ↦ ζᵖ); for Q(i), Frob_p = conjugation iff p ≡ 3
    (mod 4).
  - CLASS GROUP Cl(K) = I_K/P_K (fractional ideals modulo principal ones);
    h_K = |Cl(K)| is FINITE, and h_K = 1 ⇔ O_K is a PID ⇔ O_K is a UFD.
    MINKOWSKI BOUND: every class contains an integral ideal of norm ≤ M_K =
    (n!/nⁿ)(4/π)^r₂ √|d_K|; so Cl(K) is generated by primes of norm ≤ M_K.
    Examples: h = 1 for Q(i), Q(√−2), Q(√−7), Q(√−163), Q(√2), Q(∛2),
    Q(ζ₇); h = 2 for Q(√−5), Q(√10); h = 3 for Q(√−23); h = 4 (cyclic) for
    Q(√−14); Cl ≅ (Z/2)² for Q(√−21). Imaginary quadratic h = 1 exactly for
    d = −1, −2, −3, −7, −11, −19, −43, −67, −163 (Heegner-Stark-Baker).
  - GEOMETRY OF NUMBERS: MINKOWSKI'S THEOREM: a convex, centrally symmetric
    S ⊆ Rⁿ with vol(S) > 2ⁿ covol(Λ) contains a non-zero point of the
    lattice Λ. O_K embeds as a lattice of covolume 2^(−r₂)√|d_K| in K ⊗ R ≅
    R^r₁ × C^r₂. Consequences: finiteness of h_K, |d_K| > 1 for K ≠ Q
    (so some prime ramifies), HERMITE: finitely many K with |d_K| ≤ X.
  - DIRICHLET UNIT THEOREM: O_K^× ≅ μ_K × Z^(r₁+r₂−1), μ_K = roots of unity
    in K (finite, cyclic). Proof via the LOG EMBEDDING ℓ(u) = (log|σᵢ(u)|
    with weight 2 at complex places): kernel = μ_K, image a full lattice
    in the hyperplane ∑xᵢ = 0. REGULATOR R_K = |det| of any (r₁ + r₂ − 1)
    minor of the log matrix of fundamental units (R = log ε for real
    quadratic). N(u) = ±1 for units.
  - UNITS IN PRACTICE: imaginary quadratic: O^× = {±1}, except μ₄ in Q(i),
    μ₆ in Q(√−3). Real quadratic: ε = fundamental solution of PELL x² − dy²
    = ±1 (±4 when d ≡ 1 mod 4), found by the continued fraction of √d:
    1 + √2, 2 + √3, (1 + √5)/2, 5 + 2√6, 8 + 3√7, 3 + √10, (3 + √13)/2.
    Q(∛2): ε = 1 + ∛2 + ∛4 = 1/(∛2 − 1); x³ − x − 1: ε = α (≈ 1.32472).
  - p-ADIC NUMBERS: vₚ(pᵏa/b) = k, |x|ₚ = p^(−vₚ(x)): NON-ARCHIMEDEAN
    (|x + y|ₚ ≤ max(|x|ₚ, |y|ₚ), equality if |x|ₚ ≠ |y|ₚ; every triangle
    is isosceles, every point of a ball is its centre). OSTROWSKI: every
    non-trivial absolute value on Q is equivalent to |·|_∞ or some |·|ₚ.
    Qₚ = completion; Zₚ = {|x|ₚ ≤ 1} = lim← Z/pᵏ, compact; x = ∑_{i≥k}
    aᵢpⁱ uniquely (0 ≤ aᵢ < p); −1 = ∑(p − 1)pⁱ; ∑aₙ converges ⇔ aₙ → 0.
    PRODUCT FORMULA: ∏_v |x|_v = 1 for x ∈ Q^×.
  - HENSEL'S LEMMA: f ∈ Zₚ[x], f(a) ≡ 0, f′(a) ≢ 0 (mod p) ⇒ a unique root
    α ∈ Zₚ with α ≡ a (strong form: |f(a)|ₚ < |f′(a)|ₚ²). Hence μ_(p−1) ⊆
    Zₚ (TEICHMÜLLER lifts ω(a) = lim a^(pᵏ)); for p odd a unit u is a
    square in Zₚ iff (u/p) = 1; a 2-adic unit is a square iff u ≡ 1
    (mod 8). Qₚ^× ≅ Z × μ_(p−1) × (1 + pZₚ) and |Qₚ^×/Qₚ^×²| = 4 (p odd),
    8 (p = 2).
  - LOCAL FIELDS: fields complete for a discrete valuation with finite
    residue field (equivalently, non-discrete locally compact fields ≠ R,
    C): finite extensions of Qₚ, and F_q((t)). Data: O, uniformiser π,
    residue field k, n = ef. For each n there is a UNIQUE unramified
    extension of degree n (Qₚ(ζ_(pⁿ−1))); Eisenstein polynomials give the
    totally ramified ones (Qₚ(ζₚ), Qₚ(ᵖ⁻¹√p)). Qₚ has 3 quadratic
    extensions for p odd, 7 for p = 2.
  - LOCAL-GLOBAL: K ⊗_Q Qₚ ≅ ∏_{𝔓|p} K_𝔓 with [K_𝔓 : Qₚ] = e_𝔓f_𝔓;
    K ⊗ R ≅ R^r₁ × C^r₂. HASSE-MINKOWSKI: a quadratic form over Q is
    isotropic iff it is isotropic over R and every Qₚ; fails for cubics
    (Selmer: 3x³ + 4y³ + 5z³ = 0).
  - ADELES: A_K = ∏′ K_v, the RESTRICTED product of all completions with
    respect to O_v (all but finitely many coordinates integral), topology
    generated by ∏_{v∈S} U_v × ∏_{v∉S} O_v. A_Q = Q + (Ẑ × R) with Q ∩
    (Ẑ × R) = Z: K is DISCRETE in A_K and A_K/K is COMPACT (fundamental
    domain Ẑ × [0, 1) for Q). STRONG APPROXIMATION: K is dense in the
    adeles with any ONE place removed (for Q: Z is dense in Ẑ, CRT).
  - IDELES: J_K = A_K^× = ∏′ K_v^× w.r.t. O_v^×, with the restricted
    product topology (NOT the subspace topology of A_K: inversion would not
    be continuous). CONTENT |a| = ∏|a_v|_v; K^× ⊆ J¹ = ker|·| by the product
    formula, K^× discrete, and J¹/K^× is COMPACT (this one fact contains both
    the finiteness of h_K and the unit theorem).
    J_K → I_K, (a_v) ↦ ∏𝔭_v^(v(a_v)) is onto with kernel U_K = ∏_{v|∞} K_v^× ×
    ∏O_v^×, so J_K/K^×U_K ≅ Cl(K). For Q: J_Q = Q^× × (R_{>0} × Ẑ^×) and
    C_Q = J_Q/Q^× ≅ R_{>0} × Ẑ^×.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Let K = Q(√d), d ≠ 0, 1 squarefree. Prove that O_K = Z[√d] if d ≡ 2, 3
    (mod 4) and O_K = Z[ω], ω = (1 + √d)/2, if d ≡ 1 (mod 4), and compute
    d_K. Is (3 + √−7)/2 an algebraic integer? What is [O_K : Z[√5]] in
    Q(√5)?
    Answer: d_K = 4d, respectively d. Yes (root of x² − 3x + 4). Index 2.
    Teacher explains: Let α = a + b√d with a, b ∈ Q, b ≠ 0. Its minimal
    polynomial is x² − 2a x + (a² − db²), so α ∈ O_K ⇔ 2a ∈ Z and a² − db²
    ∈ Z. Write A = 2a ∈ Z. Then d(2b)² = A² − 4(a² − db²) ∈ Z; since d is
    squarefree, a prime in the denominator of 2b would appear squared in
    the denominator, so B = 2b ∈ Z. The condition becomes
       A² − dB² ≡ 0 (mod 4).
    Squares are 0 or 1 mod 4. If d ≡ 2 or 3 (mod 4), the only solution is
    A, B both even, i.e. a, b ∈ Z: O_K = Z[√d]. If d ≡ 1 (mod 4) the
    condition is A ≡ B (mod 2): a, b both integers or both halves of odd
    integers, i.e. α ∈ Z + Zω. Discriminants: for the basis {1, √d},
       det ( Tr 1    Tr √d  ) = det ( 2   0  ) = 4d;
           ( Tr √d   Tr d   )       ( 0   2d )
    for {1, ω}: Tr ω = 1 and ω² = ω + (d − 1)/4, so Tr ω² = 1 + (d − 1)/2
    = (d + 1)/2 and the determinant is 2·(d + 1)/2 − 1² = d. For
    (3 + √−7)/2: −7 ≡ 1 (mod 4) and A = 3, B = 1 are both odd, so it is
    integral; indeed its trace is 3 and its norm (9 + 7)/4 = 4. In
    Q(√5), disc(Z[√5]) = 20 = [O_K : Z[√5]]²·5, so the index is 2.
    Common mistakes: thinking Z[√d] is always the ring of integers (it is
    not integrally closed when d ≡ 1 mod 4: the golden ratio (1 + √5)/2 is
    missing); forgetting d_K ≡ 0, 1 (mod 4). Now you try: find O_K and d_K
    for Q(√−7) and for Q(√−6). (Z[(1 + √−7)/2], −7; Z[√−6], −24)

D2. In R = Z[√−5]: (a) explain why R is a Dedekind domain but not a UFD;
    (b) with 𝔭 = (2, 1 + √−5), 𝔮 = (3, 1 + √−5), 𝔮′ = (3, 1 − √−5), show
    𝔭² = (2), 𝔮𝔮′ = (3), 𝔭𝔮 = (1 + √−5), and hence factor (6) into primes;
    (c) show 𝔭 is not principal; (d) explain why Z[√−3] is NOT Dedekind.
    Answer: (6) = 𝔭²𝔮𝔮′; the two factorisations 2·3 and (1 + √−5)(1 − √−5)
    are regroupings (𝔭²)(𝔮𝔮′) = (𝔭𝔮)(𝔭𝔮′).
    Teacher explains: (a) R = O_K for K = Q(√−5) (−5 ≡ 3 mod 4, D1), so it
    is integrally closed; it is Zⁿ as a group, hence Noetherian; a non-zero
    prime 𝔓 contains the integer N(α) = αᾱ for any α ≠ 0 in 𝔓, so R/𝔓 is
    a finite domain, hence a field: dimension 1. It is not a UFD: 2, 3,
    1 ± √−5 are irreducible (norms 4, 9, 6, 6, and no element has norm 2
    or 3 since a² + 5b² ∉ {2, 3}) and not associates. (b) 𝔭² = (4, 2 + 2√−5,
    (1 + √−5)²) = (4, 2 + 2√−5, −4 + 2√−5). Every generator is divisible by
    2, and 2 = 6 − 4 = (2 + 2√−5) − (−4 + 2√−5) − 4 lies in 𝔭², so 𝔭² =
    (2). Similarly 𝔮𝔮′ = (9, 3 ± 3√−5, 6) ∋ 9 − 6 = 3 and all generators
    are multiples of 3: 𝔮𝔮′ = (3). 𝔭𝔮 = (6, 2 + 2√−5, 3 + 3√−5, −4 +
    2√−5) contains (3 + 3√−5) − (2 + 2√−5) = 1 + √−5, and each generator
    is a multiple of 1 + √−5 (6 = (1 + √−5)(1 − √−5)), so 𝔭𝔮 = (1 + √−5).
    R/𝔭 ≅ F₂ and R/𝔮 ≅ F₃ (N(𝔭) = 2, N(𝔮) = 3), so these ideals are prime and
    (6) = 𝔭²𝔮𝔮′. (c) If 𝔭 = (α) then |N(α)| = N(𝔭) = 2, i.e. a² + 5b² = 2:
    impossible. (d) In Z[√−3], 𝔭 = (2, 1 + √−3) has (1 + √−3)² = −2 +
    2√−3 ∈ 2𝔭, so 𝔭² = 2𝔭 with 𝔭 ≠ (2): cancellation fails, 𝔭 is not
    invertible. The culprit: (1 + √−3)/2 is integral but not in Z[√−3].
    Common mistakes: "irreducible = prime" (2 is irreducible but not prime
    in R: 2 | 6 = (1 + √−5)(1 − √−5) but 2 divides neither factor); forgetting
    to show the ideals are prime. Now you try: factor (3 + √−5). (𝔭·𝔭₇,
    𝔭₇ = (7, 3 + √−5), since N = 14)

D3. Let K = Q(α), α = ∛2. (a) Prove O_K = Z[α] and d_K = −108. (b) Factor
    2, 3, 5, 7 and 31 in O_K, giving e, f, g, and check ∑eᵢfᵢ = 3.
    Answer: (2) = (α)³; (3) = (α + 1)³; (5) = 𝔭₅𝔭₂₅ (f = 1, 2); (7) is
    inert (f = 3); (31) = 𝔭𝔭′𝔭″ (f = 1 each).
    Teacher explains: (a) disc(x³ − 2) = −27·4 = −108 = −2²·3³ and disc(Z[α])
    = [O_K : Z[α]]²·d_K, so the index divides 6. KEY LEMMA: if the minimal
    polynomial of α is Eisenstein at p, then p ∤ [O_K : Z[α]]. x³ − 2 is
    Eisenstein at 2, and the minimal polynomial of α − 1 is (x + 1)³ − 2 =
    x³ + 3x² + 3x − 1 — not Eisenstein; use α + 1 instead: (x − 1)³ − 2 =
    x³ − 3x² + 3x − 3, Eisenstein at 3, and Z[α + 1] = Z[α]. So the index
    is 1, O_K = Z[α], d_K = −108. (b) DEDEKIND-KUMMER applies to every p.
    Mod 2: x³, so (2) = (2, α)³ = (α)³ (N(α) = 2). Mod 3: x³ − 2 ≡ (x + 1)³,
    so (3) = (3, α + 1)³ = (α + 1)³, because N(α + 1) = 3 (constant term of
    (x − 1)³ − 2). Mod 5: x³ − 2 ≡ (x + 2)(x² − 2x − 1): (5) = (5, α + 2)·
    (5, α² − 2α − 1), e = 1, f = 1 + 2. Mod 7: the cubes mod 7 are 0, ±1,
    so x³ − 2 has no root and, being a cubic, is irreducible: (7) is prime,
    f = 3. Mod 31: 4³ = 64 ≡ 2, and 31 ≡ 1 (mod 3) gives three cube roots
    of 2: 4, 7, 20 (7³ = 343 = 11·31 + 2): (31) = (31, α − 4)(31, α − 7)
    (31, α − 20). In each case ∑eᵢfᵢ = 3. Ramified primes: 2 and 3, the
    primes dividing −108. Common mistakes: applying Dedekind-Kummer
    without checking p ∤ [O_K : Z[α]] (Dedekind's field x³ + x² − 2x + 8:
    the polynomial gives x(x + 1)² mod 2 yet 2 splits completely);
    thinking an irreducible cubic mod p can factor as 2 + 1 without a root.
    Now you try: factor 11. (11 ≡ 2 mod 3, cubing is a bijection mod 11,
    one root 7: (11) = 𝔭₁₁𝔭₁₂₁)

D4. Prove the splitting law in K = Q(√d) (d squarefree): an odd prime p
    splits, is inert or ramifies according as (d/p) = 1, −1, 0; p = 2
    splits if d ≡ 1 (mod 8), is inert if d ≡ 5 (mod 8), ramifies if
    d ≡ 2, 3 (mod 4). Deduce "p ramifies ⇔ p | d_K", and describe all
    primes of Q(√−5).
    Answer: apply Dedekind-Kummer to x² − d or x² − x − (d − 1)/4. In
    Q(√−5): 2, 5 ramify; p splits iff p ≡ 1, 3, 7, 9 (mod 20); the split
    primes above p are principal iff p ≡ 1, 9 (mod 20).
    Teacher explains: For p odd, p ∤ [O_K : Z[√d]] ∈ {1, 2}, so factor
    x² − d mod p: two distinct roots if d is a non-zero square (split, e =
    f = 1, g = 2), irreducible if d is a non-residue (inert, f = 2), and
    x² if p | d (ramified, (p) = (p, √d)²). For p = 2 and d ≡ 1 (mod 4) use
    ω, root of x² − x − (d − 1)/4: mod 2 this is x(x − 1) if (d − 1)/4 is
    even (d ≡ 1 mod 8) and x² + x + 1, irreducible, if it is odd (d ≡ 5
    mod 8). If d ≡ 2, 3 (mod 4), x² − d ≡ x² or (x + 1)² mod 2: ramified.
    Since d_K = d or 4d, the ramified primes are exactly those dividing
    d_K. For d = −5 (d_K = −20): (−5/p) = (−1/p)(5/p) = (−1)^((p−1)/2)
    (p/5) by reciprocity. Tabulate p mod 20:
       p ≡ 1: (+)(+)  3: (−)(−)  7: (−)(−)  9: (+)(+)      → split
       p ≡ 11: (−)(+) 13: (+)(−) 17: (+)(−) 19: (−)(+)     → inert.
    Since h = 2 (D6), a split 𝔭 is principal, 𝔭 = (a + b√−5), iff p =
    a² + 5b²; genus theory shows this happens iff p ≡ 1, 9 (mod 20): 29 =
    3² + 5·2², 41 = 6² + 5·1², while 3, 7, 23 are not of this form (their
    primes lie in the non-trivial class, like 𝔭 above 2). Common mistakes:
    using (d/2) (the Legendre symbol is not defined at 2); forgetting that
    for d ≡ 1 mod 4 one must use ω, not √d, at p = 2. Now you try: how do
    13, 43 and 61 behave in Q(√−5)? (13 inert; 43 ≡ 3: split, non-principal;
    61 ≡ 1: split, 61 = 4² + 5·3²)

D5. Let ζ = ζₘ, K = Q(ζ), O_K = Z[ζ]. (a) For m = p prime show (p) =
    (1 − ζ)^(p−1) and N(1 − ζ) = p. (b) For a prime q ∤ m show that q is
    unramified with f = ord_m(q), g = φ(m)/f. (c) For m = 7 and q = 2,
    describe the decomposition group, inertia group and Frobenius, and
    identify the decomposition field.
    Answer: (c) f = 3, g = 2, D = {1, σ₂, σ₄}, I = 1, Frob = σ₂ : ζ ↦ ζ²;
    the decomposition field is Q(√−7).
    Teacher explains: (a) Φ_p(x) = 1 + x + ... + x^(p−1) = ∏_{k=1}^{p−1}
    (x − ζᵏ); put x = 1: p = ∏(1 − ζᵏ). Each (1 − ζᵏ)/(1 − ζ) = 1 + ζ + ...
    + ζ^(k−1) ∈ O_K, and its inverse (1 − ζ)/(1 − ζᵏ) is of the same form
    (ζ = (ζᵏ)ʲ with kj ≡ 1 mod p), so it is a unit. Hence (p) = (1 −
    ζ)^(p−1). As ∑eᵢfᵢ = p − 1, (1 − ζ) is prime with e = p − 1, f = 1:
    TOTALLY RAMIFIED, and N(1 − ζ) = Φ_p(1) = p. (b) q ∤ m ⇒ xᵐ − 1 is
    separable mod q, so Φₘ mod q has distinct roots: e = 1. A primitive
    m-th root of unity lies in F_(qᶠ) iff m | qᶠ − 1, i.e. iff ord_m(q) | f;
    so every irreducible factor of Φₘ mod q has degree f = ord_m(q), and g
    = φ(m)/f. (c) 2 has order 3 mod 7 (2³ = 8 ≡ 1), so (2) = 𝔓𝔓′ with f = 3.
    Gal(K/Q) = {σ_a : ζ ↦ ζᵃ} ≅ (Z/7)^×. Since e = 1, I_𝔓 = 1 and D_𝔓 ≅
    Gal(F₈/F₂) is generated by Frob_𝔓, the element acting as x ↦ x² on the
    residue field: σ₂. D = ⟨σ₂⟩ = {1, σ₂, σ₄}, order ef = 3. Its fixed
    field is generated by the Gauss period η = ζ + ζ² + ζ⁴, which satisfies
    η² + η + 2 = 0, so η = (−1 + √−7)/2 and the decomposition field is
    Q(√−7). Check: 2 splits in Q(√−7) (−7 ≡ 1 mod 8) and each factor is
    then inert in the cubic extension K/Q(√−7). Common mistakes: using
    ord_q(m) instead of ord_m(q); forgetting that the ramified prime of
    Q(ζ₇) is 7 (not every prime dividing φ(7)). Now you try: how do 11 and
    19 split in Q(ζ₅)? (11 ≡ 1: completely, 4 primes; 19 ≡ −1: f = 2, g = 2)

D6. Compute the class group of K = Q(√−23) using the Minkowski bound, and
    check that h(Q(√−5)) = 2.
    Answer: Cl(Q(√−23)) ≅ Z/3, generated by 𝔭 = (2, ω), ω = (1 + √−23)/2,
    with 𝔭³ = (ω − 2). Cl(Q(√−5)) ≅ Z/2.
    Teacher explains: d_K = −23, n = 2, r₂ = 1, so M_K = (2/4)(4/π)√23 =
    (2/π)√23 ≈ 3.053: Cl is generated by primes of norm 2 and 3. ω has
    minimal polynomial x² − x + 6, and N(a + bω) = a² + ab + 6b². Mod 2
    and mod 3, x² − x + 6 ≡ x(x − 1), so (2) = 𝔭𝔭̄ with 𝔭 = (2, ω) and
    (3) = 𝔮𝔮̄ with 𝔮 = (3, ω). NON-PRINCIPAL: for b ≠ 0, a² + ab + 6b² =
    (a + b/2)² + 23b²/4 ≥ 23/4 > 3, so there is no element of norm 2 or 3.
    RELATIONS: N(ω) = 6 and ω ∈ 𝔭 ∩ 𝔮, so (ω) = 𝔭𝔮, i.e. [𝔮] = [𝔭]⁻¹.
    Look for norm 8 = 2³: a = −2, b = 1 gives N(ω − 2) = 4 − 2 + 6 = 8, and
    ω − 2 ∈ 𝔭 but ω − 2 ∉ (2), so (ω − 2) cannot contain 𝔭𝔭̄ = (2) as a
    factor: (ω − 2) = 𝔭³. Thus [𝔭] has order exactly 3, [𝔭̄] = [𝔭]⁻¹,
    [𝔮] = [𝔭]⁻¹, [𝔮̄] = [𝔭], and Cl(K) = ⟨[𝔭]⟩ ≅ Z/3. For Q(√−5): M =
    (2/π)√20 ≈ 2.85, so only 𝔭₂ = (2, 1 + √−5) matters; it is not
    principal (D2) and 𝔭₂² = (2), so h = 2. Common mistakes: using √d
    instead of √|d_K| in M_K; concluding the order of [𝔭] from its norm
    alone — always find an explicit principal power. Now you try: show
    Cl(Q(√−14)) ≅ Z/4. (M ≈ 4.76; 𝔭₃ = (3, 1 + √−14) has 𝔭₃² non-principal
    (no a² + 14b² = 9 except (3)), and (5 + 2√−14) = 𝔭₃⁴, norm 81)

D7. State and prove Minkowski's convex body theorem. Use it (a) to prove
    that every prime p ≡ 1 (mod 4) is a sum of two squares, and (b) to show
    that |d_K| > 1 for every number field K ≠ Q, so that some prime
    ramifies in K.
    Answer: vol(S) > 2ⁿ covol(Λ) ⇒ S ∩ Λ ≠ {0}; (a) use the lattice
    {(a, b) : b ≡ ua mod p}, u² ≡ −1; (b) 1 ≤ M_K forces √|d_K| ≥ π/2 > 1.
    Teacher explains: BLICHFELDT: if T ⊆ Rⁿ is measurable with vol(T) >
    covol(Λ), two distinct points of T differ by a lattice vector (project
    T onto the torus Rⁿ/Λ of volume covol(Λ): the map cannot be injective,
    or volumes would add up to at most covol(Λ)). Apply it to T = ½S:
    x − y ∈ Λ with x, y ∈ ½S; by symmetry −y ∈ ½S, by convexity ½(2x) +
    ½(−2y) = x − y ∈ S. □ (a) Pick u with u² ≡ −1 (mod p) (possible as
    p ≡ 1 mod 4). Λ = {(a, b) ∈ Z² : b ≡ ua (mod p)} has index p in Z², so
    covol p. The disc a² + b² < 2p has area 2πp > 4p, so it contains (a, b)
    ≠ 0 of Λ; then a² + b² ≡ a²(1 + u²) ≡ 0 (mod p) and 0 < a² + b² < 2p,
    hence a² + b² = p. Example: p = 13, u = 5 (25 ≡ −1): 13 = 2² + 3².
    (b) Minkowski's theorem applied to O_K ⊆ R^r₁ × C^r₂ gives, in every
    ideal class, an integral ideal of norm ≤ M_K; since norms are ≥ 1,
       √|d_K| ≥ (nⁿ/n!)(π/4)^r₂ ≥ (nⁿ/n!)(π/4)^(n/2) =: bₙ.
    b₂ = 2·π/4 ≈ 1.571, b₃ ≈ 3.13, and b_(n+1)/bₙ = (1 + 1/n)ⁿ√(π/4) ≥
    2·0.886 > 1, so bₙ > 1 for all n ≥ 2. Thus |d_K| > 1, and a prime
    dividing d_K ramifies. So Q has NO unramified extension — unlike most
    number fields, e.g. Q(√−5) ⊆ Q(√−5, i) is unramified. Common mistakes:
    forgetting that S must be CONVEX and SYMMETRIC (a thin annulus has large
    volume and no lattice points); strict vs non-strict inequality (for
    compact S, ≥ suffices). Now you try: write 29 as a sum of two squares
    by this method. (u = 12: 29 = 2² + 5²)

D8. State the Dirichlet unit theorem, sketch its proof, and apply it to
    K = Q(∛2): find the unit rank, show ε = 1 + ∛2 + ∛4 is a unit, and
    compute the regulator. Give the unit rank of Q(ζ₇), Q(√2, √3), Q(⁴√2).
    Answer: O_K^× = {±1} × εᶻ, R_K = log ε ≈ 1.34738; ranks 2, 3, 2.
    Teacher explains: THEOREM: O_K^× ≅ μ_K × Z^(r₁+r₂−1). Proof sketch:
    define ℓ : O_K^× → R^(r₁+r₂), ℓ(u) = (log|σ₁u|, ..., log|σ_r₁ u|,
    2log|τ₁u|, ..., 2log|τ_r₂ u|). It is a homomorphism into the hyperplane
    H = {∑xᵢ = 0} (because |N(u)| = 1). (i) Kernel: units all of whose
    conjugates have |·| = 1 form a finite set (bounded integer
    coefficients of the minimal polynomial), hence a finite group = μ_K.
    (ii) ℓ(O_K^×) is discrete, so a lattice of rank ≤ r₁ + r₂ − 1. (iii)
    Full rank: Minkowski's theorem produces elements of bounded norm
    that are small at all but one chosen place; finitely many principal
    ideals of bounded norm give units whose log vectors span H. For
    K = Q(∛2): r₁ = 1, r₂ = 1 (∛2 is real, the other two cube roots are
    complex), so the rank is 1 + 1 − 1 = 1, and μ_K = {±1} (K is real).
    ε·(∛2 − 1) = ∛8 − 1 = 1, so ε is a unit; ε ≈ 3.84732. It is
    FUNDAMENTAL: Artin's inequality |d_K| < 4u³ + 24 for a unit u > 1 in a
    complex cubic field gives u³ > (108 − 24)/4 = 21, u > 2.758 for the
    fundamental unit u, and ε = uᵏ with k ≥ 2 would force ε ≥ 2.758² ≈
    7.6. The regulator is log ε ≈ 1.34738. Ranks: Q(ζ₇) has r₁ = 0, r₂ =
    3: rank 2; Q(√2, √3) is totally real of degree 4: rank 3; Q(⁴√2) has
    r₁ = 2 (±⁴√2), r₂ = 1: rank 2. Common mistakes: counting complex
    embeddings instead of complex PAIRS; forgetting μ_K (e.g. Q(ζ₇) has
    μ = μ₁₄, 14 roots of unity). Now you try: the unit rank and μ_K of
    Q(ζ₅)? (rank 1, generated with μ₁₀ by (1 + √5)/2)

D9. (a) Find the fundamental unit of Q(√7) from the continued fraction of
    √7 and describe all solutions of x² − 7y² = 1. (b) For Q(√13) show ε =
    (3 + √13)/2 is the fundamental unit, has norm −1, and that ε³ is the
    fundamental unit of the order Z[√13]; solve x² − 13y² = ±1.
    Answer: (a) 8 + 3√7; x + y√7 = ±(8 + 3√7)ᵏ, k ∈ Z. (b) ε³ = 18 + 5√13;
    x² − 13y² = −1: (18, 5), = +1: (649, 180) = ε⁶.
    Teacher explains: For d ≡ 2, 3 (mod 4), units of Z[√d] are the x + y√d
    with x² − dy² = ±1. A unit u > 1 has x, y > 0 (u, ±ū = ±1/u, their
    signs force it), so the smallest unit > 1 is the one with smallest
    y > 0 — and it generates (the units > 0 form the group εᶻ, by
    discreteness: if εᵏ < u < εᵏ⁺¹ then 1 < uε⁻ᵏ < ε, contradiction).
    Continued fraction: √7 = [2; 1, 1, 1, 4] (period 4), convergents 2/1,
    3/1, 5/2, 8/3, and 8² − 7·3² = 1. So ε = 8 + 3√7 (norm +1; the even
    period means no solution of x² − 7y² = −1 — consistent with −1 not
    being a square mod 7). All solutions: ±(8 + 3√7)ᵏ; e.g. (8 + 3√7)² =
    127 + 48√7. (b) 13 ≡ 1 (mod 4), so units are (x + y√13)/2 with x² −
    13y² = ±4. y = 1: x² = 13 ± 4 = 9 gives x = 3 and norm (9 − 13)/4 =
    −1. Smallest possible y, so ε = (3 + √13)/2 is fundamental. ε² = (11 +
    3√13)/2, ε³ = 18 + 5√13 ∈ Z[√13]; one checks ε, ε² ∉ Z[√13] (halves),
    so ε³ generates Z[√13]^× modulo ±1. x² − 13y² = −1: 18² − 13·5² = 324
    − 325 = −1; = +1: ε⁶ = 649 + 180√13. Regulator log ε ≈ 1.19476. (Here
    √13 = [3; 1, 1, 1, 1, 6] has ODD period 5, which is why −1 is a norm.)
    Common mistakes: forgetting half-integer units when d ≡ 1 (mod 4);
    assuming a unit of norm −1 exists whenever −1 is a square mod every
    p | d (fails for d = 34). Now you try: the fundamental unit of Q(√6)
    and of Q(√11). (√6 = [2; 2, 4]: 5 + 2√6; 10 + 3√11)

D10. (a) Compute the 5-adic expansions of −1 and 1/3. (b) State and prove
     Hensel's lemma. (c) Solve x² = −1 in Z₅ to 5 digits, and identify the
     root as a Teichmüller lift. (d) State Ostrowski's theorem.
     Answer: (a) −1 = 4 + 4·5 + 4·5² + ...; 1/3 = 2 + 3·5 + 1·5² + 3·5³ +
     1·5⁴ + ... (c) x = 2 + 1·5 + 2·5² + 1·5³ + 3·5⁴ + ... = 2057 mod 5⁵.
     Teacher explains: (a) ∑_{i≥0} 4·5ⁱ = 4/(1 − 5) = −1, the series
     converging because |4·5ⁱ|₅ = 5⁻ⁱ → 0. For 1/3: 1/3 − 2 = −5/3, so
     1/3 = 2 + 5·(−1/3); and −1/3 = 8/(1 − 25) = (3 + 1·5)(1 + 5² + 5⁴ +
     ...), digits 3, 1, 3, 1, ... Hence 1/3 = 2 + 3·5 + 1·5² + 3·5³ + ...
     (check: 3·(2 + 3·5) = 51 ≡ 1 mod 25). (b) HENSEL: f ∈ Zₚ[x], a ∈ Zₚ
     with f(a) ≡ 0 and f′(a) ≢ 0 (mod p) ⇒ a unique α ∈ Zₚ, α ≡ a (mod p),
     f(α) = 0. Proof (lift one digit at a time): if f(aₖ) ≡ 0 (mod pᵏ),
     put aₖ₊₁ = aₖ + tpᵏ. Taylor: f(aₖ + tpᵏ) ≡ f(aₖ) + tpᵏf′(aₖ) (mod
     pᵏ⁺¹), so we need t ≡ −(f(aₖ)/pᵏ)·f′(aₖ)⁻¹ (mod p): a unique t, since
     f′(aₖ) ≡ f′(a) is a unit. The aₖ form a Cauchy sequence converging in
     the complete ring Zₚ. (c) f = x² + 1, a₁ = 2, f′ = 2x. Mod 25: (2 +
     5t)² + 1 ≡ 5 + 20t ⇒ 1 + 4t ≡ 0, t = 1, a₂ = 7. Mod 125: 7² + 1 = 50 =
     25·2, 2 + 14t ≡ 0 (mod 5), t = 2, a₃ = 57. Mod 625: 57² + 1 = 3250 =
     125·26, 26 + 114t ≡ 1 + 4t ≡ 0, t = 1, a₄ = 182. Mod 5⁵: 182² + 1 =
     33125 = 625·53, 53 + 364t ≡ 3 + 4t ≡ 0, t = 3, a₅ = 2057. Since
     μ₄ ⊆ Z₅ by Hensel applied to x⁴ − 1, this root is the TEICHMÜLLER lift
     ω(2) = lim 2^(5ᵏ) (indeed 2^(5⁴) ≡ 2057 mod 5⁵). (d) OSTROWSKI: every
     non-trivial absolute value on Q is equivalent to |·|_∞ or to |·|ₚ for
     exactly one prime p (equivalent = a positive power of it).
     Common mistakes: "carrying" in the wrong direction (p-adic digits
     carry to the RIGHT, towards higher powers); applying Hensel when
     f′(a) ≡ 0 (x² − 2 has no root in Z₂; x² − 17 does but needs the strong
     form, |f(a)|₂ < |f′(a)|₂²). Now you try: the
     first four 7-adic digits of the root of x² = 2 with x ≡ 3 (mod 7).
     (3, 1, 2, 6: x ≡ 2166 mod 7⁴)

D11. (a) Prove Qₚ^× ≅ Z × μ_(p−1) × (1 + pZₚ). (b) For p odd show that
     Qₚ^×/Qₚ^×² has order 4 and list the three quadratic extensions of Qₚ
     with their e and f. (c) What changes for p = 2? (d) Relate this to the
     splitting of p in Q(√−5), computing Q(√−5) ⊗ Qₚ for p = 2, 3, 7, 13.
     Answer: (b) classes 1, u, p, up (u a non-residue unit): Qₚ(√u) has
     e = 1, f = 2; Qₚ(√p), Qₚ(√up) have e = 2, f = 1. (c) 8 classes {±1,
     ±5, ±2, ±10}, 7 quadratic extensions. (d) ramified field, Q₃ × Q₃,
     Q₇ × Q₇, the unramified quadratic extension of Q₁₃.
     Teacher explains: (a) Every x ∈ Qₚ^× is pⁿu with n = vₚ(x), u ∈ Zₚ^×.
     Reduction Zₚ^× → F_p^× is onto with kernel 1 + pZₚ, and it SPLITS:
     xᵖ⁻¹ − 1 has p − 1 simple roots mod p, which lift by Hensel, so μ_(p−1)
     ⊆ Zₚ maps isomorphically onto F_p^×. Hence u = ω(u)·⟨u⟩ with ⟨u⟩ ∈
     1 + pZₚ. (b) For p odd every element a of 1 + pZₚ is a square (Hensel
     on x² − a at x = 1: f′(1) = 2 is a unit), and μ_(p−1)/(squares) ≅ Z/2.
     With the factor Z/2Z from pᶻ we get Qₚ^×/Qₚ^×² ≅ (Z/2)², classes
     1, u, p, up. The three non-trivial classes give the three quadratic
     extensions: Qₚ(√u) has residue field F_(p²) (x² − u is irreducible
     mod p): UNRAMIFIED, e = 1, f = 2; x² − p and x² − up are Eisenstein:
     TOTALLY RAMIFIED, e = 2, f = 1. (c) For p = 2, x² − a has f′ = 2x ≡ 0,
     so Hensel needs |a − 1|₂ < |2|₂² = 1/4: a unit is a square iff a ≡ 1
     (mod 8). Z₂^×/Z₂^×² ≅ (Z/8)^× = {±1, ±5}, and with 2ᶻ we get 8 classes,
     7 quadratic extensions; the unramified one is Q₂(√5) = Q₂(√−3) =
     Q₂(ζ₃). (d) Q(√−5) ⊗ Qₚ = Qₚ[x]/(x² + 5). p = 3: −5 ≡ 1 is a square mod
     3, so √−5 ∈ Q₃ and the algebra is Q₃ × Q₃ (3 splits, D4). p = 7: −5 ≡ 2
     ≡ 3² (mod 7): Q₇ × Q₇ (split). p = 13: −5 ≡ 8 is a non-residue mod 13
     (squares 1, 4, 9, 3, 12, 10): the field Q₁₃(√−5), unramified of degree
     2 (13 inert). p = 2: −5 ≡ 3 (mod 8) is in the class of −5 ≠ 5, so
     Q₂(√−5) is ramified (2 | d_K = −20). This is the general rule K ⊗ Qₚ ≅
     ∏_{𝔓|p} K_𝔓 with [K_𝔓 : Qₚ] = e_𝔓f_𝔓. Common mistakes: thinking Qₚ(√u)
     depends on the choice of non-residue u (it does not: u/u′ is a
     square); forgetting that √−1 ∈ Q₅ (so Q₅(i) is not an extension).
     Now you try: are 7, −7 and 6 squares in Q₃? in Q₂? (Q₃: 7 yes,
     −7 no, 6 no; Q₂: 7 no, −7 yes, 6 no)

D12. (a) Define the adeles A_Q and prove A_Q = Q + (Ẑ × R) with Q ∩ (Ẑ ×
     R) = Z; deduce that Q is discrete and A_Q/Q compact. Write a = (a_∞ =
     π, a₂ = 1/4, a₃ = 1/3, aₚ = 0 otherwise) as r + b, b ∈ Ẑ × [0, 1). (b)
     Prove J_Q = Q^× × (R_{>0} × Ẑ^×) and decompose the idele (a_∞ = 3, a₂ =
     1/4, a₃ = 6, aₚ = 1 otherwise); find its content. (c) Explain why J_K
     does not carry the subspace topology and how J_K sees Cl(K).
     Answer: (a) r = 31/12, b_∞ = π − 31/12 ≈ 0.52493. (b) a = (3/4)·u with
     u_∞ = 4, u₂ = 1/3, u₃ = 8, uₚ = 4/3; content |a| = 4.
     Teacher explains: A_Q = {(a_∞, a₂, a₃, ...) : aₚ ∈ Zₚ for all but
     finitely many p}, with basic open sets U_∞ × ∏_{p∈S} Uₚ × ∏_{p∉S} Zₚ;
     Q sits inside diagonally (a rational is in Zₚ for all p not dividing
     its denominator). (a) Given a, let S = {p : aₚ ∉ Zₚ}. For p ∈ S the
     principal part rₚ ∈ Z[1/p] of aₚ satisfies aₚ − rₚ ∈ Zₚ, and rₚ ∈ Z_q
     for q ≠ p; so r = ∑_{p∈S} rₚ ∈ Q has a − r ∈ Ẑ × R. Adjusting r by an
     integer puts the real part in [0, 1). If r ∈ Q ∩ (Ẑ × R) then r ∈ Zₚ
     for all p: r ∈ Z. Hence A_Q/Q ≅ (Ẑ × R)/Z, the continuous image of
     the compact Ẑ × [0, 1]; and Q ∩ (Ẑ × (−½, ½)) = {0}, so Q is discrete.
     For the example: r₂ = 1/4, r₃ = 1/3, r = 7/12; check 1/4 − 7/12 = −1/3
     ∈ Z₂, 1/3 − 7/12 = −1/4 ∈ Z₃, −7/12 ∈ Zₚ (p ≥ 5). π − 7/12 ≈ 2.558, so
     take r = 7/12 + 2 = 31/12, b_∞ = π − 31/12 ≈ 0.52493. (b) Ideles:
     aₚ ∈ Zₚ^× for almost all p. Given a, put q = sign(a_∞)∏ₚ p^(vₚ(aₚ)) ∈
     Q^× (a finite product); then q⁻¹a has positive real part and unit
     p-parts. Uniqueness: a positive rational that is a p-adic unit for
     every p is 1. Example: q = 2⁻²·3¹ = 3/4, u = a/q: u_∞ = 4, u₂ = (1/4)/
     (3/4) = 1/3 ∈ Z₂^×, u₃ = 6·4/3 = 8 ∈ Z₃^×, uₚ = 4/3 ∈ Zₚ^× (p ≥ 5).
     Content |a| = |3|·|1/4|₂·|6|₃ = 3·4·(1/3) = 4 = u_∞ (|q| = 1 by the
     PRODUCT FORMULA ∏_v|q|_v = 1). So C_Q = J_Q/Q^× ≅ R_{>0} × Ẑ^×, and
     C_Q¹ ≅ Ẑ^× is compact. (c) Let aₙ have pₙ (the n-th prime) at the
     place pₙ and 1 elsewhere. In A_Q, aₙ → 1 (aₙ − 1 is integral
     everywhere and 0 at any fixed finite set of places for large n), but
     aₙ⁻¹ has 1/pₙ ∉ Z_(pₙ) and does not converge: inversion is not
     continuous, so J_K gets the restricted product topology (equivalently,
     embed a ↦ (a, a⁻¹) in A_K × A_K). The map J_K → I_K, a ↦ ∏𝔭^(v_𝔭(a_𝔭)),
     is onto with kernel U_K = ∏_{v|∞}K_v^× × ∏O_𝔭^×; principal ideles go
     to principal ideals, so J_K/(K^×U_K) ≅ Cl(K). Common mistakes:
     treating A_Q as the full product ∏Qₚ × R (then Q would not be
     discrete); thinking |a| = 1 for every idele (only for elements of J¹).
     Now you try: the content of (a_∞ = 1/2, a₂ = 8, a₅ = 5, aₚ = 1 else).
     (1/2 · 1/8 · 1/5 = 1/80)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Rings of integers and discriminants. Give d_K (a-j), disc(f) (k-n),
    d_K (o-s), and answer t)-v).
    a) Q(i)          b) Q(√2)         c) Q(√−3)        d) Q(√5)
    e) Q(√−6)        f) Q(√15)        g) Q(√−19)       h) Q(√33)
    i) Q(√12)        j) Q(√−18)
    k) x³ − x − 1    l) x³ + x + 1    m) x³ − 3x + 1   n) x³ − 2
    o) Q(ζ₅)         p) Q(ζ₇)         q) Q(ζ₈)         r) Q(ζ₁₂)
    s) Q(ζ₉)
    t) the sign of d_K for Q(⁴√2), Q(ζ₇), Q(∛2)
    u) is O_K = Z[α] for α a root of x³ − x − 1? of x³ − 3x + 1?
    v) can 12 or −15 be the discriminant of a number field? can 2?
    Answers:
      a) −4      b) 8       c) −3      d) 5       e) −24     f) 60
      g) −19     h) 33      i) 12 (= Q(√3))       j) −8 (= Q(√−2))
      k) −23     l) −31     m) 81 = 3⁴           n) −108
      o) 125 = 5³           p) −7⁵ = −16807       q) 256 = 2⁸
      r) 144                s) −3⁹ = −19683
      t) −, −, − (sign (−1)^r₂ with r₂ = 1, 3, 1)
      u) yes (disc −23 squarefree); yes (x ↦ x − 1 gives x³ − 3x² + 3,
         Eisenstein at 3)
      v) 12 yes (Q(√3)); −15 yes (Q(√−15)); 2 no (≢ 0, 1 mod 4)

M2. Norms, traces, minimal polynomials, integrality.
    a) N and Tr of 3 + 2√5              b) N, Tr, min poly of (1 + √−7)/2
    c) N(1 + i), N(3 + 4i)              d) N(5 + 2√6)
    e) min poly, N, Tr of √2 + √3       f) N(1 + ∛2), N(2 + ∛2)
    g) N(a + b∛2 + c∛4) in general; then N(1 + ∛2 + ∛4)
    h) Tr(∛2), Tr(∛4), Tr(∛2·∛4) in Q(∛2)
    i) Tr_{Q(ζ₇)/Q}(ζ₇), N(1 − ζ₇), N(1 + ζ₇)
    j) Is (1 + √3)/2 integral?          k) Is (1 + √5)/2 integral?
    l) Is (3 + 4i)/5 integral?          m) Is (1 + √−3)/2 a root of unity?
    n) Is (1 + ∛2 + ∛4)/3 integral?     o) disc(1, ∛2, ∛4)
    p) Tr and N of ζ₅ + ζ₅⁻¹ in Q(√5)
    Answers:
      a) −11, 6              b) 2, 1, x² − x + 2        c) 2, 25
      d) 1 (a unit)          e) x⁴ − 10x² + 1, 1, 0      f) 3, 10
      g) a³ + 2b³ + 4c³ − 6abc; 1 (a unit)
      h) 0, 0, 6             i) −1, 7, 1 (1 + ζ₇ is a unit: Φ₇(−1) = 1)
      j) no (norm −1/2)      k) yes (x² − x − 1)
      l) no (norm 1 but trace 6/5)                m) yes: ζ₆ (x² − x + 1)
      n) no (norm 1/27)      o) −108
      p) −1 and −1 (ζ₅ + ζ₅⁻¹ = (−1 + √5)/2, root of x² + x − 1)

M3. Splitting in quadratic fields: split (S), inert (I) or ramified (R)?
    Give the factorisation, by elements where the ideals are principal.
    a) Q(i), 2       b) Q(i), 5       c) Q(i), 7       d) Q(i), 13
    e) Q(√2), 7      f) Q(√2), 3      g) Q(√2), 17     h) Q(√−3), 7
    i) Q(√−3), 5     j) Q(√−3), 3     k) Q(√−7), 2     l) Q(√5), 2
    m) Q(√5), 11     n) Q(√5), 5      o) Q(√13), 3     p) Q(√17), 2
    q) Q(√−23), 13   r) Q(√10), 3     s) Q(√−6), 5     t) Q(√−10), 7
    Answers:
      a) R: (1 + i)²              b) S: (2 + i)(2 − i)
      c) I                        d) S: (3 + 2i)(3 − 2i)
      e) S: (3 + √2)(3 − √2)      f) I (2 is not a square mod 3)
      g) S: (5 + 2√2)(5 − 2√2)    h) S: 7 = N((5 + √−3)/2)
      i) I                        j) R: (√−3)²
      k) S: 2 = ((1 + √−7)/2)((1 − √−7)/2)
      l) I (5 ≡ 5 mod 8)          m) S: 11 = N((7 + √5)/2)
      n) R: (√5)²                 o) S: 3 = −((1 + √13)/2)((1 − √13)/2)

M11. Adeles and ideles of Q.
     a) adele? (i) aₚ = 1/p for all p (ii) aₚ = p for all p, a_∞ = 0

        a₇ = 1/7, else 1); check |a| = u_∞

        else 1)

        Z in Z₂ × Z₃ × R; Q in Q₂ × Q₃ × R

     i) vol(A_K/K) = covol(O_K ⊆ K ⊗ R) for K = Q, Q(i), Q(√−5), Q(√2)
     j) J_Q/Q^×; the order of J_K/K^×U_K for K = Q(√−5), Q(√−23)

M12. True (T) or false (F)? Give a counterexample for F.
     a) Every Dedekind domain is a UFD.
     b) Every PID is a Dedekind domain.
     c) Z[√−3] is the ring of integers of Q(√−3).
     d) Every ring of integers O_K is of the form Z[α].
     e) An irreducible element of O_K generates a prime ideal.
     f) Every ideal of a Dedekind domain is generated by ≤ 2 elements.
     g) If x² − d ≡ ∏gᵢ^eᵢ mod p, then p factors in Q(√d) as ∏(p, gᵢ(√d))^eᵢ.
     h) Every number field K ≠ Q has a ramified prime.
     i) Every real quadratic field has a unit of norm −1.
     j) O_K^× is finite iff K = Q or K is imaginary quadratic.
     k) If p splits completely in K then p ∤ d_K.
     l) Decomposition groups are always cyclic.
     m) Qₚ can be made into an ordered field.
     n) Every |·|ₚ-Cauchy sequence in Q converges in Q.
     o) Z is dense in Zₚ, and Zₚ is compact and open in Qₚ.
     p) J_Q/Q^× is compact.
     q) J_Q with the subspace topology of A_Q is a topological group.
     r) The Hasse principle holds for all homogeneous cubic equations.
     s) Cl(K) is trivial iff O_K is a UFD.
     Answers:
       a) F: Z[√−5]                  b) T
       c) F: O_K = Z[(1 + √−3)/2]
       d) F: Dedekind's field x³ + x² − 2x + 8 (2 divides every index)
       e) F: 2 in Z[√−5]             f) T
       g) F: d = 5, p = 2 ((x + 1)² mod 2, but 2 is inert)
       h) T (Minkowski: |d_K| > 1)  i) F: Q(√3) (2 + √3 has norm 1)
       j) T (r₁ + r₂ − 1 = 0)       k) T (e = 1 for every 𝔓 | p)
       l) F: 2 in Q(ζ₈): D = (Z/8)^× ≅ Z/2 × Z/2 (only D/I is cyclic)
       m) F: 1 − p is a square in Qₚ (p odd), −7 in Q₂, so −1 is a sum of
          squares
       n) F: the Hensel approximations 2, 7, 57, 182, 2057, ... to √−1
          (5-adically)
       o) T
       p) F: the content maps it onto R_{>0}; J¹/Q^× ≅ Ẑ^× is compact
       q) F: inversion is not continuous (D12)
       r) F: Selmer's 3x³ + 4y³ + 5z³ = 0
       s) T (a Dedekind domain is a UFD iff it is a PID)

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: make every abstract statement concrete in Z[√−5] and Z[i]
  before generalising: draw the lattice O_K in the plane, shade an ideal
  as a sublattice, and count cosets to see N(𝔞). Always check "is O_K =
  Z[α]?" before Dedekind-Kummer, always write ∑eᵢfᵢ = n as a sanity
  check, and for class groups follow the fixed recipe: Minkowski bound →
  primes below it → which are principal (solve the norm equation) →
  relations from elements of small composite norm. For p-adics, practise
  digit-by-digit Hensel lifting by hand and think of Zₚ as "integers with
  infinitely many digits to the left"; the isosceles rule answers most
  |·|ₚ questions at once. Harder: prove that O_K is Dedekind and that
  fractional ideals are invertible; prove finiteness of h_K and the full
  unit theorem; prove Dedekind's discriminant theorem via the different;
  compute Cl(Q(√−65)) ≅ Z/2 × Z/4 and Cl(Q(√79)) ≅ Z/3; show that
  Z[ζ₂₃] is not a UFD (Kummer, h = 3); prove Ostrowski's theorem and
  Krasner's lemma; show that A_K/K is compact and J¹/K^× is compact and
  deduce both finiteness theorems from it; state the analytic class
  number formula and check it for Q(i) (L(1, χ₋₄) = π/4); and read Tate's
  thesis to see ζ-functions as integrals over the ideles.


================================================================================
TOPIC 12.16 — ANALYTIC NUMBER THEORY: RIEMANN ZETA FUNCTION, L-FUNCTIONS,
SIEVE METHODS, CIRCLE METHOD
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - ARITHMETIC FUNCTIONS AND DIRICHLET SERIES: μ, φ, τ, σ, Λ (Λ(pᵏ) = log p,
    else 0), Dirichlet convolution (f ∗ g)(n) = ∑_{d|n} f(d)g(n/d), with
    μ ∗ 1 = ε, Λ ∗ 1 = log, φ ∗ 1 = id. F(s) = ∑ f(n)n⁻ˢ multiplies under
    ∗: ∑τ(n)n⁻ˢ = ζ², ∑μ(n)n⁻ˢ = 1/ζ, ∑Λ(n)n⁻ˢ = −ζ′/ζ, ∑φ(n)n⁻ˢ =
    ζ(s − 1)/ζ(s). PARTIAL (ABEL) SUMMATION and the DIRICHLET HYPERBOLA
    METHOD: ∑_{n≤x} τ(n) = x log x + (2γ − 1)x + O(√x).
  - RIEMANN ZETA: ζ(s) = ∑n⁻ˢ = ∏_p (1 − p⁻ˢ)⁻¹ for Re s > 1 (EULER
    PRODUCT; so ζ(s) ≠ 0 there and ∑1/p = ∞). Continuation: ζ(s) =
    s/(s − 1) − s∫₁^∞ {x}x⁻ˢ⁻¹ dx on Re s > 0, simple pole at s = 1 with
    residue 1, ζ(s) = 1/(s − 1) + γ + O(s − 1); ζ(σ) < 0 on (0, 1).
  - FUNCTIONAL EQUATION (via θ(1/t) = √t θ(t)): Λ(s) = π^(−s/2)Γ(s/2)ζ(s)
    = Λ(1 − s); ξ(s) = ½s(s − 1)Λ(s) is entire of order 1. Asymmetric form
    ζ(s) = 2ˢπˢ⁻¹ sin(πs/2)Γ(1 − s)ζ(1 − s). TRIVIAL ZEROS −2, −4, ...;
    ζ(0) = −½, ζ(−n) = −Bₙ₊₁/(n + 1) (ζ(−1) = −1/12); NON-TRIVIAL ZEROS
    ρ = β + iγ lie in 0 < β < 1, symmetric under ρ ↦ ρ̄, 1 − ρ.
  - ZEROS: N(T) = (T/2π)log(T/2π) − T/2π + 7/8 + S(T) + O(1/T), S(T) =
    O(log T) (RIEMANN-VON MANGOLDT). First zero ½ + 14.134725i. RIEMANN
    HYPOTHESIS (RH): all β = ½. ζ(1 + it) ≠ 0 (3-4-1 inequality 3 + 4cos θ
    + cos 2θ ≥ 0); de la Vallée Poussin ZERO-FREE REGION σ > 1 −
    c/log(|t| + 2).
  - CHEBYSHEV AND THE PNT: θ(x) = ∑_{p≤x} log p, ψ(x) = ∑_{n≤x} Λ(n) =
    log lcm(1, ..., x); Chebyshev: ψ(x) ≍ x (via the binomial C(2n, n)).
    PRIME NUMBER THEOREM: ψ(x) ~ x ⇔ π(x) ~ x/log x ⇔ M(x) = ∑_{n≤x} μ(n)
    = o(x); with error π(x) = li(x) + O(x e^(−c√log x)). Mertens: ∑_{p≤x}
    1/p = log log x + M + o(1), ∏_{p≤x}(1 − 1/p) ~ e^(−γ)/log x.
  - EXPLICIT FORMULA (Perron + contour shift): ψ₀(x) = x − ∑_ρ x^ρ/ρ −
    log 2π − ½log(1 − x⁻²). RH ⇔ ψ(x) = x + O(√x log²x) ⇔ π(x) = li(x) +
    O(√x log x). Each zero is an "oscillation" of amplitude x^β/|ρ|.
  - DIRICHLET CHARACTERS mod q: homomorphisms (Z/q)* → C*, extended by 0;
    φ(q) of them; ORTHOGONALITY ∑_{χ} χ̄(a)χ(n) = φ(q)𝟙_{n≡a}; principal
    χ₀; parity χ(−1) = ±1; CONDUCTOR and PRIMITIVE characters; real
    (quadratic) characters are Kronecker symbols χ_D.
  - L-FUNCTIONS: L(s, χ) = ∑χ(n)n⁻ˢ = ∏(1 − χ(p)p⁻ˢ)⁻¹; entire for χ ≠
    χ₀. L(1, χ) ≠ 0 (complex χ: ∏_χ L(σ, χ) ≥ 1; real χ: ∑_{d|n}χ(d) ≥ 0
    and Landau's theorem) ⇒ DIRICHLET'S THEOREM: ∑_{p≡a (q)} 1/p = ∞ for
    (a, q) = 1. PNT in APs π(x; q, a) ~ li(x)/φ(q); SIEGEL-WALFISZ
    (uniform for q ≤ (log x)ᴬ, ineffective); possible SIEGEL ZERO.
  - GAUSS SUMS: τ(χ) = ∑_a χ(a)e(a/q), e(x) = e^(2πix); |τ(χ)| = √q for
    primitive χ; τ(χ_p) = √p or i√p (p ≡ 1 or 3 mod 4). Functional
    equation of L(s, χ) with root number τ(χ)/(iᵏ√q). PÓLYA-VINOGRADOV:
    |∑_{n≤N} χ(n)| ≤ √q log q. CLASS NUMBER FORMULA h(−d) = w√d L(1,χ)/2π.
  - SIEVES: S(𝒜, 𝒫, z) = #{n ∈ 𝒜 : (n, P(z)) = 1}. LEGENDRE/ERATOSTHENES:
    S = ∑_{d|P(z)} μ(d)|𝒜_d|, error 2^π(z). BRUN'S pure sieve (truncated
    Möbius): twin primes ≤ x are ≪ x(log log x)²/log²x, so ∑_{twin} 1/p <
    ∞. SELBERG Λ² SIEVE: S ≤ X/G(z) + ∑_{d<z²}3^ω(d)|r_d|; gives π(x) ≤
    (2 + o(1))x/log x, twin primes ≤ (8C₂ + o(1))x/log²x, BRUN-TITCHMARSH.
  - LARGE SIEVE: ∑_{q≤Q}∑*_{a mod q} |∑_{n≤N} aₙ e(an/q)|² ≤ (N + Q²)∑|aₙ|².
    BOMBIERI-VINOGRADOV: ∑_{q≤Q} max_{(a,q)=1}|ψ(x; q, a) − x/φ(q)| ≪
    x(log x)⁻ᴬ for Q = √x(log x)⁻ᴮ ("GRH on average", level ½). PARITY
    PROBLEM; Chen: every large even n = p + P₂.
  - CIRCLE METHOD: r(n) = ∫₀¹ S(α)ˢ e(−nα) dα. MAJOR ARCS near a/q (small
    q) give SINGULAR SERIES 𝔖(n) × SINGULAR INTEGRAL; MINOR ARCS are
    bounded by WEYL/VINOGRADOV estimates for exponential sums. Ternary
    Goldbach (Vinogradov 1937, Helfgott 2013): R₃(n) ~ ½𝔖(n)n² for odd n.
    WARING: r_{s,k}(n) ~ (Γ(1 + 1/k)ˢ/Γ(s/k))𝔖(n)n^(s/k − 1) for s large;
    g(2) = 4, g(3) = 9, g(4) = 19, G(4) = 16.
  - EXPONENTIAL SUMS: ∑_{a mod q} e(an/q) = q𝟙_{q|n}; RAMANUJAN SUMS
    c_q(n) = μ(q/(q,n))φ(q)/φ(q/(q,n)); quadratic Gauss sums |∑e(an²/p)| =
    √p; WEYL'S INEQUALITY via squaring/differencing.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. (a) Prove the Euler product ζ(s) = ∏_p (1 − p⁻ˢ)⁻¹ for Re s > 1 and
    deduce ζ(s) ≠ 0 there. (b) Deduce that ∑_p 1/p diverges. (c) Compute
    ∏_{p≤5}(1 − p⁻²)⁻¹ and compare with ζ(2).
    Answer: (a) unique factorisation; (b) log ζ(σ) = ∑_p p^(−σ) + O(1)
    → ∞ as σ → 1⁺; (c) 900/576 = 1.5625 versus π²/6 ≈ 1.64493.
    Teacher explains: (a) Fix σ = Re s > 1. Each factor is a geometric
    series (1 − p⁻ˢ)⁻¹ = ∑_{k≥0} p^(−ks), absolutely convergent. Multiplying
    the finitely many factors with p ≤ P and using UNIQUE FACTORISATION,
       ∏_{p≤P}(1 − p⁻ˢ)⁻¹ = ∑_{n : all prime factors ≤ P} n⁻ˢ,
    so |ζ(s) − ∏_{p≤P}| ≤ ∑_{n>P} n^(−σ) → 0 as P → ∞. The product
    converges absolutely (∑|p⁻ˢ| < ∞) and no factor vanishes, so ζ(s) ≠ 0.
    (b) Taking logarithms for real σ > 1,
       log ζ(σ) = ∑_p ∑_{k≥1} p^(−kσ)/k = ∑_p p^(−σ) + E,
    with 0 ≤ E ≤ ∑_p ∑_{k≥2} p⁻ᵏ = ∑_p 1/(p(p − 1)) ≤ 1. Since ζ(σ) ≥
    ∫₁^∞ x^(−σ) dx = 1/(σ − 1) → ∞, ∑_p p^(−σ) → ∞; as p^(−σ) < 1/p,
    ∑1/p = ∞. (This is Euler's analytic proof that there are infinitely
    many primes; Mertens refines it to ∑_{p≤x} 1/p = log log x + 0.2615 +
    o(1).) (c) (4/3)(9/8)(25/24) = 900/576 = 1.5625; the missing factors
    p ≥ 7 bring it up to ζ(2) = π²/6. Common mistakes: taking logs of the
    Euler product for Re s ≤ 1 (the product diverges there); forgetting
    that "product converges" means the limit is NON-zero. Now you try:
    compute ∏_{p≤3}(1 − p⁻²)⁻¹ and show ∑ μ(n)n⁻ˢ = 1/ζ(s) for Re s > 1.
    (3/2; invert each Euler factor: 1/ζ = ∏(1 − p⁻ˢ))

D2. (a) Show ζ(s) = s/(s − 1) − s∫₁^∞ {x}x⁻ˢ⁻¹ dx for Re s > 1 and use it
    to continue ζ to Re s > 0 with a simple pole of residue 1 at s = 1.
    (b) Show ζ(s) = 1/(s − 1) + γ + O(s − 1). (c) Using η(s) = ∑(−1)ⁿ⁻¹n⁻ˢ
    prove ζ(σ) < 0 for 0 < σ < 1.
    Answer: (a) partial summation with ⌊x⌋ = x − {x}; (b) 1 − ∫₁^∞ {x}x⁻²
    dx = γ; (c) η(σ) > 0 and η = (1 − 2^(1−s))ζ with 1 − 2^(1−σ) < 0.
    Teacher explains: (a) For Re s > 1, ∑_{n≤N} n⁻ˢ = ∫_{1⁻}^N x⁻ˢ d⌊x⌋.
    Integrating by parts and letting N → ∞ (⌊N⌋N⁻ˢ → 0),
       ζ(s) = s∫₁^∞ ⌊x⌋x⁻ˢ⁻¹ dx = s∫₁^∞ x⁻ˢ dx − s∫₁^∞ {x}x⁻ˢ⁻¹ dx
            = s/(s − 1) − s∫₁^∞ {x}x⁻ˢ⁻¹ dx.
    Since 0 ≤ {x} < 1, the last integral converges absolutely and locally
    uniformly for Re s > 0, so it is holomorphic there: the formula gives
    the continuation, and s/(s − 1) = 1 + 1/(s − 1) shows the only pole is
    at 1, residue 1. (b) At s = 1 the regular part is 1 − ∫₁^∞ {x}x⁻² dx.
    Now ∫₁^N {x}x⁻² dx = ∫₁^N x⁻¹dx − ∑_{n<N} n∫ₙ^(n+1) x⁻² dx = log N −
    ∑_{n<N} 1/(n + 1) → 1 − γ. So the constant term is γ ≈ 0.57722.
    (c) η(s) = (1 − 2·2⁻ˢ)ζ(s) for Re s > 1 (remove twice the even terms);
    η converges for real σ > 0 as an alternating series with decreasing
    terms, so by continuation the identity holds on (0, 1). There
    η(σ) ≥ 1 − 2^(−σ) > 0 (group terms in pairs) and 1 − 2^(1−σ) < 0, so
    ζ(σ) < 0: NO real zeros in (0, 1). Numerically ζ(½) = −1.46035 and
    η(½) = (√2 − 1)·1.46035 ≈ 0.60490. Common mistakes: thinking ∑n⁻ˢ
    itself converges for 0 < Re s < 1 (it never does); forgetting the
    factor s in front of the integral. Now you try: evaluate ∫₁^∞ {x}x⁻²
    dx. (1 − γ ≈ 0.42278)

D3. Using θ(t) = ∑_{n∈Z} e^(−πn²t) and θ(1/t) = √t θ(t), prove that Λ(s)
    = π^(−s/2)Γ(s/2)ζ(s) extends to C ∖ {0, 1} with Λ(s) = Λ(1 − s).
    Deduce the trivial zeros, ζ(0), ζ(−1) and ζ(−3).
    Answer: Λ(s) = −1/s − 1/(1 − s) + ∫₁^∞ ω(t)(t^(s/2) + t^((1−s)/2))
    dt/t, ω = (θ − 1)/2; ζ(−2k) = 0, ζ(0) = −½, ζ(−1) = −1/12, ζ(−3) =
    1/120.
    Teacher explains: For Re s > 1, ∫₀^∞ e^(−πn²t) t^(s/2) dt/t =
    π^(−s/2)Γ(s/2)n⁻ˢ; summing (Tonelli), Λ(s) = ∫₀^∞ ω(t)t^(s/2) dt/t with
    ω(t) = ∑_{n≥1} e^(−πn²t). The theta relation reads ω(1/t) = √t ω(t) +
    (√t − 1)/2. Split ∫₀^∞ = ∫₀¹ + ∫₁^∞ and put t ↦ 1/t in ∫₀¹:
       ∫₀¹ ω(t)t^(s/2) dt/t = ∫₁^∞ ω(t)t^((1−s)/2) dt/t + 1/(s − 1) − 1/s.
    Hence Λ(s) = 1/(s − 1) − 1/s + ∫₁^∞ ω(t)(t^(s/2) + t^((1−s)/2)) dt/t.
    Because ω(t) = O(e^(−πt)), the integral is entire, and the whole
    expression is visibly symmetric under s ↔ 1 − s. CONSEQUENCES:
    (i) At s = −2k (k ≥ 1) Γ(s/2) has a pole while Λ is finite, so
    ζ(−2k) = 0: the trivial zeros. (ii) Near s = 0, Λ(s) ~ −1/s and Γ(s/2)
    ~ 2/s, so ζ(0)·2/s ~ −1/s: ζ(0) = −½. (iii) In the asymmetric form ζ(s)
    = 2ˢπˢ⁻¹ sin(πs/2)Γ(1 − s)ζ(1 − s):
       ζ(−1) = ½·π⁻²·sin(−π/2)·Γ(2)·ζ(2) = −(1/(2π²))(π²/6) = −1/12,
       ζ(−3) = ⅛·π⁻⁴·sin(−3π/2)·Γ(4)·ζ(4) = 6/(8·90) = 1/120.
    The non-trivial zeros are the zeros of ξ(s) = ½s(s − 1)Λ(s); they lie
    in the critical strip and are symmetric about Re s = ½ and the real
    axis. Also ξ(0) = ξ(1) = ½. Common mistakes: "ζ(−1) = −1/12 means
    1 + 2 + 3 + ... = −1/12" (the series diverges; −1/12 is the value of the
    continuation); forgetting sin(πs/2) at odd negative s. Now you try:
    compute ζ(−5). (2⁻⁵π⁻⁶·(−1)·120·π⁶/945 = −1/252)

D4. (a) Show ψ(n) = log lcm(1, 2, ..., n). (b) Prove Chebyshev's bounds
    θ(x) ≤ x log 4 and ψ(2n) ≥ n log 4 − log(2n + 1), and deduce π(x) ≍
    x/log x. (c) Compute ψ(30) and θ(30).
    Answer: (a) v_p(lcm) = ⌊log n/log p⌋; (b) via C(2m + 1, m) ≤ 4ᵐ and
    C(2n, n) ≥ 4ⁿ/(2n + 1); (c) ψ(30) = log 2329089562800 ≈ 28.4765,
    θ(30) = log 6469693230 ≈ 22.5904.
    Teacher explains: (a) The power of p in lcm(1, ..., n) is the largest
    k with pᵏ ≤ n, i.e. the number of prime powers pᵏ ≤ n, and each such
    pᵏ contributes Λ(pᵏ) = log p to ψ(n). (b) UPPER: N = C(2m + 1, m) equals
    C(2m + 1, m + 1), both occur in (1 + 1)^(2m+1), so N ≤ 2^(2m) = 4ᵐ. Every
    prime m + 1 < p ≤ 2m + 1 divides the numerator (2m + 1)!/(m + 1)! but
    not m!, so ∏_{m+1<p≤2m+1} p ≤ 4ᵐ, i.e. θ(2m + 1) − θ(m + 1) ≤ m log 4.
    Induction on n (split n = 2m + 1 or use θ(2m) = θ(2m − 1)) gives θ(n) ≤
    n log 4. LOWER: C(2n, n) is the largest of 2n + 1 terms summing to
    4ⁿ, so it is ≥ 4ⁿ/(2n + 1). Each prime power dividing C(2n, n) is
    ≤ 2n (Legendre's formula: v_p = ∑_k (⌊2n/pᵏ⌋ − 2⌊n/pᵏ⌋), each term 0
    or 1 and 0 once pᵏ > 2n), so C(2n, n) | lcm(1..2n) and ψ(2n) ≥ n log 4
    − log(2n + 1). Since ψ(x) = θ(x) + O(√x log x), π(x) ≥ θ(x)/log x and
    θ(x) ≥ (1 − ε)(log x)(π(x) − x^(1−ε)), one gets (log 2 − ε)x/log x ≤
    π(x) ≤ (log 4 + ε)x/log x for large x. (c) lcm(1..30) =
    2⁴·3³·5²·7·11·13·17·19·23·29 = 2329089562800 and 30# = 6469693230.
    Common mistakes: confusing θ (one log p per prime) with ψ (one per
    prime power); forgetting that the difference ψ − θ = θ(√x) + θ(x^(1/3))
    + ... is only O(√x log x). Now you try: ψ(10) and θ(10). (log 2520 ≈
    7.8320, log 210 ≈ 5.3471)

D5. Prove that ζ(1 + it) ≠ 0 for real t ≠ 0, using 3 + 4cos θ + cos 2θ ≥
    0. Explain how this leads to the prime number theorem.
    Answer: |ζ(σ)³ζ(σ + it)⁴ζ(σ + 2it)| ≥ 1 for σ > 1; a zero at 1 + it
    would force the left side → 0.
    Teacher explains: For σ > 1, log ζ(s) = ∑_n (Λ(n)/log n) n⁻ˢ, so
       log|ζ(σ + it)| = ∑_n (Λ(n)/log n) n^(−σ) cos(t log n).
    Since 3 + 4cos θ + cos 2θ = 2(1 + cos θ)² ≥ 0, with θ = t log n,
       3 log ζ(σ) + 4 log|ζ(σ + it)| + log|ζ(σ + 2it)| ≥ 0,
    i.e. |ζ(σ)³ζ(σ + it)⁴ζ(σ + 2it)| ≥ 1. Suppose ζ(1 + it) = 0 with t ≠ 0.
    As σ → 1⁺: ζ(σ)³ ~ (σ − 1)⁻³ (simple pole), ζ(σ + it)⁴ = O((σ − 1)⁴)
    (a zero of order ≥ 1), and ζ(σ + 2it) stays bounded (1 + 2it is not
    the pole). The product is O(σ − 1) → 0, contradicting ≥ 1. The weights
    3, 4, 1 matter: 4 > 3 lets the zero beat the pole. ROUTE TO PNT: −ζ′/ζ
    = ∑Λ(n)n⁻ˢ then has only the simple pole at s = 1 (residue 1) on the
    closed half-plane Re s ≥ 1, and a Tauberian theorem (Wiener-Ikehara, or
    Newman's contour argument) gives ψ(x) ~ x, hence π(x) ~ x/log x. The
    quantitative version of the same inequality gives the zero-free region
    σ > 1 − c/log(|t| + 2) and π(x) = li(x) + O(x exp(−c√log x)). Common
    mistakes: using cos θ ≥ −1 alone (weights 1, 1 do not work); forgetting
    that the method needs t ≠ 0 for boundedness of ζ(σ + 2it). Now you
    try: evaluate 3 + 4cos θ + cos 2θ at θ = π and θ = 2π/3. (0 and ½)

D6. (a) State the explicit formula for ψ(x) and explain why RH is
    equivalent to ψ(x) = x + O(√x log²x). (b) Use the Riemann-von
    Mangoldt formula to estimate N(100) and compare with the true value 29.
    (c) Bound the contribution of the first pair of zeros at x = 100.
    Answer: (a) ψ₀(x) = x − ∑_ρ x^ρ/ρ − log 2π − ½log(1 − x⁻²);
    (b) ≈ 29.002; (c) ≤ 2·10/14.1347 ≈ 1.415.
    Teacher explains: (a) PERRON: for c > 1, ψ₀(x) = (1/2πi)∫_{(c)}
    (−ζ′/ζ)(s)xˢ ds/s (ψ₀ averages the jump at prime powers). Shift the
    line of integration to the left: the residues are x at s = 1 (pole of
    ζ), −x^ρ/ρ at each zero ρ, −(ζ′/ζ)(0) = −log 2π at s = 0, and
    x^(−2k)/(2k) at the trivial zeros, which sum to −½log(1 − x⁻²). If all
    β = ½ then |x^ρ| = √x, and the truncated formula ψ(x) = x −
    ∑_{|γ|≤T} x^ρ/ρ + O(x log²(xT)/T) with T = √x and ∑_{|γ|≤T} 1/|ρ| ≪
    log²T gives ψ(x) = x + O(√x log²x). Conversely, if ψ(x) − x = O(x^(½+ε))
    then −ζ′(s)/ζ(s) − s/(s − 1) = s∫₁^∞ (ψ(x) − x)x⁻ˢ⁻¹ dx is holomorphic
    for Re s > ½ + ε: no zeros there. (b) T/2π = 15.91549, log(T/2π) =
    2.76729, so N(100) ≈ 44.0428 − 15.9155 + 0.875 = 29.0023; the true count
    is 29 (S(100) is small). (c) The pair ρ = ½ ± 14.1347i contributes
    −2Re(x^ρ/ρ), of size at most 2√x/|ρ| = 20/14.1347 ≈ 1.415. Compare ψ(100)
    ≈ 94.045: the gap 100 − 94.045 needs many zeros plus log 2π ≈ 1.838.
    Common mistakes: treating ∑x^ρ/ρ as absolutely convergent (it is not:
    ∑1/|ρ| diverges; the sum is over |γ| ≤ T, T → ∞); forgetting that N(T)
    counts zeros with 0 < γ ≤ T only. Now you try: estimate N(50) and
    compare with the true value 10. (≈ 9.42)

D7. (a) Write down all Dirichlet characters mod 5 and mod 8. (b) State and
    check the orthogonality relation that detects n ≡ 2 (mod 5).
    (c) Which characters are primitive, real, odd?
    Answer: mod 5: χⱼ(2ᵏ) = iʲᵏ (j = 0..3); mod 8: four real characters
    χ₀, χ₋₄, χ₈, χ₋₈; 𝟙_{n≡2} = ¼∑_χ χ̄(2)χ(n).
    Teacher explains: (Z/5)* is cyclic, generated by 2 (2, 4, 3, 1), so a
    character is fixed by χ(2), a 4th root of unity. With χⱼ(2) = iʲ:
          a  :  1    2    3    4
          χ₀ :  1    1    1    1
          χ₁ :  1    i   −i   −1
          χ₂ :  1   −1   −1    1     (the Legendre symbol (a/5))
          χ₃ :  1   −i    i   −1
    (b) ∑_{χ mod q} χ̄(a)χ(n) = φ(q) if n ≡ a, 0 otherwise (for (a, q) = 1).
    Check a = 2, n = 3: ¼(1 + (−i)(−i) + (−1)(−1) + (i)(i)) = ¼(1 − 1 + 1 −
    1) = 0; n = 2: ¼(1 + 1 + 1 + 1) = 1. (c) Mod 5 (prime) every χ ≠ χ₀ is
    primitive; χ₂ is real and even (χ₂(4) = 1); χ₁, χ₃ = χ̄₁ are complex
    and odd (χ(−1) = χ(4) = −1). (Z/8)* = {1, 3, 5, 7} ≅ (Z/2)², so all
    four characters are real:
          a   :  1    3    5    7
          χ₀  :  1    1    1    1
          χ₋₄ :  1   −1    1   −1    (induced from mod 4, conductor 4)
          χ₈  :  1   −1   −1    1    ((2/n): even, primitive)
          χ₋₈ :  1    1   −1   −1    ((−2/n): odd, primitive)
    The number of primitive characters mod 8 is 2 = φ(8) − φ(4). WHY IT
    MATTERS: π(x; q, a) = (1/φ(q))∑_χ χ̄(a)∑_{p≤x}χ(p) + O(1) turns primes
    in progressions into sums over characters, and imprimitive χ can
    always be replaced by the primitive character inducing them. Common
    mistakes: forgetting χ(n) = 0 for (n, q) > 1; thinking a real character
    must be even. Now you try: how many characters mod 7 are real, and
    how many primitive characters are there mod 15? (2; 3 = 1·3)

D8. Prove Dirichlet's theorem: for (a, q) = 1, ∑_{p≡a (mod q)} 1/p = ∞.
    Include the proof that L(1, χ) ≠ 0 for complex and for real χ ≠ χ₀,
    and check L(1, χ₋₄) numerically.
    Answer: ∑_{p≡a} p^(−σ) = (1/φ(q))log(1/(σ − 1)) + O(1) as σ → 1⁺,
    provided every L(1, χ) ≠ 0; L(1, χ₋₄) = 1 − ⅓ + ⅕ − ... = π/4.
    Teacher explains: For σ > 1, by orthogonality,
       ∑_{p≡a} p^(−σ) = (1/φ(q)) ∑_χ χ̄(a) ∑_p χ(p)p^(−σ),
    and ∑_p χ(p)p^(−σ) = log L(σ, χ) + O(1). For χ₀ this is log ζ(σ) + O(1)
    → ∞. For χ ≠ χ₀, L(s, χ) is holomorphic near 1 (partial summation:
    the partial sums of χ are bounded), so log L(σ, χ) stays bounded IF
    L(1, χ) ≠ 0. COMPLEX χ: ∑_χ log L(σ, χ) = φ(q)∑_{pᵏ≡1} 1/(k p^(kσ))
    ≥ 0, so ∏_χ L(σ, χ) ≥ 1. If L(1, χ) = 0 with χ ≠ χ̄, then also
    L(1, χ̄) = 0: two zeros against the single pole of L(s, χ₀), and the
    product → 0 as σ → 1⁺. Contradiction. REAL χ: F(s) = ζ(s)L(s, χ) =
    ∑ r(n)n⁻ˢ with r(n) = ∑_{d|n} χ(d) multiplicative, r(pᵏ) = 1 + χ(p)
    + ... + χ(p)ᵏ ≥ 0, and r(m²) ≥ 1. If L(1, χ) = 0, the zero cancels the
    pole and F is entire. LANDAU: a Dirichlet series with non-negative
    coefficients is singular at its real abscissa of convergence, so F
    would converge for all real s > 0; but F(½) ≥ ∑ r(m²)/m ≥ ∑1/m = ∞.
    Contradiction. Numerically L(1, χ₋₄) = arctan 1 = π/4 ≈ 0.78540, and
    r(n)·4 = r₂(n), the number of ways to write n as a sum of two squares.
    Quantitatively π(x; q, a) ~ li(x)/φ(q); Siegel-Walfisz makes this
    uniform for q ≤ (log x)ᴬ but with an ineffective constant, because a
    real zero β very close to 1 (a SIEGEL ZERO) cannot be ruled out
    effectively. Data: π(10⁴; 4, 1) = 609 and π(10⁴; 4, 3) = 619
    (Chebyshev's bias). Common mistakes: forgetting that the real case
    needs a separate argument (the ∏ ≥ 1 trick only gives one zero, which
    the pole can absorb). Now you try: compute r(25) and r(9) for χ₋₄ and
    compare with r₂(25) = 12, r₂(9) = 4. (3 and 1)

D9. (a) Define the Gauss sum τ(χ) and prove |τ(χ)| = √q for primitive χ.
    (b) Compute τ(χ) for the Legendre symbol mod 5 and mod 3. (c) Prove the
    Pólya-Vinogradov inequality |∑_{M<n≤M+N} χ(n)| ≤ √q log q (q ≥ 3
    prime). (d) Use the class number formula to find h(−23).
    Answer: (a) Parseval; (b) √5 and i√3; (c) expand χ in additive
    characters; (d) L(1, χ₋₂₃) = 3π/√23, so h = 3.
    Teacher explains: (a) Put S(n) = ∑_{a mod q} χ(a)e(an/q). For (n, q) = 1
    substitute a ↦ an⁻¹: S(n) = χ̄(n)τ(χ); for primitive χ also S(n) = 0
    when (n, q) > 1. Parseval over n mod q gives ∑_n |S(n)|² = q∑_a |χ(a)|²
    = qφ(q), while the left side is φ(q)|τ(χ)|². So |τ(χ)|² = q. (b) mod 5:
    τ = e(1/5) − e(2/5) − e(3/5) + e(4/5) = 2cos(2π/5) − 2cos(4π/5) =
    2(0.30902) + 2(0.80902) = 2.23607 = √5. mod 3: e(1/3) − e(2/3) =
    2i sin(2π/3) = i√3. (Gauss: τ(χ_p) = √p if p ≡ 1, i√p if p ≡ 3 mod 4.)
    (c) For χ primitive, χ(n) = (1/τ(χ̄))∑_{a mod q} χ̄(a)e(an/q). Summing
    over M < n ≤ M + N and using |∑_n e(an/q)| ≤ 1/(2‖a/q‖),
       |∑χ(n)| ≤ (1/√q)∑_{a=1}^{q−1} 1/(2‖a/q‖) ≤ (1/√q)·q∑_{1≤a≤q/2} 1/a
    ≤ √q log q (with a little care in the harmonic sum). The bound is
    independent of N: character sums are "square-root cancelled" on long
    intervals. The same Gauss sum is the ROOT NUMBER in the functional
    equation Λ(s, χ) = (τ(χ)/(iᵏ√q))Λ(1 − s, χ̄), Λ(s, χ) = (q/π)^((s+k)/2)
    Γ((s + k)/2)L(s, χ), k = 0 (χ even) or 1 (χ odd). (d) h(−d) =
    w√d L(1, χ₋d)/(2π) with w = 2 for d > 4: h(−23) = 2√23(3π/√23)/(2π)
    = 3. Common mistakes: forgetting primitivity in S(n) = χ̄(n)τ(χ) (it
    fails for (n, q) > 1 otherwise); taking |τ| = √q for imprimitive χ
    (e.g. τ(χ₀ mod p) = μ(p) = −1). Now you try: h(−7) from L(1, χ₋₇) =
    π/√7. (1)

D10. (a) Derive Legendre's formula S(x, z) = ∑_{d|P(z)} μ(d)⌊x/d⌋ for
     the number of n ≤ x with no prime factor ≤ z, and use it to compute
     π(100). (b) Explain why the error term forces z to be tiny,
     and how Brun's truncation gives ∑_{p, p+2 prime} 1/p < ∞.
     Answer: (a) Φ(100; 2, 3, 5, 7) = 22, π(100) = 22 − 1 + 4 = 25;
     (b) error up to 2^π(z); Brun: π₂(x) ≪ x(log log x)²/log²x.
     Teacher explains: (a) Let P(z) = ∏_{p≤z} p. Since ∑_{d|m} μ(d) = 1 if
     m = 1 and 0 otherwise, 𝟙_{(n,P)=1} = ∑_{d|(n,P)} μ(d); summing over n ≤
     x gives S(x, z) = ∑_{d|P} μ(d)⌊x/d⌋. For x = 100, z = 10, P = 210:
        100 − (50 + 33 + 20 + 14) + (16 + 10 + 7 + 6 + 4 + 2)
            − (3 + 2 + 1 + 0) + 0 = 100 − 117 + 45 − 6 = 22.
     These 22 numbers are 1 and the 21 primes 11..97; so π(100) = 22 − 1 +
     π(10) = 25. (b) Writing ⌊x/d⌋ = x/d − {x/d} gives S = x∏_{p≤z}(1 −
     1/p) + E with |E| ≤ 2^π(z). The main term ~ e^(−γ)x/log z (Mertens) is
     only useful when 2^π(z) is smaller, i.e. z ≈ log x: π(x) ≪ x/log log x.
     BRUN replaced μ(d) by μ(d)𝟙_{ω(d)≤2k} (Bonferroni: truncations at even
     2k give upper bounds, odd give lower bounds). This allows z = x^(c/log
     log x). For twin primes sieve 𝒜 = {n(n + 2) : n ≤ x}, where each odd p
     removes 2 residue classes: main term x∏_{2<p≤z}(1 − 2/p) ≍ x/log²z,
     giving π₂(x) ≪ x(log log x)²/log²x. By partial summation ∑_{twin p}
     1/p = ∫ dπ₂(t)/t converges (Brun's constant ≈ 1.902). Data: π₂(10³) =
     35, π₂(10⁵) = 1224 twin pairs. Common mistakes: counting 1 as a prime
     or forgetting to add back the sieving primes themselves; believing a
     sieve with z = √x immediately gives an asymptotic (the error term
     2^π(z) is astronomically large). Now you try: compute Φ(120; 2, 3, 5,
     7) by Legendre's formula and deduce π(120). (120 − 141 + 56 − 8 = 27;
     π(120) = 27 − 1 + 4 = 30)
D11. (a) Explain Selberg's Λ² upper-bound sieve and derive π(x) ≤ (2 +
     o(1))x/log x. Evaluate the bound x/log z + z² at x = 10⁶, z =
     √x/log x. (b) State the large sieve inequality and the
     Bombieri-Vinogradov theorem, and explain the parity problem.
     Answer: (a) S ≤ X/G(z) + ∑|λ_{d₁}λ_{d₂}r_{[d₁,d₂]}| with G(z) ≥ log z;
     at x = 10⁶: z ≈ 72.38, bound ≈ 233537 + 5239 ≈ 238777 (true π(10⁶) =
     78498). (b) see below.
     Teacher explains: (a) For any real λ_d (d | P(z), d < z) with λ₁ = 1,
        𝟙_{(n,P(z))=1} ≤ (∑_{d|(n,P(z))} λ_d)²,
     since the right side is 1 when (n, P) = 1 and ≥ 0 always. Summing over
     n ∈ 𝒜 with |𝒜_d| = Xg(d) + r_d (g multiplicative),
        S(𝒜, z) ≤ X∑_{d₁,d₂} λ_{d₁}λ_{d₂}g([d₁, d₂])
                  + ∑|λ_{d₁}λ_{d₂}||r_{[d₁,d₂]}|.
     Minimising the quadratic form subject to λ₁ = 1 (diagonalise it) gives
     the value 1/G(z) with G(z) = ∑_{d<z, d|P} μ²(d)∏_{p|d} g(p)/(1 − g(p)),
     and the optimal λ satisfy |λ_d| ≤ 1. For 𝒜 = [1, x], g(p) = 1/p and
     G(z) = ∑_{d<z} μ²(d)/φ(d) ≥ ∑_{n<z} 1/n ≥ log z (expand 1/φ(d) =
     (1/d)∏(1 − 1/p)⁻¹ = ∑ 1/n over n with squarefree kernel d); |r_d| ≤
     1. So π(x) ≤ z + x/log z + z². With z = √x/log x this is x/(½log x −
     log log x) + O(x/log²x) = (2 + o(1))x/log x. At x = 10⁶: z = 72.382,
     log z = 4.28196, so x/log z + z² ≈ 233537 + 5239 = 238777 — a true
     but crude bound. For twin primes (g(p) = 2/p) the same machine gives
     π₂(x) ≤ (8C₂ + o(1))x/log²x, four times the Hardy-Littlewood
     prediction 2C₂x/log²x, C₂ = ∏_{p>2}(1 − (p − 1)⁻²) ≈ 0.66016; and
     BRUN-TITCHMARSH π(x; q, a) ≤ (2 + o(1))x/(φ(q)log(x/q)).
     (b) LARGE SIEVE: for any complex aₙ, M < n ≤ M + N,
        ∑_{q≤Q} ∑*_{a mod q} |∑ₙ aₙ e(an/q)|² ≤ (N − 1 + Q²)∑|aₙ|²
     (the points a/q are Q⁻²-spaced; duality + Sobolev/Gallagher). Combined
     with Vaughan's identity it proves BOMBIERI-VINOGRADOV: for each A
     there is B with ∑_{q≤√x(log x)⁻ᴮ} max_{(a,q)=1} |ψ(x; q, a) − x/φ(q)|
     ≪ x(log x)⁻ᴬ — as good as GRH on average over moduli up to √x ("level
     of distribution ½"; Elliott-Halberstam conjectures any level < 1).
     PARITY PROBLEM (Selberg): sieve axioms cannot distinguish integers
     with an even number of prime factors from those with an odd number,
     so pure sieves give upper bounds for primes but no positive lower
     bound; the best is Chen: every large even n = p + P₂ (P₂ having ≤ 2
     prime factors). Zhang (2013) and Maynard used level > ½ (smooth
     moduli) or multidimensional weights to get bounded gaps between
     primes (liminf (p_{n+1} − pₙ) ≤ 246). Common mistakes: thinking λ_d
     = μ(d) is optimal (that is Legendre, with huge error); forgetting
     that the error sum runs over d up to z², so z ≈ √X at most. Now you
     try: for g(p) = 1/p compute G(6) and compare with log 6. (d = 1, 2,
     3, 5: 1 + 1 + ½ + ¼ = 2.75 ≥ 1.79)

D12. (Ternary Goldbach by the circle method.) For odd n put R(n) =
     ∑_{p₁+p₂+p₃=n} log p₁ log p₂ log p₃. (a) Write R(n) as an integral of
     S(α) = ∑_{p≤n} log p e(pα). (b) Explain the major arc computation that
     produces ½𝔖(n)n², with 𝔖(n) = ∏_{p|n}(1 − (p − 1)⁻²)∏_{p∤n}(1 + (p −
     1)⁻³), and evaluate 𝔖(15). (c) Explain the minor arc bound and why the
     binary problem fails. (d) Why is 𝔖(n) = 0 for even n?
     Answer: (a) R(n) = ∫₀¹ S(α)³e(−nα) dα; (b) 𝔖(15) ≈ 1.41598;
     (c) sup_𝔪|S| ≪ n(log n)⁻ᴬ and ∫|S|² ≪ n log n; (d) the p = 2 factor
     is 1 − 1 = 0.
     Teacher explains: (a) ∫₀¹ e(mα) dα = 1 if m = 0 and 0 otherwise, so
     expanding S³ picks out exactly p₁ + p₂ + p₃ = n. (b) MAJOR ARCS: 𝔐 =
     ⋃ {α : |α − a/q| ≤ (log n)ᴮ/n}, q ≤ (log n)ᴮ, (a, q) = 1. Near a/q,
     split p by residue r mod q: ∑_{p≡r} log p e(pa/q)e(pβ) and Siegel-
     Walfisz (primes equidistributed mod small q) give S(a/q + β) ≈
     (μ(q)/φ(q))v(β), v(β) = ∑_{m≤n} e(mβ), because ∑*_{r mod q} e(ra/q) =
     μ(q) (a Ramanujan sum). Integrating over the arcs,
        ∫_𝔐 S³e(−nα) ≈ ∑_q (μ(q)/φ(q)³) c_q(n) · ∫_R v(β)³e(−nβ) dβ
     and the SINGULAR INTEGRAL is #{m₁ + m₂ + m₃ = n} ≈ n²/2. The SINGULAR
     SERIES ∑_q μ(q)c_q(n)/φ(q)³ is multiplicative: the factor at p is
     1 − c_p(n)/(p − 1)³, and c_p(n) = p − 1 if p | n, −1 if p ∤ n. So p | n
     gives 1 − 1/(p − 1)², p ∤ n gives 1 + 1/(p − 1)³. For n = 15: p = 2
     gives 2, p = 3 gives ¾, p = 5 gives 15/16, and ∏_{p≥7}(1 + (p − 1)⁻³)
     ≈ 1.00692; 𝔖(15) ≈ 2·0.75·0.9375·1.00692 ≈ 1.41598. For odd n,
     𝔖(n) ≥ 2C₂ ≈ 1.32 > 0. (c) MINOR ARCS 𝔪 = [0, 1] ∖ 𝔐: VINOGRADOV
     (today via Vaughan's identity and bilinear sums) gives sup_𝔪 |S(α)| ≪
     n(log n)⁻ᴬ for any A (B chosen large), and Parseval gives ∫₀¹|S|² = ∑_{p≤n}
     log²p ≪ n log n. Hence |∫_𝔪 S³e(−nα)| ≤ sup_𝔪|S|·∫|S|² = o(n²), and
     R(n) = ½𝔖(n)n² + o(n²) > 0 for large odd n (Helfgott: all odd n ≥ 7
     are sums of three primes). For TWO primes the main term is n but the
     trivial minor-arc bound ∫_𝔪|S|² ≤ n log n is already bigger: one power
     of S is not enough to exploit cancellation. (d) At p = 2, c₂(n) =
     1 for n even, so the local factor is 1 − 1/(2 − 1)³ = 0: three odd
     primes cannot sum to an even number (the local obstruction mod 2).
     Common mistakes: forgetting the weights log p (the unweighted count is
     ~ 𝔖(n)n²/(2log³n)); thinking the major arcs carry most of [0, 1] (they
     have tiny measure but carry the main term). Now you try: the local
     factor at p = 3 for n = 21, and for n = 25. (¾; 1 + ⅛ = 9/8)

M6. Zeros of ζ and the explicit formula.
    a) the ordinates γ₁, γ₂, γ₃ of the first three zeros
    b) N(T) from Riemann-von Mangoldt (without S(T)) for T = 50, 100, 1000;

    d) if ½ + 0.1 + 20i were a zero, list three more zeros

    f) the amplitude bound 2x^β/|ρ| of the first pair at x = 10⁴
    g) the constants in the explicit formula: log 2π and −½log(1 − x⁻²) at
       x = 10
    h) ∑_ρ 1/ρ (symmetric summation) = 1 + γ/2 − ½log 4π
    i) convexity, Weyl and best known exponents μ in ζ(½ + it) ≪ t^(μ+ε)
    j) the Lindelöf hypothesis value of μ; does RH imply it?
    k) the order of ξ(s) as an entire function
    l) which explicit-formula term is responsible for Chebyshev's bias?
    Answers:
      a) 14.13473, 21.02204, 25.01086
      b) 9.42, 29.00, 648.62
      c) ≈ 0.5246
      d) 0.6 − 20i, 0.4 + 20i, 0.4 − 20i (ρ̄, 1 − ρ̄, 1 − ρ)
      e) σ > 1 − 1/(10 log 102) ≈ 0.97838
      f) 2·100/14.1347 ≈ 14.15
      g) 1.83788 and 0.00503
      h) ≈ 0.02310
      i) ¼ (convexity), 1/6 (Weyl-Hardy-Littlewood), 13/84 (Bourgain)
      j) μ = 0; yes, RH ⇒ Lindelöf
      k) 1 (maximal order; ξ has infinitely many zeros, ∑1/|ρ| = ∞)
      l) the prime squares: every odd p² ≡ 1 (mod 4), so θ(x; 4, 1) −
         θ(x; 4, 3) ≈ −√x + (a sum over zeros of L(s, χ₋₄) with mean 0);
         hence usually π(x; 4, 3) > π(x; 4, 1)

M7. Dirichlet characters.
    a) the number of characters mod 7, 12, 16, 100
    b) the number of PRIMITIVE characters mod 2, 4, 7, 8, 9, 12, 15, 16, 25
    c) the number of REAL characters mod 7, 8, 15, 16, 24
    d) χ mod 7 with χ(3) = e(1/6) (3 is a generator): χ(2), χ(4), χ(6); is
       χ even or odd?
    e) χ₋₃(n) for n = 1, ..., 6
    f) χ₈(7), χ₋₈(3), χ₋₄(99), χ₅(4)
    g) ∑_{n=1}^{q} χ(n) for χ ≠ χ₀; ∑_{χ mod q} χ(n) for n ≢ 1
    h) ∑_{χ mod 8} χ(3)χ(5)
    i) the order of χ₁ mod 5 (χ₁(2) = i)
    j) the conductor of χ₋₄ regarded mod 12, and of (·/3) regarded mod 12
    k) the real non-principal character mod 9 (χ(2) = −1): primitive?
    l) for which odd primes p is χ₈(p) = (2/p) = 1?
    m) the modulus of a primitive character with χ(−1) = −1 of smallest
       modulus
    Answers:
      a) 6, 4, 8, 40
      b) 0, 1, 5, 2, 4, 1, 3, 4, 16
      c) 2, 4, 4, 4 ((Z/16)* ≅ Z/2 × Z/4), 8
      d) e(1/3), e(2/3), −1 (6 ≡ 3³); odd
      e) 1, −1, 0, 1, −1, 0
      f) 1, 1, −1, 1
      g) 0; 0
      h) 0 (∑_χ χ(15) = ∑_χ χ(7) and 7 ≢ 1 mod 8)
      i) 4
      j) 4; 3
      k) no: it is induced by (·/3), conductor 3
      l) p ≡ ±1 (mod 8)
      m) 3 (χ₋₃)

      m) 1; 1                   n) ≈ 219.7

M9. Primes in arithmetic progressions.
    a) the proportion of primes ≡ 1 (mod 4), ≡ 1 (mod 10), ≡ 3 (mod 7)
    b) the predicted π(1000; 10, a) for a = 1, 3, 7, 9, and the true values
    c) π(10⁴; 4, 1) and π(10⁴; 4, 3)
    d) π(1000; 3, 1) and π(1000; 3, 2)
    e) the least prime ≡ 1 mod 5, 7, 11, 13, 23

       the true value
    g) the GRH error term in ψ(x; q, a)

       (ignore log powers)
    j) Linnik's theorem: the least prime ≡ a (mod q) is ≪ q^L; best L?
    k) the Dirichlet density of {p : p ≡ 1 (mod 8)}
    l) the natural density of primes p for which 2 is a quadratic residue
    Answers:

      b) about 166/4 = 41.5 each; true 40, 42, 46, 38
      c) 609, 619 (bias to non-residues)    d) 80, 87
      e) 11, 29, 23, 53, 47
      f) ≈ 78636; true 39231 (the bound is off by the factor 2)
      g) O(√x log²x), uniformly in q
      h) L(1, χ₋d) ≫_ε d^(−ε), h(−d) ≫_ε d^(½−ε) (ineffective)
      i) q ≤ (log x)ᴬ; q ≤ √x = 10⁵
      j) L = 5 (Xylouris); GRH gives any L > 2
      k) ¼                                   l) ½ (p ≡ ±1 mod 8)

       Littlewood's s ≥ 2ᵏ + 1 for k = 3, 4

      f) 4, 9, 19, 4, 16; 23 and 239

         0.01429)

      n) 190569292 vs ≈ 1.99281 × 10⁸ (4.6% high)

M12. True (T) or false (F)? Give a counterexample or reason for F.
     a) ζ(s) ≠ 0 for Re s > 1.
     b) ζ(s) has no zeros with Re s < 0.
     c) ∑_p 1/p converges.
     d) ∑ 1/p over twin primes p converges.
     e) L(s, χ) has a pole at s = 1 for every χ mod q.
     f) L(1, χ) = 0 is possible for a real character χ ≠ χ₀.
     g) For a real primitive χ mod q, τ(χ)² = χ(−1)q.
     h) |τ(χ)| = √q for every character mod q.
     i) π(x) < li(x) for all x ≥ 3.
     j) The PNT is equivalent to M(x) = ∑_{n≤x} μ(n) = o(x).
     k) The Legendre sieve with z = √x gives π(x) ~ x/log x.
     l) Selberg's sieve alone gives π(x) ≫ x/log x.
     m) Every odd n ≥ 7 is a sum of three primes.
     n) Bombieri-Vinogradov implies GRH.
     o) The non-trivial zeros are symmetric about Re s = ½.
     p) A Dirichlet series with non-negative coefficients can be analytic
        at its real abscissa of convergence.
     q) Siegel's theorem is effective.
     r) The Euler product for ζ converges at s = ½.
     s) RH implies ψ(x) = x + O(x^(½+ε)).
     t) The Selberg sieve upper bound for twin primes matches the
        Hardy-Littlewood conjecture.
     Answers:
       a) T (Euler product)       b) F: −2, −4, ...
       c) F (Euler)               d) T (Brun)
       e) F: only for χ₀; L(s, χ) is entire for χ ≠ χ₀
       f) F (Dirichlet; the ζ·L trick with Landau)
       g) T (τ(χ)τ(χ̄) = χ(−1)q)  h) F: τ(χ₀ mod 7) = −1
       i) F: Littlewood, sign changes infinitely often
       j) T                       k) F: x∏_{p≤√x}(1 − 1/p) ~ 2e^(−γ)x/log x
                                     ≈ 1.123x/log x; the error dominates
       l) F: parity problem (Selberg's λ(n) example)
       m) T (Vinogradov for large n; Helfgott for all)
       n) F: it is an average over q only
       o) T (ξ(s) = ξ(1 − s) and ξ(s̄) = ξ(s)‾)
       p) F (Landau's theorem)    q) F: the constant is ineffective
       r) F: ∏(1 − p^(−½))⁻¹ diverges (∑p^(−½) = ∞)
       s) T                       t) F: it is 4 times too large

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: keep ONE dictionary on the board — "primes ↔ Λ ↔ −ζ′/ζ,
  squarefree ↔ μ² ↔ ζ(s)/ζ(2s), progressions ↔ characters ↔ L(s, χ)" —
  and translate every question into it before computing. Drill partial
  summation until it is automatic, since nearly every asymptotic in M5
  is one line of it. For the functional equation, have students first
  compute ζ(−1) and ζ(−3) mechanically, then see the theta proof. For
  sieves, do Legendre by hand for x = 100 before any theory: the error
  term 2^π(z) and Mertens' 2e^(−γ) then explain why smarter sieves exist.
  For the circle method, start from ∫₀¹ e(mα) dα = 𝟙_{m=0} and compute
  local factors p by p. Harder: prove the zero-free region
  quantitatively and deduce the de la Vallée Poussin error term; prove
  Siegel's theorem and explain its ineffectivity; derive the large sieve
  via Gallagher's lemma and deduce Bombieri-Vinogradov from Vaughan's
  identity; prove Selberg's G(z) ≥ log z carefully and the Brun-
  Titchmarsh inequality; carry out Vinogradov's minor arc estimate; and
  read Maynard's bounded gaps proof as a multidimensional Selberg sieve.
